# Course overview  

# Course overview

## **Course Title**

**Stagehand Fundamentals:** How to Work Your First Load-In, Show Call, and Load-Out

**Alternative titles:**

**The Crew Blueprint:** Entry-Level Stagehand Training  
 Concert Crew Basics: A Beginner’s Guide to Live Event Production  
 Stagehand 101: Safety, Gear, Load-In, Load-Out, and Crew Etiquette

**My favorite for a public marketplace is:**

**Stagehand Fundamentals:** How to Work Your First Load-In, Show Call, and Load-Out

It is clear, searchable, and beginner-friendly.

## **Course Positioning**

This should be an entry-level career-readiness course, not an official OSHA course.

I would include OSHA-style safety awareness, worker rights, PPE, hazard recognition, and jobsite expectations, but avoid claiming it gives an OSHA card or certification. OSHA says Outreach courses are basic hazard-awareness classes and are not considered certifications or licenses; OSHA cards come from OSHA-authorized trainers after completing 10-hour or 30-hour Outreach classes.

**A good disclaimer would be:**

This course is a stagehand orientation and safety-awareness course. It does not replace employer-provided safety training, site-specific training, union training, equipment certification, or an OSHA 10/30 course.

That is important because OSHA states that employers are responsible for training workers on the specific hazards of their job, and Outreach training does not fulfill training requirements found in OSHA standards.

# **Full Course Plan**

## **Course Length**

**For a marketplace course, I would aim for:**

6 to 8 modules  
 35 to 50 short lessons  
 4 to 6 hours of video  
 Downloadable checklists and quick-reference guides  
 End-of-course quiz or practical self-assessment

This is deep enough to feel valuable but not so long that beginners get overwhelmed.

# **Module 1: Welcome to the Live Event World**

**Purpose:** Give learners the big picture before they walk into a venue.

This module should explain what stagehands do, where they fit in, and what kinds of calls they may work.

### **Lessons**

What a Stagehand Actually Does

The Difference Between Load-In, Show Call, and Load-Out

**Common Work Environments:** Arenas, Theaters, Convention Centers, Outdoor Shows

**Who Is on the Crew:** Leads, Department Heads, Riggers, Audio, Lighting, Video, Carpenters, Truck Loaders

What Makes a New Stagehand Valuable

What This Course Will and Will Not Teach You

### **Key Outcome**

By the end, learners should understand that being a stagehand is not just “moving boxes.” It is working safely, following instructions, communicating clearly, and supporting a much larger production.

# **Module 2: Safety Mindset Before Skillset**

**Purpose:** Introduce safety as the foundation of being employable.

This is where your OSHA idea fits, but I would frame it as jobsite safety awareness for live events, not “OSHA training.”

OSHA emphasizes that workers have the right to a safe workplace, training in a language they understand, required safety equipment, and the ability to speak up about hazards without retaliation.

### **Lessons**

Why Safety Comes Before Speed

Worker Rights and Employer Responsibilities

The Difference Between Hazard, Risk, and Unsafe Behavior

**Stop Work Mindset:** When to Ask Questions or Get a Lead

Incident Reporting and Near Misses

Why You Should Never Pretend You Know Something

How to Listen During a Safety Briefing

### **Practical Activity**

Learner identifies hazards from example jobsite scenarios:

Open cable path

Forklift moving nearby

Stacked road cases

Wet floor

Working under rigging

Poor lighting during load-out

### **Key Outcome**

Learners should understand that safety awareness is part of professionalism, not a separate topic.

# **Module 3: PPE, Clothing, and What to Bring**

**Purpose:** Prepare learners to show up dressed correctly and equipped.

This is one of the most useful beginner modules because many new people fail before the work even starts because they are not dressed right.

OSHA notes that workers should use required safety gear and receive training on it, and employers are generally responsible for required PPE in many cases.

### **Lessons**

**Show Blacks:** What They Are and Why They Matter

Work Boots, Gloves, Hard Hats, High-Vis, Eye Protection, and Hearing Protection

What to Bring in Your Work Bag

What Not to Bring

Hydration, Food, and Long-Call Preparation

**Personal Readiness:** Sleep, Transportation, and Call Time Discipline

How to Avoid Looking Unprepared on Day One

### **Downloadable**

New Stagehand Call Checklist

**Include:**

Black shirt

Black pants

Work boots

Gloves

Water

Snacks

ID

Phone charger

Pen/Sharpie

Small flashlight/headlamp

Weather layer if outdoor

Required PPE if assigned

### **Key Outcome**

Learners should know exactly how to show up for their first call.

# **Module 4: Venue and Jobsite Awareness**

**Purpose:** Teach learners how to move through a venue without becoming a hazard.

This is where many new stagehands struggle. They do not know where to stand, where not to stand, how to stay clear, or how to read the room.

### **Lessons**

The First 15 Minutes on Site

How to Find Your Lead or Department

Stage, Dock, Truck, Ramp, Floor, Backstage, FOH, and Barricade Basics

Restricted Areas and Why They Matter

Forklifts, Carts, Pallet Jacks, Pushers, and Moving Equipment

Overhead Hazards and Working Under Rigging

Cable Paths, Trip Hazards, and Clean Work Areas

Emergency Exits, Fire Lanes, and Keeping Paths Clear

### **Practical Activity**

**Learner reviews a simple venue map and identifies:**

Safe walking routes

Truck dock

Stage

Emergency exits

High-traffic zones

Areas to avoid without direction

### **Key Outcome**

Learners should know how to enter a site, observe, and avoid creating problems.

# **Module 5: Load-In Fundamentals**

**Purpose:** Teach the basic rhythm of a load-in.

This is one of the core modules. Instead of making “load-in/load-out” one broad module, I would split them. Load-in and load-out feel different, move at different speeds, and have different risks.

### **Lessons**

What Load-In Means

Truck Unload Basics

**Road Cases:** How They Move, Stack, Lock, and Tip

Pushing vs. Pulling vs. Guiding Cases

Ramps, Dock Plates, and Pinch Points

**Building the Room:** Stage, Audio, Lighting, Video, Backline, Rigging

**Department Sorting:** Getting the Right Case to the Right Place

**Cable Awareness:** Feeder, Socapex, Audio Lines, Data, and General Cable Paths

When to Move Fast and When to Slow Down

How to Take Direction Without Needing Everything Explained Twice

### **Practical Activity**

“Load-In Flow Exercise”  
 Learner puts basic tasks in order:

Check in

Safety briefing

Truck unload

Sort cases

Stage build

Department setup

Cable runs

Final cleanup

### **Key Outcome**

Learners should understand the flow of a load-in well enough to be useful, even if they are brand new.

# **Module 6: Stagehand Communication and Crew Etiquette**

**Purpose:** Teach the unspoken rules.

This may become one of your most valuable modules. New stagehands often fail because of attitude, poor communication, phone use, wandering, or not knowing how to receive direction.

### **Lessons**

How to Talk to Leads and Department Heads

The Right Way to Ask Questions

Radio Basics and Common Callouts

Hand Signals and Nonverbal Communication

“Copy,” “Heard,” “Standing By,” and Other Useful Responses

Phone Etiquette on a Call

How to Stay Busy Without Freelancing

How to Handle Correction Without Taking It Personally

Respecting Touring Crew, Local Crew, and Venue Staff

What Gets You Asked Back

### **Downloadable**

Stagehand Communication Cheat Sheet

**Include phrases like:**

“Who is my lead?”

“Where do you want this case?”

“Can you show me the safe way to do that?”

“Copy.”

“Standing by.”

“I have not been trained on that.”

“Do you want this staged or opened?”

### **Key Outcome**

Learners should know how to behave like someone leads want to keep around.

# **Module 7: Department Basics**

**Purpose:** Give learners a simple overview of the major departments without pretending to fully train them in each one.

This module helps students understand what is happening around them.

### **Lessons**

Audio Department Basics

Lighting Department Basics

Video Department Basics

Staging and Carpentry Basics

Rigging Awareness for Non-Riggers

Backline and Instruments

Props, Wardrobe, and Production Support

Why You Should Not Touch Department Gear Without Direction

How to Help Without Getting in the Way

### **Important Positioning**

This should be awareness-level only. For example, do not teach people to rig, operate lifts, run power, or perform specialized work without proper hands-on training and employer authorization.

### **Key Outcome**

Learners should understand the language and basic purpose of each department.

# **Module 8: Tools, Gear, and Handling Basics**

**Purpose:** Teach practical handling skills and basic gear recognition.

This is a “confidence builder” module. Beginners feel less lost when they can recognize common equipment.

### **Lessons**

Common Stagehand Tools

C-Wrenches, Multitools, Flashlights, Tape, and Markers

**Road Case Parts:** Lids, Casters, Brakes, Labels, Handles

Cable Coiling Basics

**Tape Basics:** Gaff Tape vs. Electrical Tape vs. Spike Tape

Pipe, Drape, Decks, Truss, Motors, Cases, and Carts

Lifting and Team Carry Basics

Pinch Points, Crush Points, and Stored Energy

Keeping Gear Organized

### **Practical Activity**

**Gear ID quiz:**

Road case

Truss

Deck

Cable ramp

Motor

Shackles

Gaff tape

Feeder cable

Audio snake

Barricade

### **Key Outcome**

Learners should recognize common items and understand when they should ask before touching something.

# **Module 9: Load-Out Fundamentals**

**Purpose:** Teach the faster, more chaotic side of the job.

Load-out needs its own module because it is often where fatigue, speed, and poor communication create problems.

### **Lessons**

What Makes Load-Out Different From Load-In

**Strike Order:** Why Everything Comes Down in a Sequence

Staying Alert When Everyone Is Tired

Case Packing and Label Matching

Cable Strike and Coil Discipline

Truck Pack Awareness

Trash, Tape, Sweep, and Final Walkthroughs

Avoiding “Hurry Up and Get Hurt” Mistakes

How to Finish Strong

### **Practical Activity**

Learner reviews a load-out scenario and identifies unsafe choices:

Running with cases

Pulling cable without checking the other end

Blocking dock doors

Standing behind a rolling case on a ramp

Leaving trash/tape behind

### **Key Outcome**

Learners should understand that load-out is not just “tear it down fast.” It is controlled, sequenced, and safety-focused.

# **Module 10: Getting Hired, Getting Called Back, and Growing**

**Purpose:** Help learners turn training into work.

This makes the course more valuable than simple jobsite instruction. It gives learners a path forward.

### **Lessons**

Where Stagehand Work Comes From

Labor Companies, Venues, Unions, Crewing Apps, and Referrals

What to Put on a Beginner Resume

How to Talk About Limited Experience Honestly

**Reliability:** The Skill That Gets You Rebooked

Building a Reputation

Moving From General Stagehand to Department Specialty

**Next Skills to Learn:** OSHA 10, Forklift Awareness, Rigging Awareness, Audio, Lighting, Video, Carpentry

Your First 30-Day Development Plan

### **Downloadable**

First 30 Days as a Stagehand Plan

**Week 1:** Learn call etiquette, PPE, basic terms  
 Week 2: Practice communication, case handling, cable awareness  
 Week 3: Learn department names and basic gear  
 Week 4: Ask leads for feedback and identify next skill path

### **Key Outcome**

Learners leave with an action plan, not just information.

# **Suggested Final Course Structure**

**I would package it like this:**

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Module | Title | Purpose |
| 1 | Welcome to the Live Event World | Understand the job and the industry |
| 2 | Safety Mindset Before Skillset | OSHA-style awareness, rights, hazards |
| 3 | PPE, Clothing, and What to Bring | Show up prepared |
| 4 | Venue and Jobsite Awareness | Move safely through the worksite |
| 5 | Load-In Fundamentals | Understand the beginning of the call |
| 6 | Communication and Crew Etiquette | Learn how to be useful and professional |
| 7 | Department Basics | Understand audio, lighting, video, staging, rigging awareness |
| 8 | Tools, Gear, and Handling Basics | Recognize and handle common equipment |
| 9 | Load-Out Fundamentals | Finish the call safely and effectively |
| 10 | Getting Hired and Getting Called Back | Turn the training into real work |

# **Course Promise**

**A strong course promise would be:**

By the end of this course, learners will understand how live event crews work, how to prepare for a call, how to recognize common jobsite hazards, how load-ins and load-outs flow, how to communicate with leads, and how to show up as the kind of entry-level stagehand people want to call back.

# Module 3

# **Module 3: PPE, Clothing, and What to Bring**

## **Module Purpose**

This module prepares new stagehands to show up dressed correctly, equipped properly, and ready for the realities of a live event call.

Many new workers struggle before the work even starts because they arrive late, wear the wrong clothing, forget basic items, misunderstand PPE expectations, or fail to prepare for a long physical day.

This module does not replace employer-provided safety training, site-specific training, union training, venue rules, equipment certification, or OSHA training. Learners should always follow the call sheet, employer instructions, venue policies, and direction from their lead.

## **Module Learning Objective**

By the end of this module, the learner should be able to explain how to dress for a call, identify common PPE, prepare a basic work bag, avoid bringing inappropriate items, and show up ready for their first day of stagehand work.

## **Key Outcome**

Learners should know exactly how to prepare for their first call so they arrive looking professional, ready to work, and able to follow site expectations safely.

# Lesson 3.1

# **Module 3, Lesson 1: Show Blacks: What They Are and Why They Matter**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to the idea of “show blacks” and explains why clothing matters in live event work. The goal is to help beginners understand that dressing correctly is part of professionalism, safety, and respecting the production environment.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain what show blacks are, why they are commonly required, and how to dress appropriately for a stagehand call.

“Show blacks” usually means plain black, work-appropriate clothing worn by stagehands, technicians, and backstage crew.

Black clothing helps crew members blend into backstage, stage, and performance environments so they do not distract from the show.

Exact clothing requirements can vary depending on the employer, venue, event type, call type, and work area.

For load-in and load-out, clothing must do more than look correct. It must also allow safe movement, protect the body, and avoid creating hazards.

Site safety requirements always come before appearance standards. If a site requires high-visibility clothing, a hard hat, safety vest, or other PPE, those safety requirements override the expectation to wear all black.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

In live production, what you wear matters before you ever touch a road case.

Many stagehand calls require what people call “show blacks.” This usually means a plain black shirt, black pants, and dark work-appropriate footwear.

The purpose is simple: crew members are not supposed to draw attention away from the show. If you are backstage, near the stage, or visible to the audience, black clothing helps you blend into the background.

Show blacks also help the crew look organized and professional. When everyone is dressed appropriately, the production looks cleaner and more intentional.

But show blacks are not just about looking the part. Your clothing also needs to be safe for the job.

Stagehand work can involve pushing road cases, lifting gear, walking long distances, working around cables, moving through tight spaces, and being near carts, forklifts, barricade, staging, and other equipment. Your clothing should let you move safely and should not get caught on handles, casters, corners, cables, or hardware.

Avoid loose dangling clothing, open-toed shoes, sandals, jewelry that can snag, and anything that makes it hard to move safely.

A basic beginner outfit might include a plain black t-shirt or long-sleeve shirt, black work pants, and work boots. Outdoor calls may require layers, rain gear, sun protection, or weather-appropriate clothing.

Some calls may require high-visibility clothing instead of all black, especially around trucks, docks, forklifts, outdoor sites, or low-light work areas. If the site requires high-vis or other PPE, follow the site requirement.

The most important rule is to read the call details. If the employer says show blacks, wear show blacks. If the employer says high-vis is required, wear high-vis. If you are unsure, ask before the call.

Showing up dressed correctly tells the crew that you paid attention, you respect the work, and you came prepared.

#### ***What Counts as Good Show Blacks***

Good choices usually include:

● Plain black t-shirt or black long-sleeve shirt  
 ● Black work pants or black durable pants  
 ● Black or dark work boots  
 ● Clothing that fits securely and allows movement  
 ● Weather-appropriate layers when needed  
 ● Rain gear, warm layers, or sun protection for outdoor calls when appropriate

#### ***What to Avoid***

Avoid:

● Bright colors unless specifically assigned  
 ● Large logos or distracting graphics  
 ● Shorts unless your employer clearly allows them  
 ● Sandals, slides, open-toed shoes, or fashion sneakers  
 ● Loose scarves, dangling jewelry, or clothing that can snag  
 ● Clothing that is too tight to move in safely  
 ● Clothing that is too baggy and can catch on gear  
 ● Clothing with offensive language or graphics

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Check the call instructions before arriving and follow the required clothing and PPE rules for the specific site.

Show blacks are common in live production, but safety requirements come first. If the call requires high-vis, hard hats, eye protection, hearing protection, or other PPE, follow the site rules. Do not ignore required PPE just to stay in all black.

Show blacks help the crew look professional and stay visually out of the way, but clothing must also be safe, durable, and appropriate for the work being performed.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Show blacks are part of stagehand professionalism. Dress in a way that matches the call, protects your body, allows safe movement, and shows the crew that you came prepared.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I were called for my first stagehand job tomorrow, would my clothing make me look prepared or unprepared?

Question: Why do stagehands often wear show blacks?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stagehands often wear show blacks so they can look professional, blend into backstage or performance environments, and avoid distracting from the show while still wearing clothing that is safe and practical for the work.

# **Module 3, Lesson 2: Work Boots, Gloves, Hard Hats, High-Vis, Eye Protection, and Hearing Protection**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to common PPE and protective gear used in live event work. The goal is to help beginners recognize common protective equipment and understand why it may be required on a jobsite.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common PPE used by stagehands and explain why each item may be required on a call.

PPE stands for personal protective equipment.

Common PPE in stagehand work may include work boots, gloves, hard hats, high-visibility clothing, eye protection, and hearing protection.

Not every item is required on every call. PPE requirements depend on the employer, venue, work area, hazards, department, and assigned task.

PPE must fit properly, be in good condition, and be used as instructed.

PPE does not replace awareness, training, or safe work practices. It is one layer of protection.

If required PPE is missing, damaged, does not fit, or you do not know how to use it, stop and notify your lead or supervisor before starting the task.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

PPE is one of the first things a new stagehand needs to understand.

PPE means personal protective equipment. It is the gear that helps protect you from hazards on the job.

In live event work, PPE may include work boots, gloves, hard hats, high-vis clothing, eye protection, hearing protection, or other equipment required by the site.

Work boots help provide support, traction, and basic foot protection. Stagehands may spend long hours on concrete, push heavy cases, walk through loading docks, move across uneven outdoor ground, or work around rolling equipment.

Some sites may require protective or safety-toe footwear when there is danger from falling objects, rolling objects, puncture hazards, or other jobsite hazards. Do not assume regular sneakers are acceptable unless the call details clearly say they are.

Gloves help protect your hands from rough cases, sharp edges, splinters, cable, metal, road case handles, and pinch points. Gloves do not make your hands invincible, but they can reduce common cuts, scrapes, and friction injuries.

Gloves should match the task. Do not assume one pair of gloves is right for every job. Around moving machinery, rotating parts, or tasks requiring fine control, ask what hand protection is appropriate.

Hard hats may be required where there is potential for head injury from falling objects, overhead work, lifts, rigging activity, temporary structures, or other site-specific hazards. If the site requires a hard hat, wear it correctly and keep it on until you are told the area is clear.

High-visibility clothing helps other workers see you, especially around trucks, forklifts, carts, pallet jacks, loading docks, outdoor sites, low light, and moving equipment. Sometimes high-vis is more important than show blacks. Follow the site requirement.

Eye protection may be required when there is dust, debris, flying particles, overhead work, tool use, or other eye hazards. Use site-approved eye protection and wear it as instructed.

Hearing protection may be needed around loud concerts, amplified audio, generators, machinery, or other high-noise areas. Do not assume cotton, earbuds, or headphones are proper hearing protection unless the site has approved them for that purpose.

As a beginner, your job is not to guess what PPE is required. Your job is to read the call instructions, follow site rules, use PPE correctly, and ask questions when you are unsure.

#### ***Common PPE and Why It Matters***

#### ***Work Boots***

Work boots help provide support, traction, and foot protection during long physical calls. Some sites may require safety-toe or other protective footwear.

#### ***Gloves***

Gloves help protect your hands from rough materials, sharp edges, friction, and some pinch-point exposure. Gloves must match the task.

#### ***Hard Hat***

A hard hat helps protect your head in areas with overhead hazards, falling-object hazards, lifts, rigging activity, or site-specific hard hat requirements.

#### ***High-Visibility Clothing***

High-vis clothing makes you easier to see around trucks, forklifts, loading docks, outdoor sites, vehicle movement, and low-light conditions.

#### ***Eye Protection***

Eye protection helps protect your eyes from dust, debris, flying particles, tool-related hazards, and other site-specific eye hazards.

#### ***Hearing Protection***

Hearing protection helps protect your hearing in loud environments such as concerts, amplified events, generator areas, and other high-noise work zones.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Check the call instructions before arriving and follow the required clothing and PPE rules for the specific site.

#### ***Important PPE Habits***

● Read the call instructions before arriving  
 ● Use PPE required by the site  
 ● Inspect PPE before using it  
 ● Do not use damaged PPE  
 ● Make sure PPE fits properly  
 ● Wear PPE the way it is designed to be worn  
 ● Do not modify PPE  
 ● Ask if you do not know how to use something  
 ● Report missing, damaged, or poorly fitting PPE  
 ● Do not remove required PPE until you are told it is safe to do so

Required PPE and PPE training are usually employer or site responsibilities, but workers also need to use required PPE correctly and report problems. If you are told PPE is required but you do not have it, do not quietly enter the work area and hope it is fine. Notify your lead or supervisor before starting the task.

PPE does not replace safe work practices. It supports them. The right PPE must be matched to the hazard, fit properly, be in good condition, and be used correctly.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

PPE helps protect you from jobsite hazards, but it only works when it is appropriate, undamaged, fitted correctly, and used as instructed. When in doubt, ask your lead.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I arrived at a call and saw everyone wearing hard hats and high-vis, would I know to stop and confirm what PPE is required before entering the work area?

Question: What should you do if required PPE is damaged, missing, does not fit, or you do not know how to use it?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stop and tell a lead or supervisor. Do not begin work that requires PPE until you understand what is required and have PPE that is appropriate, undamaged, properly fitted, and used correctly.

# **Module 3, Lesson 3: What to Bring in Your Work Bag**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson helps learners prepare a simple, practical work bag for their first stagehand call. The goal is to reduce beginner stress and help learners arrive ready for a long, physical, and sometimes unpredictable workday.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to list basic items to bring to a stagehand call and explain why each item is useful.

A prepared stagehand brings the basics needed to get through the call without becoming a burden on the crew.

The work bag should be practical, not overloaded.

Learners should bring personal items that support safety, hydration, communication, and readiness while still following venue and employer rules.

Learners should bring any clothing, footwear, basic items, or PPE that the call instructions tell them to bring. If a site requires PPE that they were not issued or do not have, they should notify the lead or employer before starting the task.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Your work bag does not need to be expensive or complicated. It just needs to help you get through the call.

Stagehand work can run long. You may be walking across a large venue, waiting between tasks, working late, dealing with weather, or moving between departments. A good work bag helps you stay ready without constantly needing to borrow basic items.

Start with the essentials.

Bring your ID. Some venues require identification for check-in, security, badges, parking, or payroll verification.

Bring water. Live event work can be physical, and you may not always have quick access to a water fountain or break area. A refillable water bottle is one of the easiest ways to stay ready.

Bring snacks or simple food that will not make a mess. Long calls can stretch past normal meal times. A protein bar, trail mix, fruit, or simple packed food can help you keep your energy up.

Bring a phone charger or battery pack. Your phone may be needed for call details, parking instructions, contact information, emergency communication, timekeeping, or schedule updates. Do not use your phone casually during work, but make sure it can stay charged.

Bring a pen and a Sharpie. These are simple tools, but they are useful for notes, labels, tape, paperwork, or quick reminders.

Bring a small flashlight or headlamp. Many venues have dark backstage areas, dim work zones, trucks, cable paths, or outdoor areas. A light helps you see without relying only on your phone.

Bring gloves if the call instructions tell you to bring them or if your employer expects you to provide basic work gloves. Some employers provide required PPE, and some expect workers to bring certain basic items. Follow the call instructions.

Bring weather layers for outdoor work. A hoodie, rain shell, hat, or extra socks may make a long outdoor call much more manageable.

The goal is not to bring every tool you own. The goal is to bring enough that you can take care of yourself, follow instructions, and avoid slowing down the crew.

#### ***Basic Beginner Work Bag Checklist***

● ID  
 ● Water bottle  
 ● Snacks or simple food  
 ● Phone charger or battery pack  
 ● Pen  
 ● Sharpie  
 ● Small flashlight or headlamp  
 ● Work gloves, if instructed or expected  
 ● Weather layer for outdoor calls  
 ● Extra socks for long, cold, or wet calls  
 ● Any required paperwork or call information  
 ● Any clothing, footwear, or PPE listed in the call instructions

#### ***Helpful But Optional Items***

● Small notebook  
 ● Extra black shirt  
 ● Basic personal first-aid items, such as bandages for minor personal use  
 ● Sunscreen for outdoor work  
 ● Beanie or warm layer for cold venues  
 ● Small towel or bandana  
 ● Deodorant or basic hygiene items for long days

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Personal first-aid items do not replace workplace injury reporting. If you are injured at work, report it according to site procedure.

Do not bring specialized tools, knives, blades, radios, harnesses, or task-specific PPE unless you are trained, authorized, and allowed to use them. Some venues have security rules about tools, blades, liquids, bags, and personal items. Always follow employer and venue policies.

A good work bag helps you stay ready, but it should not turn you into someone who tries to do work you have not been assigned or trained to do.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Your work bag should help you stay prepared, hydrated, reachable, and ready for the call. Bring practical basics, follow the call instructions, and avoid bringing items that create security or safety problems.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I had to work a six-hour load-in that turned into a ten-hour day, would I have enough water, food, and basic supplies to stay useful?

Question: What are three basic items a new stagehand should consider bringing to a call?

**Best Answer:**  
 Water, ID, and a phone charger are three strong examples. Other useful items include snacks, gloves if instructed, a Sharpie, a pen, and a small flashlight or headlamp.

# **Module 3, Lesson 4: What Not to Bring**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners that preparation is not only about what they bring. It is also about what they leave at home. The goal is to help beginners avoid items, habits, and distractions that create safety, security, or professionalism problems.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify items and behaviors that should be avoided on a stagehand call.

Bringing the wrong items can make a new stagehand look unprofessional or create safety and security issues.

Learners should avoid unnecessary valuables, prohibited items, distracting items, unsafe clothing, and anything that interferes with work.

Venue security rules matter. Some buildings may restrict knives, tools, bags, liquids, or other items.

Learners should never report to work impaired. If a medication or medical condition may affect alertness, balance, or reaction time, they should follow employer policy and seek appropriate guidance.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Being prepared does not mean bringing everything you own.

A new stagehand should bring the basics, but they should also avoid bringing items that create problems. Some items are distracting. Some are unsafe. Some may violate venue security rules. Some simply get in the way.

Do not bring valuables you do not need. Expensive jewelry, large amounts of cash, personal electronics, designer bags, or unnecessary items can be lost, stolen, damaged, or distracting. A live event worksite is not the place to manage extra valuables.

Do not report to work impaired. Stagehand work requires awareness, balance, communication, and judgment. You may be working around heavy equipment, moving cases, cables, trucks, lifts, ramps, or overhead work. Showing up impaired is unsafe and unprofessional.

If you take medication that may affect alertness, balance, or reaction time, follow employer policy and speak with the appropriate supervisor or medical professional as needed. Do not ignore anything that could affect your ability to work safely.

Do not bring guests, friends, pets, or anyone who is not assigned to the call. A jobsite is controlled for safety, access, security, and accountability. If someone is not on the call, they should not be there.

Do not bring unnecessary tools unless you were told to bring them and are allowed to use them. A beginner with a bag full of tools can create concern if they are not trained or authorized. It can also create security problems at the venue.

Do not bring a bad attitude. This may sound simple, but it matters. Complaining, arguing, acting too good for basic tasks, or ignoring direction can get noticed quickly.

Do not bring the mindset that you need to prove you know everything. The safest beginner is honest, alert, and willing to ask questions.

The goal is to show up clean, focused, prepared, and easy to work with.

#### ***Items to Avoid***

● Unnecessary valuables  
 ● Large bags that get in the way  
 ● Open-toed shoes or unsafe footwear  
 ● Loose jewelry or dangling accessories  
 ● Alcohol or impairing substances  
 ● Guests, friends, children, or pets  
 ● Personal speakers or distracting entertainment  
 ● Unapproved tools or blades  
 ● Clothing with offensive graphics or language  
 ● Anything prohibited by venue security  
 ● Anything that distracts you from the work or creates a safety issue

#### ***Behaviors to Avoid Bringing With You***

● Complaining before the work starts  
 ● Acting like basic tasks are beneath you  
 ● Ignoring the call instructions  
 ● Wandering away without telling anyone  
 ● Using your phone constantly  
 ● Touching gear without direction  
 ● Guessing instead of asking  
 ● Trying to impress people by taking unsafe shortcuts  
 ● Arguing with leads, venue staff, or security  
 ● Acting impaired, distracted, or careless

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Different venues have different security rules. An item that is allowed at one venue may be prohibited at another. Read the call details, follow security instructions, and ask your employer or lead before bringing questionable items.

Being professional means knowing what to bring and what not to bring. The wrong item or behavior can create safety, security, and trust problems before the work even begins.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Bring what helps you work safely and professionally. Leave behind anything that distracts you, violates site rules, creates safety issues, or makes you look unprepared.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Is there anything I normally carry that could create a problem at a secured venue or backstage work area?

Question: Why should a beginner avoid bringing unapproved tools to a call?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because tools may be restricted by venue security, may not be needed for the assigned work, and may encourage the beginner to perform tasks they are not trained or authorized to do.

# **Module 3, Lesson 5: Hydration, Food, and Long-Call Preparation**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson prepares learners for the physical reality of long stagehand calls. The goal is to help beginners understand that hydration, food, pacing, and personal care affect safety and performance.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why hydration, food, and long-call preparation matter for stagehand work.

Stagehand calls can be physically demanding and may last longer than expected.

Poor hydration, lack of food, lack of sleep, and poor preparation can make a worker less alert and less safe.

Learners should prepare for delays, extended calls, outdoor conditions, late nights, and limited access to food.

For hot outdoor calls, learners should hydrate regularly and pay attention to signs of heat stress or heat illness.

If a learner feels dizzy, overheated, faint, confused, weak, or unable to work safely, they should notify a lead immediately and follow site procedures.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Stagehand work can be harder on your body than it looks from the outside.

A call might be scheduled for four hours, but the day can change. Trucks may arrive late. The schedule may shift. Weather may slow things down. A room may need to be reset. Load-out may run late. You may be on your feet for hours.

That is why hydration and food are part of being prepared.

Bring water. Do not wait until you feel exhausted to start drinking. If you are working outdoors, pushing cases, moving barricade, walking long distances, or working in a hot venue, hydration becomes even more important.

For hot outdoor calls, drink water regularly instead of waiting until you feel thirsty. For longer hot calls, electrolytes may help replace what is lost through sweat. Avoid relying on energy drinks, alcohol, or sugary drinks as your main hydration plan.

Bring food or snacks that are easy to carry and easy to eat during a break. You may not always have time to leave the venue. You may not know where food is nearby. You may not get a meal exactly when you expect it.

Choose food that helps you keep steady energy. Heavy meals, too much sugar, or not eating at all can leave you feeling sluggish, shaky, or distracted.

Long-call preparation also means thinking ahead. Do you have layers if the venue is cold? Do you have sunscreen for an outdoor call? Do you have rain gear if the weather changes? Do you have medication you are allowed and need to carry? Do you have a plan for transportation if the call ends late?

Being prepared does not mean expecting the worst. It means respecting the reality of live events: things change.

A prepared stagehand is easier to work with because they are not constantly distracted by hunger, thirst, dead phone batteries, missing layers, or preventable problems.

#### ***Long-Call Preparation Checklist***

Before the call, ask yourself:

● Do I have enough water?  
 ● Do I have snacks or food?  
 ● Did I eat before arriving?  
 ● Did I check the weather?  
 ● Do I have layers if needed?  
 ● Do I have a phone charger?  
 ● Do I know where to park or check in?  
 ● Do I know how I am getting home if the call runs late?  
 ● Did I get enough sleep to work safely?  
 ● Do I know what to do if I start feeling overheated or unwell?

#### ***Good Food and Snack Options***

● Protein bars  
 ● Trail mix  
 ● Fruit  
 ● Sandwiches  
 ● Crackers  
 ● Electrolyte packets, if appropriate  
 ● Simple meals that are easy to pack  
 ● Food that will not spoil quickly or make a mess

#### ***Outdoor Call Considerations***

For outdoor work, consider:

● Extra water  
 ● Electrolytes for longer hot calls, if appropriate  
 ● Sunscreen  
 ● Hat or weather-appropriate headwear  
 ● Rain layer  
 ● Extra socks  
 ● Warm layer for night work  
 ● Footwear that can handle dirt, grass, mud, or uneven ground  
 ● A plan for shade, breaks, and changing weather when directed by the site

#### ***Heat and Fatigue Warning Signs***

Tell a lead immediately if you experience:

● Dizziness  
 ● Confusion  
 ● Faintness  
 ● Nausea  
 ● Heavy weakness  
 ● Muscle cramps  
 ● Feeling overheated  
 ● Headache with heat exposure  
 ● Trouble focusing  
 ● Feeling unable to continue safely

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

If you feel dizzy, overheated, faint, disoriented, or unable to work safely, tell a lead immediately. Do not try to “tough it out” in a way that puts yourself or others at risk.

If heat illness symptoms appear, stop work, notify a lead immediately, and follow emergency or site procedures.

Hydration and food are not just personal comfort. They affect your focus, stamina, communication, judgment, and ability to work safely.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Long calls are easier and safer when you prepare your body before the work starts. Bring water, bring food, check the weather, plan for delays, and take care of yourself so you can support the crew.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Have I ever shown up to physical work without enough food, water, or sleep? How did it affect my attitude and performance?

Question: Why should a stagehand bring water and snacks to a call?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because calls can be long, physical, and unpredictable. Water and snacks help the stagehand stay focused, energized, and ready to work safely.

# **Module 3, Lesson 6: Personal Readiness: Sleep, Transportation, and Call Time Discipline**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners that professionalism begins before they arrive. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that sleep, transportation, and call time discipline directly affect whether they are trusted and called back.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe how sleep, transportation planning, and punctuality affect stagehand reliability.

A stagehand who is late, tired, lost, or unprepared creates problems for the crew.

Call time means ready to work, not just arriving nearby.

Transportation should be planned before the day of the call.

Rest matters because stagehand work requires alertness, physical movement, communication, and safe decision-making.

Learners should check for last-minute updates before leaving for the call.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Your call does not start when you touch the first road case. It starts with how you prepare before you arrive.

One of the fastest ways to lose trust is to be late. In live production, timing matters. Trucks, crews, departments, venues, and schedules are connected. If you are late, someone else may have to cover your work, or the crew may start short-handed.

Call time means the time you are expected to be ready. If your call time is 8:00 a.m., that does not mean parking at 8:00. It does not mean walking through security at 8:00. It does not mean texting someone at 8:00 because you cannot find the entrance.

It means you are checked in, dressed, prepared, and available for direction.

Transportation planning is part of the job. Before the call, know where the venue is, where to park, where to enter, and how long it may take to get through security. Large venues can have multiple entrances, loading docks, gates, parking lots, and backstage access points. Give yourself extra time.

Before leaving, check for last-minute updates to call time, parking, entrance location, PPE requirements, or weather-related instructions. Live events change quickly, and the most prepared workers pay attention to updates.

Sleep also matters. Stagehand work can be physical, repetitive, and fast-moving. You may be around moving equipment, cables, trucks, ramps, lifts, and overhead work. If you are exhausted before the call starts, you are more likely to miss instructions, make mistakes, or react slowly.

This does not mean every stagehand will always have perfect sleep. Live events can involve early mornings, late nights, and odd schedules. But a professional stagehand does their best to show up alert, sober, prepared, and ready to work.

Reliability is one of the most valuable traits a beginner can build. You may not know every department yet, but if you show up early, ready, and focused, leads will notice.

#### ***Call Time Discipline***

Good call time discipline means:

● Knowing the call time  
 ● Confirming the location  
 ● Checking for updates before leaving  
 ● Planning parking  
 ● Allowing time for traffic  
 ● Allowing time for security  
 ● Finding the check-in point  
 ● Being dressed before work starts  
 ● Having PPE ready  
 ● Checking in with the correct person  
 ● Being ready for direction at the assigned time

#### ***Transportation Planning***

Before the call, check:

● Venue address  
 ● Parking instructions  
 ● Loading dock or crew entrance location  
 ● Security requirements  
 ● Badge or ID requirements  
 ● Public transit options, if needed  
 ● Rideshare pickup and drop-off realities  
 ● Backup plan if your first transportation option fails  
 ● Whether the call may end late at night  
 ● Whether the venue has multiple entrances or restricted access points

#### ***Readiness Questions***

Before leaving home, ask yourself:

● Am I dressed correctly?  
 ● Do I have my ID?  
 ● Do I have water and snacks?  
 ● Do I have required PPE or assigned items?  
 ● Do I know where I am going?  
 ● Do I know where to park?  
 ● Do I know where to check in?  
 ● Have I checked for updates?  
 ● Am I alert enough to work safely?

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Fatigue can affect awareness, judgment, communication, and reaction time. If you are too tired to work safely, communicate with your lead or employer. Do not hide a safety concern because you are afraid to speak up.

Being on time means being ready to work at call time. Personal readiness is part of professional readiness.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A reliable stagehand plans transportation, arrives early, checks for updates, gets enough rest when possible, and treats call time seriously. Being prepared before the call helps the entire crew start stronger.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If my call time was 7:00 a.m. at a large arena, what time would I need to leave home to be checked in and ready?

Question: What does call time mean?

**Best Answer:**  
 Call time means the time you are expected to be checked in, dressed, prepared, and ready to receive direction, not the time you begin looking for parking or walking into the venue.

# **Module 3, Lesson 7: How to Avoid Looking Unprepared on Day One**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson brings the module together by showing learners the common mistakes that make beginners look unprepared and how to avoid them. The goal is to help learners walk into their first call with confidence, humility, and readiness.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify specific actions that help a new stagehand look prepared and professional on their first day.

Preparedness is visible. Leads notice how a new stagehand arrives, listens, dresses, communicates, and responds to direction.

Looking prepared does not mean acting like an expert. It means showing up ready, honest, attentive, and safe.

A beginner should ask questions instead of pretending, follow the chain of command, and avoid freelancing.

Before moving, opening, coiling, or cleaning up gear, learners should confirm that it is part of their assigned task.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Your first day as a stagehand can feel intimidating. You may not know the gear, the departments, the venue, or the pace of the work yet.

That is normal.

You do not need to look like an expert on day one. You need to look prepared.

Prepared looks like showing up early, dressed correctly, with the basics you were told to bring. Prepared looks like knowing your call time, bringing ID, having water, wearing proper footwear, and having required PPE ready.

Prepared sounds like asking, “Who is my lead?” or “Where would you like me?” or “Can you show me the safe way to do that?”

Prepared does not sound like pretending. If you do not know something, say so. A lead can work with honest inexperience. What creates problems is when someone acts confident, guesses, moves gear without direction, or says they understand when they do not.

One of the biggest beginner mistakes is freelancing. Freelancing means making up your own tasks without direction. You may think you are helping by moving a case, coiling a cable, opening a lid, or cleaning something up. But if you were not told to do it, you might be moving gear away from where it belongs, touching another department’s equipment, interrupting a sequence, or creating a safety issue.

Before moving, opening, coiling, or cleaning up gear, confirm that it is part of your assigned task.

Another mistake is disappearing. If you finish a task, check back in. Do not wander, hide, sit down without being released, or wait for someone to find you.

Another mistake is being glued to your phone. Your phone may be necessary for call information or emergency communication, but constant phone use during work tells the crew you are not engaged.

The best first-day stagehand is not the person who talks the most. It is the person who listens, follows direction, works safely, communicates clearly, and keeps checking in.

#### ***Day One Preparedness Checklist***

Before leaving home:

● Read the call details  
 ● Check for updates  
 ● Confirm the location  
 ● Confirm the call time  
 ● Dress correctly  
 ● Wear proper footwear  
 ● Pack ID  
 ● Pack water  
 ● Pack snacks  
 ● Pack phone charger  
 ● Bring required PPE or assigned items  
 ● Check weather for outdoor calls  
 ● Plan transportation  
 ● Leave early

When you arrive:

● Check in  
 ● Find your lead or department  
 ● Listen during instructions  
 ● Ask questions if unclear  
 ● Stay with your assigned group  
 ● Confirm before moving or opening gear  
 ● Keep pathways clear  
 ● Stay off your phone unless necessary  
 ● Report hazards or confusion  
 ● Check back in when tasks are complete

#### ***Common Day One Mistakes***

● Arriving exactly at call time instead of ready at call time  
 ● Wearing the wrong clothing  
 ● Wearing unsafe shoes  
 ● Forgetting ID  
 ● Forgetting water  
 ● Ignoring PPE instructions  
 ● Pretending to know something  
 ● Moving gear without being told  
 ● Taking direction from random people  
 ● Wandering away  
 ● Complaining about basic tasks  
 ● Using the phone during work  
 ● Not asking for clarification  
 ● Acting like safety questions are a weakness

#### ***Better Beginner Phrases***

Use phrases like:

● “Who should I report to?”  
 ● “Where do you want this case?”  
 ● “Do you want this staged or opened?”  
 ● “I have not been trained on that yet.”  
 ● “Can you show me the safe way to do that?”  
 ● “I’m finished with that task. What would you like me on next?”  
 ● “I’m not sure if this is safe to move. Who should I check with?”  
 ● “Can you clarify that before I start?”  
 ● “Is this part of my assigned task?”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Looking prepared does not mean saying yes to everything. If a task is unclear, unsafe, or outside your training, pause and get direction. It is better to ask a question than to guess and create a safety issue.

Prepared beginners are not expected to know everything. They are expected to show up ready, listen carefully, work safely, communicate honestly, and ask before guessing.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

You do not need to be an expert on your first day. You need to be prepared, safe, honest, and easy to direct. That is what makes a beginner worth calling back.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What would a crew lead notice about me in the first 10 minutes of a call?

Question: What is one of the best ways to avoid looking unprepared on day one?

**Best Answer:**  
 Arrive early, dressed correctly, with the required basics, then check in with your lead, listen carefully, and ask questions instead of guessing.

# End of Module 3 Quiz

# **Module 3 Quiz: PPE, Clothing, and What to Bring**

## **Quiz Instructions**

Choose the best answer for each question. This quiz checks your understanding of show blacks, basic PPE, work bag preparation, what not to bring, hydration, long-call readiness, transportation planning, and how to avoid looking unprepared on day one.

**Recommended passing score:** 80%

**Total questions:** 20

# **Multiple Choice**

## **1. What is the main purpose of show blacks?**

A. To make stagehands look more important than other workers  
 B. To help crew members look professional and avoid distracting from the show  
 C. To replace required PPE  
 D. To make all workers wear the exact same brand of clothing

**Correct Answer:** B

## **2. Which of the following is usually a good example of show blacks?**

A. Bright red shirt, shorts, and sandals  
 B. Plain black shirt, black work pants, and work boots  
 C. White formal shirt and dress shoes  
 D. Graphic shirt, ripped jeans, and flip-flops

**Correct Answer:** B

## **3. What should come first if there is a conflict between show blacks and required safety gear?**

A. Looking good for the audience  
 B. Wearing whatever is most comfortable  
 C. Site safety requirements and required PPE  
 D. Ignoring the safety gear unless someone complains

**Correct Answer:** C

## **4. Why should stagehands avoid loose dangling clothing or jewelry?**

A. It may look too formal  
 B. It can get caught on gear, handles, casters, cables, or hardware  
 C. It makes workers too visible  
 D. It is never allowed in any workplace

**Correct Answer:** B

## **5. What does PPE stand for?**

A. Production Planning Equipment  
 B. Personal Protective Equipment  
 C. Performance Preparation Entry  
 D. Professional Production Experience

**Correct Answer:** B

## **6. Which item is an example of common PPE or protective gear for stagehand work?**

A. Hard hat  
 B. Costume jewelry  
 C. Personal speaker  
 D. Sunglasses worn only for style

**Correct Answer:** A

## **7. What should a new stagehand do if required PPE is missing, damaged, does not fit, or they do not know how to use it?**

A. Start working anyway and hope it is fine  
 B. Borrow random PPE from another worker without asking  
 C. Notify a lead or supervisor before starting the task  
 D. Ignore the task and leave the venue

**Correct Answer:** C

## **8. Why might high-visibility clothing be required on some calls?**

A. To make the crew stand out to the audience  
 B. To help workers be seen around trucks, forklifts, docks, outdoor sites, or low-light areas  
 C. To replace all other PPE  
 D. To show that the worker is in charge

**Correct Answer:** B

## **9. Why should gloves match the task?**

A. Because all gloves protect against every hazard  
 B. Because the wrong gloves may be ineffective or create problems for certain tasks  
 C. Because gloves are only worn for appearance  
 D. Because gloves are never needed for stagehand work

**Correct Answer:** B

## **10. Which of the following should a beginner usually bring in their work bag?**

A. ID, water, snacks, phone charger, pen or Sharpie, and small flashlight or headlamp  
 B. Expensive jewelry, personal speaker, and loose accessories  
 C. Unapproved blades, alcohol, and oversized bags  
 D. Nothing, because the venue always provides everything

**Correct Answer:** A

## **11. Why is it useful to bring a phone charger or battery pack?**

A. So you can stay on social media during the call  
 B. So your phone can stay available for call details, emergency communication, or schedule updates  
 C. So you can ignore your lead  
 D. So you can record the show without permission

**Correct Answer:** B

## **12. What should a stagehand do before bringing specialized tools, knives, blades, radios, or harnesses?**

A. Bring them automatically to look prepared  
 B. Use them without telling anyone  
 C. Confirm they are allowed and only use them if trained, authorized, and assigned  
 D. Hide them from security

**Correct Answer:** C

## **13. Which item should a stagehand avoid bringing to a call?**

A. Water bottle  
 B. ID  
 C. Phone charger  
 D. Alcohol or impairing substances

**Correct Answer:** D

## **14. Why should guests, friends, children, or pets not be brought to a stagehand call?**

A. Because they might get bored  
 B. Because jobsites are controlled areas with safety, access, security, and accountability requirements  
 C. Because stagehands are not allowed to talk to anyone  
 D. Because venues never allow people inside

**Correct Answer:** B

## **15. Why are hydration and food part of being prepared?**

A. Because stagehand calls can be long, physical, and unpredictable  
 B. Because eating during work is always required  
 C. Because water replaces PPE  
 D. Because snacks make workers qualified for specialized tasks

**Correct Answer:** A

## **16. What should a stagehand do if they feel dizzy, overheated, faint, confused, weak, or unable to work safely?**

A. Keep working and say nothing  
 B. Tell a lead immediately and follow site procedures  
 C. Leave without notifying anyone  
 D. Drink an energy drink and ignore the symptoms

**Correct Answer:** B

## **17. What does call time mean?**

A. The time you start looking for parking  
 B. The time you begin driving to the venue  
 C. The time you should be checked in, dressed, prepared, and ready for direction  
 D. The time the show begins

**Correct Answer:** C

## **18. Why should a stagehand check for updates before leaving for a call?**

A. Because live event details can change, including call time, parking, entrance location, PPE, or weather instructions  
 B. Because call details never matter  
 C. Because checking updates replaces arriving on time  
 D. Because updates are only for supervisors

**Correct Answer:** A

## **19. What is freelancing on a stagehand call?**

A. Working as an independent contractor  
 B. Making up your own tasks without direction  
 C. Reporting to your assigned lead  
 D. Asking for clarification before starting a task

**Correct Answer:** B

## **20. Which statement best describes a prepared beginner stagehand?**

A. Someone who pretends to know everything  
 B. Someone who arrives ready, follows direction, uses required PPE, asks questions, and avoids guessing  
 C. Someone who moves gear without being assigned  
 D. Someone who ignores the call instructions but works fast

**Correct Answer:** B

# **Scenario Questions**

These can be used as written-response questions, discussion prompts, or practical review questions.

## **Scenario 1**

You arrive at a load-in wearing all black, but everyone near the dock is wearing high-visibility vests and hard hats.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not enter the work area without confirming the PPE requirement. Ask your lead or supervisor what PPE is required and follow the site safety rules. Safety requirements come before show blacks.

## **Scenario 2**

You brought gloves, but the task involves working near moving machinery or equipment where you are not sure what hand protection is appropriate.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Pause and ask your lead what hand protection is appropriate for the task. Gloves should match the hazard and the work being performed.

## **Scenario 3**

You are packing your work bag for your first call and are not sure what to bring.

**Question:** What basic items should you consider bringing?

**Best Answer:**  
 Bring practical basics such as ID, water, snacks, phone charger or battery pack, pen or Sharpie, small flashlight or headlamp, weather layer if needed, and any clothing, footwear, or PPE listed in the call instructions.

## **Scenario 4**

You feel overheated and dizzy during an outdoor call, but the crew is busy and moving quickly.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Tell a lead immediately and follow site procedures. Do not try to tough it out if you may be unable to work safely.

## **Scenario 5**

You finish your assigned task and see a road case nearby that looks like it might need to be moved.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not move it without direction. Check back in with your lead and confirm what you should do next. Moving gear without assignment can create confusion, delay the crew, or create a safety issue.
