# Module 4

# **Module 4: Venue and Jobsite Awareness**

## **Module Purpose**

This module teaches new stagehands how to move through a venue or jobsite without becoming a hazard to themselves, the crew, the gear, or the production.

Many beginners struggle because they do not yet know where to stand, where not to stand, who to report to, what areas are restricted, or how to recognize movement patterns around trucks, docks, forklifts, carts, cables, rigging, and emergency pathways.

## **Module Learning Objective**

By the end of this module, the learner should be able to enter a live event jobsite, identify key work areas, find their lead, recognize common movement hazards, stay clear of restricted zones, and help keep paths clean, safe, and open.

## **Important Safety Disclaimer**

This module provides venue and jobsite awareness only. It does not replace employer-provided safety training, site-specific orientation, venue rules, union training, equipment qualification or certification, emergency action plans, forklift training, lift training, rigging training, electrical training, or fire/life-safety instruction.

Always follow your employer, venue, lead, department head, and site-specific procedures.

# Lesson 4.1

# **Module 4, Lesson 1: The First 15 Minutes on Site**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands what to do during the first few minutes after arriving at a venue or jobsite. The goal is to help learners understand that the beginning of the call sets the tone for the entire day.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe the correct first steps to take after arriving on-site, including checking in, observing the environment, finding the correct reporting area, and waiting for direction.

The first 15 minutes on-site are not wasted time. They are where a new stagehand starts building trust.

A beginner should not walk into a venue and immediately start moving gear, opening cases, touching equipment, or joining a random group of workers. The first priority is to check in, identify who is in charge, understand where to report, and observe the movement of people and equipment.

A professional stagehand uses the first few minutes to slow down mentally, look around, and get oriented.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

When you arrive at a live event jobsite, your first job is not to start grabbing gear. Your first job is to get oriented.

The first 15 minutes matter because that is when you learn where you are supposed to be, who you report to, what areas are active, and what hazards are already present.

Start by checking in wherever the employer, venue, labor company, or lead instructed you to report. This may be a security entrance, loading dock, production office, crew check-in table, backstage door, or designated meeting area.

Once you are checked in, do not wander. Stay in the assigned crew area unless told otherwise. Look around and pay attention.

Where are trucks moving?  
 Where are forklifts operating?  
 Where is the stage?  
 Where are cases being pushed?  
 Where are people walking?  
 Where are cables, ramps, docks, lifts, barricades, or active work zones?

A new stagehand should avoid standing in doorways, dock paths, stage access points, truck lanes, ramp areas, backstage crossovers, or anywhere gear is moving.

This is also the time to listen. A lead may give a safety briefing, assign departments, explain the plan, or identify hazards. Missing those first instructions can create problems for the rest of the call.

The best thing a beginner can do in the first 15 minutes is simple: check in, stay alert, find the right person, listen carefully, and do not create extra problems.

#### ***First 15 Minutes Checklist***

● Arrive early enough to park, enter, and check in before call time  
 ● Check in at the correct location  
 ● Find out who your lead or department contact is  
 ● Stay with the assigned crew area  
 ● Listen for the safety briefing or work instructions  
 ● Look for moving equipment, forklifts, carts, trucks, cables, and overhead work  
 ● Stay out of doorways, ramps, docks, fire lanes, and active work paths  
 ● Do not touch gear until assigned  
 ● Ask questions if you are unsure where to stand or report

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Example Beginner Mistakes***

#### ***Bad Example 1***

A new stagehand arrives and immediately starts pushing cases because they want to look useful.

Why this is a problem:

Those cases may already be staged correctly, may belong to a specific department, or may need to stay in place until the lead gives direction.

#### ***Bad Example 2***

A new stagehand waits in the loading dock doorway because they do not know where else to stand.

Why this is a problem:

Dock doors are high-traffic areas. Trucks, forklifts, pallet jacks, carts, and cases may need that path.

#### ***Bad Example 3***

A new stagehand misses the safety briefing because they are looking around the venue.

Why this is a problem:

The safety briefing may include important information about restricted areas, hazards, emergency exits, weather, equipment movement, or call structure.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Do not assume a space is safe just because other people are standing there. If you are unsure where to wait, ask your lead. Walkways, work areas, and access paths should be kept clear, orderly, and safe for the crew.

If you see a hazard, report it. If you can safely correct a simple housekeeping issue without touching department gear or entering a restricted area, do so. If you are unsure, stop and ask.

The first 15 minutes are about orientation, not proving yourself. Check in, observe, listen, and wait for direction before acting.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

The first 15 minutes on-site set the tone for the call. A professional beginner checks in, finds their lead, observes the jobsite, stays out of active paths, and waits for clear direction before starting work.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I arrived at a venue and did not know where to stand, what would be the safest and most professional thing to do?

Question: What should a new stagehand do before moving gear at the beginning of a call?

**Best Answer:**

Check in, find the correct lead or department contact, listen for instructions, observe the work area, and wait for direction before touching or moving gear.

# **Module 4, Lesson 2: How to Find Your Lead or Department**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands how to identify who they report to on a call. The goal is to prevent confusion, freelancing, and taking direction from the wrong person.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain how to find their lead or department and why following the correct chain of command matters.

A live event jobsite has structure. New stagehands should not assume that every person on-site can assign them work.

The lead or department head is the person responsible for giving direction, organizing tasks, answering questions, and keeping the crew aligned. A beginner should know who they report to before starting work.

If a learner does not know who their lead is, they should ask at check-in or ask the nearest clearly identified supervisor, crew lead, steward, department head, or production contact.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

One of the first things you need to know on any call is who you report to.

You may be working in a large arena with multiple crews, or a smaller venue with only a handful of people. Either way, the work needs structure. If everyone starts taking direction from random people, gear ends up in the wrong place, tasks get duplicated, and safety problems can happen.

Your lead is your main point of contact. That may be a crew lead, labor coordinator, department head, steward, venue supervisor, production manager, or another assigned person depending on the job.

When you check in, ask a simple question:

“Who do I report to?”  
 or  
 “Which department am I with?”

Once you know your lead, stay connected to that person or department. If another person gives you a task and you are unsure whether they have authority to do so, confirm with your lead. This is not disrespectful. It is how you prevent confusion.

For example, someone may say, “Take that case over there.” But if that person is not your lead and the case belongs to another department, moving it may cause problems.

Finding your lead is not just about knowing who is in charge. It is about knowing where your instructions come from, where to ask questions, and how to stay useful without freelancing.

#### ***Useful Phrases***

● “Who is my lead today?”  
 ● “Which department am I assigned to?”  
 ● “Where should I report?”  
 ● “Who should I check in with when this task is finished?”  
 ● “I was asked to move this. Do you want me to do that?”  
 ● “I’m finished with that task. What would you like me on next?”  
 ● “Should I stay with this department or help somewhere else?”

#### ***Example Situations***

#### ***Situation 1***

You arrive at the venue and see several groups working.

Best action:

Check in and ask who your lead or department contact is before joining a group.

#### ***Situation 2***

Someone you do not know tells you to move cases to the dock.

Best action:

Confirm with your lead before moving the cases, especially if you do not know who the person is or where the cases belong.

#### ***Situation 3***

You finish a task and do not know what to do next.

Best action:

Return to your lead and ask for the next assignment.

#### ***Situation 4***

You are assigned to audio, but someone from another department asks you to help move video gear.

Best action:

Ask your lead whether they want you to assist or stay with your assigned department.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Confusion can become a safety issue. Taking direction from the wrong person can send workers into restricted zones, active equipment paths, rigging areas, or unfinished work areas. When unsure, stop and confirm.

A new stagehand should always know who they report to. If you do not know, ask before starting work.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Finding your lead is one of the first signs of professionalism. A new stagehand should check in, identify their point of contact, follow the correct chain of command, and confirm direction before taking tasks from random people on-site.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If three different people gave me three different instructions, how would I know which one to follow?

Question: Why is it important for a new stagehand to find their lead or department before starting work?

**Best Answer:**

Because the lead or department contact gives the correct direction, organizes the work, answers questions, and helps prevent confusion or unsafe freelancing.

# **Module 4, Lesson 3: Stage, Dock, Truck, Ramp, Floor, Backstage, FOH, and Barricade Basics**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces new stagehands to common venue and jobsite areas. The goal is to help learners understand basic location terms so they can follow directions more confidently.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common work areas in a venue and explain why each area requires awareness and caution.

Stagehands hear location terms constantly. A beginner may be told to go to the dock, stage left, FOH, backstage, the truck, the ramp, the floor, or barricade.

Understanding these basic areas helps the learner follow instructions and avoid standing in the wrong place.

This lesson is awareness-level only. Venue layouts vary, and learners should always confirm local terms with their lead.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Every venue is different, but many live event jobsites use similar location terms.

The stage is the main performance or presentation area. It may be a permanent theater stage, a temporary stage, a deck system, a platform, or a large concert stage. Stage areas can be busy, especially during load-in and load-out, so do not stand in the middle of the stage unless assigned there.

The dock is where trucks load and unload. This area often has trailers, dock plates, forklifts, pallet jacks, ramps, carts, road cases, and crew traffic. The dock is not a hangout area. It is one of the busiest and most hazardous parts of many jobsites.

The truck or trailer is where gear is packed for transport. Truck loading follows a plan. Cases, carts, truss, cable trunks, and department gear often need to go in a specific order. Do not climb into a truck, stack cases, or move gear into a trailer unless directed.

A ramp is used to move gear between different heights or levels. Ramps can be dangerous because rolling cases, carts, and heavy gear can gain speed. Never stand below a moving case on a ramp, and do not block ramp paths.

The floor usually refers to the main event floor, arena floor, ballroom floor, convention floor, or work area where gear is being staged or moved. Floors may have cable paths, taped areas, chairs, barricade, carts, lifts, or active build zones.

Backstage is the area behind or around the stage where crew, performers, gear, dressing rooms, quick-change areas, or production support may be located. Backstage can include restricted or sensitive areas, so beginners should not wander there without direction.

FOH means “front of house.” In venue language, it can refer to public or audience-facing areas. In production language, FOH often means the audio, lighting, or production control position located in the audience area. Either way, do not place cases, food, bags, tools, or gear at FOH unless told to by your lead or the department responsible for that area.

Barricade is the crowd-control barrier used at many concerts and live events. It may be placed in front of the stage, around mix positions, near entrances, or around restricted areas. Barricade can be heavy and awkward. Move it only when assigned and with enough help.

Learning these terms helps you understand where you are, where gear is going, and where not to stand.

#### ***Common Area Terms***

#### ***Stage***

The main performance or presentation area.

#### ***Dock***

The truck loading and unloading area.

#### ***Truck / Trailer***

The transport space where gear is packed.

#### ***Ramp***

A sloped path used to move gear between levels.

#### ***Floor***

The main work or event area.

#### ***Backstage***

Crew, performer, and production support areas behind or near the stage.

#### ***FOH***

Front of house. This may mean public-facing venue areas or the production control position in the audience area.

#### ***Barricade***

Crowd-control barrier used to separate people, equipment, or restricted areas.

#### ***Example Directions a Beginner Might Hear***

● “Take this to stage right.”  
 ● “Bring those cases to the dock.”  
 ● “Keep this ramp clear.”  
 ● “Do not block FOH.”  
 ● “This barricade goes downstage.”  
 ● “Stay out of backstage unless your lead sends you there.”  
 ● “Leave that case on the floor near lighting world.”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: During load-out, slow down enough to keep control, protect the sequence, and ask before moving gear you do not understand.

Location awareness prevents accidents. Docks, ramps, trucks, and active floor areas often involve moving equipment, uneven surfaces, or limited visibility. Stay alert and do not stand in travel paths.

Do not run through docks, ramps, backstage areas, truck lanes, or active work zones. Move with purpose, but stay controlled.

Knowing basic venue terms helps a stagehand follow direction, move safely, and avoid blocking important work areas.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A new stagehand does not need to know every venue term immediately, but they should learn the basics. Stage, dock, truck, ramp, floor, backstage, FOH, and barricade are common areas that require awareness, caution, and clear direction.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If someone told me to bring a case to the dock or keep FOH clear, would I understand what they mean?

Question: Why should a new stagehand avoid standing in dock doors, ramps, or active floor paths?

**Best Answer:**

Because those areas are used for moving trucks, carts, road cases, forklifts, pallet jacks, and crew traffic. Standing there can block work or create a safety hazard.

# **Module 4, Lesson 4: Restricted Areas and Why They Matter**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands why certain areas are restricted and why they should never enter those areas without permission.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain what restricted areas are, why they exist, and what to do if they are unsure whether they are allowed to enter.

Restricted areas are not about making the job difficult. They exist to protect people, performers, crew, equipment, venue property, and the production.

A restricted area may be blocked off by signs, tape, cones, barricade, closed doors, security, or verbal instruction. Some restricted areas may not be obvious, so learners should ask if unsure.

Beginners should never assume they are allowed somewhere just because a door is open or another worker walked through it.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

On a live event site, not every area is open to every worker.

Some areas are restricted because of safety. Others are restricted because of security, privacy, production control, performer needs, expensive equipment, or venue rules.

Restricted areas may include rigging zones, lift zones, backstage rooms, dressing rooms, production offices, control areas, truck lanes, electrical areas, catwalks, rooftops, seating sections under construction, barricaded zones, or areas where crews are working overhead.

Sometimes restricted areas are clearly marked. You may see cones, caution tape, barricade, signs, closed doors, or security staff. Other times, the boundary may be explained verbally during a safety briefing.

As a new stagehand, your rule is simple: do not enter a restricted area unless you are assigned to go there by the proper lead.

Do not follow someone through a door just because they look like they know where they are going. Do not walk under rigging because it seems like a shortcut. Do not enter dressing room hallways, control rooms, catwalks, trucks, or backstage areas without direction.

Restricted areas protect you and the production. Entering the wrong area can put you under overhead work, in the path of moving equipment, near electrical hazards, inside private performer areas, or in places where timing and access are tightly controlled.

If you are unsure whether you are allowed somewhere, stop and ask.

#### ***Common Restricted Areas***

● Rigging zones  
 ● Areas under overhead work  
 ● Lift operating areas  
 ● Truck lanes  
 ● Electrical rooms or power areas  
 ● Production offices  
 ● Dressing rooms  
 ● Performer hallways  
 ● Control booths  
 ● FOH mix positions unless assigned  
 ● Catwalks  
 ● Roof access points  
 ● Areas marked with cones, tape, barricade, or signs  
 ● Any area your lead tells you to avoid

#### ***Example Situations***

#### ***Situation 1***

You see a shortcut across an area where riggers are working overhead.

Best action:

Do not take the shortcut. Stay clear and use an approved walking route.

#### ***Situation 2***

A door backstage is open and you see people walking through it.

Best action:

Do not assume you are allowed in. Ask your lead if you need to enter that area.

#### ***Situation 3***

You are told to bring a case near FOH, but the area is taped off.

Best action:

Stop before entering and confirm with your lead or the department in charge.

#### ***Situation 4***

You see barricade around a lift.

Best action:

Stay outside the barricade unless directed by the proper lead.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Restricted areas may contain hazards that are not obvious to beginners. These can include overhead work, moving lifts, electrical equipment, incomplete structures, active truck movement, or private/security-sensitive spaces.

Do not cross tape, cones, barricade, signs, closed doors, or verbal boundaries unless you are authorized and directed.

Restricted areas exist for a reason. Do not cross a boundary just because it looks convenient or because someone else did.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Restricted areas protect people, gear, performers, crew, and the production. A new stagehand should never enter a restricted area without direction, even if the area looks open or convenient.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Have I ever assumed I was allowed somewhere just because the door was open or someone else walked through?

Question: What should a new stagehand do if they are unsure whether they are allowed to enter an area?

**Best Answer:**

Stop and ask their lead or the appropriate department before entering.

# **Module 4, Lesson 5: Forklifts, Carts, Pallet Jacks, Pushers, and Moving Equipment**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands how to stay aware around common moving equipment. The goal is to prevent beginners from walking into equipment paths, riding equipment improperly, or standing where they cannot be seen.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common moving equipment on a jobsite and describe safe awareness habits around it.

Live event sites often include moving equipment such as forklifts, carts, pallet jacks, pushers, road cases, lifts, dollies, and rolling racks.

A new stagehand does not need to operate this equipment unless they are trained, authorized, and assigned. Their main responsibility is to stay alert, keep distance, avoid blind spots, follow marked paths, and never ride equipment unless it is designed and authorized for that purpose.

Not every moving device is regulated the same way, but every moving device can still create struck-by, crush, pinch, and foot hazards.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Live event jobsites are full of moving equipment.

You may see forklifts moving pallets, cases, barricade, staging, truss, generators, or production materials. You may see pallet jacks moving freight. You may see carts, pushers, road cases, hampers, cable trunks, rolling racks, dollies, or lifts moving through the venue.

As a new stagehand, your job is not to compete with moving equipment. Your job is to stay aware of it.

Forklifts and other powered industrial trucks can have blind spots. The operator may not always see you, especially if they are carrying a large load or backing up. Never assume the operator knows where you are. Make eye contact when possible, follow hand signals, and stay out of the path of travel.

Do not walk under raised forks, raised loads, or suspended equipment. Do not ride on forklifts, pallet jacks, carts, cases, or pushers. Do not stand between moving equipment and a wall, dock, truck, stage, barricade, or stack of cases.

Pallet jacks and carts may look simple, but they can still create crush, pinch, and foot injuries. Rolling cases can pick up speed on ramps. A heavy cart can be difficult to stop once it is moving.

If you are pushing a case or cart, move with control. Look where you are going. Use enough people for the weight and size. Communicate before turning corners, entering doorways, crossing ramps, or moving through crowded areas.

Do not run through active work areas. Moving quickly does not mean moving recklessly.

Do not wear earbuds or headphones in active work areas unless your employer specifically allows it. You need to hear horns, callouts, equipment movement, and emergency instructions.

When moving equipment is active, stay alert. Keep your head up, avoid phone use, listen for horns or callouts, and give equipment the space it needs.

#### ***Common Moving Equipment***

#### ***Forklift***

Used to lift and move heavy materials. Only trained, evaluated, authorized, and assigned operators should operate forklifts.

#### ***Pallet Jack***

Used to move palletized loads. Can still create foot, pinch, and crush hazards.

#### ***Pushers / Tugs***

Powered or manual equipment used to move heavy carts or case trains. Stay clear unless assigned.

#### ***Road Cases***

Rolling cases used to transport production gear. Heavy cases can tip, roll, or crush feet and hands.

#### ***Carts and Dollies***

Used to move gear, cable, scenic pieces, instruments, or supplies.

#### ***Rolling Racks***

Often used for wardrobe, soft goods, pipe, drape, or other equipment. They can tip if overloaded or pushed carelessly.

#### ***Example Situations***

#### ***Situation 1***

A forklift is backing up with a large load, and you need to cross behind it.

Best action:

Wait. Do not cross until the operator has stopped or clearly signaled that it is safe.

#### ***Situation 2***

A road case starts rolling quickly down a ramp.

Best action:

Do not get below it or try to stop it alone if it is unsafe. Call out, clear the path, and get help.

#### ***Situation 3***

Someone tells you to hop on a pallet jack for a ride.

Best action:

Do not ride. Pallet jacks are not transportation for workers.

#### ***Situation 4***

A cart is too heavy to steer safely by yourself.

Best action:

Stop and ask for help before continuing.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Keep your head up around moving gear, control your path, and never assume equipment operators or other crew members can see you.

#### ***Safe Awareness Habits***

● Keep your head up around moving equipment  
 ● Stay out of blind spots  
 ● Make eye contact with operators when possible  
 ● Do not assume equipment will stop for you  
 ● Do not walk under raised forks or loads  
 ● Do not ride forklifts, pallet jacks, cases, carts, or pushers  
 ● Keep hands and feet away from pinch points  
 ● Do not stand between moving equipment and fixed objects  
 ● Communicate before turning corners or entering doorways  
 ● Ask for help with heavy or awkward loads  
 ● Stop if you lose control of a case, cart, or load  
 ● Do not run through docks, ramps, truck lanes, backstage areas, or active work zones  
 ● Do not wear earbuds or headphones in active work areas unless specifically allowed

Forklifts and moving equipment can seriously injure pedestrians. Stay alert, keep distance, and do not operate forklifts, powered industrial trucks, pushers, tugs, lifts, or other powered equipment unless your employer has trained, evaluated, authorized, and assigned you to operate that specific equipment on that site.

Moving equipment always deserves space and attention. Your job is to stay visible, stay clear, and never assume the operator or crew can see you.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Forklifts, carts, pallet jacks, pushers, and rolling cases are part of live event work, but they create serious movement hazards. A new stagehand should stay clear, stay visible, avoid riding equipment, and only operate equipment they are trained, evaluated, authorized, and assigned to use.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

When I am near moving equipment, am I standing somewhere the operator can clearly see me?

Question: What should a new stagehand do when a forklift or moving equipment is operating nearby?

**Best Answer:**

Stay alert, keep a safe distance, avoid blind spots, make eye contact when possible, follow direction, and never walk under raised forks or loads.

# **Module 4, Lesson 6: Overhead Hazards and Working Under Rigging**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces new stagehands to overhead hazard awareness. The goal is not to teach rigging, but to help beginners understand why they must stay clear of overhead work and never stand under suspended loads.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify basic overhead hazards and explain why they should stay out of rigging zones unless directed.

Live event worksites often include overhead activity. This may involve rigging, truss, motors, lighting fixtures, speakers, video walls, scenic pieces, points, cables, chain hoists, lifts, ladders, or tools being used above.

This lesson is awareness-level only. It should not teach learners how to rig, fly equipment, operate motors, use lifts, climb structures, or inspect rigging systems.

The main rule for beginners: stay clear of overhead work and never stand under suspended loads.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

One of the most important things a new stagehand needs to learn is this: look up.

Live event productions often involve overhead work. Riggers may be hanging points, motors, truss, speakers, lighting, video, scenic pieces, soft goods, or other production elements. Lighting crews may be working on truss. Audio crews may be flying speaker arrays. Video crews may be building LED walls. Lifts may be moving overhead. Tools or hardware may be in use above the floor.

If you are new, you are not expected to understand everything happening overhead. But you are expected to respect it.

Never stand under a suspended load. Never walk through a rigging zone as a shortcut. Never touch motors, rigging hardware, truss, points, steel, spansets, shackles, chain bags, or flown equipment unless you are trained, authorized, and specifically directed.

Overhead work can change quickly. A piece of gear that looks still may be about to move. A zone that looked open a moment ago may become restricted. A lift may rotate. A truss may move. A load may be in the air.

Your best protection is awareness. Keep your head up, listen for callouts, watch for cones or tape, and follow the directions of leads, riggers, and department heads.

Do not wear earbuds or headphones in active work areas unless your employer specifically allows it. You need to hear horns, warnings, callouts, and emergency instructions.

If you are assigned to work near an overhead area, ask where it is safe to stand and what path you should use. Do not guess.

#### ***Common Overhead Hazards***

● Suspended truss  
 ● Motors and chain hoists  
 ● Speaker arrays  
 ● Lighting fixtures  
 ● LED walls or video equipment  
 ● Scenic pieces  
 ● Tools or hardware used overhead  
 ● Lifts or ladders  
 ● Cable drops  
 ● Rigging points  
 ● Chain bags  
 ● Workers operating above floor level

#### ***Example Situations***

#### ***Situation 1***

You see a truss hanging overhead and workers adjusting equipment nearby.

Best action:

Stay clear and wait for direction. Do not walk underneath it.

#### ***Situation 2***

You are told to bring a case near the stage, but riggers are working above that area.

Best action:

Ask your lead where to stage the case safely.

#### ***Situation 3***

You see a motor chain hanging low and think it should be moved out of the way.

Best action:

Do not touch it. Notify your lead or the rigging department.

#### ***Situation 4***

A crew member yells, “Heads\!”

Best action:

Stop, protect yourself, and pay attention immediately. Follow the direction of the lead or crew in that area.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Pause before acting, look for hazards, and ask the correct lead when anything is unclear or outside your training.

#### ***Safe Awareness Habits***

● Look up when entering a new work area  
 ● Stay out of rigging zones unless directed  
 ● Never stand under suspended loads  
 ● Do not walk under active overhead work as a shortcut  
 ● Respect cones, tape, barricade, and verbal boundaries  
 ● Do not touch rigging equipment unless trained and assigned  
 ● Listen for callouts from riggers and leads  
 ● Ask where to stand if you are unsure  
 ● Move away from overhead work when told immediately  
 ● Do not wear earbuds or headphones in active work areas unless specifically allowed

This lesson is awareness only. It does not qualify anyone to perform rigging work, operate motors, climb structures, use lifts, or work with suspended loads. Rigging and overhead work require proper training, authorization, supervision, and site-specific procedures.

If it is overhead, suspended, being lifted, or part of a rigging system, treat it with serious respect. Stay clear unless trained, authorized, and directed.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Overhead hazards are serious. A new stagehand should look up, stay out of rigging zones, avoid suspended loads, and never touch rigging equipment unless properly trained, authorized, and assigned.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Do I naturally look up when I enter a stage, dock, or arena floor, or do I only look at what is directly in front of me?

Question: What is the safest beginner behavior around rigging or overhead work?

**Best Answer:**

Stay clear, never stand under suspended loads, do not touch rigging equipment, follow boundaries, and ask a lead where it is safe to stand or walk.

# **Module 4, Lesson 7: Cable Paths, Trip Hazards, and Clean Work Areas**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands how cable paths, clutter, trash, tape, cases, and poor housekeeping can create hazards. The goal is to help learners understand that keeping work areas clean is part of safety and professionalism.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common trip hazards and explain how to help keep work areas clean, organized, and safe under direction.

Cable paths are common in live events. They may include audio, lighting, video, data, feeder, power, broadcast, communication, or control lines.

Beginners should not unplug, pull, coil, tape, or move cables unless directed by the proper department.

Trip hazards can come from cables, tape balls, trash, loose cases, lids, tools, straps, packing materials, uneven surfaces, rugs, ramps, dock plates, weather, or poor lighting.

Clean work areas help the crew move safely and efficiently.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Live event jobsites can become cluttered quickly.

During load-in, gear is coming out of trucks, cases are being opened, cable is being run, tools are being used, tape is being pulled, and departments are building at the same time. During load-out, everything is moving even faster, and the crew may be tired.

That is why clean work areas matter.

A cable path is the route cables take across a stage, floor, backstage area, FOH position, dock, or venue space. Cable paths may be temporary, but they are still important. Some cables may carry power. Some may carry audio, lighting, video, data, communication, or control signals. Some may still be in use even if they look loose.

As a beginner, do not unplug, pull, coil, tape, or move cable unless the proper department or lead tells you to. Pulling the wrong cable can damage equipment, interrupt the show, create electrical hazards, or slow down the crew.

Trip hazards are also a major issue. A small object in the wrong place can cause someone to fall while carrying gear. A case lid left open can block a path. Loose tape can catch a foot. A cable crossing can become dangerous if it is not protected or marked. Trash, plastic wrap, cardboard, straps, tools, and empty water bottles can all become hazards.

Keeping a clean work area does not mean moving other departments’ gear without asking. It means picking up obvious trash when appropriate, keeping your personal items out of work paths, returning tools where directed, stacking empties where assigned, and reporting hazards you are not sure how to fix.

Do not correct a problem in a way that creates a bigger one. For example, do not pull a cable out of a walkway if you do not know what it is connected to. Do not move a road case if it was intentionally staged there. Do not relocate tools, cable, or department gear without asking.

A clean work area tells the crew that you are paying attention.

#### ***Common Trip Hazards***

● Loose cables  
 ● Cable crossings  
 ● Tape balls  
 ● Case lids  
 ● Empty water bottles  
 ● Plastic wrap  
 ● Cardboard  
 ● Tools  
 ● Straps  
 ● Road case brakes left unlocked  
 ● Uneven flooring  
 ● Ramps and dock plates  
 ● Poor lighting  
 ● Wet spots  
 ● Loose carpet or mats  
 ● Personal bags in work paths

#### ***Safe Work Area Habits***

● Keep personal items out of work paths  
 ● Do not leave trash behind  
 ● Pick up obvious trash when appropriate  
 ● Do not move department gear without permission  
 ● Ask before coiling or pulling cable  
 ● Watch your footing around cable paths  
 ● Step over cables carefully when permitted  
 ● Use assigned walkways when available  
 ● Report wet floors, loose cable, or blocked paths  
 ● Keep case lids, tools, and packing materials organized  
 ● Do not block doors, ramps, aisles, fire lanes, or emergency exits  
 ● Do not run through active work areas  
 ● Do not wear earbuds or headphones in active work areas unless specifically allowed

#### ***Example Situations***

#### ***Situation 1***

You see a cable crossing a walkway.

Best action:

Do not move it unless directed. Notify your lead or the department responsible and ask how they want it handled.

#### ***Situation 2***

You see trash, tape balls, and empty bottles in a work path.

Best action:

Clean up obvious trash if it is safe to do so, or notify your lead if the area is active or unsafe.

#### ***Situation 3***

You finish using a tool and set it on the edge of the stage.

Best action:

Do not leave tools where they can fall, trip someone, or get lost. Return the tool where directed.

#### ***Situation 4***

You see someone about to push a case through a messy cable area.

Best action:

Call attention to the hazard and ask the lead or department how to route the case safely.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Pause before acting, look for hazards, and ask the correct lead when anything is unclear or outside your training.

Do not “clean up” by moving cables, tools, cases, or equipment that belong to another department unless you are told to. Some items may be placed intentionally, still connected, or part of an active system.

If you see a hazard, report it. If you can safely correct a simple housekeeping issue without touching department gear or entering a restricted area, do so. If you are unsure, stop and ask.

A clean work area is a safer work area. Good stagehands help reduce trip hazards without interfering with department gear.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Cable paths and clutter can create serious trip and movement hazards. A new stagehand should keep personal items out of the way, clean up obvious trash when safe, avoid moving department gear without permission, and report hazards they are unsure how to correct.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Do I notice small hazards like tape balls, case lids, loose straps, or personal bags in walkways?

Question: What should a new stagehand do before moving or coiling a cable?

**Best Answer:**

Ask the proper lead or department first, because the cable may still be connected, in use, powered, or routed intentionally.

# **Module 4, Lesson 8: Emergency Exits, Fire Lanes, and Keeping Paths Clear**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands why emergency exits, fire lanes, aisles, ramps, docks, and access paths must stay clear. The goal is to help learners understand that blocked pathways are not just inconvenient; they can become serious safety problems.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify paths that must stay clear and explain what to do if they see an exit, aisle, fire lane, dock, or access route blocked.

Live event jobsites require clear paths for workers, emergency response, equipment movement, venue staff, production crew, and sometimes audience access.

Emergency exits, fire lanes, aisles, dock doors, ramps, stairways, hallways, truck paths, and access routes should not be blocked by cases, carts, cable, trash, personal bags, barricade, road boxes, or tools.

A beginner should never stage gear in front of an emergency exit, fire extinguisher, fire lane, electrical panel, stairway, ramp, dock door, or main travel path unless specifically directed by a lead and the area remains compliant with site rules.

Fire lane and emergency access requirements can vary by venue, event, and local authority, so beginners should follow site markings and report blocked access routes to their lead.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

One of the easiest mistakes for a new stagehand to make is putting something down in the wrong place.

A road case may only be there for a minute. A cart may seem out of the way. A bag may seem small. But emergency exits and exit routes cannot be blocked, even temporarily. Other access paths, ramps, dock doors, aisles, and fire lanes also need to stay clear according to venue rules, site procedures, and applicable fire/life-safety requirements.

Live event sites depend on movement. Crew members need to move gear. Emergency personnel may need access. Venue staff may need to reach doors, panels, extinguishers, or exits. Departments may need clear paths to stage equipment. In some spaces, public safety and fire code requirements may apply.

As a beginner, pay attention to anything marked “Exit,” “Emergency Exit,” “Fire Lane,” “Do Not Block,” “Electrical Panel,” “Fire Extinguisher,” or “Authorized Personnel Only.” Do not block these areas with gear or personal items.

Also pay attention to unmarked paths. The dock path, truck lane, ramp, main backstage hallway, FOH access route, barricade opening, and stage access stairs may all need to stay clear even if there is no sign.

If you are told to place a case somewhere and it looks like it may block an exit or path, ask for clarification. A simple question can prevent a serious issue:

“Is this okay here, or do you want it clear of the exit?”

Keeping paths clear is one of the most useful things a new stagehand can do. It shows awareness, safety, and respect for the whole production.

#### ***Areas That Must Stay Clear***

● Emergency exits  
 ● Fire lanes  
 ● Fire extinguishers  
 ● Electrical panels  
 ● Stairways  
 ● Ramps  
 ● Dock doors  
 ● Truck lanes  
 ● Main aisles  
 ● Backstage hallways  
 ● Stage access points  
 ● FOH access paths  
 ● Barricade openings  
 ● Lift travel paths  
 ● Medical access routes  
 ● Any area marked “Do Not Block”

#### ***Safe Pathway Habits***

● Do not stage cases in front of exits  
 ● Do not block fire extinguishers or electrical panels  
 ● Keep personal bags out of walkways  
 ● Leave dock doors and ramps clear unless actively working there  
 ● Ask before placing gear in a hallway or aisle  
 ● Move trash, tape, and small obstacles out of paths when safe  
 ● Report blocked exits or fire lanes to your lead  
 ● Watch for signs, cones, tape, and venue markings  
 ● Never assume “just for a minute” is okay  
 ● Do not run through docks, ramps, backstage areas, truck lanes, or active work zones  
 ● Move with purpose, but stay controlled

#### ***Example Situations***

#### ***Situation 1***

You are told to place a road case near a wall, but the wall has an emergency exit door.

Best action:

Ask your lead where to place it without blocking the exit.

#### ***Situation 2***

You see a pile of cable cases blocking a fire extinguisher.

Best action:

Notify your lead or the department responsible so the access can be cleared.

#### ***Situation 3***

You set your backpack in a backstage hallway.

Best action:

Move it to the approved crew area or personal item location. Do not leave personal items in walkways.

#### ***Situation 4***

A cart is parked in the dock path while no one is using it.

Best action:

Do not move it without knowing where it belongs, but notify your lead that the path may be blocked.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Never block emergency exits, fire lanes, extinguishers, electrical panels, aisles, ramps, stairways, dock paths, or access routes. If you see a blocked safety path, report it to your lead.

Fire lane and emergency access rules may vary by venue, city, event, and local authority. Your role as a beginner is to recognize blocked access, avoid creating blockages, and report concerns through the correct chain of command.

Clear paths protect everyone. A professional stagehand helps keep exits, lanes, aisles, ramps, and access routes open.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Keeping paths clear is part of jobsite awareness. A new stagehand should never block emergency exits, fire lanes, ramps, docks, aisles, stairways, or access routes, even temporarily.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

When I put down a case, bag, tool, or piece of gear, do I check whether it blocks a path someone may need?

Question: What should a new stagehand do if they notice an emergency exit or fire lane is blocked?

**Best Answer:**

Report it to their lead immediately and help clear it only if directed and safe to do so.

# Module 4 Training Activity

# **Module 4 Practical Activity: Venue Map Awareness Exercise**

## **Activity Purpose**

This activity helps learners practice reading a simple venue or jobsite layout before they enter a real call.

## **Learner Instructions**

Review a simple venue map or diagram. Identify the following areas:

● Safe walking routes  
 ● Truck dock  
 ● Stage  
 ● Emergency exits  
 ● High-traffic zones  
 ● Restricted areas  
 ● Forklift or equipment travel paths  
 ● Cable paths  
 ● Areas to avoid without direction  
 ● Where a new stagehand should check in or wait

## **Discussion Questions**

Where should a new stagehand check in first?

Which areas should stay clear at all times?

Where might forklifts, pallet jacks, carts, or road cases be moving?

Which areas should a beginner avoid without direction?

Where are the emergency exits and fire lanes?

What paths would be safest for walking?

What areas could become trip hazards during load-in or load-out?

Where could overhead work or rigging activity be happening?

What areas should not be used as shortcuts?

Where should personal bags or non-work items be kept?

## **Best Answer Summary**

A strong learner response should identify safe walking paths, truck and dock areas, the stage, emergency exits, high-traffic movement zones, and restricted areas.

The learner should understand that they should check in first, find their lead, avoid wandering, stay out of equipment paths, never stand under suspended loads, avoid restricted areas, and never block emergency access routes.

## **Module 4 Key Outcome**

By the end of Module 4, learners should know how to enter a venue, observe the jobsite, find their lead, recognize common movement hazards, stay out of restricted areas, avoid overhead and equipment hazards, respect cable paths, and help keep work areas and emergency routes clear.

# End of Module 4 Quiz

# **Module 4 Quiz: Venue and Jobsite Awareness**

## **Quiz Instructions**

Choose the best answer for each question. This quiz checks your understanding of how to enter a venue or jobsite, find your lead, recognize common work areas, stay clear of restricted zones, avoid movement hazards, respect overhead work, and keep paths clear.

**Recommended passing score:** 80%  
 Total questions: 20

# **Multiple Choice**

## **1. What is the main purpose of Module 4?**

A. To teach new stagehands how to operate forklifts  
 B. To teach new stagehands how to rig overhead equipment  
 C. To teach new stagehands how to move through a venue without becoming a hazard  
 D. To teach new stagehands how to design a stage plot

**Correct Answer:** C

## **2. What should a new stagehand do during the first few minutes after arriving on-site?**

A. Start moving cases immediately  
 B. Check in, find the correct reporting area, observe the jobsite, and wait for direction  
 C. Walk around backstage to explore the venue  
 D. Open road cases to see what department they belong to

**Correct Answer:** B

## **3. Why are the first 15 minutes on-site important?**

A. They are a good time to take a break  
 B. They help the stagehand get oriented, identify hazards, and find the correct lead  
 C. They are when stagehands choose which department they want to work in  
 D. They are only important for supervisors

**Correct Answer:** B

## **4. What should a beginner do if they do not know where to stand after checking in?**

A. Stand in the loading dock doorway  
 B. Stand near the biggest group of people  
 C. Ask their lead or check-in contact where they should wait  
 D. Walk onto the stage and wait there

**Correct Answer:** C

## **5. Who is usually the new stagehand’s main point of contact for direction?**

A. Their lead or assigned department contact  
 B. Any person wearing black clothing  
 C. Audience members  
 D. Vendors or catering staff

**Correct Answer:** A

## **6. Why should a stagehand avoid taking direction from random people on-site?**

A. Because no one is allowed to talk during load-in  
 B. Because only performers can give instructions  
 C. Because taking direction from the wrong person can create confusion, move gear incorrectly, or cause safety issues  
 D. Because beginners should make all decisions alone

**Correct Answer:** C

## **7. What does FOH usually mean in production language?**

A. Forklift operating hub  
 B. Front of house, often the audio, lighting, or production control position in the audience area  
 C. Fire overhead hazard  
 D. Freight-only hallway

**Correct Answer:** B

## **8. What is the dock?**

A. The public lobby  
 B. The truck loading and unloading area  
 C. The dressing room hallway  
 D. The audience seating area

**Correct Answer:** B

## **9. Why should a new stagehand avoid standing in dock doors, ramps, or active floor paths?**

A. Those areas are usually reserved for performers  
 B. Those areas are used for gear movement, trucks, carts, forklifts, pallet jacks, and crew traffic  
 C. Those areas are only for lunch breaks  
 D. Those areas are safer than other locations

**Correct Answer:** B

## **10. What should a new stagehand do before entering a restricted area?**

A. Enter quickly and leave quickly  
 B. Follow someone else through the door  
 C. Ask for permission or direction from the proper lead  
 D. Assume it is fine if the door is open

**Correct Answer:** C

## **11. Which of the following is an example of a restricted area?**

A. A rigging zone  
 B. A dressing room hallway  
 C. A taped-off lift area  
 D. All of the above

**Correct Answer:** D

## **12. What is the safest behavior around forklifts and other moving equipment?**

A. Assume the operator can see you  
 B. Walk behind moving equipment if you are in a hurry  
 C. Stay alert, keep distance, avoid blind spots, and make eye contact when possible  
 D. Ride on a pallet jack if someone offers

**Correct Answer:** C

## **13. When should a new stagehand operate a forklift, powered pusher, tug, lift, or other powered equipment?**

A. When they have watched someone else do it  
 B. When the crew is short-handed  
 C. Only when their employer has trained, evaluated, authorized, and assigned them to operate that specific equipment  
 D. Whenever the equipment is not being used

**Correct Answer:** C

## **14. Why should stagehands avoid wearing earbuds or headphones in active work areas unless specifically allowed?**

A. They may not match show blacks  
 B. They can prevent workers from hearing horns, callouts, equipment movement, warnings, or emergency instructions  
 C. They make it harder to talk during lunch  
 D. They are only allowed during show call

**Correct Answer:** B

## **15. What is the safest beginner behavior around rigging or overhead work?**

A. Stand under the truss to watch  
 B. Walk through the rigging zone as a shortcut  
 C. Stay clear, never stand under suspended loads, and follow direction from leads or riggers  
 D. Move rigging hardware out of the way if it looks messy

**Correct Answer:** C

## **16. What should a stagehand do if they hear someone yell “Heads\!”?**

A. Ignore it unless they are working overhead  
 B. Stop, protect themselves, pay attention immediately, and follow direction  
 C. Run toward the sound  
 D. Keep working without looking up

**Correct Answer:** B

## **17. What should a new stagehand do before moving, unplugging, pulling, taping, or coiling a cable?**

A. Ask the proper lead or department first  
 B. Move it if it looks loose  
 C. Pull it out of the way to prevent tripping  
 D. Coil it and place it in the nearest case

**Correct Answer:** A

## **18. Which of the following can create a trip hazard?**

A. Loose cables  
 B. Tape balls  
 C. Case lids and personal bags in walkways  
 D. All of the above

**Correct Answer:** D

## **19. What should a stagehand do if they notice an emergency exit or fire lane is blocked?**

A. Ignore it unless someone complains  
 B. Report it to their lead and help clear it only if directed and safe to do so  
 C. Place more gear there if there is room  
 D. Move everything without asking, including department gear

**Correct Answer:** B

## **20. Which statement best describes good venue and jobsite awareness?**

A. Moving fast, even if you are unsure  
 B. Staying alert, finding your lead, avoiding restricted areas, respecting moving equipment, and keeping paths clear  
 C. Walking around until you find something to do  
 D. Touching gear first and asking questions later

**Correct Answer:** B

# **Scenario Questions**

These can be used as written-response questions or discussion prompts.

## **Scenario 1**

You arrive at a venue for load-in. You see road cases moving, forklifts operating near the dock, and several groups of crew working in different areas. You do not know who your lead is yet.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Check in at the correct location and ask who you report to before starting work. Stay out of active paths, observe the jobsite, and wait for direction from your assigned lead or department contact.

## **Scenario 2**

Someone you do not recognize tells you to move a case into the truck. You are not sure if they are part of the truck loading crew or your assigned department.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Confirm with your lead before moving the case. Taking direction from the wrong person can move gear to the wrong place, disrupt the truck pack, or create a safety problem.

## **Scenario 3**

You see a taped-off area near the stage, but walking through it would be a faster way to get to the other side.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Do not cross the taped-off area. Restricted areas may contain hazards that are not obvious. Use an approved walking route or ask your lead where it is safe to walk.

## **Scenario 4**

A forklift is backing up with a large load, and you need to cross behind it to reach your department.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Wait until the forklift has stopped or the operator clearly signals that it is safe. Stay out of blind spots and do not assume the operator can see you.

## **Scenario 5**

You are told to bring a case near the stage, but riggers are working overhead in that area.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Do not walk under the overhead work or suspended load. Ask your lead where the case should be staged safely until the area is clear or approved for access.

## **Scenario 6**

You see a cable crossing a walkway. You are not sure what it is connected to, but it looks like a trip hazard.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Do not move, unplug, pull, tape, or coil the cable without direction. Notify your lead or the department responsible and ask how they want it handled.

## **Scenario 7**

You finish drinking a bottle of water and are tempted to set the empty bottle behind a road case until later.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Throw it away in the proper place if it is safe to do so. Trash, bottles, tape balls, and small items can become trip hazards in active work areas.

## **Scenario 8**

You are asked to place a road case near a wall, but you notice the wall has an emergency exit door.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Ask your lead where to place the case without blocking the emergency exit. Emergency exits and exit routes must stay clear, even temporarily.

## **Scenario 9**

You are working load-out and people are moving quickly. You feel pressure to run through the backstage hallway to keep up.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Do not run through active work areas. Move with purpose, but stay controlled. Running can cause falls, collisions, and unsafe reactions around moving gear.

## **Scenario 10**

You see a personal backpack sitting in a narrow backstage hallway where crew members are pushing cases.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Report it to your lead or move it only if it is clearly yours and safe to relocate. Personal items should be kept in approved areas, not in work paths.
