# Module 5

# **Module 5: Load-In Fundamentals**

## **Module Overview**

Load-in is one of the most important parts of live event work. It is the process of bringing production equipment into a venue and helping build the show before the audience arrives.

For a new stagehand, load-in may be one of the first calls they ever work. This module teaches the basic rhythm of a load-in so learners understand what is happening around them, how gear moves, how departments get set up, and how to stay useful without creating safety problems.

Load-in can feel fast, loud, and confusing at first. Trucks are being unloaded, road cases are moving, departments are sorting gear, staging may be built, cable may be run, and crew leads are trying to keep everything on schedule.

The goal of this module is not to train a beginner to perform specialized department work. The goal is to help them recognize the flow of load-in, follow direction, identify common hazards, and know when to ask for help.

## **Safety and Training Reminder**

This module is for entry-level awareness only. It does not authorize a learner to operate forklifts, powered equipment, lifts, rigging systems, electrical systems, stage machinery, or specialized production equipment.

Always follow employer training, venue rules, site-specific procedures, union rules where applicable, and directions from authorized leads or supervisors.

Every venue, employer, union, production, and event may have different procedures. This course gives general awareness, but the site-specific briefing and assigned lead direction always control the work.

## **Module Learning Objective**

By the end of this module, learners should be able to explain the basic flow of a load-in, recognize common load-in tasks and hazards, and understand how to move, sort, and handle gear safely under direction.

## **Module Lessons**

What Load-In Means

Truck Unload Basics

**Road Cases:** How They Move, Stack, Lock, and Tip

Pushing vs. Pulling vs. Guiding Cases

Ramps, Dock Plates, and Pinch Points

**Building the Room:** Stage, Audio, Lighting, Video, Backline, Rigging

**Department Sorting:** Getting the Right Case to the Right Place

**Cable Awareness:** Recognizing Cable Hazards and Knowing When Not to Touch

When to Move Fast and When to Slow Down

How to Take Direction Without Needing Everything Explained Twice

# Lesson 5.1

# **Module 5, Lesson 1: What Load-In Means**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to the meaning of load-in and explains why it is one of the most important parts of live event production. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that load-in is not random labor. It is an organized process that prepares the venue for the event.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain what load-in means and describe the general purpose of a load-in call.

Load-in is the process of moving production equipment into a venue and preparing the space for the event.

This may include unloading trucks, pushing road cases, sorting equipment, setting up staging, helping departments get gear to the right location, assisting with cable runs under direction, moving barricade, and keeping work areas clear.

For beginners, load-in is usually where they start learning how live event crews work. It shows them how gear moves, how departments communicate, and how much organization happens before the audience ever arrives.

Load-in is not just about speed. It is about working safely, following direction, staying aware, and helping the crew build the show in the correct order.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Load-in is the beginning of the physical setup for a live event.

Before the audience arrives, before the show starts, and before the room looks finished, the crew has to bring in the equipment that makes the event possible.

That equipment may arrive in trucks, trailers, vans, carts, or freight areas. It may include road cases, staging, barricade, audio equipment, lighting equipment, video gear, backline, cables, scenic pieces, tools, and production supplies.

As a new stagehand, you may be asked to unload trucks, push cases, move gear to assigned areas, help keep pathways clear, report hazards, or assist departments under direction.

At first, load-in can feel chaotic. There may be forklifts moving, leads calling out instructions, cases rolling across the floor, and multiple departments working at the same time.

But a good load-in is not chaos. It is a coordinated process.

Every case, cable, deck, barricade piece, and piece of gear usually has a reason for going where it goes. Your job as a beginner is not to guess that reason. Your job is to listen, follow direction, ask questions when needed, and move safely.

Load-in builds the show. A good stagehand helps that process happen without slowing the crew down or creating hazards.

#### ***Common Load-In Tasks***

Checking in with the correct lead or department

Listening during the safety briefing

Unloading trucks under direction

Pushing road cases

Sorting cases by department

Moving staging, barricade, chairs, risers, or scenic pieces under direction

Helping with cable paths under direction

Keeping aisles, exits, docks, and work areas clear

Cleaning up trash, tape, packing material, or loose items

Reporting hazards such as blocked exits, damaged gear, loose cable, spills, or unsafe movement to a lead

Checking back in when a task is complete

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Move with purpose during load-in, but confirm where gear goes before touching, opening, or relocating it.

Do not assume you should move something just because it looks like it is in the way. Gear, cable, cases, barricade, or tools may be staged for a specific reason. If something appears unsafe or blocked, report it to your lead instead of moving it without direction.

Load-in is the organized process of bringing production equipment into the venue and building the show safely, efficiently, and in the correct order.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Load-in is where the show starts coming together. A new stagehand should focus on safety, listening, following direction, moving gear carefully, reporting hazards, and checking in with leads instead of guessing.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I walked into my first load-in and saw cases everywhere, would I know to ask for direction instead of guessing where things go?

Question: What is load-in?

**Best Answer:**  
 Load-in is the process of moving production equipment into a venue and helping build or prepare the event space before the show or event begins.

# **Module 5, Lesson 2: Truck Unload Basics**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners the basics of truck unloads during load-in. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that unloading a truck is an organized process that requires direction, spacing, communication, and awareness.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe the basic expectations of a truck unload and identify common safety concerns around trucks, docks, and moving gear.

Truck unloads are often one of the first major tasks during load-in.

Equipment is usually packed in a specific order. Cases, carts, decks, truss, barricade, cable trunks, and department gear may all need to come off the truck in sequence.

New stagehands should not climb into trucks, move gear, stack cases, operate powered equipment, or change the unload order unless directed by the lead, truck loader, or appropriate supervisor.

The dock area can be busy and hazardous. There may be ramps, dock plates, forklifts, pallet jacks, moving carts, uneven surfaces, low lighting, or tight spacing.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

A truck unload is one of the most common parts of load-in.

When a truck arrives, it may be packed tightly with cases, carts, decks, lighting gear, audio gear, video gear, barricade, cable, and other production equipment. That gear is usually packed in an intentional order.

To a new stagehand, it may look like everything just needs to come off the truck as quickly as possible. But truck unloading is not random.

Some gear may need to come off first because another department needs it right away. Some cases may be stacked in a certain way so they do not shift. Some pieces may be heavy, awkward, fragile, or part of a specific truck pack plan.

That is why you should always follow the direction of the truck lead, dock lead, department head, or assigned supervisor.

Do not climb into a truck unless you are told to and safe access is provided. Do not pull cases from the stack unless you are told to. Do not remove straps, bars, decking beams, or load-control equipment unless you have been directed and understand what you are doing.

Around trucks and docks, awareness is critical. Watch your feet, hands, and body position. Stay out of pinch points. Keep clear of rolling cases. Do not stand behind a case on a ramp. Watch for forklifts, pallet jacks, carts, and other workers.

Forklifts and powered equipment create serious struck-by and caught-between hazards. Stay outside marked travel paths, keep distance from moving equipment, and do not assume the operator sees you.

A safe truck unload depends on communication, spacing, and controlled movement.

#### ***Basic Truck Unload Expectations***

Check in before joining the truck line

Listen for the unload plan

Follow direction from the truck lead or assigned supervisor

Keep hands and feet clear of pinch points

Do not stand behind rolling gear on ramps

Watch for forklifts, carts, pallet jacks, and other moving equipment

Stay out of forklift and powered-equipment travel paths

Make eye contact with equipment operators when possible

Never assume an equipment operator sees you

Move cases only where directed

Do not open cases unless instructed

Do not stack gear randomly

Keep dock paths and exits clear

Communicate if something is too heavy or unsafe to move alone

#### ***Do Not Do This Unless Authorized***

Do not operate forklifts, powered pallet jacks, tuggers, lift gates, scissor lifts, boom lifts, or other powered equipment.

Do not remove load bars, straps, decking beams, or restraints unless directed by the truck lead or authorized supervisor.

Do not climb onto stacked gear.

Do not jump from the truck or dock.

Do not stand between the truck and dock.

Do not stand between a case and a wall.

Do not stand behind rolling gear.

Do not enter the trailer unless assigned and safe access is provided.

Do not use manual pallet jacks unless trained, allowed by site procedure, and assigned.

#### ***Common Hazards During Truck Unload***

Rolling road cases

Pinch points between cases

Cases shifting inside trailers

Dock edges

Ramps and dock plates

Forklifts and pallet jacks

Poor lighting

Tight spaces

Heavy or awkward gear

Rushing or crowding the truck door

Uneven walking surfaces

Blocked exits or travel paths

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: During load-out, slow down enough to keep control, protect the sequence, and ask before moving gear you do not understand.

Never stand between moving equipment and a fixed object such as a wall, truck, dock, barricade, stage edge, or another case. This can create a crush hazard.

Truck unloads are controlled, organized, and directed. A new stagehand should not guess the unload order, operate equipment without authorization, or move gear without instruction.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A truck unload is not just “take everything off the truck.” It is an organized process that requires direction, spacing, awareness, authorization, and safe movement.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I am standing at a truck unload and do not know what case comes next, what should I do?

Question: Why should a new stagehand not randomly pull cases from a truck?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because trucks are usually packed in a specific order, and moving the wrong case can create confusion, damage gear, slow the unload, or create a safety hazard.

# **Module 5, Lesson 3: Road Cases: How They Move, Stack, Lock, and Tip**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to road cases and explains why they must be handled carefully. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that road cases can be heavy, expensive, unstable, and dangerous when moved incorrectly.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify basic road case handling concerns, including casters, brakes, lids, stacking, tipping, and controlled movement.

Road cases are used to transport and protect production equipment.

They may contain audio gear, lighting gear, video equipment, cables, tools, wardrobe, instruments, backline, scenic items, or other show materials.

Road cases can be heavy, awkward, tall, narrow, or top-heavy. Some roll easily. Others may have bad casters, locked wheels, loose lids, damaged latches, or uneven weight.

New stagehands should move cases under direction and pay attention to brakes, labels, handles, lids, casters, slopes, and the path of travel.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Road cases are one of the most common things you will handle as a stagehand.

A road case is a protective case used to move production equipment from one place to another. They are built to travel, roll, stack, and protect gear. But that does not mean they are harmless.

Some road cases are small and easy to move. Others are large, heavy, tall, awkward, or expensive. Some may contain delicate equipment. Some may be top-heavy and easier to tip than they look.

Before moving a case, take a quick visual check. Are the brakes locked? Are the latches closed? Is the lid secure? Are the wheels damaged or stuck? Is the path clear?

Do not assume every case should be opened. Many cases are moved closed and opened later by the correct department.

When moving cases, control matters. Do not run. Do not ride cases. Do not shove cases blindly around corners. Do not push a case into a crowded area without checking the path.

Tall cases and narrow cases can tip. Cases on ramps can gain speed. Cases with bad casters may pull to one side. Cases with locked brakes may not move smoothly.

Do not stack cases unless directed by a lead or department head. Some cases are designed to stack; others are not. Stacking the wrong case, stacking too high, or stacking on an uneven surface can cause gear to fall or tip.

If a case feels too heavy, unstable, stuck, damaged, or unsafe, stop and ask for help.

#### ***Road Case Parts to Notice***

Casters: The wheels under the case

Brakes: Wheel locks that keep the case from rolling

Handles: Safe gripping points

Latches: Hardware that keeps lids closed

Lids: Removable or hinged covers

Labels: Department, tour, or case information

Arrows or markings: Instructions about orientation or handling

Stacking features: Case shapes or hardware designed for safe stacking

#### ***Safe Road Case Habits***

Check that brakes are released before pushing

Make sure latches and lids appear secure before moving

Use handles when available

Keep hands clear of edges and pinch points

Watch for uneven floors, ramps, cables, and thresholds

Slow down around corners

Use a spotter when visibility is blocked

Do not push a case into people

Do not open cases unless told to

Do not stack cases unless directed

Ask for help with heavy, damaged, or unstable cases

Report damaged wheels, broken brakes, loose lids, or unstable stacks to a lead

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Keep your head up around moving gear, control your path, and never assume equipment operators or other crew members can see you.

A road case can injure someone if it tips, rolls into a person, crushes a hand, or pins someone against a wall, dock, stage, or another case. Move cases with control, not force.

Road cases are common, but they are not simple. They must be moved with awareness, control, and direction.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Road cases protect important gear, but they can also create hazards. Good stagehands move cases carefully, watch the path, use handles, respect brakes and casters, and ask before opening, stacking, or moving anything they are unsure about.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Would I know what to check before pushing a road case across a venue?

Question: What should you do if a road case feels unstable or too heavy to control?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stop and ask for help or direction. Do not force it, run with it, or try to control an unsafe case alone.

# **Module 5, Lesson 4: Pushing vs. Pulling vs. Guiding Cases**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners the difference between pushing, pulling, and guiding cases. The goal is to help new stagehands understand safer body positioning and communication when moving rolling equipment.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain when pushing, pulling, or guiding may be used and why body position matters when moving cases.

Most road cases should be moved with control and clear visibility.

Pushing is often safer than pulling on level surfaces with a clear path, but the safest method depends on the case, slope, surface, visibility, and direction from the lead.

Pulling may be needed for short adjustments, tight spaces, or positioning, but it can be risky if the case rolls toward the worker or if the worker cannot see where they are going.

Guiding means helping steer, spot, or control a case while someone else pushes. It is especially useful with large cases, blind corners, ramps, doorways, and crowded areas.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Moving a road case is not just about getting it from one place to another. It is about controlling the case the entire time.

In many situations, pushing is safer than pulling. When you push, you can use your body position better, see more of the path ahead, and avoid walking backward for long distances.

Pulling can create problems. If you pull a heavy case, it can roll toward you, catch your feet, hit your legs, or pin you if the floor slopes. Pulling also often means you are walking backward or sideways, which can make it harder to see hazards.

That does not mean pulling is never used. Sometimes a case needs to be pulled slightly to reposition it, move it away from a wall, or turn it in a tight space. But pulling should be controlled and short when possible.

Guiding is another important skill. A guide helps steer, spot, or communicate. If a large case blocks the view of the person pushing, another crew member may guide from the front or side. The guide watches for people, cables, corners, doorways, ramps, and obstacles.

Communication matters. If you are moving a case with another person, talk before you move. Know where it is going. Use simple callouts like “ready,” “push,” “slow,” “stop,” “hold,” “watch your feet,” or “corner.”

Everyone should stop immediately when someone calls “stop.”

Never assume someone else sees the same hazard you see.

#### ***Pushing***

Best used when:

You have a clear path

You can control the case

You can see where you are going

The case is not too tall to block visibility

The floor is level or manageable

You are following lead direction

#### ***Pulling***

Use caution when:

Repositioning a case

Moving in tight spaces

Adjusting a case away from a wall

Moving only a short distance

Directed by a lead or experienced crew member

Avoid pulling when:

Walking backward for long distances

Moving downhill or down a ramp

The case is heavy or unstable

You cannot see behind you

The case may roll into your legs or feet

#### ***Guiding***

Helpful when:

A case is tall or blocks visibility

Moving around corners

Going through doors

Moving through crowds

Approaching ramps or dock plates

Navigating tight backstage spaces

Moving through elevators, thresholds, or narrow paths

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Keep your head up around moving gear, control your path, and never assume equipment operators or other crew members can see you.

Never place yourself downhill from a heavy rolling case where it could roll into you. Do not try to stop a heavy case with your body.

Moving cases safely requires control, communication, and proper body position. Pushing, pulling, and guiding all have different uses, but guessing can create hazards.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Good case movement is controlled case movement. Know when to push, when to pull carefully, when to guide, and when to stop and ask for help.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I were helping move a tall case around a corner, how could I help guide it safely?

Question: Why is pushing often safer than pulling a road case?

**Best Answer:**  
 Pushing often gives better control, better body position, and better visibility. Pulling can cause the case to roll toward the worker or require walking backward.

# **Module 5, Lesson 5: Ramps, Dock Plates, and Pinch Points**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners about common load-in hazards around ramps, dock plates, and pinch points. The goal is to help new stagehands understand why transitions between trucks, docks, floors, and ramps require extra caution.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common hazards around ramps, dock plates, and pinch points and describe safer behaviors when moving gear through these areas.

Ramps and dock plates are common during load-in, especially when moving gear from trucks to venue floors.

These areas can create hazards because cases may gain speed, wheels may catch, surfaces may shift, and workers may be crowded into narrow spaces.

Pinch points happen when hands, feet, or body parts can be caught between moving gear and another object.

Common pinch points include between cases, between a case and a wall, between a case and a truck, between a wheel and a dock plate, or between gear and a doorway.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Ramps and dock plates deserve extra attention during load-in.

A ramp is any sloped surface used to move gear between different heights. A dock plate is a metal bridge between a truck and a loading dock or between two uneven surfaces.

Both are useful, but both can be hazardous.

When a case rolls onto a ramp, gravity can take over quickly. A case that felt easy to control on flat ground may suddenly become faster and heavier on a slope.

Dock plates can also be tricky. Wheels may catch on the edge. Plates may be uneven. The surface may be slick, noisy, or crowded. If too many people try to move through the same space at once, the risk goes up.

Do not place, move, adjust, or remove a dock plate unless authorized. If a dock plate looks unstable, misaligned, wet, damaged, or unsafe, stop and report it.

Pinch points are another major concern. A pinch point is any place where part of your body can get caught between objects. Your fingers can be pinched between road cases. Your foot can get caught under a wheel. Your body can be trapped between a rolling case and a wall.

The best way to avoid pinch points is to stay aware of where your hands, feet, and body are before the gear moves.

Do not wrap your fingers around edges that may close against another case. Do not put your foot in front of a wheel. Do not stand between a case and a wall, truck, dock, or stage edge.

Do not crowd ramps. Only the needed workers should be on or near the ramp while gear is moving. Extra people standing nearby can create trip hazards and reduce escape space.

When moving gear over ramps or dock plates, slow down, communicate, and follow direction.

#### ***Common Ramp Hazards***

Cases rolling too fast

Loss of control on slopes

Standing downhill from gear

Wet or slick ramp surfaces

Crowded ramp traffic

Poor visibility

Wheels catching on ramp edges

Trying to stop heavy gear with your body

#### ***Common Dock Plate Hazards***

Uneven transition points

Wheels catching on plate edges

Slippery metal surfaces

Noise that makes communication harder

Gaps between truck and dock

Heavy gear shifting as it crosses

Crowding at the truck door

Unstable, damaged, or misaligned plates

#### ***Common Pinch Points***

Between two road cases

Between a case and a wall

Between a case and a truck

Between a case and a dock edge

Between a case and a doorway

Between handles, lids, and latches

Under casters or wheels

Around moving carts or pallet jacks

#### ***Safer Behaviors***

Slow down at ramps and dock plates

Keep hands on safe handles when available

Watch your feet

Stay out of the downhill path of rolling gear

Communicate with anyone helping move the case

Do not crowd narrow paths

Do not force a case over an edge

Ask for help with heavy or unstable gear

Stop if something feels unsafe

Report unstable ramps, dock plates, or walking surfaces to a lead

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Keep your head up around moving gear, control your path, and never assume equipment operators or other crew members can see you.

Never use your body as a brake for a heavy case. If a case starts moving too fast or feels out of control, call out and get help instead of trying to stop it alone.

Ramps, dock plates, and pinch points are high-risk areas during load-in. Move slowly, communicate clearly, and keep your body out of crush zones.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Ramps, dock plates, and pinch points are common load-in hazards. A careful stagehand slows down, watches body position, communicates, and never tries to force unsafe movement.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Where could my hands or feet get caught if I am moving a road case through a truck door or over a dock plate?

Question: What is a pinch point?

**Best Answer:**  
 A pinch point is any place where a hand, foot, or body part can be caught between moving equipment and another object.

# **Module 5, Lesson 6: Building the Room: Stage, Audio, Lighting, Video, Backline, Rigging**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to how different departments begin building the room during load-in. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that multiple teams are working at the same time and that each department has its own gear, priorities, and safety concerns.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common departments involved in building the room and understand why beginners should support departments only under direction.

During load-in, the room changes from an empty or neutral space into a working event environment.

Different departments may be setting up at the same time, including staging, audio, lighting, video, backline, rigging, production, barricade, and venue support.

Each department has specific gear and tasks. Beginners may help move items, sort cases, clear paths, or assist with basic labor, but they should not touch specialized gear without direction.

Some systems may become energized during load-in. Do not assume equipment is safe to touch just because the show has not started yet.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

During load-in, the room starts to become the show.

At the beginning, the venue may look empty. There may be a bare floor, empty stage area, open dock, or blank room. Then gear starts moving in.

The staging or carpentry crew may begin building platforms, risers, decks, scenic pieces, or stage extensions. Audio may bring in speaker systems, consoles, microphones, stands, and cable. Lighting may bring in fixtures, truss, control systems, and power-related equipment. Video may bring in screens, projectors, cameras, LED panels, or monitors.

Backline may bring in instruments, amplifiers, drum kits, keyboards, stands, and artist-related equipment. Rigging may involve overhead work, motors, points, truss, and suspended equipment.

All of these departments may be working around each other.

As a new stagehand, your job is not to jump into specialized work without being asked. Your job is to help move gear where it belongs, follow directions, keep areas clear, and stay aware of what is happening around you.

Some gear may be fragile. Some may be heavy. Some may be dangerous if handled incorrectly. Some may belong to a department that needs it placed in a very specific location.

Some systems may become energized during setup. Power, lighting, audio, video, motors, and control systems may be active during load-in.

Before touching department gear, ask.

A useful beginner is someone who helps without freelancing. That means you do not randomly open cases, move cables, stack gear, or reorganize equipment unless you are told to.

#### ***Common Departments During Load-In***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Department | What They May Be Doing | Beginner Reminder |
| Staging / Carpentry | Building decks, risers, platforms, scenic pieces | Lift safely and follow placement direction |
| Audio | Setting speakers, consoles, microphones, stands, and cable | Do not move or coil audio lines unless directed |
| Lighting | Setting fixtures, truss, control, and lighting cable | Lighting gear can be heavy, delicate, and may involve energized systems |
| Video | Setting screens, cameras, projectors, LED, monitors | Video gear is often fragile and expensive |
| Backline | Setting instruments, amps, drums, keyboards | Do not touch instruments unless directed |
| Rigging | Handling overhead systems, motors, truss, points | Stay clear unless specifically trained, authorized, and directed |
| Production | Managing show logistics and support needs | Follow chain of command |
| Barricade / Site | Setting crowd control, bike rack, fencing, or floor layout | Watch fingers, feet, and team movement |

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Rigging and overhead work must only be performed by trained, authorized personnel. New stagehands should never stand under suspended loads, enter controlled rigging zones without direction, move rigging hardware, or touch motors, truss, points, bridles, steels, shackles, or spansets unless specifically trained, authorized, and assigned.

Building the room is a coordinated process between multiple departments. Beginners should support the process by following direction, not by guessing what needs to happen next.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Load-in brings many departments together at once. A good stagehand understands the big picture, respects each department’s gear, and helps only in the way they are directed.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I see a lighting case sitting near the stage, should I open it to help, or should I wait for direction?

Question: Why should a beginner avoid touching department gear without direction?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because department gear may be fragile, expensive, dangerous, energized, staged for a specific reason, or require specialized knowledge to handle safely.

# **Module 5, Lesson 7: Department Sorting: Getting the Right Case to the Right Place**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners why department sorting matters during load-in. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that cases are not all the same and that putting gear in the wrong place can slow down the entire production.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why department sorting is important and describe how to move cases to assigned locations without guessing.

During load-in, road cases and gear often need to be sorted by department.

Cases may be marked for audio, lighting, video, rigging, backline, wardrobe, production, scenic, or other departments.

A case placed in the wrong location can delay the crew, create extra movement, block pathways, or interfere with another department.

New stagehands should read labels when available, listen to directions, confirm locations, and ask if they are unsure.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

One of the most useful things a new stagehand can do during load-in is help get the right case to the right place.

When cases come off a truck, they may not all go to the same location. Some may go to stage left. Some may go to stage right. Some may go to front of house. Some may go backstage, to dimmer beach, video world, audio world, wardrobe, production office, backline world, or another assigned area.

At first, these names may be unfamiliar. That is okay.

What matters is that you do not guess.

Many cases have labels, color codes, tape marks, department names, tour markings, or case numbers. These markings help the crew know where the gear belongs.

Labels help, but they are not always current or obvious. If a label conflicts with what a lead told you, ask before moving the case.

If a lead says, “Take these audio cases to stage right,” that means take those specific cases to that specific area. It does not mean open them, reorganize them, or move other nearby cases.

If you do not understand where something goes, ask. A simple question can prevent a lot of wasted time.

Misplaced cases create problems. A department may be waiting on a case that is sitting in the wrong corner of the room. A case in the wrong place may block a cable path, emergency exit, dock door, forklift route, extinguisher, electrical panel, stairway, or access path. It may also need to be moved multiple times, which wastes energy and increases risk.

Good sorting keeps the load-in moving.

#### ***Common Department Labels***

Audio

Lighting

Video

Backline

Rigging

Staging

Carpentry

Wardrobe

Production

Props

Scenic

Cable

Tools

FOH

Stage Left

Stage Right

#### ***Sorting Habits for Beginners***

Listen carefully to the assigned location

Repeat back the instruction if needed

Read labels before moving cases

Move only the cases you were assigned

Do not open cases unless directed

Keep cases out of aisles and exits

Do not block dock doors or equipment paths

Do not block emergency exits, fire lanes, ramps, electrical panels, extinguishers, first aid stations, forklift lanes, stairways, or marked access paths

Ask if a label or location does not make sense

Check back in when the task is complete

#### ***Example Situations***

**Situation 1:**  
 A lead says, “Take these three lighting cases to stage left.”

Best action:  
 Move those three cases to stage left, leave them closed, and check back in.

**Situation 2:**  
 You see two cases marked “Audio” and one marked “Video” sitting together.

Best action:  
 Do not assume they all go to the same place. Ask where each should be staged.

**Situation 3:**  
 You do not know where “FOH” is.

Best action:  
 Ask, “Where is front of house?” before moving the case.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Do not stage cases in front of emergency exits, fire lanes, dock doors, ramps, electrical panels, extinguishers, first aid stations, forklift lanes, stairways, or marked access paths. A case may be in the right department area but still be staged in an unsafe location.

Department sorting keeps the load-in organized. The right case in the right place helps every department work faster and safer.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Sorting cases is one of the most helpful beginner tasks during load-in. A good stagehand reads labels, listens to directions, moves only assigned gear, avoids blocking critical paths, and asks when unsure.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Would I rather ask where a case goes, or guess and possibly make the whole crew move it again?

Question: What should you do if you do not know where a case belongs?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask your lead or the appropriate department before moving it. Do not guess.

# **Module 5, Lesson 8: Cable Awareness: Recognizing Cable Hazards and Knowing When Not to Touch**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to basic cable awareness during load-in. The goal is not to train beginners to run, connect, disconnect, repair, or troubleshoot cable. The goal is to help them recognize that cables are important, department-specific, and can create hazards if moved incorrectly.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why cable awareness matters and identify basic safety concerns around cable paths during load-in.

Cables are used by many departments, including audio, lighting, video, power, data, backline, and production.

Some cables may be heavy, fragile, expensive, energized, connected, staged for a reason, or part of a specific department plan.

Beginners should not plug in, unplug, pull, coil, tape, move, repair, or reroute cables unless directed by the appropriate lead or department.

Cable paths can create trip hazards and must be kept organized, protected, and clear when possible.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Cables are everywhere during load-in.

Audio needs cable. Lighting needs cable. Video needs cable. Backline may need cable. Production may need cable. Power distribution may involve large feeder cables. Data lines may support control systems, video systems, networks, or other equipment.

To a beginner, some cables may look the same. But they are not all the same.

A feeder cable is not the same as an audio line. Socapex is not the same as a microphone cable. Data cable is not the same as power cable. Some cables are heavy. Some are delicate. Some may be connected to important systems. Some may be staged in a certain place because a department is about to use them.

That is why beginners should not randomly move cable.

Never unplug something because it looks unused. Never pull a cable without knowing what is on the other end. Never coil cable without being told how that department wants it handled. Different departments may have different methods for handling cable.

Cable awareness means noticing cable paths, avoiding trip hazards, protecting cables from damage, and asking before touching anything you are not assigned to handle.

If you are helping with cable under direction, listen carefully. Ask where the cable starts, where it ends, how it should be routed, and whether there are any areas to avoid.

#### ***Cable Terms Learners May Hear on a Jobsite***

You are not expected to identify, connect, disconnect, troubleshoot, or handle these cables on your own. This list is only to help you understand words you may hear during load-in.

Feeder cable: Large power cable, often heavy and specialized

Socapex: Multi-circuit cable often used in lighting systems

Audio lines: Cables for microphones, speakers, consoles, and audio systems

Data cable: Cable used for control, network, lighting, video, or communication systems

Video cable: Cable used for screens, cameras, projectors, or video systems

Extension cable: General power cable, but still should be handled under direction

Cable trunks: Cases or trunks used to store and move cable

#### ***Cable Awareness Habits***

Do not plug in or unplug cable unless directed

Do not pull cable without knowing what it is connected to

Do not coil department cable unless asked

Do not repair, tape, or modify damaged cable

Watch for trip hazards

Step over cable carefully when necessary

Do not drive carts or cases over exposed cable unless directed and protected

Keep cable paths clear when assigned

Report damaged cable to a lead

Ask before moving cable that is not assigned to you

#### ***Warning Signs to Report***

Damaged cable jacket

Exposed conductors

Heat

Smoke

Burning smell

Sparking

Wet cable or cable near standing water

Cable stretched tightly across a path

Cable creating a trip hazard

Cable being crushed by cases, carts, doors, or lifts

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Pause before acting, look for hazards, and ask the correct lead when anything is unclear or outside your training.

Power-related cable can be dangerous and should only be handled, connected, disconnected, repaired, taped, or managed by trained and authorized personnel. If you are unsure what a cable is, do not touch it. Ask.

Cable awareness is about respect and caution. Cables may look simple, but they can affect safety, power, sound, lights, video, and the entire show.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Cables are critical to the show and can create hazards when handled incorrectly. A new stagehand should stay aware of cable paths, avoid damaging or moving cable without direction, and ask before touching anything uncertain.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I see a cable stretched across the floor, do I know whether I should move it, protect it, report it, or leave it alone?

Question: What should a new stagehand do before pulling or coiling a cable?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask the appropriate lead or department for direction and make sure the cable is safe and assigned to be moved.

# **Module 5, Lesson 9: When to Move Fast and When to Slow Down**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners how to understand pace during load-in. The goal is to help new stagehands recognize that working efficiently does not mean rushing blindly, and that some moments require slowing down for safety and accuracy.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain the difference between working efficiently and rushing unsafely during load-in.

Load-in often has time pressure, but speed should never replace safety.

There are moments when the crew needs to move efficiently, such as clearing trucks, moving assigned cases, or completing simple tasks.

There are also moments when the crew must slow down, such as around ramps, dock plates, forklifts, overhead work, heavy lifts, unclear instructions, cable paths, tight spaces, doors, thresholds, elevators, blind corners, or unfamiliar tasks.

A good stagehand learns to match the pace of the crew while still staying controlled and safe.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Load-in is often on a schedule.

The trucks need to unload. The stage needs to be built. Departments need their gear. The room needs to be ready before doors open or before the event begins.

Because of that, you may feel pressure to move fast.

But moving fast is not the same as working well.

A good stagehand works with urgency, but not recklessness. Urgency means you stay focused, do not waste time, pay attention, and complete tasks efficiently. Recklessness means rushing, guessing, running, ignoring hazards, or moving gear without control.

There are times when you should move with purpose. For example, if a lead tells you to take a group of cases to a specific area and the path is clear, you should stay focused and get it done.

There are also times when you must slow down. Slow down near ramps. Slow down around forklifts. Slow down when visibility is blocked. Slow down when moving through doorways. Slow down when carrying heavy or awkward items. Slow down when instructions are unclear.

Slow down if you are fatigued, overheated, dizzy, dehydrated, losing focus, or unsure of your footing. Report concerns early. Trying to push through unsafe fatigue can lead to mistakes and injuries.

If something feels unsafe, stop and ask.

A strong stagehand is not the person who moves the fastest at all times. A strong stagehand is the person who knows how to move efficiently while staying safe and accurate.

#### ***Move With Purpose When***

The task is clear

The path is safe

The case or item is under control

You know where the gear is going

You are moving with the crew

You are not creating a hazard

You can complete the task safely

#### ***Slow Down When***

You are near ramps or dock plates

You are near forklifts or moving equipment

You are around rigging or overhead work

You are moving heavy, tall, or unstable cases

You are carrying awkward items

You are near doorways, corners, elevators, thresholds, or tight spaces

You do not understand the instruction

You are unsure whether something is safe

You are tired, dizzy, overheated, dehydrated, or losing focus

The path is crowded or poorly lit

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Never run with road cases, cable, tools, staging pieces, or production equipment. Running increases the chance of trips, collisions, dropped gear, and injuries.

Efficiency means controlled, focused work. Rushing means unsafe, careless work. Good stagehands learn the difference.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Load-in requires urgency, but not recklessness. Move with purpose when the task is clear and safe. Slow down when the risk increases, when you are tired, or when you are unsure.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Can I move with urgency while still staying aware of people, gear, and hazards around me?

Question: What should never be sacrificed just to move faster during load-in?

**Best Answer:**  
 Safety, control, communication, and accuracy should never be sacrificed just to move faster.

# **Module 5, Lesson 10: How to Take Direction Without Needing Everything Explained Twice**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners how to receive and act on instructions during load-in. The goal is to help new stagehands become easier to direct by listening carefully, confirming instructions, completing tasks correctly, and checking back in.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe how to receive instructions clearly and follow through without creating confusion or needing repeated explanations.

Load-in is busy, and leads may not have time to explain every instruction multiple times.

New stagehands can become more useful by listening fully, repeating back key details, asking focused questions, completing the task as assigned, and checking back in when done.

Taking direction well does not mean pretending to understand. It means paying attention and asking for clarification before acting if something is unclear.

Taking direction also does not mean ignoring safety. If an instruction seems unsafe, unclear, or outside your training, stop and ask for clarification.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

One of the fastest ways to become valuable during load-in is to take direction well.

Leads are often managing many things at once. They may be tracking trucks, departments, schedules, safety concerns, gear locations, and crew assignments. When they give you a task, they need to trust that you are listening.

Taking direction well starts before you move.

Stop what you are doing, listen to the full instruction, and make sure you understand the task. Pay attention to what item, where it goes, how it should be handled, and what should happen when you are done.

For example, if a lead says, “Take these four audio cases to stage right and leave them closed,” the important details are: these four cases, audio, stage right, leave them closed.

Do not take five cases. Do not take them to stage left. Do not open them. Do not leave without checking back in if that is expected.

If you only understood part of the instruction, ask. That is not a weakness. It is better to ask one clear question than to guess and create a problem.

A useful phrase is, “Just to confirm, you want these four cases to stage right, closed?”

Once the task is done, check back in. Do not disappear, wander, or start freelancing. A simple “That’s done. What’s next?” makes you easier to manage and more useful to the crew.

#### ***How to Take Direction Well***

Stop and listen.

Let the lead finish speaking.

Identify the task.

Identify the gear or item.

Identify the destination.

Identify any special instruction.

Repeat back the critical details when needed.

Ask if something is unclear.

Complete the task as assigned.

Check back in when finished.

#### ***Useful Phrases***

“Copy.”

“Where do you want this staged?”

“Do you want these opened or left closed?”

“Just to confirm, these go to stage right?”

“Copy — these four audio cases to stage right, leave them closed.”

“I have not been trained on that yet.”

“Can you show me the safe way to do that?”

“That task is done. What’s next?”

“I’m not sure what that term means. Can you point me in the right direction?”

“I want to make sure I do this safely. Is this the path you want me to use?”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Mistakes to Avoid***

Walking away before the instruction is finished

Pretending to understand

Moving the wrong case

Taking gear to the wrong location

Opening cases without being told

Starting random tasks after finishing

Disappearing from your assigned area

Arguing instead of listening

Asking the same question repeatedly because you were not paying attention

Ignoring a hazard because you are trying to move quickly

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

If an instruction seems unsafe, unclear, or outside your training, stop and ask for clarification. Taking direction does not mean ignoring safety.

If you believe an instruction creates an immediate hazard, do not argue or ignore the lead. Stop, communicate clearly, and ask for clarification.

A good stagehand does not need to know everything, but they do need to listen carefully, confirm when needed, follow the instruction, and check back in.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Taking direction well makes you easier to trust. Listen carefully, confirm key details, do the task exactly as assigned, and check back in when finished.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

When someone gives me a task, do I listen for the item, location, special instruction, and what to do when finished?

Question: What should you do if you only understood part of a lead’s instruction?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask for clarification before starting the task. It is better to ask once than to guess and create confusion or a safety issue.

# Practical Activity

# **Module 5 Practical Activity: Load-In Flow Exercise**

## **Activity Purpose**

This activity helps learners understand the general order of a load-in. The goal is not to teach that every load-in happens exactly the same way, but to help beginners recognize the common flow of tasks.

## **Instructions**

Put the following load-in tasks in the most logical order.

Truck unload

Final cleanup

Safety briefing

Cable runs

Stage build

Check in

Department setup

Sort cases

## **Suggested Correct Order**

Check in

Safety briefing

Truck unload

Sort cases

Stage build

Department setup

Cable runs

Final cleanup

## **Discussion Note**

Every event is different. Some tasks may overlap, happen in a different order, or repeat throughout the day. The sequence may change because of truck timing, rigging, venue rules, weather, department needs, or safety concerns.

Always follow the actual site plan and lead direction.

## **Safety Discussion Prompt**

**Ask learners:**

What could go wrong if a new stagehand starts moving cases, cable, or department gear before checking in and receiving direction?

## **Best Discussion Points**

Gear may be moved to the wrong location

A department may lose track of needed equipment

Emergency exits or access paths may get blocked

Cable or gear may be damaged

The worker may enter a restricted or unsafe area

The worker may interfere with forklifts, rigging, or department setup

The crew may have to redo work

# Module 5 Quiz

# **Module 5 Quiz: Load-In Fundamentals**

## **Quiz Instructions**

Choose the best answer for each question. This quiz checks your understanding of load-in flow, truck unloads, road cases, ramps, department sorting, cable awareness, pacing, authorization, and taking direction.

**Recommended passing score:** 80%  
 Total questions: 25

## **Multiple Choice**

### **1. What is load-in?**

A. The process of removing equipment after the event  
 B. The process of moving production equipment into a venue and preparing the show  
 C. The audience entering the venue  
 D. The break between shifts

**Correct Answer:** B

### **2. What should a new stagehand do first when arriving for load-in?**

A. Start moving cases immediately  
 B. Open the nearest road case  
 C. Check in and find out who they report to  
 D. Walk around the venue alone

**Correct Answer:** C

### **3. Why should truck unloads be directed by a lead or assigned supervisor?**

A. Because trucks are usually packed in a specific order  
 B. Because beginners are never allowed near trucks  
 C. Because road cases are always empty  
 D. Because unloading does not require communication

**Correct Answer:** A

### **4. What is a common hazard around trucks and docks?**

A. Quiet rooms  
 B. Rolling cases, dock edges, ramps, and moving equipment  
 C. Too much seating  
 D. Ticket scanners

**Correct Answer:** B

### **5. What should you do before moving a road case?**

A. Check the path, handles, brakes, and direction from the lead  
 B. Open it to see what is inside  
 C. Push it as fast as possible  
 D. Stack it on another case

**Correct Answer:** A

### **6. Why can tall or narrow road cases be dangerous?**

A. They are always empty  
 B. They can be unstable or tip if moved incorrectly  
 C. They cannot roll  
 D. They are never used during load-in

**Correct Answer:** B

### **7. Why is pushing often safer than pulling a road case?**

A. It always requires less effort  
 B. It gives better control, body position, and visibility  
 C. It allows the case to move faster  
 D. It lets you walk backward through the venue

**Correct Answer:** B

### **8. When should you slow down while moving gear?**

A. Around ramps, dock plates, corners, forklifts, and tight spaces  
 B. Only when the show is over  
 C. Never  
 D. Only when no one is watching

**Correct Answer:** A

### **9. What is a pinch point?**

A. A place where workers take breaks  
 B. A place where a body part can get caught between moving equipment and another object  
 C. A type of road case label  
 D. A tool used by lighting crew

**Correct Answer:** B

### **10. What should you never use your body as?**

A. A communication tool  
 B. A brake for a heavy rolling case  
 C. A way to ask questions  
 D. A way to stay alert

**Correct Answer:** B

### **11. Why should beginners avoid touching department gear without direction?**

A. It may be fragile, dangerous, expensive, energized, or staged for a specific reason  
 B. It is never used during load-in  
 C. It is always safe to move  
 D. It belongs to the audience

**Correct Answer:** A

### **12. Which department may handle speakers, consoles, microphones, and audio cable?**

A. Audio  
 B. Wardrobe  
 C. Catering  
 D. Ticketing

**Correct Answer:** A

### **13. Which department may handle screens, projectors, cameras, or LED panels?**

A. Video  
 B. Security  
 C. Parking  
 D. Catering

**Correct Answer:** A

### **14. What should a beginner do around rigging and overhead work?**

A. Stay clear unless trained, authorized, and directed  
 B. Stand under suspended loads to watch  
 C. Move motors without asking  
 D. Touch rigging gear to help speed things up

**Correct Answer:** A

### **15. Why is department sorting important?**

A. It keeps gear organized and helps each department work efficiently  
 B. It makes the load-in slower on purpose  
 C. It allows beginners to open every case  
 D. It prevents leads from giving direction

**Correct Answer:** A

### **16. What should you do if you do not know where a case belongs?**

A. Guess based on where other cases are  
 B. Ask your lead or the appropriate department  
 C. Leave it in an exit path  
 D. Open it and decide for yourself

**Correct Answer:** B

### **17. What should a new stagehand do before pulling or coiling a cable?**

A. Make sure no one is looking  
 B. Ask the appropriate lead or department for direction  
 C. Cut the tape off quickly  
 D. Pull hard to see where it goes

**Correct Answer:** B

### **18. Why should you never unplug a cable just because it looks unused?**

A. It may still be connected to an active or important system  
 B. All cables are decorative  
 C. Cables do not matter during load-in  
 D. Only beginners can unplug cables

**Correct Answer:** A

### **19. What is the difference between urgency and recklessness?**

A. Urgency is focused and controlled; recklessness is unsafe and careless  
 B. They mean the same thing  
 C. Recklessness is always better  
 D. Urgency means ignoring instructions

**Correct Answer:** A

### **20. What should you do after completing a task during load-in?**

A. Wander around the venue  
 B. Start opening random cases  
 C. Check back in with your lead  
 D. Leave the site

**Correct Answer:** C

### **21. What should you do if a forklift is moving near your path?**

A. Hurry across before it gets closer  
 B. Assume the operator sees you  
 C. Stop, stay clear, make sure it is safe to proceed, and follow site traffic rules  
 D. Walk behind it quickly

**Correct Answer:** C

### **22. Who should operate powered equipment such as forklifts, powered pallet jacks, or lifts?**

A. Any stagehand who feels comfortable  
 B. Only trained and authorized personnel  
 C. Whoever is closest  
 D. New workers during load-in

**Correct Answer:** B

### **23. What should you do if a dock plate looks unstable or unsafe?**

A. Move faster across it  
 B. Adjust it yourself without telling anyone  
 C. Stop and report it to a lead or authorized person  
 D. Push the case harder

**Correct Answer:** C

### **24. What should you do if you see damaged cable?**

A. Tape it yourself and keep working  
 B. Ignore it if the show has not started  
 C. Report it to a lead or appropriate department  
 D. Unplug it immediately

**Correct Answer:** C

### **25. What does this course authorize you to do?**

A. Operate forklifts  
 B. Connect power  
 C. Perform rigging work  
 D. Recognize basic load-in flow and work safely under direction

**Correct Answer:** D

# **Scenario Questions**

These can be used as written-response questions or discussion prompts.

## **Scenario 1**

You are helping unload a truck. A case looks easy to move, but no one has told you where it goes.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not guess. Ask the truck lead, crew lead, or appropriate department where the case should go before moving it.

## **Scenario 2**

You are pushing a road case and notice the path ahead goes down a ramp.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Slow down, communicate with anyone helping, stay out of the downhill path, and ask for help if the case feels heavy or hard to control.

## **Scenario 3**

A lead tells you, “Take these four lighting cases to stage left and leave them closed.” You are not sure where stage left is.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask for clarification before moving the cases. It is better to confirm the location than to take the gear to the wrong place.

## **Scenario 4**

You see a cable stretched across the floor and think it might be a trip hazard.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not move or unplug it without direction. Report it to a lead or the appropriate department and ask what should be done.

## **Scenario 5**

You finish moving cases to the assigned area and do not know what to do next.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Check back in with your lead and ask for the next task. Do not wander away or start random work.

## **Scenario 6**

You see a forklift moving near the dock, and you need to cross the same area.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stop, stay clear, follow site traffic rules, and make sure it is safe before crossing. Never assume the operator sees you.

## **Scenario 7**

A dock plate looks uneven, wet, or unstable.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not move gear across it and do not try to adjust it yourself unless authorized. Stop and report it to a lead or authorized person.

## **Scenario 8**

A lead asks you to help with a task you have not been trained or authorized to do.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Communicate clearly and ask for direction. You can say, “I have not been trained on that yet. Can someone show me the safe way, or would you like me to help with something else?”

## **Scenario 9**

You notice a case staged in front of an emergency exit.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Report it to your lead and ask where it should be moved. Do not ignore blocked exits or access paths.

## **Scenario 10**

You are tired, overheated, and starting to lose focus during a long load-in.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Slow down, communicate with your lead, hydrate if allowed, and report that you are not feeling fully focused. Pushing through unsafe fatigue can lead to mistakes or injury.
