# Module 6

# **Module 6: Stagehand Communication and Crew Etiquette**

## **Module Purpose**

This module teaches new stagehands the communication habits and crew etiquette that help them succeed on real calls. A beginner may not know every tool, department, or technical term yet, but they can still be valuable by listening well, asking good questions, following the chain of command, respecting site rules, and behaving professionally.

Many new stagehands struggle not because they cannot do the physical work, but because they do not understand the unspoken rules of the jobsite. This module helps learners understand how to talk to leads, how to respond to direction, when to ask questions, how to use phones appropriately, and how to avoid freelancing or getting in the way.

This module is awareness-level training. It does not replace employer-provided training, site-specific safety procedures, union training, equipment certification, or OSHA-required training.

## **Learning Objective**

By the end of this module, learners should be able to communicate clearly with leads and crew members, ask questions professionally, respond to direction using common call language, and demonstrate the crew etiquette expected of an entry-level stagehand.

## **Module Lessons**

How to Talk to Leads and Department Heads

The Right Way to Ask Questions

Radio Basics and Common Callouts

Hand Signals and Nonverbal Communication

“Copy,” “Heard,” “Standing By,” and Other Useful Responses

Phone Etiquette on a Call

How to Stay Busy Without Freelancing

How to Handle Correction Without Taking It Personally

Respecting Touring Crew, Local Crew, and Venue Staff

What Gets You Asked Back

# Lesson 6.1

# **Module 6, Lesson 1: How to Talk to Leads and Department Heads**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands how to communicate with leads and department heads respectfully and effectively. The goal is to help learners understand that good communication starts with knowing who gives direction, listening carefully, and keeping responses clear and professional.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain how to speak with leads and department heads in a way that supports safety, organization, and crew efficiency.

Leads and department heads are responsible for assigning tasks, organizing the crew, and keeping the work moving safely.

A new stagehand should identify their lead early in the call and check in with that person when they need direction.

Communication should be clear, respectful, and brief. Long explanations are usually not needed on a busy jobsite.

A stagehand should avoid arguing, interrupting, talking over instructions, or taking direction from random people without confirming the chain of command.

Good communication helps prevent mistakes, delays, duplicated work, misplaced gear, and safety issues.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

When you arrive on a call, one of the first things you need to know is who you report to.

Your lead or department head is your main point of contact. This is the person who assigns work, answers questions, and tells you where to go next. On some calls, you may have one general crew lead. On other calls, you may be assigned to a specific department, such as audio, lighting, video, staging, carpentry, truck loading, or production support.

The way you talk to leads matters.

A good stagehand does not need to sound overly formal, but they do need to be respectful, clear, and ready to listen. When a lead gives you instructions, stop what you are doing if it is safe to do so, look in their direction, listen carefully, and confirm what you heard.

For example, if your lead says, “Take these four cases to stage right and leave them closed,” a good response would be:

“Copy. Four cases to stage right, leave them closed.”

That short response tells the lead that you heard the instruction and understood the important details.

Avoid arguing, guessing, interrupting, or trying to explain why you would do it differently. If something is unclear or seems unsafe, ask a direct question before acting.

Your job as a new stagehand is not to control the call. Your job is to support the call by following the correct direction, communicating clearly, and checking in when you need help.

#### ***Good Ways to Talk to Leads***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Situation | Professional Response |
| You do not know who to report to | “Who is my lead for this call?” |
| You finished a task | “That task is done. What would you like me on next?” |
| You did not understand the instruction | “Can you clarify where you want this staged?” |
| You are unsure if something is safe | “I’m not sure if this is safe to move. Should I leave it or get someone from the department?” |
| You are asked to do something outside your training | “I have not been trained or authorized to do that. Can someone show me the safe way, or would you like me on another task?” |

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Clear communication is part of safety. If you do not understand an instruction, do not guess. Ask before acting, especially around moving equipment, cables, trucks, ramps, lifts, rigging, electrical gear, restricted areas, or any task outside your training.

A good stagehand communicates with leads clearly, briefly, and respectfully. They listen first, confirm direction, ask questions when needed, and avoid freelancing.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Knowing how to talk to leads is one of the most important beginner skills. Clear, respectful communication helps the crew stay organized, prevents mistakes, and shows that you are reliable and easy to work with.

Review prompt:

If I finished a task and did not know what to do next, would I check in with my lead or start guessing?

Question: Who should a new stagehand usually go to for direction?

**Best Answer:**  
 Their assigned lead, department head, or designated point of contact.

# **Module 6, Lesson 2: The Right Way to Ask Questions**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands how to ask questions without slowing down the crew or pretending they understand something they do not. The goal is to help learners see that asking the right question at the right time is a sign of professionalism, not weakness.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to ask clear, useful questions when instructions are unclear, unsafe, or outside their training.

New stagehands are not expected to know everything.

Asking questions is better than guessing.

Questions should be direct, specific, and related to the task.

The best time to ask is before starting a task that is unclear, unfamiliar, or potentially unsafe.

A stagehand should avoid asking questions just to avoid work, challenge authority, or overcomplicate simple instructions.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

One of the biggest mistakes a new stagehand can make is pretending they understand something when they do not.

On a live event call, guessing can create real problems. You might move the wrong case, coil the wrong cable, block a pathway, interrupt another department, enter a restricted area, or create a safety issue.

That is why good stagehands ask questions.

But there is a right way to ask.

A good question is short, clear, and connected to the task. Instead of saying, “What are we doing?” you might ask, “Do you want these cases staged by audio world or taken straight to stage left?”

Instead of saying, “I don’t get it,” you might say, “Can you show me which side you want this pushed from?”

The goal is not to ask endless questions. The goal is to get enough clarity to do the task correctly and safely.

There are three times when you should always ask before acting:

First, ask if the instruction is unclear.

Second, ask if the task seems unsafe.

Third, ask if the task involves something you have not been trained or authorized to do.

Leads usually prefer a beginner who asks one good question over a beginner who guesses and creates a problem.

#### ***Examples of Better Questions***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Weak Question | Better Question |
| “What do I do?” | “Where would you like these cases staged?” |
| “Is this right?” | “Do you want the label facing out?” |
| “Can I move this?” | “Is this cable cleared to move, or should I wait for audio?” |
| “Where does this go?” | “Does this case go to stage left or back to the truck?” |
| “How do I do that?” | “Can someone show me the safe way to do that before I try it?” |

#### ***When to Ask Immediately***

Ask before acting if:

You do not understand the instruction.

You are unsure who gave the instruction.

You do not know where the gear belongs.

You are asked to operate equipment you have not been trained or authorized to operate.

A task involves rigging, lifts, electrical equipment, powered equipment, overhead work, or restricted areas.

Something appears unsafe.

A cable, case, barricade, truss piece, deck, or piece of gear may still be in use.

You are not sure whether the task belongs to your department.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Never let embarrassment keep you from asking a safety-related question. A short question can prevent an injury, damaged equipment, or a serious delay.

A professional stagehand does not pretend to know everything. They ask clear questions when they need direction, especially when safety or training is involved.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Asking good questions is part of being safe and reliable. The best questions are clear, specific, and asked before a mistake is made.

Review prompt:

Would I rather ask one clear question or guess and risk creating a bigger problem?

Question: What should you do if you are given an instruction but do not fully understand it?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask for clarification before starting the task.

# **Module 6, Lesson 3: Radio Basics and Common Callouts**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces beginners to basic radio communication and common callouts used on live event jobsites. The goal is not to train learners for advanced radio operation, but to help them understand simple, professional radio behavior if they are assigned or allowed to use one.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe basic radio etiquette and identify common callouts used to communicate on a busy jobsite.

Not every beginner will be given a radio.

If assigned a radio, use it only for work-related communication.

Radio communication should be short, clear, and professional.

Do not talk over others.

Listen before transmitting.

Common responses include “copy,” “stand by,” and “say again.”

Radio terms can vary by employer, venue, production, or department.

Sensitive, private, or unnecessary conversations do not belong on the radio.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

On some calls, you may see leads, department heads, security, production staff, or crew members using radios.

A radio is not a toy and it is not for casual conversation. It is a work tool used to communicate quickly across a busy venue or jobsite.

As a new stagehand, you may not be given a radio right away. That is normal. Radios are often assigned to people who need to coordinate specific tasks, departments, or areas.

If you are given a radio, the first rule is to listen before you speak. Make sure no one else is talking. Then press the button, pause for a brief moment, speak clearly, and release the button when you are finished.

Keep your message short. For example: “Copy. On my way to stage left.” That is better than a long explanation.

If you did not hear something, say, “Say again.” If you heard and understood, say, “Copy.” If you need a moment before responding or acting, say, “Stand by.”

Do not joke around on the radio. Do not use profanity. Do not share private information. Do not clog the channel with unnecessary chatter.

Good radio use helps the crew work safely and efficiently. Bad radio use creates confusion.

#### ***Common Radio Responses***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Phrase | Common Meaning |
| “Copy” | I heard and understood. |
| “Heard” | I heard you. |
| “Say again” | Please repeat that. |
| “Stand by” | Wait a moment or I need a moment before responding. |
| “On my way” | I am heading there now. |
| “Clear” | The area, task, or communication is complete, depending on context. |
| “Go for \\\[name\\\]” | I am answering your call. |
| “Radio check” | Checking whether the radio is working. |

#### ***Basic Radio Etiquette***

Listen before transmitting.

Keep messages short.

Speak clearly.

Use names or departments when needed.

Do not interrupt emergency or safety communication.

Do not use the radio for jokes or side conversations.

Avoid giving instructions unless you are authorized to do so.

Keep sensitive information off the radio.

Follow the radio procedures used by your employer, venue, or production.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Emergency or safety-related radio traffic takes priority. If you hear urgent communication, stay off the channel unless you are directly involved or asked to respond.

Radio communication should be clear, short, and professional. A radio is a work tool, not a place for casual conversation.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Radio basics are about listening first, speaking clearly, and keeping communication professional. Beginners should only use radios when assigned or authorized.

Review prompt:

If I were given a radio, would I know how to keep my communication short and useful?

Question: What does “copy” usually mean?

**Best Answer:**  
 It means “I heard and understood.”

# **Module 6, Lesson 4: Hand Signals and Nonverbal Communication**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands why nonverbal communication matters on loud, busy jobsites. The goal is to help learners understand that hand signals, eye contact, gestures, and body positioning can help crews communicate when verbal communication is difficult.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why nonverbal communication is important and identify basic ways stagehands communicate without relying only on speech.

Live event jobsites can be loud.

Verbal communication may be difficult near trucks, forklifts, carts, concerts, power tools, crowds, or large crews.

Hand signals and nonverbal cues should be simple, visible, and understood by the people involved.

A new stagehand should not invent unclear signals during critical tasks.

For equipment movement, one person should usually guide the communication so the crew does not receive mixed signals.

If a signal is unclear, stop if safe to do so and ask for clarification.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Not every jobsite is quiet enough for normal conversation.

During load-in or load-out, you may be around trucks, forklifts, carts, road cases, motors, music, tools, or large crews. In those environments, shouting is not always effective.

That is where nonverbal communication matters.

A lead may point to where a case should go. A crew member may raise a hand to signal stop. Someone guiding a case may use a slow-down gesture. A person near a doorway may wave you through or hold up a hand to wait.

These signals are useful only when they are clear and understood.

As a beginner, do not assume everyone understands a signal you made up. During critical movement, especially with heavy cases, ramps, lifts, trucks, or tight spaces, follow the person assigned to give direction.

Nonverbal communication also includes your body position. Standing in the wrong place can create confusion or danger. For example, if a crew is pushing a case through a doorway, do not stand in the travel path. If someone is guiding a case, look at them and follow their direction.

Good stagehands pay attention not only to what is being said, but also to what is happening around them.

#### ***Common Nonverbal Signals***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Signal | Common Meaning |
| Raised open hand | Stop or hold position |
| Pointing | Direction or location |
| Slow downward motion | Slow down |
| Waving forward | Come ahead or continue |
| Thumbs up | Ready, good, or confirmed, depending on context |
| Eye contact with a lead | Pay attention or prepare for direction |
| Clear stop gesture | Stop immediately, depending on site practice |

#### ***Important Reminder***

Hand signals can vary by crew, venue, department, employer, or production. Do not rely on a signal unless you understand what it means in that specific situation.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

When moving heavy equipment, clear communication matters. If you cannot hear or understand direction, stop moving if safe to do so and ask for clarification. Never continue pushing or pulling equipment when the direction is unclear.

Nonverbal communication helps crews work in loud or busy environments, but it must be clear, understood, and connected to safe movement.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Good stagehands watch as well as listen. Hand signals, gestures, and body language can help the crew communicate, but unclear signals should never replace direct clarification when safety is involved.

Review prompt:

If I could not hear a lead during a loud load-out, would I know to stop and clarify instead of guessing?

Question: Why is nonverbal communication important on live event jobsites?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because jobsites can be loud and busy, and crews may need to communicate direction, stopping, slowing down, or positioning without relying only on speech.

# **Module 6, Lesson 5: “Copy,” “Heard,” “Standing By,” and Other Useful Responses**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands common short responses used in live event communication. The goal is to help learners respond to direction in a way that shows they are listening, understand the task, and are ready to act.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to use common jobsite responses such as “copy,” “heard,” and “standing by” appropriately.

Short responses help keep communication clear.

“Copy” usually means the instruction was heard and understood.

“Heard” means the message was received.

“Standing by” means waiting and ready.

“Say again” means the message needs to be repeated.

A stagehand should not say “copy” if they do not understand the instruction.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Live event crews often use short phrases to keep communication moving.

You may hear words like “copy,” “heard,” “stand by,” or “say again.” These responses are useful because they quickly tell the other person whether you heard them, understood them, or need more information.

For example, if your lead says, “Bring those two cases to stage right,” and you understand the instruction, you can respond with:

“Copy. Two cases to stage right.”

That response confirms both the instruction and the important detail.

But do not say “copy” if you do not actually understand. If you missed part of the instruction, say, “Say again?” or “Can you clarify which two cases?”

It is better to ask than to pretend.

“Standing by” means you are waiting and ready. For example, if a lead says, “Hold there for a second,” you might respond, “Standing by.”

Short professional responses help the crew know where you are, what you understood, and whether the task is moving forward.

#### ***Useful Responses***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Response | When to Use It |
| “Copy.” | You heard and understood. |
| “Heard.” | You heard the message. |
| “Standing by.” | You are waiting and ready. |
| “Say again?” | You need the instruction repeated. |
| “Can you clarify?” | You need more detail before acting. |
| “On my way.” | You are heading to the location. |
| “Task complete.” | You finished the assigned task. |
| “Checking in.” | You are returning to your lead for the next task. |
| “I have not been trained or authorized to do that.” | You need proper instruction, authorization, or reassignment. |

#### ***Examples***

Lead: “Take these barricade pieces to the dock.”  
 Good response: “Copy. Barricade to the dock.”

Lead: “Hold there until audio clears that cable.”  
 Good response: “Standing by.”

Lead: “Move those cases next to the black traveler.”  
 Good response if unsure: “Can you clarify which traveler you mean?”

Lead: “Hop on that lift and bring it over here.”  
 Good response if not trained: “I have not been trained or authorized to operate that lift.”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Never confirm an instruction you do not understand. Saying “copy” when you are confused can lead to unsafe work, misplaced gear, or unnecessary delays.

Short responses help the crew communicate quickly, but honesty matters. Only confirm that you understand when you actually understand.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Useful jobsite responses are short, clear, and honest. A good stagehand confirms direction when they understand and asks for clarification when they do not.

Review prompt:

Have I ever said “yes” or “got it” when I really did not understand something?

Question: What should you say if you did not hear or understand an instruction?

**Best Answer:**  
 “Say again?” or “Can you clarify?”

# **Module 6, Lesson 6: Phone Etiquette on a Call**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands how phone use can affect professionalism, safety, and trust on a call. The goal is to help learners understand when phone use is inappropriate, when it may be acceptable, and why phones should never distract from the job.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe professional phone etiquette during a stagehand call.

Phones can create distractions and safety risks.

Phone use during active work can make a stagehand look unreliable or unavailable.

A phone may be useful for call details, maps, emergency contact, scheduling, or work-related communication, but it should not interfere with the job.

Taking photos or videos may be prohibited by the venue, artist, client, employer, or production.

Never post backstage, production, client, artist, credential, schedule, or private event content without permission.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Phone use is one of the fastest ways for a new stagehand to make a bad impression.

On a live event jobsite, there is usually equipment moving, people giving direction, and hazards that require your attention. If you are looking down at your phone, you may miss an instruction, step into a travel path, block someone, or fail to notice a hazard.

That does not mean your phone has no place on a call. You may need it for directions, call details, emergency contact, or work-related communication. But once you are checked in and working, your attention should be on the job.

Do not scroll social media during active work. Do not wear earbuds unless they are specifically allowed and safe for the task. Do not take personal calls in the middle of a task unless it is an emergency and you have stepped away appropriately.

Photos and videos are another serious issue. Many productions do not allow backstage photos, artist photos, client content, set designs, rehearsals, credentials, schedules, or production details to be posted. Even if something looks interesting, it may be private, confidential, or restricted.

If you want to take a photo, ask first. If the answer is no, respect it.

Professional phone etiquette is simple: be present, stay aware, and do not let your phone become a distraction or a trust issue.

#### ***Appropriate Phone Use***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Appropriate | Not Appropriate |
| Checking call address before arrival | Scrolling during active work |
| Contacting dispatch or a supervisor if needed | Texting while pushing gear |
| Emergency communication | Taking unauthorized backstage photos |
| Checking schedule during break | Posting client, artist, venue, or production details |
| Using phone light only if safe and appropriate | Wearing earbuds when you need to hear directions |

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Do not use your phone while moving equipment, walking through active work zones, crossing truck areas, standing near forklifts, handling cable, working near stage edges, working near rigging activity, or operating any assigned equipment.

Phones should support the job, not distract from it. A professional stagehand stays present, aware, and respectful of privacy.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Phone etiquette affects both safety and reputation. Keep your phone away during active work unless it is needed for a work-related or emergency reason.

Review prompt:

Would my lead see me as focused and available, or distracted and unavailable?

Question: Why can phone use be a problem during a stagehand call?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because it can distract the stagehand from directions, hazards, moving equipment, and professional responsibilities.

# **Module 6, Lesson 7: How to Stay Busy Without Freelancing**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands how to remain useful when they are between assigned tasks without making independent decisions that could create problems. The goal is to help learners understand the difference between being proactive and freelancing.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain how to stay useful without moving gear, changing setups, or starting tasks without direction.

A good stagehand does not disappear when a task is finished.

A good stagehand checks back in with their lead.

Being proactive is good, but freelancing is risky.

Freelancing means starting tasks, moving gear, opening cases, coiling cable, changing layouts, entering restricted areas, or helping another department without direction.

Safe ways to stay useful include checking in, keeping assigned areas clear, staging yourself where directed, and waiting ready.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

One of the most important beginner habits is learning what to do when your task is finished.

Some new stagehands finish a task and then disappear. Others start moving random gear because they want to look busy. Both can create problems.

The best move is simple: check back in.

Say, “That task is done. What would you like me on next?”

This tells your lead that the work is complete and that you are available.

There is a difference between being proactive and freelancing. Being proactive means staying alert, checking in, cleaning only what you have been told to clean, keeping yourself ready, and looking for ways to support the crew under direction.

Freelancing means making decisions without direction. That could include moving a road case, opening a case, coiling a cable, taking gear to another area, changing a setup, entering a restricted area, or joining another department without being assigned.

Freelancing may look helpful, but it can cause confusion. A case may have been staged exactly where it needed to be. A cable may still be connected. A department may have a specific order for packing gear. A pathway may need to stay clear for a reason you do not know.

If you want to help, ask first.

A valuable stagehand stays visible, ready, and available without creating extra problems.

#### ***Good Ways to Stay Busy***

Check back in with your lead.

Ask, “What would you like me on next?”

Stay near the assigned work area unless told otherwise.

Keep yourself ready for direction.

Help keep assigned pathways clear if directed.

Watch and learn without standing in the way.

Help teammates with assigned tasks when approved.

Stay hydrated during appropriate breaks.

Reset your gloves or PPE so you are ready for the next task.

#### ***Freelancing Examples to Avoid***

Moving road cases without direction.

Opening cases without permission.

Coiling cables without department approval.

Touching lighting, audio, video, rigging, electrical, backline, or automation gear without being asked.

Leaving your assigned area to help somewhere else without telling your lead.

Changing stage, chair, barricade, or gear layouts on your own.

Loading gear into a truck without direction from the truck lead.

Entering restricted areas without permission.

Directing other workers unless assigned to do so.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Freelancing can create safety problems. Gear, cables, cases, barricade, staging pieces, and work areas may be part of a larger plan. Do not move or alter them without direction.

Being useful does not mean doing random work. It means staying available, checking in, and following the crew’s plan.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Good stagehands stay busy by staying available, not by freelancing. Always check in before starting a new task or moving gear on your own.

Review prompt:

If I saw a case that looked out of place, would I move it or ask first?

Question: What should you do when you finish an assigned task?

**Best Answer:**  
 Check back in with your lead and ask what they want you on next.

# **Module 6, Lesson 8: How to Handle Correction Without Taking It Personally**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson helps new stagehands understand that correction is normal on live event crews. The goal is to teach learners how to receive feedback professionally, adjust quickly, and avoid becoming defensive.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to respond to correction in a calm, professional way and use feedback to improve their work.

Correction is part of the job.

A lead correcting you does not automatically mean you are failing.

Do not argue, blame, make excuses, or take correction personally.

Respond by listening, acknowledging, and adjusting.

If correction involves safety, take it seriously and change the behavior immediately.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

At some point, you will be corrected on a call.

A lead may tell you to move faster, slow down, stand somewhere else, stop touching something, change how you are pushing a case, or redo a task.

That is normal.

Live event work moves quickly, and leads are responsible for keeping the crew organized and safe. Sometimes correction will sound direct. It may not come with a long explanation. That does not mean it is personal.

The best way to handle correction is to listen, acknowledge it, and adjust.

For example, if a lead says, “Don’t stand there. That’s a travel path,” a good response is:

“Copy,” and then move.

Do not argue. Do not roll your eyes. Do not explain why you thought it was fine. Do not blame someone else. Just adjust.

If you do not understand the correction, ask a clear question after you have stopped the unsafe or incorrect behavior. For example:

“Copy. Where would you like me to stand instead?”

Correction can be one of the fastest ways to learn. A stagehand who receives correction well is easier to train and more likely to be called back.

#### ***Good Responses to Correction***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Situation | Good Response |
| You are standing in the wrong place | “Copy. Where should I move?” |
| You moved the wrong case | “Understood. I’ll move it back and check before moving the next one.” |
| You pushed a case too fast | “Copy. I’ll slow it down.” |
| You touched gear you were not assigned to | “Understood. I’ll check before touching department gear.” |
| You misunderstood an instruction | “Thanks for clarifying. I’ll correct it now.” |

#### ***Poor Responses to Correction***

Avoid:

Arguing.

Making excuses.

Blaming another crew member.

Ignoring the correction.

Acting offended.

Saying “I know” when you clearly missed something.

Repeating the same mistake after being corrected.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

If correction involves safety, respond immediately. Safety corrections are not personal. They are meant to prevent injury, damage, or a dangerous situation.

Correction is part of learning the job. The way you respond to correction can affect whether people trust you on future calls.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A professional stagehand does not take correction personally. They listen, adjust, and use feedback to become safer and more reliable.

Review prompt:

When someone corrects me, do I get defensive, or do I adjust and keep working?

Question: What is the best way to respond when a lead corrects your work?

**Best Answer:**  
 Listen, acknowledge the correction, adjust your behavior, and ask a clear question if you need clarification.

# **Module 6, Lesson 9: Respecting Touring Crew, Local Crew, and Venue Staff**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands how to respect the different groups of people working on a live event. The goal is to help learners understand that touring crew, local crew, venue staff, labor providers, vendors, security, and production teams all have roles that must work together.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why respect for all crew groups is important and how to behave professionally around touring crew, local crew, and venue staff.

Live events require cooperation between many groups.

Touring crew may travel with the production and know the show’s systems and layout.

Local crew provides labor and support at the venue.

Venue staff know the building, policies, access areas, emergency procedures, and site rules.

A new stagehand should treat everyone professionally.

Do not talk down to people, gossip, complain loudly, or create conflict between groups.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

A live event is not built by one person or one group.

You may work around touring crew, local crew, venue staff, security, production managers, vendors, drivers, caterers, stage managers, and department specialists.

Each group has a role.

Touring crew usually travels with the artist, show, or production. They may know exactly how their gear is packed, where it goes, and how they want the show built.

Local crew supports the production at that specific venue or city. Local crew may provide the labor needed to unload trucks, move gear, build areas, assist departments, and load everything out.

Venue staff know the building. They may understand access points, fire lanes, emergency exits, loading docks, house rules, restricted areas, and building systems.

As a new stagehand, your job is to respect all of these groups. Do not assume one group is more important than another. Do not talk badly about touring crew, venue staff, or other locals. Do not get involved in arguments or politics on-site.

Professional respect means listening, following the correct direction, staying in your lane, and remembering that everyone is there to get the show done safely and successfully.

#### ***Professional Respect Looks Like***

Listening when a lead or department head gives direction.

Being polite to venue staff and security.

Not entering restricted areas.

Not touching touring gear without direction.

Not criticizing how another crew works.

Not gossiping about artists, clients, or crew members.

Following house rules.

Asking questions through the correct chain of command.

Treating all workers with basic respect.

#### ***Examples***

**Situation 1:** A touring audio tech tells you not to touch a stack of cases.  
 Best action: Respect the instruction and check with your lead if you need a new task.

**Situation 2:** Security tells you a hallway is restricted.  
 Best action: Do not argue. Ask your lead where you should access the work area.

**Situation 3:** Venue staff tells the crew not to block a fire lane.  
 Best action: Help keep the area clear if directed and report the issue to your lead.

**Situation 4:** Another crew member starts complaining loudly about the touring crew.  
 Best action: Do not join in. Stay professional and focused on the work.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Venue rules, restricted areas, fire lanes, dock procedures, emergency exits, and access-control rules exist for safety and legal reasons. Respect them even if you do not fully understand the reason in the moment.

Respecting the whole crew means understanding that every group has a role. Professional stagehands do not create conflict, gossip, or ignore site rules.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Live events depend on cooperation. A professional stagehand respects touring crew, local crew, venue staff, and everyone else working to make the event happen.

Review prompt:

Do I treat every person on-site as part of the team, even if they are not in my department?

Question: Why should a new stagehand respect venue staff?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because venue staff understand the building, rules, access areas, emergency procedures, and site-specific requirements.

# **Module 6, Lesson 10: What Gets You Asked Back**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands the behaviors that lead to repeat work. The goal is to help learners understand that getting called back is usually based on reliability, safety, communication, attitude, and trust.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify the professional habits that make a stagehand more likely to be rebooked for future calls.

Getting asked back is not just about strength or technical knowledge.

Leads remember workers who are on time, prepared, safe, respectful, and easy to direct.

A stagehand who communicates well and follows direction reduces stress for the crew.

Bad habits can prevent future calls, even if the person is physically capable.

Your reputation is built one call at a time.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

In stagehand work, your reputation matters.

People remember who showed up on time. They remember who listened. They remember who worked safely, stayed focused, and checked in after finishing a task.

They also remember who disappeared, complained, ignored direction, stayed on their phone, moved gear without asking, argued with correction, or created safety concerns.

Getting asked back is not about being perfect on your first day. Beginners are expected to be beginners. What matters is whether you are coachable, reliable, and safe.

A lead is more likely to rebook someone who asks good questions than someone who pretends to know everything. They are more likely to call back someone who communicates clearly than someone who disappears. They are more likely to trust someone who says, “I have not been trained or authorized to do that,” than someone who attempts unsafe work to look confident.

Every call is a chance to build your reputation.

If you want to be asked back, show up prepared, work safely, follow direction, communicate clearly, respect the crew, stay off your phone during active work, and finish strong.

#### ***Habits That Get You Asked Back***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Habit | Why It Matters |
| Arriving on time | Shows reliability |
| Wearing proper clothing and required PPE | Shows readiness and safety awareness |
| Listening carefully | Prevents mistakes |
| Asking good questions | Prevents guessing |
| Following direction | Supports the crew plan |
| Working safely | Protects people, gear, and venue |
| Checking in after tasks | Keeps you useful |
| Handling correction well | Shows coachability |
| Respecting everyone on-site | Builds trust |
| Finishing strong | Shows professionalism even when tired |

#### ***Habits That Can Keep You From Being Asked Back***

Showing up late.

Being unprepared.

Ignoring safety instructions.

Pretending to know tasks you have not been trained or authorized to do.

Moving gear without direction.

Taking excessive phone breaks.

Disappearing during the call.

Complaining constantly.

Arguing with leads.

Disrespecting touring crew, local crew, venue staff, security, or vendors.

Repeating the same mistake after correction.

Creating hazards by rushing, guessing, or freelancing.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Never try to impress people by doing unsafe work. A stagehand who works safely and honestly is more valuable than one who takes risks to look experienced.

Getting asked back comes from trust. Trust is built through reliability, safety, communication, respect, and coachability.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A stagehand gets asked back by being someone leads can trust. You do not need to know everything on day one, but you do need to show up prepared, listen, communicate, work safely, and keep improving.

Practice Activity

Review prompt:

If a lead described how I worked today, would they say I made the call easier or harder?

Question: What is one of the biggest reasons a beginner gets asked back?

**Best Answer:**  
 They are reliable, safe, coachable, respectful, and easy to direct.

# **Module 6 Practical Activity: Communication Scenario Practice**

## **Activity Purpose**

This activity helps learners practice choosing professional responses in common stagehand communication situations.

## **Instructions**

Read each scenario and choose the best response. The goal is to practice clear communication, safe decision-making, and professional crew etiquette.

## **Scenario 1**

You finish moving four road cases to stage left. Your lead is nearby, but you have not been given another task.

What should you say?

A. Nothing. Just walk around until someone notices you.  
 B. “That task is done. What would you like me on next?”  
 C. Start opening the cases to see what is inside.  
 D. Leave the area and look for another department to help.

**Best Answer:** B

## **Scenario 2**

A lead gives you instructions, but you only understood part of what they said.

What should you say?

A. “Copy,” even though you are unsure.  
 B. “Can you clarify where you want these staged?”  
 C. Guess based on where other cases are going.  
 D. Ask another beginner what they think.

**Best Answer:** B

## **Scenario 3**

You see a cable on the ground and think it might need to be coiled.

What should you do?

A. Coil it immediately.  
 B. Pull it hard to see where it goes.  
 C. Ask the lead or appropriate department whether it is cleared to move.  
 D. Cut it loose if it is in the way.

**Best Answer:** C

## **Scenario 4**

You are given a radio for the call. Someone gives you an instruction over the radio that you understand.

What is a good response?

A. “Copy.”  
 B. “What are you talking about?”  
 C. Say nothing and hope they know you heard.  
 D. Use the radio to joke with another crew member.

**Best Answer:** A

## **Scenario 5**

A lead corrects you and tells you that you are standing in a travel path.

What should you do?

A. Argue that you were only there for a second.  
 B. Move and say, “Copy. Where would you like me to stand?”  
 C. Ignore them.  
 D. Tell another crew member the lead was rude.

**Best Answer:** B

## **Scenario 6**

You see a touring crew member working on gear and you want to help.

What should you do first?

A. Start touching the gear so they see you are useful.  
 B. Ask your lead or the touring crew member if help is needed and what you should do.  
 C. Open the nearest case.  
 D. Move their tools to make space.

**Best Answer:** B

## **Scenario 7**

You are asked to operate a lift, but you have not been trained or authorized.

What should you say?

A. “Sure, I can figure it out.”  
 B. “I have not been trained or authorized to operate that.”  
 C. “I watched someone do it once.”  
 D. Nothing. Just try it carefully.

**Best Answer:** B

## **Scenario 8**

You are waiting for the next task and feel awkward standing still.

What should you do?

A. Start moving cases that look out of place.  
 B. Check in with your lead.  
 C. Leave the work area.  
 D. Scroll on your phone until someone calls you.

**Best Answer:** B

## **Scenario 9**

You are told to move a case through a doorway, but you cannot hear the person giving hand signals.

What should you do?

A. Keep pushing and hope you understood.  
 B. Stop if safe to do so and ask for clarification.  
 C. Push faster to get through the doorway.  
 D. Let go of the case and walk away.

**Best Answer:** B

## **Scenario 10**

You want to take a photo backstage because the setup looks interesting.

What should you do first?

A. Take the photo quickly before anyone notices.  
 B. Post it and delete it later if someone complains.  
 C. Ask whether photos are allowed and follow the site or production rule.  
 D. Only take photos of gear, not people.

**Best Answer:** C

# Module 6 Quiz

# **Module 6 Quiz: Stagehand Communication and Crew Etiquette**

## **Quiz Instructions**

Choose the best answer for each question. This quiz checks your understanding of professional communication, crew etiquette, radio basics, phone use, asking questions, receiving correction, avoiding freelancing, and getting called back.

**Recommended passing score:** 80%  
 Total questions: 20

# **Multiple Choice**

## **1. Who should a new stagehand usually go to for direction?**

A. Any person standing nearby  
 B. Audience members  
 C. Their assigned lead, department head, or designated point of contact  
 D. The person who looks the busiest

**Correct Answer:** C

## **2. What should you do if you finish an assigned task?**

A. Leave the area and find another department  
 B. Open nearby cases to see what needs to be done  
 C. Check in with your lead and ask what they want you on next  
 D. Stand around without saying anything

**Correct Answer:** C

## **3. Why is clear communication important on a stagehand call?**

A. It makes the job sound more technical  
 B. It helps prevent mistakes, delays, confusion, and safety issues  
 C. It allows beginners to avoid work  
 D. It replaces the need for safety training

**Correct Answer:** B

## **4. What should you do if you do not understand an instruction?**

A. Say “copy” and guess  
 B. Ask another new stagehand what they think  
 C. Ignore the instruction  
 D. Ask for clarification before starting the task

**Correct Answer:** D

## **5. Which is the best example of a clear question?**

A. “What are we doing?”  
 B. “Where would you like these cases staged?”  
 C. “Why are we doing it this way?”  
 D. “Is this whatever?”

**Correct Answer:** B

## **6. When should you ask before acting?**

A. When the instruction is unclear  
 B. When the task seems unsafe  
 C. When the task involves equipment you are not trained or authorized to use  
 D. All of the above

**Correct Answer:** D

## **7. What does “copy” usually mean?**

A. I heard and understood  
 B. I disagree  
 C. I am leaving the area  
 D. I need a break

**Correct Answer:** A

## **8. What should you say if you did not hear or understand something?**

A. “Whatever.”  
 B. “Say again?” or “Can you clarify?”  
 C. “Copy.”  
 D. Nothing

**Correct Answer:** B

## **9. What does “standing by” usually mean?**

A. I am waiting and ready  
 B. I am done for the day  
 C. I am ignoring the instruction  
 D. I am taking over the task

**Correct Answer:** A

## **10. What is the first rule of using a radio professionally?**

A. Talk as much as possible  
 B. Use it for jokes so the crew relaxes  
 C. Listen before transmitting  
 D. Leave it on the floor when not needed

**Correct Answer:** C

## **11. Which type of information should not be shared over the radio?**

A. Short work-related updates  
 B. Task confirmations  
 C. Private, sensitive, or unnecessary information  
 D. Location updates when assigned

**Correct Answer:** C

## **12. Why are hand signals and nonverbal communication useful on live event jobsites?**

A. Because jobsites can be loud, busy, and difficult to communicate in verbally  
 B. Because talking is never allowed  
 C. Because they replace all safety procedures  
 D. Because beginners should make up their own signals

**Correct Answer:** A

## **13. What should you do if a hand signal or direction is unclear while moving equipment?**

A. Keep moving and hope you understood  
 B. Stop if safe to do so and ask for clarification  
 C. Push faster to finish the task  
 D. Ignore the person giving direction

**Correct Answer:** B

## **14. Why can phone use be a safety problem during active work?**

A. It can distract you from directions, hazards, moving equipment, and work areas  
 B. It makes the battery drain faster  
 C. It makes the call last longer  
 D. It is only a problem during show call

**Correct Answer:** A

## **15. What should you do before taking photos or videos backstage?**

A. Take them quickly before anyone notices  
 B. Ask whether photos are allowed and follow the site or production rule  
 C. Only post them if the event is over  
 D. Send them to friends but do not post them publicly

**Correct Answer:** B

## **16. What does freelancing mean on a stagehand call?**

A. Working as an independent contractor  
 B. Starting tasks, moving gear, opening cases, or changing setups without direction  
 C. Asking your lead what to do next  
 D. Helping with an assigned task

**Correct Answer:** B

## **17. Which is an example of freelancing?**

A. Checking in after finishing a task  
 B. Asking whether a cable is cleared to move  
 C. Moving a road case without direction because it looks out of place  
 D. Waiting where your lead told you to stand

**Correct Answer:** C

## **18. What is the best way to respond when a lead corrects you?**

A. Argue and explain why you were right  
 B. Ignore the correction  
 C. Listen, acknowledge it, adjust, and ask a clear question if needed  
 D. Blame another crew member

**Correct Answer:** C

## **19. Why should a new stagehand respect venue staff?**

A. They usually know the building, rules, access areas, emergency exits, and site-specific procedures  
 B. They are always in charge of every department  
 C. They replace the crew lead  
 D. They only handle ticket sales

**Correct Answer:** A

## **20. Which statement best describes what gets a new stagehand asked back?**

A. They work fast even when unsafe  
 B. They pretend to know everything  
 C. They are reliable, safe, coachable, respectful, and easy to direct  
 D. They move gear without asking so they look busy

**Correct Answer:** C

# **Scenario Questions**

These can be used as written-response questions, discussion prompts, or practical review questions.

## **Scenario 1**

You finish moving four road cases to stage left. Your lead is nearby, but you have not been given another task.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Check in with your lead and say something like, “That task is done. What would you like me on next?”

## **Scenario 2**

A lead gives you instructions, but you only understood part of what they said.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask for clarification before starting. You could say, “Can you clarify where you want these staged?” or “Say again?”

## **Scenario 3**

You see a cable on the ground and think it might need to be coiled.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not move or coil it without direction. Ask your lead or the appropriate department whether the cable is cleared to move.

## **Scenario 4**

You are given a radio for the call. Someone gives you an instruction over the radio that you understand.

**Question:** What is a professional response?

**Best Answer:**  
 Say “Copy” or briefly repeat the important detail, such as “Copy. On my way to stage left.”

## **Scenario 5**

A lead corrects you and tells you that you are standing in a travel path.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Move immediately and respond professionally. A good response would be, “Copy. Where would you like me to stand?”

## **Scenario 6**

You see a touring crew member working on gear and you want to help.

**Question:** What should you do first?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask your lead or the touring crew member if help is needed and what you should do. Do not touch touring gear without direction.

## **Scenario 7**

You are asked to operate a lift, but you have not been trained or authorized.

**Question:** What should you say?

**Best Answer:**  
 “I have not been trained or authorized to operate that.” Then wait for proper instruction, authorization, or reassignment.

## **Scenario 8**

You are waiting for the next task and feel awkward standing still.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Check in with your lead instead of starting random work. Ask, “What would you like me on next?”

## **Scenario 9**

You are moving a case through a doorway, but the person guiding you gives a hand signal you do not understand.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stop if safe to do so and ask for clarification. Do not keep pushing when direction is unclear.

## **Scenario 10**

You want to take a photo backstage because the setup looks interesting.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask whether photos are allowed and follow the employer, venue, client, or production rule. Do not take or post unauthorized photos or videos.
