# Module 7

# **Module 7: Department Basics**

## **Module Overview**

Live event production is made up of multiple departments working together. Audio, lighting, video, staging, rigging, backline, wardrobe, props, and production support all have different responsibilities, tools, risks, and workflows.

For a new stagehand, the goal is not to become an expert in every department right away. The goal is to understand what each department does, recognize basic terms, stay out of the way when specialized work is happening, and help safely when directed.

This module gives learners a beginner-friendly overview of the major departments they may see on a call. It also reinforces one of the most important rules in live production: do not touch, move, connect, disconnect, operate, adjust, open, or modify department gear unless you are trained, authorized, and specifically directed.

This module is awareness-level only. It does not train or authorize learners to perform rigging, electrical work, lift operation, forklift operation, audio operation, lighting operation, video operation, carpentry, or any other specialized department task.

## **Module Learning Objective**

By the end of this module, the learner should be able to identify common live event departments, describe their basic purpose, recognize when specialized work is happening, and explain how to help without interfering or creating safety risks.

# Lesson 7.1

# **Module 7, Lesson 1: Audio Department Basics**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to the basic role of the audio department. The goal is to help new stagehands understand what audio crews do, what types of gear they may see, and why audio equipment should only be handled under direction.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe the basic purpose of the audio department and identify appropriate beginner-level ways to support audio work under supervision.

The audio department is responsible for sound. This may include speakers, microphones, audio consoles, monitor systems, amplifiers, signal cable, communication systems, and other equipment used to make performers, presenters, music, and playback sound clear to the audience and crew.

Audio equipment can be heavy, fragile, expensive, and highly organized. Some cables may carry signal. Some may be connected to active systems. Some gear may already be tested, tuned, or positioned for a specific reason.

A new stagehand may help the audio department by pushing cases, moving speaker carts, helping stage gear in assigned locations, assisting with cable paths under direction, or keeping work areas clear.

A beginner should not plug in, unplug, coil, patch, power on, power off, adjust, or move audio gear without direction from the audio crew or a lead.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

The audio department handles sound for the event. If the audience hears music, microphones, presenters, performers, playback, announcements, or monitor mixes, the audio department is involved.

On a live event call, you may see large speaker arrays, subwoofers, audio consoles, microphone stands, stage boxes, snakes, cable trunks, monitor wedges, in-ear monitor systems, and communication gear.

As a new stagehand, your role is usually support. You may be asked to push audio cases, move speaker carts, help place gear where the audio team wants it, or assist with cable runs under direction.

Audio gear is not random. A speaker case, microphone package, or cable trunk may need to go to a specific location at a specific time. A cable that looks like it is lying loose may still be connected, tested, or needed exactly where it is.

Do not assume audio gear is safe or okay to move just because it is in your path. Ask before touching it.

Good audio support means listening carefully, moving gear safely, protecting equipment, and letting the audio crew make decisions about their system.

#### ***Common Audio Gear a Beginner May See***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Gear | Basic Purpose | Beginner Reminder |
| Speakers | Send sound to the audience or stage | Heavy and often positioned carefully |
| Subwoofers | Produce low-frequency sound | Can be very heavy |
| Audio Console | Controls sound sources and mixes | Do not touch unless assigned |
| Microphones | Capture voices or instruments | Fragile and easy to damage |
| Monitor Wedges | Let performers hear themselves | Do not move unless directed |
| Audio Snakes / Stage Boxes | Carry audio signals | Ask before moving or coiling |
| Cable Trunks | Store audio cable | Keep organized as directed |

#### ***Appropriate Beginner Support Tasks***

● Push audio cases to assigned locations  
 ● Help keep audio work areas clear  
 ● Carry microphone stands when assigned  
 ● Assist with cable paths under direct supervision  
 ● Move speaker carts only when directed  
 ● Ask where gear should be staged before placing it  
 ● Avoid stepping on or rolling cases over cable

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Do not connect, disconnect, power, patch, adjust, or operate audio equipment unless you have been trained, authorized, and specifically directed. If you are unsure whether something is safe to move, ask the audio lead or your crew lead.

The audio department controls sound. A new stagehand can be useful by moving gear carefully, following direction, and not touching audio systems without permission.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Audio gear is important, organized, and often sensitive. Beginners can help the audio department by moving gear safely under direction and avoiding unauthorized handling of cables, consoles, microphones, speakers, or powered equipment.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I see an audio cable on the ground, do I know whether I should move it, or should I ask first?

Question: What should a new stagehand do before unplugging or moving an audio cable?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask the audio crew or lead first. The cable may still be connected, tested, in use, or placed intentionally.

# **Module 7, Lesson 2: Lighting Department Basics**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to the basic role of the lighting department. The goal is to help new stagehands understand what lighting crews do and why lighting gear requires careful handling, awareness, and direction.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe the basic purpose of the lighting department and explain why lighting fixtures, cable, truss, power, and control systems should not be touched without direction.

The lighting department is responsible for visual lighting used on stage, in the audience area, on scenic elements, or throughout the venue. Lighting can help the audience see performers, create mood, support cameras, highlight speakers, and build the visual look of the show.

Lighting equipment may include fixtures, moving lights, truss, dimmers, control consoles, lighting cable, data cable, power distribution, clamps, safety cables, and accessories.

Lighting work may involve heavy gear, overhead work, electrical hazards, hot surfaces, fragile lenses, moving parts, and carefully focused or programmed equipment.

A new stagehand may help by pushing lighting cases, sorting fixtures under direction, moving truss only when assigned, helping stage cable, or keeping lighting work areas clear.

A beginner should not hang fixtures, focus lights, connect power, operate lighting consoles, remove safety cables, adjust clamps, climb truss, or work on overhead systems without proper training and authorization.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

The lighting department creates the visual look of the show. Lighting helps the audience see, supports the mood of the event, and gives shape to the stage.

On a call, you may see lighting fixtures, moving lights, truss, cable, dimmers, control consoles, power distribution, gel, clamps, safety cables, and cases labeled for lighting.

Lighting gear can be heavy, expensive, fragile, hot, and sometimes powered. Some fixtures have moving parts. Some are focused or programmed for a specific position. Some lighting cable may be part of a larger system that should only be handled by trained personnel.

As a new stagehand, you may be asked to push lighting cases, move truss under supervision, help stage gear, or assist with cable paths. But you should not make technical decisions for the lighting department.

Never hang, power, unplug, focus, operate, or adjust lighting equipment unless trained, authorized, and directed.

The best way to help lighting is to follow instructions exactly, protect the gear, avoid walking under overhead work, and ask before touching anything you are unsure about.

#### ***Common Lighting Gear a Beginner May See***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Gear | Basic Purpose | Beginner Reminder |
| Lighting Fixtures | Produce light for the stage or room | Can be fragile, hot, or heavy |
| Moving Lights | Motorized fixtures that move and change effects | Do not move by hand unless directed |
| Truss | Metal structure used to support fixtures and gear | Do not climb or move without direction |
| Lighting Console | Controls lighting looks and cues | Do not touch |
| Cable | Carries power, data, or control signal | Ask before moving |
| Clamps and Safety Cables | Secure fixtures | Do not adjust without authorization |
| Dimmers / Power Gear | Manage electrical power | Specialized work only |

#### ***Appropriate Beginner Support Tasks***

● Push lighting cases to assigned locations  
 ● Help keep lighting work areas clear  
 ● Move truss only when directed and with enough people  
 ● Stage lighting cable where assigned  
 ● Avoid touching fixtures unless asked  
 ● Stay clear of overhead work  
 ● Report damaged gear or unsafe conditions to a lead

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Lighting work can involve electrical power, overhead loads, hot surfaces, and specialized equipment. Do not plug in, unplug, hang, focus, climb, operate, or adjust lighting gear unless you are trained, authorized, and specifically assigned.

Lighting creates the visual environment of the show. Beginners can support lighting crews by moving gear safely, staying alert around overhead work, and not touching lighting systems without permission.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Lighting gear should be handled carefully and only under direction. New stagehands can be valuable by helping move and stage gear safely while leaving technical lighting work to trained personnel.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why could moving a lighting fixture without asking create a problem for the show?

Question: Should a new stagehand adjust a lighting fixture if it looks slightly out of place?

**Best Answer:**  
 No. They should ask the lighting crew or lead. The fixture may be placed, focused, programmed, or secured for a specific reason.

# **Module 7, Lesson 3: Video Department Basics**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to the basic role of the video department. The goal is to help new stagehands understand common video equipment and why video gear is often delicate, expensive, and highly specific.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe the basic purpose of the video department and identify appropriate ways to support video crews without mishandling equipment.

The video department is responsible for visual content, screens, cameras, projectors, playback systems, LED walls, monitors, signal distribution, and related control systems.

Video equipment can be extremely fragile and expensive. LED panels, camera lenses, projectors, processors, fiber lines, monitors, and control equipment must be handled carefully.

A new stagehand may help push video cases, move screen components, assist with cable paths under direction, or help stage gear where the video crew requests.

A beginner should not connect, disconnect, clean, adjust, power, patch, operate, lift, or assemble video equipment without training and direction.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

The video department handles the visual content that appears on screens, LED walls, projectors, monitors, and camera systems.

At a concert, video may include IMAG screens that show the performer to the audience. At a corporate event, video may include slides, graphics, cameras, confidence monitors, livestream equipment, and large projection screens. At a festival or arena show, video may include LED walls, camera platforms, playback systems, and control rooms.

Video gear often looks simple from the outside, but it can be highly technical. A single LED panel, camera, processor, or fiber cable can be expensive and easy to damage.

As a new stagehand, you may help move video cases, stage gear, carry stands, or assist with cable paths. But you should not connect video systems, touch lenses, force connectors, adjust screens, or move delicate gear without permission.

Video crews often have specific ways they want gear handled, packed, cleaned, carried, and staged. Your job is to ask, listen, and follow direction.

#### ***Common Video Gear a Beginner May See***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Gear | Basic Purpose | Beginner Reminder |
| LED Panels | Build video walls or displays | Fragile and expensive |
| Projectors | Project images onto screens | Avoid touching lenses or adjustments |
| Cameras | Capture live video | Do not move or adjust unless directed |
| Screens | Display video content | Handle only under direction |
| Monitors | Display video for crew, performers, or presenters | Do not unplug or move without asking |
| Video Cable / Fiber | Carries video signal | Can be delicate |
| Processors / Control Gear | Manage video systems | Do not touch |

#### ***Appropriate Beginner Support Tasks***

● Push video cases carefully  
 ● Ask where video gear should be staged  
 ● Help keep video work areas clear  
 ● Carry stands or hardware when assigned  
 ● Assist with cable paths under direction  
 ● Avoid touching lenses, screens, LED faces, or connectors  
 ● Report damaged gear instead of trying to fix it

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Video gear can be fragile, expensive, and sensitive to improper handling. Never force a connector, clean a screen or lens, adjust a camera, assemble LED panels, or power video equipment unless trained and directed.

The video department manages the visual display systems of the event. Beginners can help most by moving gear carefully, protecting delicate equipment, and asking before touching anything.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Video gear requires careful handling. A new stagehand should support the video department by following direction, moving gear safely, and avoiding unauthorized handling of screens, cameras, projectors, LED panels, lenses, and cables.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why is it risky to treat video gear like regular road cases or general equipment?

Question: What should a new stagehand do if they see a video cable or fiber line in the way?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask the video crew or lead before moving it. Some video lines are delicate, active, or placed intentionally.

# **Module 7, Lesson 4: Staging and Carpentry Basics**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to staging and carpentry work in live events. The goal is to help new stagehands understand the basic purpose of decks, risers, scenic pieces, platforms, and stage structures without teaching them to build or modify structures independently.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe the basic role of staging and carpentry crews and explain why stage structures should only be assembled, adjusted, or moved under direction.

Staging and carpentry crews may work with stage decks, risers, platforms, scenic elements, stairs, railings, soft goods, set pieces, barricade, and other structural or visual elements.

This work often involves heavy objects, pinch points, trip hazards, alignment, leveling, hardware, and structural safety.

A new stagehand may help carry decks, move risers, stage scenic pieces, pass hardware, or keep the work area organized under direction.

A beginner should not remove hardware, modify structures, climb on incomplete platforms, adjust railings, remove braces, move scenic pieces, or build structures without proper training and direction.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

The staging and carpentry department helps create the physical structure of the event. This may include stage decks, risers, platforms, ramps, stairs, scenic pieces, set walls, soft goods, and other elements that performers, presenters, crew, or equipment may depend on.

This department is important because stage structures must be assembled correctly. A platform that is not seated properly, a stair unit that is not secured, or a scenic piece that is moved without direction can create serious hazards.

As a new stagehand, you may help move decks, hand off hardware, carry scenic pieces, or place risers where directed. You may also help keep work areas clear and organized.

Do not assume that a stage piece is safe to walk on, climb, move, or adjust just because it looks finished. Some structures may be in progress. Some may be waiting for final approval. Some may need specific hardware, bracing, or leveling before they are safe.

Your job is to follow directions, use safe lifting and team-carry habits, watch your hands and feet, and ask before touching or moving structural pieces.

#### ***Common Staging and Carpentry Items***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Item | Basic Purpose | Beginner Reminder |
| Stage Decks | Create raised performance surfaces | Heavy; watch pinch points |
| Risers | Raised platforms for drums, speakers, or performers | Move only as directed |
| Scenic Pieces | Visual set elements | Can be fragile or unstable |
| Stairs / Ramps | Access to platforms | Must be placed and secured correctly |
| Railings | Fall protection or crowd separation | Do not remove or adjust |
| Soft Goods | Curtains, drape, fabric elements | Keep clean and off unsafe surfaces |
| Hardware | Bolts, pins, clamps, legs, braces | Do not remove randomly |

#### ***Appropriate Beginner Support Tasks***

● Carry stage decks with a team when assigned  
 ● Move risers under direction  
 ● Pass hardware to trained crew members  
 ● Keep staging areas clear of trash and loose items  
 ● Watch for pinch points during deck movement  
 ● Avoid walking on incomplete structures  
 ● Ask before moving scenic pieces

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Do not build, modify, climb, adjust, remove hardware from, or use staging structures unless you have been told they are safe and you are assigned to do so. Incomplete staging can create fall, collapse, pinch, and trip hazards.

Staging and carpentry crews build the physical environment of the show. Beginners can help by moving materials safely, watching for hazards, and never treating unfinished structures as safe.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Staging and carpentry work affects the physical safety of the production. Beginners should assist only under direction, use safe handling habits, and never modify or use structures without authorization.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why should I avoid stepping onto a platform unless I know it is complete and safe?

Question: What should a new stagehand do before moving a scenic piece?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask the staging, carpentry, or production lead for direction. Scenic pieces may be fragile, positioned intentionally, or unsafe to move without enough people.

# **Module 7, Lesson 5: Rigging Awareness for Non-Riggers**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson gives non-riggers a basic awareness of rigging work. The goal is not to teach rigging, but to help new stagehands recognize rigging activity, respect exclusion zones, and understand why overhead work requires specialized training.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why rigging is specialized work and describe safe behavior around rigging zones and overhead hazards.

Rigging involves lifting, suspending, supporting, or securing equipment above the stage, audience area, or worksite. This may include truss, motors, points, steel, spansets, shackles, hoists, and flown systems.

Rigging is specialized work. It requires training, experience, calculations, inspection, communication, and strict safety practices.

A new stagehand should not perform rigging work, operate motors, attach rigging hardware, remove rigging hardware, climb structures, stand under suspended loads, or enter rigging zones without direction.

The main responsibility for non-riggers is awareness: stay clear, look up, listen to warnings, obey barriers, and report concerns to a lead.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Rigging is one of the most serious areas of live event production. It involves equipment that may be lifted, flown, suspended, or supported overhead.

You may see riggers working with truss, motors, steel, shackles, spansets, ropes, lifts, baskets, or roof points. You may also hear callouts or warnings when something is moving overhead.

As a new stagehand, you are not being trained to rig in this course. Your job is to recognize rigging activity and stay safe around it.

Never stand under suspended loads. Never walk into a rigging zone without direction. Never touch motors, rigging hardware, ropes, points, steel, or flown equipment unless you are trained, authorized, and specifically assigned.

Even if something looks simple, rigging decisions are not guesses. A small mistake can create serious consequences for workers, performers, audience members, equipment, and the venue.

The safest beginner behavior around rigging is simple: stay clear, pay attention, follow instructions, and ask your lead before entering or working near rigging areas.

#### ***Common Rigging Terms a Beginner May Hear***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Term | Basic Meaning | Beginner Reminder |
| Truss | Metal structure used to support gear | Do not climb or move without direction |
| Motor / Hoist | Lifts or supports loads | Do not operate |
| Point | Overhead location used for rigging | Specialized work only |
| Shackle | Rigging connector | Do not attach or remove |
| Spanset / Sling | Lifting or support component | Do not handle without training |
| Fly / Flown | Suspended overhead | Stay clear unless directed |
| Rigging Zone | Area where overhead work is happening | Do not enter without permission |

#### ***Safe Beginner Behavior Around Rigging***

● Stay clear of rigging zones  
 ● Do not stand under suspended loads  
 ● Look up when entering active work areas  
 ● Obey barriers, caution tape, and lead instructions  
 ● Do not touch motors, steel, shackles, spansets, or rigging hardware  
 ● Do not distract riggers during critical work  
 ● Report unsafe conditions to a lead

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

This lesson is awareness-level only. It does not train or authorize learners to perform rigging work. Rigging should only be performed by trained and authorized personnel following employer, venue, manufacturer, and jobsite procedures.

Rigging involves overhead loads and specialized systems. Non-riggers should respect rigging zones, stay out from under suspended loads, and never touch rigging equipment without training and authorization.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Rigging is specialized, high-risk work. A new stagehand’s responsibility is not to rig, but to stay aware, stay clear, and respect all overhead work zones.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What should I do if I see work happening overhead and I am not sure whether it is safe to walk through the area?

Question: What is the safest action for a new stagehand near an active rigging zone?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stay clear, avoid suspended loads, and wait for direction from a lead or authorized person.

# **Module 7, Lesson 6: Backline and Instruments**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to backline and instrument support. The goal is to help new stagehands understand what backline gear is, why it must be handled carefully, and how beginners can help without damaging instruments or disrupting the show setup.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe what backline means and explain why instruments, amplifiers, pedals, stands, and performer gear should not be moved or adjusted without direction.

Backline usually refers to musician equipment used on stage. This may include drum kits, guitar amps, bass amps, keyboards, stands, pedals, instrument cases, risers, and other performer-specific gear.

Backline gear may be personal, rented, tuned, labeled, patched, or positioned for a performer. Small adjustments can create major problems.

A new stagehand may help move backline cases, push risers, carry stands, or stage gear under direction.

A beginner should not tune instruments, move pedals, adjust amps, open instrument cases, change settings, sit at drum kits, play instruments, or move performer gear without permission.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Backline is the equipment musicians use on stage. This can include drums, amps, keyboards, guitars, basses, pedals, stands, cases, stools, risers, and other performance gear.

Backline can be personal and extremely important to the performer. A guitar may be tuned a certain way. A pedalboard may be patched in a specific order. A keyboard may have custom sounds loaded. A drum kit may be set to exact heights and angles.

As a new stagehand, you may help move backline cases or place equipment where the backline tech tells you. You may help push a drum riser or carry stands under direction.

But you should never treat instruments like casual equipment. Do not play them, test them, tune them, move them, adjust knobs, open cases, or change positions unless you are specifically told to do so.

The best way to help backline is to be careful, ask before touching anything, and respect that performer gear may be personal, fragile, and show-critical.

#### ***Common Backline Gear***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Gear | Basic Purpose | Beginner Reminder |
| Drum Kit | Percussion setup for drummer | Do not sit at or adjust |
| Guitar / Bass Amps | Amplify instruments | Do not change settings |
| Keyboards | Musical instruments or controllers | Do not power or play |
| Pedalboards | Effects control for musicians | Do not step on or move |
| Instrument Stands | Hold guitars, basses, or other instruments | Move only when directed |
| Backline Cases | Protect instruments and gear | Follow labels and direction |
| Drum Risers | Raised platforms for drum kits | Push only under direction |

#### ***Appropriate Beginner Support Tasks***

● Push backline cases carefully  
 ● Carry stands when assigned  
 ● Help place cases in assigned areas  
 ● Move risers only with direction and enough people  
 ● Avoid touching instruments directly unless told  
 ● Keep drinks, food, and tools away from instruments  
 ● Ask before opening any backline case

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Do not play, tune, adjust, move, power, open, or change backline gear without permission. Instruments and musician equipment may be personal, fragile, expensive, and set for exact performance needs.

Backline gear belongs to the performance side of the show. It should be handled with care and only under direction from the backline tech, artist crew, department lead, or production lead.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Backline gear is performance-critical. A new stagehand should treat instruments and musician equipment with extra care and never touch, play, adjust, or move them without authorization.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why could turning one knob on an amplifier or moving one pedal cause a problem?

Question: What should a new stagehand do before opening an instrument case?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask for permission from the backline tech, lead, or appropriate department. Instrument cases should not be opened without direction.

# **Module 7, Lesson 7: Props, Wardrobe, and Production Support**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to props, wardrobe, and production support roles. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that not all important show gear is large, heavy, or technical. Some items are small, delicate, personal, scheduled, or show-critical.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe the basic purpose of props, wardrobe, and production support and explain why these areas require respect, organization, and permission before handling.

Props are objects used during a performance, presentation, event, or show. Wardrobe includes clothing, costumes, shoes, accessories, and performer-related apparel. Production support may include tables, chairs, signage, credentials, hospitality items, office supplies, radios, schedules, paperwork, and other operational needs.

These items may look simple, but they may be important to timing, continuity, performer comfort, branding, or show operation.

A new stagehand may help move wardrobe racks, set tables, carry production supplies, move props under direction, or keep support areas clear.

A beginner should not wear, test, rearrange, borrow, remove, open, alter, clean, or move props or wardrobe without permission.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Not every department is built around large road cases, truss, speakers, or screens. Some departments work with smaller items that are still very important to the show.

Props may include items used on stage, in a scene, at a podium, during a presentation, or as part of a performance. Wardrobe may include costumes, show blacks, performer clothing, shoes, hats, accessories, or quick-change items. Production support may include supplies, credentials, radios, schedules, signage, hospitality items, tables, chairs, and backstage setups.

To a beginner, some of these items may look ordinary. But ordinary does not mean unimportant. A prop may be preset for a specific cue. A wardrobe item may be fitted to one performer. A radio may be assigned to one crew member. A sign or credential may control access to a restricted area.

As a new stagehand, you may be asked to help move wardrobe racks, carry bins, set production tables, place chairs, or move props under direction. But you should always ask before handling items that belong to props, wardrobe, artists, performers, or production offices.

The safest rule is simple: if it is not yours and you were not told to touch it, do not touch it.

#### ***Common Items in These Areas***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Area | Common Items | Beginner Reminder |
| Props | Hand props, set items, podium items, show objects | May be preset for timing |
| Wardrobe | Costumes, clothing, shoes, racks, accessories | Do not wear, move, or alter |
| Production Support | Radios, credentials, signage, schedules, office supplies | May control communication or access |
| Hospitality | Food, drinks, dressing room items | Respect boundaries and privacy |
| Artist Areas | Personal items, cases, wardrobe, instruments | Do not enter or touch without permission |

#### ***Appropriate Beginner Support Tasks***

● Move wardrobe racks when assigned  
 ● Carry production supplies under direction  
 ● Set tables or chairs as instructed  
 ● Help keep hallways and support areas clear  
 ● Ask before moving props or wardrobe  
 ● Respect dressing rooms and artist areas  
 ● Return found items to a lead instead of guessing where they belong

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Props, wardrobe, and production items may be show-critical, personal, confidential, or access-controlled. Do not borrow, wear, move, clean, alter, open, or remove these items without direction.

Small items can be just as important as large gear. Props, wardrobe, and production support require organization, respect, and permission before handling.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Props, wardrobe, and production support items may look simple, but they can be essential to the show. Beginners should ask before touching them and respect artist, performer, and production areas.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why might moving a small prop without asking cause a problem during the show?

Question: What should a new stagehand do if they find a prop or wardrobe item left in a hallway?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not guess where it belongs. Report it to a lead or the appropriate department so it can be handled correctly.

# **Module 7, Lesson 8: Why You Should Not Touch Department Gear Without Direction**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson reinforces one of the most important beginner rules in live events: do not touch department gear without direction. The goal is to help learners understand that unauthorized handling can create safety problems, damage equipment, disrupt workflow, or affect the show.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why department gear should not be moved, connected, disconnected, adjusted, opened, or operated without permission.

Department gear may be placed intentionally, connected to active systems, waiting for inspection, assigned to a specific crew, or staged for a specific sequence.

Moving gear without direction can create confusion, delay work, damage equipment, block access, break signal flow, create trip hazards, or create safety risks.

A new stagehand may want to help, but helpfulness without direction can become a problem.

The correct beginner habit is to ask first, confirm the task, and follow instructions exactly.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

One of the biggest mistakes a new stagehand can make is touching department gear without direction.

This usually happens with good intentions. A beginner sees a case, cable, prop, microphone stand, lighting fixture, or piece of hardware and thinks, “I’ll help by moving this.”

But in live production, gear placement is often intentional. A case may be staged for the next step. A cable may still be connected. A light may already be focused. A microphone may be placed for a performer. A road case may be packed in a specific order. A prop may be preset for a cue.

When you move gear without asking, you may create more work for the crew. You may also create a safety issue or damage something expensive.

Helping does not mean guessing. Helping means asking, listening, and doing the assigned task correctly.

A simple question can prevent a major problem: “Do you want this moved, or should I leave it where it is?”

#### ***Examples of Problems Caused by Touching Gear Without Direction***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Action | Possible Problem |
| Moving a case | It may have been staged for a department |
| Unplugging a cable | It may still be active or needed |
| Coiling a cable | It may be connected or part of a tested path |
| Adjusting a light | It may already be focused or programmed |
| Moving a microphone | It may be placed for a performer or presenter |
| Opening a case | It may belong to a department or artist |
| Moving a prop | It may be preset for a cue |
| Changing amp settings | It may affect the performer’s sound |

#### ***Safe Beginner Phrases***

● “Do you want this moved?”  
 ● “Where should this case go?”  
 ● “Is this cable clear to coil?”  
 ● “Should I leave this where it is?”  
 ● “Who does this belong to?”  
 ● “Can someone from the department clear this first?”  
 ● “I have not been trained on that. Can you show me the safe way?”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Never connect, disconnect, power, operate, adjust, climb, move, open, or modify department gear unless you are trained, authorized, and specifically directed.

Good stagehands do not freelance with other departments’ gear. They ask first, follow direction, and protect the workflow of the production.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Do not touch department gear without direction. Asking first protects people, equipment, timing, and the overall production.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Have I ever tried to be helpful by touching something without asking? Why could that be risky on a live event call?

Question: What should a new stagehand do if they see a cable that appears to be in the way?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask the lead or appropriate department before moving it. The cable may still be connected, active, tested, or intentionally placed.

# **Module 7, Lesson 9: How to Help Without Getting in the Way**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners how to be useful around different departments without interfering. The goal is to help new stagehands understand how to stay available, communicate clearly, and support the crew safely.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe practical ways to help departments while avoiding unsafe or disruptive behavior.

A new stagehand can be helpful without knowing every technical detail. Helpful behavior includes checking in, listening, staying nearby but out of the way, asking before touching gear, keeping paths clear, and completing assigned tasks.

Getting in the way often happens when beginners wander, stand in work zones, block paths, touch gear, interrupt specialized work, disappear, or start tasks without direction.

The best beginner approach is to be alert, available, coachable, and clear.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

A new stagehand does not need to know everything to be useful. In fact, one of the most valuable skills for a beginner is knowing how to help without getting in the way.

Every department has its own workflow. Audio may be testing signal. Lighting may be focusing fixtures. Video may be building screens. Rigging may be moving loads overhead. Backline may be setting performer gear. Staging may be assembling platforms.

If you step into those workflows without direction, you can slow people down or create a hazard.

The right way to help is to check in with your lead, ask where you are needed, and complete assigned tasks carefully. Stay close enough to be available, but not so close that you block movement or interrupt department work.

Pay attention to body language and work zones. If people are carrying heavy gear, clear the path. If someone is giving instructions, stop talking and listen. If a department is doing technical work, give them space.

Being helpful is not about doing random tasks. It is about supporting the plan.

#### ***Helpful Beginner Behavior***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Helpful Behavior | What It Looks Like |
| Check in often | Ask your lead what is next |
| Stay available | Remain nearby without blocking work |
| Keep paths clear | Move out of travel lanes, doors, ramps, and docks |
| Ask before touching gear | Confirm before moving anything |
| Listen actively | Repeat instructions if needed |
| Communicate completion | Tell your lead when the task is done |
| Respect departments | Let specialists handle specialized work |
| Watch for hazards | Report issues instead of ignoring them |

#### ***Behaviors That Get in the Way***

● Standing in doorways, ramps, truck paths, or cable paths  
 ● Wandering away from your assigned area  
 ● Starting random tasks without direction  
 ● Touching gear from another department  
 ● Interrupting technical conversations  
 ● Blocking crews carrying heavy equipment  
 ● Asking questions at unsafe or high-pressure moments  
 ● Pretending to understand when you do not  
 ● Disappearing when the crew needs help

#### ***Safe Beginner Phrases***

● “Where would you like me?”  
 ● “What should I do next?”  
 ● “Is this a safe place to stand?”  
 ● “Do you want me to stay here or check back in?”  
 ● “I’m finished with that task. What’s next?”  
 ● “I don’t want to be in the way. Where should I stage?”  
 ● “Can I help move this, or should I leave it for the department?”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Do not confuse being available with standing in the middle of active work. Stay close enough to receive direction, but clear of moving equipment, overhead work, truck paths, forklift routes, doorways, ramps, and department work zones.

A helpful stagehand supports the crew’s plan. They do not freelance, block work, disappear, or touch gear without direction.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

You do not need advanced technical knowledge to be useful. You need awareness, communication, patience, and the discipline to help only where directed.

Module 7 Key Takeaways

● Every department has a specific purpose and workflow.  
 ● Audio handles sound systems and related equipment.  
 ● Lighting handles the visual lighting systems of the show.  
 ● Video handles screens, cameras, projectors, LED walls, and visual content systems.  
 ● Staging and carpentry handle physical structures, decks, risers, platforms, and scenic pieces.  
 ● Rigging is specialized overhead work and should only be performed by trained and authorized workers.  
 ● Backline includes instruments and musician equipment.  
 ● Props, wardrobe, and production support items may look simple but can be show-critical.  
 ● Beginners should never touch department gear without direction.  
 ● The best way to help is to ask, listen, stay aware, and avoid freelancing.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Where should I stand if I want to be available but not in the way?

Question: What should a new stagehand do after finishing an assigned task?

**Best Answer:**  
 Check back in with their lead, report that the task is complete, and ask what to do next.

# **End of Module 7 Transition**

Now that you understand the basic departments on a live event site, the next step is learning how to recognize common tools, gear, cases, cables, and handling situations. Module 8 will focus on tools, gear, and handling basics so you can feel more confident around the equipment you may see on your first calls.

# Module 7 Quiz

# **Module 7 Quiz: Department Basics**

## **Quiz Instructions**

Choose the best answer for each question. This quiz checks your understanding of live event departments, department gear, safe beginner behavior, and why entry-level stagehands should not touch, move, connect, disconnect, adjust, operate, or modify specialized equipment without direction.

This is an awareness-level quiz. Passing this quiz does not certify or authorize you to perform rigging, electrical work, lift operation, forklift operation, audio operation, lighting operation, video operation, carpentry, or any other specialized department task.

**Recommended passing score:** 80%  
 Total questions: 20

## **Multiple Choice**

## **1. What is the main purpose of Module 7?**

A. To train learners to operate all department equipment  
 B. To give learners awareness of major live event departments  
 C. To certify learners as riggers  
 D. To teach learners how to run a show independently

**Correct Answer:** B

## **2. What is the audio department mainly responsible for?**

A. Sound systems, microphones, speakers, consoles, and audio signal flow  
 B. Costumes and wardrobe racks  
 C. Stage decks and risers  
 D. Lighting fixtures and focus

**Correct Answer:** A

## **3. What should a new stagehand do before moving or unplugging an audio cable?**

A. Move it quickly if it looks messy  
 B. Ask the audio crew or lead first  
 C. Coil it and put it in any open case  
 D. Pull it until the other end comes loose

**Correct Answer:** B

## **4. Which of the following is an appropriate beginner-level support task for the audio department?**

A. Repatching the audio console  
 B. Adjusting microphones without asking  
 C. Pushing audio cases to assigned locations  
 D. Turning speakers on and off

**Correct Answer:** C

## **5. What is the lighting department mainly responsible for?**

A. Food and hospitality  
 B. Visual lighting, fixtures, control, and lighting-related systems  
 C. Ticketing and audience seating  
 D. Performer clothing

**Correct Answer:** B

## **6. Why should a beginner not adjust a lighting fixture without direction?**

A. The fixture may already be focused, programmed, secured, or positioned intentionally  
 B. Lighting gear is never used during live events  
 C. Lighting fixtures are always fake  
 D. Only audience members adjust lights

**Correct Answer:** A

## **7. Which item is commonly associated with the lighting department?**

A. Moving light  
 B. Drum throne  
 C. Wardrobe rack  
 D. Catering table

**Correct Answer:** A

## **8. What is the video department mainly responsible for?**

A. Screens, cameras, projectors, LED walls, playback, and video systems  
 B. Forklift maintenance  
 C. Food service  
 D. Dressing room security

**Correct Answer:** A

## **9. Why should video gear be handled carefully?**

A. It is usually disposable  
 B. It is often fragile, expensive, and technically specific  
 C. It does not matter where it is placed  
 D. It is safer to force connectors if they do not fit

**Correct Answer:** B

## **10. What should a new stagehand do if they see a video cable or fiber line in the way?**

A. Pull it out of the way immediately  
 B. Cut it if it creates a trip hazard  
 C. Ask the video crew or lead before moving it  
 D. Wrap it around the nearest case

**Correct Answer:** C

## **11. What do staging and carpentry crews commonly work with?**

A. Stage decks, risers, platforms, scenic pieces, stairs, and ramps  
 B. Microphone mixes only  
 C. Ticket scanners  
 D. Artist meals

**Correct Answer:** A

## **12. Why should a new stagehand avoid walking on incomplete staging?**

A. It may not be fully secured, leveled, braced, or approved for use  
 B. It is always decorative only  
 C. It belongs to the audience  
 D. It cannot cause injury

**Correct Answer:** A

## **13. What is rigging in live event production?**

A. The sale of merchandise  
 B. The lifting, suspending, supporting, or securing of equipment overhead  
 C. The process of checking tickets  
 D. The cleaning of dressing rooms

**Correct Answer:** B

## **14. What should a non-rigger do near an active rigging zone?**

A. Stand under the load to watch  
 B. Touch rigging hardware to help  
 C. Stay clear, avoid suspended loads, and wait for direction  
 D. Operate the motor if no one is nearby

**Correct Answer:** C

## **15. Which statement about rigging is correct?**

A. Anyone can perform rigging if they are strong enough  
 B. Rigging is specialized work that requires training and authorization  
 C. Rigging is only used in theaters  
 D. Rigging hardware can be moved freely by beginners

**Correct Answer:** B

## **16. What is backline?**

A. Musician and performance equipment such as drums, amps, keyboards, instruments, and pedals  
 B. Audience seating  
 C. Venue parking equipment  
 D. Fire exits and emergency maps

**Correct Answer:** A

## **17. What should a new stagehand avoid doing with backline gear?**

A. Moving backline cases when directed  
 B. Carrying stands when assigned  
 C. Playing, tuning, adjusting, opening, or moving instruments without permission  
 D. Asking where a case should go

**Correct Answer:** C

## **18. Why can props and wardrobe be important even if they look simple?**

A. They may be preset, personal, show-critical, fitted, timed, or assigned to a specific performer  
 B. They are usually trash  
 C. They are never used during a performance  
 D. They are only decorations for the crew room

**Correct Answer:** A

## **19. What is the safest rule for department gear?**

A. Touch it if it looks like it needs to be moved  
 B. Open cases to find out what is inside  
 C. Do not touch, move, connect, disconnect, adjust, or operate it without direction  
 D. Move it first and ask later

**Correct Answer:** C

## **20. What should a new stagehand do before operating powered equipment such as a forklift, lift, motor, or other specialized machinery?**

A. Try it if they have watched someone else do it  
 B. Operate it only if the crew is in a hurry  
 C. Make sure they are trained, authorized, and specifically assigned by the employer or appropriate lead  
 D. Ask another beginner for a quick explanation

**Correct Answer:** C

# **Scenario Questions**

These can be used as written-response questions or discussion prompts.

## **Scenario 1**

You see an audio cable running across the stage. It looks like it might be in the way, but no one has told you to move it.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not move it without direction. Ask the audio crew or your lead whether the cable is clear to move, coil, or leave in place.

## **Scenario 2**

A lighting fixture looks slightly crooked. You think you can straighten it by hand.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not adjust it. Ask the lighting crew or lead. The fixture may already be focused, programmed, secured, or positioned intentionally.

## **Scenario 3**

You are near an LED wall case, and someone asks you to help move video gear. You are not sure which side of the panel is safe to touch.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask the video crew how they want it handled before touching it. Video gear can be fragile and expensive, and some surfaces or connectors can be easily damaged.

## **Scenario 4**

You see riggers working overhead and a load being lifted.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stay clear of the rigging zone, do not stand under the suspended load, and wait for direction from a lead or authorized person.

## **Scenario 5**

A guitar case is sitting backstage. You are curious what is inside.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not open it. Backline and instrument cases may contain personal, fragile, tuned, or show-critical gear. Only open or move them if directed by the backline tech, artist crew, or lead.

## **Scenario 6**

You find a prop sitting in a hallway and think it may have been forgotten.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not move it to a random location. Report it to your lead or the props or production department so it can be handled correctly.

## **Scenario 7**

You finish pushing several lighting cases to their assigned area.

**Question:** What should you do next?

**Best Answer:**  
 Check back in with your lead, let them know the task is complete, and ask what they want you to do next.

## **Scenario 8**

A crew member tells you to operate a lift, forklift, motor, or other powered equipment, but you have not been trained or authorized to use it.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not operate the equipment. Tell the lead that you have not been trained or authorized for that task and ask for another assignment or proper direction.
