# Module 8

# **Module 8: Tools, Gear, and Handling Basics**

## **Module Overview**

### **Module Purpose**

This module introduces new stagehands to common tools, equipment, road cases, cables, tape, and basic handling concepts they may see on a live event jobsite.

The goal is not to train learners as technicians, riggers, electricians, carpenters, forklift operators, or department specialists. The goal is to help beginners recognize common gear, understand basic handling expectations, avoid unsafe assumptions, and know when to ask before touching, moving, opening, coiling, adjusting, or operating anything.

Many new stagehands feel lost because they do not recognize the equipment around them. This module gives learners practical vocabulary so they can follow direction more confidently, communicate clearly, and avoid creating problems for the crew.

### **Learning Objectives**

By the end of this module, learners should be able to:

Identify common stagehand tools and basic gear.

Recognize major parts of a road case.

Understand basic cable-handling awareness.

Explain the difference between common types of tape.

Recognize common equipment such as decks, truss, carts, motors, pipe, and drape.

Understand basic lifting and team-carry expectations.

Identify pinch points, crush points, and stored-energy hazards.

Explain why gear organization matters during load-in and load-out.

Know when to stop and ask before touching, moving, opening, coiling, adjusting, or operating equipment.

### **Important Safety Disclaimer**

This module is awareness-level only.

It does not qualify a learner to operate powered tools, lifts, forklifts, rigging equipment, electrical systems, motors, hoists, stage automation, or specialized department gear.

A new stagehand should only use tools, move gear, or assist with equipment when they have been trained, assigned, and directed by the appropriate lead or department.

# **Module 8 Summary**

Module 8 introduced the tools, gear, and handling basics that new stagehands are likely to encounter on live event jobsites.

The most important lesson is that recognizing gear does not automatically mean a beginner is trained or authorized to use it.

A strong entry-level stagehand knows how to identify common tools and equipment, follow direction, handle basic items carefully, communicate during team movement, avoid pinch and crush hazards, and keep gear organized.

## **Module 8 Key Takeaways**

Tools should only be used when assigned and understood.

Road cases should be read, respected, and handled carefully.

Cables should never be pulled, coiled, unplugged, repaired, or rerouted without direction.

Tape can help or hurt depending on how it is used.

Recognizing truss, motors, shackles, decks, and cable does not qualify a beginner to work on them.

Safe lifting requires communication, control, and asking for help when needed.

Pinch points, crush points, and stored energy are common hazards.

Organized gear keeps the crew safer and more efficient.

When unsure, stop and ask.

# Lesson 8.1

# **Module 8, Lesson 1: Common Stagehand Tools**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to common tools they may see or carry on a stagehand call. The focus is on recognition, safe habits, and understanding that tools should only be used when appropriate, permitted, and understood.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common stagehand tools and explain why beginners should not use tools they have not been trained or authorized to use.

Stagehands may see many tools on a jobsite, but not every tool is meant for every person to use.

Common stagehand tools may include:

Adjustable wrench

C-wrench

Multitool

Flashlight or headlamp

Work gloves

Tape measure

Sharpie or paint marker

Utility knife

Small notepad

Radio, if assigned

Gaff tape or spike tape, if assigned

Some tools are simple, but they can still create hazards if used carelessly. A utility knife can cut people, cable jackets, soft goods, cases, or scenic materials. A wrench can damage hardware if used incorrectly. Tape can create trip hazards if placed poorly. Flashlights and headlamps can distract others if pointed into someone’s eyes.

The most important beginner rule is this:

Do not use a tool just because you own it or see someone else using it.

Use tools only when you have been assigned the task, understand what the tool is for, and know how to use it safely.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

When you start working as a stagehand, you may see people carrying tools on their belt, in their pocket, or in a work bag. Some of these tools are simple. Some are specialized. Some belong to a specific department.

It can be tempting to think that carrying more tools makes you look more experienced. But tools do not make you valuable by themselves. Judgment does.

A good stagehand knows when to use a tool, when not to use a tool, and when to ask before touching something.

For example, a multitool may be useful for small tasks, but it should not be used to cut cable, modify gear, open equipment, adjust hardware, or repair something unless you have been told to do so by the appropriate lead.

A utility knife may be useful for cutting tape or packaging, but it can also damage equipment or injure someone if used carelessly.

As a beginner, your job is not to show off how many tools you have. Your job is to be prepared, listen carefully, and use only the tools that are appropriate for your assigned task.

If you are not sure whether you should use a tool, ask your lead.

#### ***Common Beginner-Friendly Tools***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Tool | What It May Be Used For | Beginner Reminder |
| Work gloves | Protecting hands during approved handling tasks | Gloves do not make every task safe |
| Flashlight/headlamp | Seeing in dark areas during load-in or load-out | Do not shine light into someone’s eyes |
| Sharpie/marker | Marking labels or notes when assigned | Do not mark gear unless directed |
| Tape measure | Measuring distance or placement when assigned | Confirm what needs to be measured |
| Adjustable wrench | General hardware tasks when assigned | Do not tighten or loosen hardware without direction |
| Multitool | Small basic tasks | Do not cut, open, pry, or modify gear without permission |
| Utility knife | Cutting tape, cardboard, or packaging when assigned | Cut away from yourself and others |

#### ***Examples***

#### ***Good Example***

A lead says, “Cut this piece of tape off the floor and throw it away.”

The stagehand uses a utility knife carefully, cuts away from their body, keeps their free hand clear, removes the tape, and disposes of it properly.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Bad Example***

A stagehand sees a cable taped down and cuts it loose without asking.

That cable may still be in use, may belong to a department, or may have been intentionally secured. Cutting it loose could create a hazard or disrupt the setup.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Never use tools to alter, repair, loosen, tighten, cut, open, or disconnect equipment unless you have been trained, assigned, and authorized.

Tools are useful only when paired with training, direction, and good judgment.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A beginner does not need to carry every tool. A good new stagehand uses the right tool only when directed, understands the task, and asks before doing anything that could damage gear or create a hazard.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I own a tool, does that automatically mean I should use it on a call?

Question: What should a new stagehand do before using a tool on production gear?

**Best Answer:**  
 Make sure they have been assigned the task, understand the safe use of the tool, and have permission from the appropriate lead or department.

# **Module 8, Lesson 2: C-Wrenches, Multitools, Flashlights, Tape, and Markers**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson explains several common items new stagehands may carry or see on the job: c-wrenches, multitools, flashlights, tape, and markers.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe basic uses and safety expectations for common small stagehand tools.

Small tools can be helpful, but they can also cause problems when used without permission.

A c-wrench or adjustable wrench may be used around bolts, clamps, or hardware, but beginners should not tighten, loosen, remove, or adjust hardware unless assigned.

A multitool can be useful for small tasks, but it should not be treated as a replacement for proper tools or department-specific equipment.

A flashlight or headlamp is useful during dark load-outs, backstage movement, truck loading, and outdoor work, but it should be used with awareness.

Tape and markers are common, but beginners should not label, tape, spike, or mark anything without direction.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Some of the most common tools you will see on a stagehand call are small enough to fit in your pocket or work bag. These include c-wrenches, multitools, flashlights, tape, and markers.

A c-wrench may look simple, but using it incorrectly can damage hardware or create a safety problem. Just because you see a bolt does not mean you should tighten it. Just because something looks loose does not mean you should adjust it. Hardware may belong to a specific department, and changing it without direction can cause serious problems.

A multitool is also useful, but it should not be used to cut cables, pry open cases, remove hardware, or modify equipment unless you are told to do so.

A flashlight is one of the most useful beginner tools. Many load-outs happen in dark rooms, backstage hallways, trucks, or outdoor sites. But you still need to be careful where you point it. Do not shine bright light into someone’s eyes, into an audience area during a show, or toward people operating equipment.

Markers are useful for labeling, but only when labeling is assigned. Random markings can confuse departments or damage gear.

Tape is one of the most common items in production, but tape has rules too. Different tape has different uses, and placing tape incorrectly can leave residue, damage surfaces, hide labels, or create trip hazards.

#### ***Tool Awareness Table***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Item | Common Use | What Beginners Should Avoid |
| C-wrench | Hardware-related tasks when assigned | Adjusting clamps, bolts, truss, decks, or rigging hardware without direction |
| Multitool | Small utility tasks | Cutting cable, prying cases, or modifying gear |
| Flashlight/headlamp | Seeing in dark work areas | Blinding others or distracting operators |
| Gaff tape | Temporary securing or marking when assigned | Taping cables poorly or damaging surfaces |
| Spike tape | Position marks when assigned | Marking without permission |
| Sharpie/paint marker | Labels or notes when assigned | Writing on gear without direction |

#### ***Examples***

#### ***Good Example***

A lead asks you to mark four road cases with temporary labels. You confirm what should be written and where the labels should go.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Bad Example***

You see an unlabeled case and write “lighting” on it because you think that is where it belongs.

That may create confusion if the case belongs to another department or needs to stay unmarked.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Never adjust hardware, cut anything, tape anything down, or mark gear unless you are sure you have permission.

Small tools can create big problems when used without direction.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

C-wrenches, multitools, flashlights, tape, and markers are useful, but beginners should use them carefully and only when assigned. The safest habit is to ask before altering, marking, cutting, taping, or adjusting anything.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Could using a simple tool without permission cause confusion, damage, or a safety issue?

Question: Why should a beginner avoid tightening or loosening hardware without direction?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because the hardware may belong to a specific department or may be part of a structure, system, or setup that requires trained personnel.

# **Module 8, Lesson 3: Road Case Parts**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners how to recognize the basic parts of a road case and understand why cases should be handled with care.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common road case parts and explain why cases should not be opened, moved, stacked, or tipped without direction.

Road cases protect production equipment during transport. They may carry lighting fixtures, audio equipment, video gear, cables, backline, wardrobe, tools, scenic pieces, or other important items.

Common road case parts include:

Lid

Latches

Hinges

Handles

Casters

Brakes

Labels

Skids or feet

Corners and edges

Foam or interior padding

Cases may look similar, but they are not all the same. Some are heavy. Some are top-heavy. Some have fragile gear inside. Some must stay upright. Some should not be stacked. Some are assigned to a department and should only be opened by that department.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Road cases are everywhere in live production. They are used to move and protect gear from the truck to the venue and back again.

To a beginner, a road case may look like just a box with wheels. But every case has a purpose, and many cases are packed in a specific way.

The labels tell the crew what the case is, where it belongs, and sometimes how it should be handled. Casters allow the case to roll, but they can also create hazards if the case gets away from you. Brakes help keep a case from moving, but brakes should not be trusted on every surface without awareness.

Latches keep lids closed. Handles help with controlled movement. Corners protect the case. Foam or padding inside may protect delicate equipment.

Never assume a case is empty. Never assume a case is light. Never assume it is okay to open a case. Never assume it is okay to stack another case on top of it.

When in doubt, ask where the case goes and how it should be handled.

#### ***Road Case Parts Table***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Case Part | Purpose | Beginner Awareness |
| Lid | Covers and protects contents | Do not remove or open unless directed |
| Latches | Hold lids or doors closed | Watch fingers and confirm they are secured |
| Hinges | Allow lids or doors to open | Keep hands clear |
| Handles | Help control movement | Do not use damaged handles |
| Casters | Wheels that allow the case to move | Watch rolling direction and floor conditions |
| Brakes | Help stop movement | Use when directed, but stay aware |
| Labels | Identify case contents or destination | Read labels before moving |
| Corners/edges | Protect case structure | Watch for sharp or hard contact points |
| Interior foam/padding | Protects gear inside | Do not remove or disturb |

#### ***Examples***

#### ***Good Example***

A lead says, “Take this audio case to stage right and leave it closed.”

The stagehand checks the label, moves the case carefully, keeps it upright, leaves it closed, and checks back in.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Bad Example***

A stagehand opens a case to “see what is inside” because they are curious.

That can expose expensive gear, disrupt packing order, or interfere with a department’s setup.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Keep your head up around moving gear, control your path, and never assume equipment operators or other crew members can see you.

Do not open cases, remove lids, stack cases, stand on cases, ride cases, or move cases without direction.

A road case is not just a box. It is part of a department’s system for protecting, organizing, and moving gear.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Road cases protect valuable equipment and keep departments organized. Beginners should read labels, handle cases carefully, and avoid opening, stacking, or moving cases without direction.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why might opening a case without permission create a problem?

Question: What should a new stagehand check before moving a road case?

**Best Answer:**  
 They should check the label, confirm direction from their lead, understand where the case is going, and move it safely without opening or altering it.

# **Module 8, Lesson 4: Cable Coiling Basics**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces basic cable awareness and explains why beginners should only coil, pull, move, or disconnect cables under direction from the appropriate department.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why cable handling requires care and why cables should not be pulled, coiled, unplugged, repaired, or moved without permission.

Cables are everywhere in live production. They may carry power, audio, video, data, lighting control, communication, or other signals.

A beginner may see cables such as:

Audio cable

Power cable

Feeder cable

Socapex cable

Ethernet/data cable

Video cable

Speaker cable

Intercom cable

Extension cable

Cables can look similar but serve very different purposes.

Some cables may be energized. Some may be fragile. Some may be expensive. Some may be connected to equipment. Some may be part of a carefully planned cable path.

A new stagehand should never unplug, cut, pull, coil, drag, tape, repair, or reroute cable unless assigned by the appropriate lead or department.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Cable work is one of the most common things you will see on a production site. You may see cables running across the stage, through cable ramps, along walls, backstage, in the air, or near power distribution areas.

As a beginner, you may be asked to help move or coil cable. But cable handling is not random.

Different departments use different cable types. Audio cables, lighting cables, video cables, data cables, and power cables may all have different handling requirements. Some cables are heavy. Some are delicate. Some must be coiled a certain way. Some should not be pulled from one end without someone watching the other end.

The safest rule is simple: do not touch cable unless you have been told to.

If you are told to coil cable, ask how the department wants it coiled if you are unsure. Do not yank it. Do not drag connectors across the floor. Do not pull cable around corners without checking. Do not disconnect anything unless you are specifically instructed to do so.

Cable work requires patience, communication, and awareness.

#### ***Cable Handling Awareness***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Situation | Safer Beginner Response |
| You see a loose cable | Ask before moving it |
| You are told to coil cable | Confirm the method if unsure |
| A cable seems stuck | Stop and communicate |
| You do not know what the cable is | Ask the appropriate department |
| A cable crosses a walkway | Tell a lead; do not randomly reroute it |
| A cable is connected to equipment | Do not unplug it unless specifically directed |
| A cable looks damaged | Report it; do not repair it |

#### ***Important Cable Reminders***

Never pull a cable if you do not know what it is connected to.

Never unplug a cable without clear direction.

Never cut tape holding cable unless assigned.

Never repair damaged cable.

Never coil cable in a method you have not been shown.

Never drag connectors through dirt, water, sharp edges, or traffic paths.

Never step on, drive over, or place heavy objects on cables unless they are properly protected and approved by the department.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Keep your head up around moving gear, control your path, and never assume equipment operators or other crew members can see you.

Power-related cable can be especially dangerous. Do not handle feeder, power distribution, electrical connections, or energized cable unless you have been trained, assigned, and authorized by the appropriate department.

Cable may look simple, but it can carry signal, power, communication, or control. Always ask before moving or handling it.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Cable handling requires direction and care. Beginners should never assume a cable is safe to move, unplug, tape, repair, coil, or reroute without permission.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why is pulling a cable without checking the other end unsafe?

Question: What should a beginner do if they are not sure what a cable is or whether it is safe to move?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stop and ask the appropriate lead or department before touching, pulling, coiling, repairing, or unplugging it.

# **Module 8, Lesson 5: Tape Basics**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners the basic differences between common production tapes and explains why tape should be used carefully.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify basic uses for gaff tape, electrical tape, and spike tape, while understanding that tape should only be used where appropriate and when directed.

Tape is common in live production, but not all tape is the same.

Common types include:

Gaff tape

Electrical tape

Spike tape

Console tape or labeling tape

Painter’s tape

Duct tape, although it is often not preferred for production surfaces

Gaff tape is commonly used in production because it is strong, usually cloth-based, and often easier to remove than duct tape. It may be used for temporary cable securing, labeling, marking, or other assigned tasks.

Electrical tape is used for electrical-related purposes by trained or authorized personnel. Beginners should not use electrical tape to repair, modify, or insulate cables unless trained and authorized.

Spike tape is usually used to mark positions, placements, or locations on stage or floor surfaces.

Tape can damage floors, leave residue, create trip hazards, cover labels, hide problems, or confuse the crew if used incorrectly.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Tape is one of the most common supplies in live production. You may see tape on floors, cables, cases, consoles, stage decks, walls, props, and equipment.

But tape is not just tape.

Gaff tape is commonly used because it can temporarily secure or mark things without leaving the same kind of mess that some other tapes can leave. Spike tape is usually used for marking positions, such as where a case, chair, scenic piece, or performer mark may go. Electrical tape has specific uses and should not be used casually by beginners.

The important thing to understand is that tape can solve problems, but it can also create them.

Poorly taped cable can become a trip hazard. Tape on the wrong surface can damage paint, flooring, or finish. Tape placed over labels can confuse departments. Tape left behind after load-out makes the crew look bad and may damage the venue.

As a beginner, use tape only when assigned and ask what kind of tape should be used.

#### ***Tape Comparison Table***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Tape Type | Common Use | Beginner Reminder |
| Gaff tape | Temporary securing, cable management, marking when assigned | Do not tape randomly or over labels |
| Spike tape | Position marks and stage marks | Do not mark surfaces without permission |
| Electrical tape | Electrical-related use by trained or authorized people | Do not use to repair cables |
| Painter’s tape | Temporary light-duty marking on some surfaces | Confirm before using |
| Duct tape | General-purpose tape, often not preferred on show sites | May leave residue or damage surfaces |

#### ***Examples***

#### ***Good Example***

A lead tells you to tape down a short cable crossing a low-traffic area and shows you where to tape it.

You use the assigned tape and make sure the cable is secured smoothly without loose edges.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Bad Example***

A stagehand tapes cable across an emergency exit path because it is the fastest route.

That creates a serious hazard and may block a required path of travel.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Never tape across emergency exits, fire lanes, doorways, stairs, ramps, or high-traffic routes without direction and proper protection.

Tape is useful only when the right tape is used in the right place for the right reason.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Tape is common in production, but it should be used carefully. Beginners should know the difference between tape types and ask before taping, marking, repairing, or securing anything.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Could bad tape placement create a safety hazard?

Question: Why should beginners avoid using electrical tape to repair damaged cable?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because damaged cable should be handled by trained and authorized personnel. A beginner should report the issue instead of attempting a repair.

# **Module 8, Lesson 6: Pipe, Drape, Decks, Truss, Motors, Cases, and Carts**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to common equipment they may see on live event jobsites and helps them understand which items are safe to recognize but not operate, adjust, assemble, or move without training.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to recognize common production equipment and explain why some gear should only be handled by trained or authorized personnel.

Common equipment may include:

Pipe and drape

Stage decks

Risers

Truss

Chain motors

Road cases

Carts

Barricade

Cable ramps

Ladders

Soft goods

Scenic pieces

Some items may be simple to move under direction. Others are part of structural, overhead, electrical, or specialized systems.

A beginner should understand the difference between recognizing gear and being qualified to assemble, operate, rig, adjust, or modify it.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Live event jobsites are full of gear. Some items are easy to recognize once you have seen them a few times. Others may look simple but are connected to serious safety concerns.

Pipe and drape is often used in convention centers, ballrooms, trade shows, backstage areas, and event spaces. It may divide rooms, hide backstage areas, or create booths.

Decks and risers are used to build stage surfaces or platforms. They may involve legs, braces, clamps, guardrails, stairs, or other components. Beginners should only assist under direction and should not modify or remove structural parts.

Truss is commonly used to support lighting, video, scenic elements, or other production equipment. Truss may be ground-supported or flown overhead. Beginners should not climb, rig, alter, load, or modify truss without proper training and authorization.

Motors, hoists, rigging points, shackles, spansets, steels, and overhead hardware are specialized rigging-related equipment. Beginners should stay clear unless directed and should not touch, move, connect, disconnect, or operate these items.

Carts and road cases may seem simple, but they can still create crush hazards, pinch points, and runaway movement on ramps or uneven floors.

The goal in this lesson is recognition, not qualification. You are learning what gear is called so you can follow directions better and know when to ask.

#### ***Common Gear Table***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Gear | What It Is | Beginner Awareness |
| Pipe and drape | Uprights, bases, crossbars, and fabric used to divide or mask space | Move or assemble only when assigned |
| Decks | Stage platform pieces | Heavy; may involve structural connections |
| Risers | Elevated platforms | Watch edges, stairs, and stability |
| Truss | Aluminum structure often used for lighting/video support | Do not climb, rig, load, or modify |
| Motors/hoists | Equipment used to lift flown systems | Rigging-related; do not touch unless trained and authorized |
| Cases | Protective transport boxes | Read labels and move with care |
| Carts | Rolling transport equipment | Watch speed, corners, ramps, and pinch points |
| Barricade | Crowd-control barrier system | Heavy; move only under direction |
| Cable ramps | Protective ramps for cable paths | Watch trip hazards and proper placement |

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Keep your head up around moving gear, control your path, and never assume equipment operators or other crew members can see you.

Recognizing equipment does not mean you are authorized to use it. Do not assemble, operate, lift, rig, climb, energize, repair, or modify equipment unless trained, assigned, and authorized.

Learn the names of common gear so you can communicate clearly, but never mistake recognition for qualification.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A new stagehand should be able to recognize common equipment, but they should not operate, assemble, alter, repair, or move specialized gear without training and direction.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What is the difference between knowing what a motor is and being allowed to use one?

Question: Should a beginner touch or move rigging hardware just because it is sitting nearby?

**Best Answer:**  
 No. Rigging-related equipment should only be handled by trained and authorized personnel under proper direction.

# **Module 8, Lesson 7: Lifting and Team Carry Basics**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces safe lifting awareness and team-carry expectations for new stagehands.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe basic lifting awareness, team-carry communication, and when to ask for help.

Stagehand work can involve physical labor, but strength alone is not enough.

Safe handling requires:

Knowing when to ask for help.

Checking the path before moving.

Communicating before lifting.

Lifting only what you can safely control.

Avoiding twisting while carrying.

Watching fingers, feet, corners, and edges.

Moving at the pace of the team.

Setting items down together.

A team carry should be coordinated. One person may call the movement so everyone lifts, turns, walks, and sets down at the same time.

Common callouts include:

“Ready?”

“Lift on three.”

“Walking.”

“Turning.”

“Step.”

“Down on three.”

“Stop.”

“Set.”

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Stagehand work often involves moving heavy or awkward equipment. That does not mean you should lift everything by yourself.

One of the fastest ways to get hurt is to act stronger than you are, rush a lift, or fail to communicate during a team carry.

Before lifting, look at the item. Is it heavy? Awkward? Sharp? Fragile? Top-heavy? Does it have handles? Is the path clear? Are there stairs, ramps, cables, doors, or people nearby?

If the item seems too heavy or awkward, ask for help.

During a team carry, communication matters. Everyone should know when the lift starts, where the item is going, when the team is turning, and when the item is being set down.

Do not surprise your partner by changing direction suddenly. Do not let go without warning. Do not try to be a hero. Controlled movement is better than fast movement.

#### ***Team Carry Basics***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Step | What to Do |
| Check the item | Look for weight, handles, sharp edges, balance, and fragility |
| Check the path | Look for cables, ramps, stairs, people, doors, and obstacles |
| Communicate | Agree on direction and callouts |
| Lift together | Move on the same count |
| Walk together | Match pace and communicate turns |
| Set together | Lower on command and keep hands clear |

#### ***Examples***

#### ***Good Example***

Two stagehands are moving a deck piece. One says, “Ready? Lift on three. One, two, three.” They move together, call out a turn, and set it down together.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Bad Example***

One stagehand lifts their side first, starts walking, and turns without warning the other person.

That can strain the other person, drop the item, or injure someone.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Do not lift anything that feels too heavy, unstable, awkward, or unsafe. Ask for help, use assigned handling equipment, or get direction from a lead.

Safe lifting is not about proving strength. It is about control, communication, and teamwork.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Lifting and carrying are common parts of stagehand work, but they should be done with communication and control. Ask for help when needed and never sacrifice safety to look strong.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Would I rather ask for help or risk dropping gear and hurting someone?

Question: What should a stagehand do before a team carry?

**Best Answer:**  
 Check the item, check the path, communicate the plan, and lift together on a clear count.

# **Module 8, Lesson 8: Pinch Points, Crush Points, and Stored Energy**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to three important hazard concepts: pinch points, crush points, and stored energy.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to define pinch points, crush points, and stored energy in simple terms and identify common situations where these hazards may appear.

A pinch point is a place where a body part can get caught between two objects.

A crush point is a place where a person or body part can be trapped or crushed by moving, falling, rolling, or shifting equipment.

Stored energy is energy held inside an object or system that can release suddenly. This may include tension, compression, gravity, pressure, springs, suspended loads, rolling cases, stretched cables, loaded ramps, or equipment under strain.

These hazards are common around:

Road cases

Carts

Ramps

Dock plates

Doors

Decks

Truss

Motors

Cable pulls

Ratchet straps

Stacked gear

Barricade

Lifts and moving equipment

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Some jobsite hazards are obvious. Others are hidden in movement.

A pinch point happens when your hand, finger, foot, or other body part can get caught between two objects. For example, your fingers could get caught between a road case and a wall, between two cases, in a case lid, near a caster, or between deck pieces.

A crush point is more serious. This happens when a person can be trapped or crushed by moving equipment. For example, standing behind a rolling case on a ramp can create a crush hazard. Standing between a cart and a wall can create a crush hazard. Standing under overhead work creates a serious hazard.

Stored energy means something has force waiting to be released. A stretched cable, loaded strap, leaning stack of cases, suspended load, compressed spring, or heavy object on an incline may move suddenly when released.

The best way to protect yourself is to keep your body out of the line of fire. Do not put your hands where you cannot see them. Do not stand between moving objects. Do not stand downhill from a rolling case. Do not stand under suspended loads. Do not pull, release, loosen, or disconnect anything unless you understand what energy may be involved and have been directed by the appropriate lead.

#### ***Hazard Examples***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Hazard Type | Example | Safer Habit |
| Pinch point | Fingers between two road cases | Use handles and keep hands clear |
| Pinch point | Case lid closing | Keep fingers out of hinges and edges |
| Crush point | Standing between cart and wall | Stay out of the travel path |
| Crush point | Standing behind a case on a ramp | Stay clear of downhill movement |
| Stored energy | Tensioned strap | Do not release without direction |
| Stored energy | Suspended load | Stay clear and never stand underneath |

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Never place yourself between moving gear and a fixed object. Never stand under suspended loads. Never release straps, cables, hardware, or equipment under tension unless trained and assigned.

If something can move, shift, roll, fall, snap, swing, or release suddenly, it may create a hazard.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Pinch points, crush points, and stored energy are common in live event work. A safe stagehand keeps their body out of danger zones and asks before releasing, moving, or adjusting anything under tension or load.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Where could my hands or feet get caught during a case move?

Question: What is a pinch point?

**Best Answer:**  
 A pinch point is a place where a body part can get caught between two objects, such as between road cases, lids, doors, carts, or equipment.

# **Module 8, Lesson 9: Keeping Gear Organized**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson explains why gear organization matters during load-in, show setup, and load-out.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain how gear organization supports safety, speed, department workflow, and professional crew behavior.

Gear organization is not just about neatness. It affects safety, efficiency, and communication.

Disorganized gear can:

Block exits.

Create trip hazards.

Slow down departments.

Cause cases to go to the wrong place.

Lead to lost tools or parts.

Damage equipment.

Delay truck loading.

Make load-out more chaotic.

Beginners can help by following staging directions, reading labels, keeping paths clear, checking back in, and avoiding random moves.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

One of the easiest ways for a new stagehand to be helpful is to keep gear organized.

On a production site, every case, cart, cable, tool, and piece of equipment usually has a reason for being where it is. Gear may be sorted by department, truck-pack order, stage location, or setup sequence.

If you move something randomly, you may think you are helping, but you may actually be creating more work.

For example, a case sitting near stage left may be exactly where lighting needs it. A stack of cable may be waiting for audio. A cart may be staged for load-out. A lid may be placed somewhere because the department needs to repack the case later.

Organization also affects safety. Gear left in walkways can create trip hazards. Cases blocking emergency exits or fire lanes can create serious problems. Loose tape, trash, and hardware can cause injuries or damage.

A good beginner follows the organization plan already in place. Ask where things go. Keep labels visible. Keep pathways clear. Do not mix departments. Do not hide tools or parts. Do not throw away items unless you know they are trash.

#### ***Gear Organization Habits***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Good Habit | Why It Matters |
| Read labels before moving cases | Helps gear get to the right department |
| Keep pathways clear | Reduces trip hazards and improves workflow |
| Do not mix departments | Prevents confusion and delays |
| Ask where items should be staged | Keeps the job organized |
| Keep case lids with the correct case | Helps departments repack properly |
| Do not throw away unknown items | Prevents loss of parts, hardware, or labels |
| Check back in when finished | Helps leads keep track of progress |

#### ***Examples***

#### ***Good Example***

A lead says, “Stage all audio cases along this wall with labels facing out.”

The stagehand places the cases neatly, keeps the path clear, and makes sure labels can be read.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Bad Example***

A stagehand moves cases to random empty spaces because they want the floor to look cleaner.

That may separate departments from their gear and slow down the setup.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Never block emergency exits, fire lanes, aisles, stairs, dock doors, electrical panels, first aid stations, or access routes with gear.

Organized gear helps the crew work faster, safer, and with fewer mistakes.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Keeping gear organized is one of the simplest ways to be useful. A good stagehand follows staging directions, keeps paths clear, respects department systems, and avoids moving gear without permission.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Could moving gear without asking make someone else’s job harder?

Question: Why should case labels face outward when staging gear?

**Best Answer:**  
 So leads and department crews can quickly identify what the case is and where it belongs.

# Module 8 Quiz

# **Module 8 Quiz: Tools, Gear, and Handling Basics**

## **Quiz Instructions**

Choose the best answer for each question. This quiz checks your understanding of common stagehand tools, road cases, cables, tape, gear recognition, lifting basics, pinch points, crush points, stored energy, and gear organization.

**Recommended passing score:** 80%  
 Total questions: 20

# **Multiple Choice**

## **1. What is the main purpose of Module 8?**

A. To certify learners as riggers  
 B. To train learners to repair production equipment  
 C. To help beginners recognize common tools and gear and understand safe handling awareness  
 D. To teach learners how to operate motors and hoists

**Correct Answer:** C

## **2. What should a new stagehand do before using a tool on production gear?**

A. Use it if they brought the tool from home  
 B. Ask another beginner if it looks safe  
 C. Make sure they are assigned, trained, and authorized to use it  
 D. Try it first and ask questions later

**Correct Answer:** C

## **3. Which item is commonly useful for seeing in dark backstage areas or during load-out?**

A. Flashlight or headlamp  
 B. Shackle  
 C. Chain motor  
 D. Stage deck

**Correct Answer:** A

## **4. Why should beginners avoid tightening or loosening hardware without direction?**

A. Hardware is never used in live events  
 B. It may belong to a department or be part of a structure or system requiring trained personnel  
 C. It is always decorative  
 D. It is faster to leave all hardware loose

**Correct Answer:** B

## **5. What is a road case used for?**

A. Selling tickets  
 B. Protecting and transporting production equipment  
 C. Seating audience members  
 D. Replacing stage stairs

**Correct Answer:** B

## **6. Which part of a road case helps identify where it belongs or what it contains?**

A. Label  
 B. Caster  
 C. Hinge  
 D. Foam padding

**Correct Answer:** A

## **7. What should a new stagehand do before opening a road case?**

A. Open it to see what is inside  
 B. Ask for permission or direction from the appropriate lead or department  
 C. Open only the cases that look heavy  
 D. Remove the lid and place it wherever there is space

**Correct Answer:** B

## **8. Why should a beginner avoid pulling a cable if they do not know what it is connected to?**

A. It may still be connected, in use, energized, fragile, or part of a planned cable path  
 B. Cables are never important  
 C. Pulling cable is always safe  
 D. Cables should always be moved as fast as possible

## **9. What should a stagehand do if they see a damaged cable?**

A. Repair it with electrical tape  
 B. Cut out the damaged section  
 C. Report it to the appropriate lead or department

## **10. What is gaff tape commonly used for?**

A. Permanent electrical repair  
 B. Temporary securing, marking, or cable management when assigned  
 C. Rigging overhead loads  
 D. Replacing broken hardware

**Correct Answer:** B

## **11. Why should beginners avoid using electrical tape to repair damaged cable?**

A. Electrical tape is only decorative  
 B. Damaged cable should be handled by trained and authorized personnel  
 C. Electrical tape is never used anywhere  
 D. Damaged cable does not matter

**Correct Answer:** B

## **12. What is spike tape commonly used for?**

A. Marking positions or placements  
 B. Repairing motors  
 C. Connecting truss  
 D. Replacing road case latches

**Correct Answer:** A

## **13. What is truss commonly used for?**

A. Food service  
 B. Supporting lighting, video, scenic, or rigging systems  
 C. Audience seating  
 D. Cleaning floors

**Correct Answer:** B

## **14. What should a beginner do around motors, hoists, shackles, and rigging hardware?**

A. Touch them only if they look unused  
 B. Move them out of the way without asking  
 C. Stay clear unless trained, assigned, and authorized  
 D. Practice connecting them during breaks

**Correct Answer:** C

## **15. What should a stagehand check before a team carry?**

A. The item, the path, the plan, and the communication callouts  
 B. Only whether the item looks expensive  
 C. Only whether the lead is watching  
 D. Nothing, as long as there are two people

**Correct Answer:** A

## **16. Which callout would be appropriate during a team carry?**

A. “Lift on three.”  
 B. “Guess where I’m going.”  
 C. “Drop it whenever.”  
 D. “Run.”

**Correct Answer:** A

## **17. What is a pinch point?**

A. A place where food is served  
 B. A location where a body part can get caught between two objects  
 C. A type of tape mark  
 D. A storage area for cables

**Correct Answer:** B

## **18. What is a crush point?**

A. A place where a person or body part can be trapped or crushed by moving or shifting equipment  
 B. A location where cases are painted  
 C. A soft section of carpet  
 D. A cable label

**Correct Answer:** A

## **19. What is stored energy?**

A. Food stored for long calls  
 B. Energy held in an object or system that can release suddenly

## **20. Why is gear organization important?**

A. It only makes the room look nicer  
 B. It supports safety, efficiency, department workflow, and fewer mistakes

# **Scenario Questions**

These can be used as written-response questions, discussion prompts, or practical review questions.

## **Scenario 1**

You see a cable taped to the floor. It looks like it might be in the way, but no one has told you to move it.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not cut, pull, unplug, or move the cable without direction. Ask your lead or the appropriate department before touching it.

## **Scenario 2**

A road case is sitting near stage left with its label facing the wall. You are asked to stage cases neatly along the wall.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Move the case only as directed, keep the path clear, and place it with the label facing outward so the department can identify it easily.

## **Scenario 3**

You are asked to help carry a heavy deck piece with another stagehand. You are not sure where it is going.

**Question:** What should you do before lifting?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask where it is going, check the path, communicate with your partner, agree on callouts, and lift together on a clear count.

## **Scenario 4**

You see a shackle and motor sitting near the stage. They look like they need to be moved.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not touch, move, connect, disconnect, or adjust rigging-related equipment unless trained, assigned, and authorized. Ask a lead or stay clear.

## **Scenario 5**

During load-out, someone tells you to hurry and push a cart down a ramp. The ramp is crowded and you are not sure you can control the cart.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stop and communicate. Do not move the cart until the path is clear and you have enough help to control it safely. Speed should never replace safety.
