# Module 9

# **Module 9: Load-Out Fundamentals**

## **Module Overview**

Load-out is the process of striking, packing, organizing, and removing production equipment after an event. For many new stagehands, load-out will be one of the first types of calls they work.

Load-out can feel faster, louder, darker, and more chaotic than load-in. The show is over, the crew is tired, and the production needs to leave the venue. That combination can create risk if people stop communicating, start rushing, or begin moving gear without direction.

This module teaches new stagehands how to approach load-out with control, awareness, communication, and safety. The goal is not to teach beginners how to lead a strike, pack a truck, disconnect power, operate equipment, or perform specialized department work. Those tasks require employer direction, site-specific training, and proper authorization.

The goal is to help learners understand the rhythm of load-out so they can support the crew without creating hazards or confusion.

Load-out is not just “tear it down fast.” It is a sequenced process. Gear comes down in a planned order. Cases need to match the correct equipment. Cables must be struck carefully. Pathways must stay clear. Trucks must be loaded under direction.

A good new stagehand understands that finishing strong matters. The last part of a call is often when people are most tired, but it is also when focus matters most.

## **Learning Objectives**

By the end of this module, learners should be able to:

Explain how load-out is different from load-in.

Understand why strike order matters.

Recognize the risks that come with fatigue, speed, and poor communication.

Describe beginner-safe habits for case packing, cable strike, truck pack awareness, cleanup, and final walkthroughs.

Identify unsafe load-out choices and explain safer alternatives.

Understand how to finish a call professionally.

# Lesson 9.1

# **Module 9, Lesson 1: What Makes Load-Out Different From Load-In**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to the unique pace and pressure of load-out. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that load-out may feel faster than load-in, but it still requires structure, communication, and safety.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain how load-out is different from load-in and why safety awareness becomes even more important after the event ends.

Load-in is about building the show.

Load-out is about striking the show and getting the gear out.

Load-out often happens late at night or after a long day.

People may be tired, hungry, sore, or mentally checked out.

The environment may be darker, louder, and more crowded.

Equipment may be moving in multiple directions at once.

Speed matters, but safety still comes first.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Load-out happens after the event is over. The audience may be gone, the show may be finished, and the crew may be ready to leave, but the work is not done.

In many ways, load-out can be more challenging than load-in.

During load-in, the crew is building the show. Gear is coming off trucks, departments are setting up, and there is usually a planned build process.

During load-out, everything has to come down, get packed, move back to the dock, and load into trucks in the correct order.

The pace can feel much faster. People are tired. The venue may be darker. Multiple departments may be striking gear at the same time. Cases, carts, cables, trash, tools, and people may all be moving through the same space.

This is why load-out requires focus.

A new stagehand may feel pressure to move fast, but rushing without awareness creates problems. Running with cases, pulling cable without checking the other end, blocking doors, standing behind rolling cases on ramps, or moving gear without direction can create serious hazards.

The best load-out workers are not reckless. They are controlled. They listen, communicate, watch their surroundings, and move with purpose.

Your job during load-out is to help the crew finish safely and efficiently. That means following the strike order, staying with your assigned task, checking in when finished, and asking questions before touching gear you are unsure about.

#### ***Load-In vs. Load-Out***

Load-in brings gear into the venue and builds the show.

Load-out strikes gear, packs it, and removes it from the venue.

Load-in often starts with fresh crews.

Load-out often happens when crews are tired.

Load-in may feel organized around building departments.

Load-out may feel compressed because multiple departments are trying to clear at once.

Load-in requires patience and direction.

Load-out requires patience, direction, and extra awareness.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Move with purpose during load-in, but confirm where gear goes before touching, opening, or relocating it.

Do not treat load-out like a race. Moving quickly is useful only when it is controlled, directed, and safe. If you are unsure whether something should move, where it should go, or whether it is safe to touch, stop and ask.

Load-out is faster, but it is not random. It is a controlled process that depends on communication, sequencing, and awareness.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Load-out can feel fast and chaotic, but new stagehands should stay controlled, follow direction, communicate clearly, and remember that safety still comes before speed.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why might a tired crew be more likely to make mistakes during load-out?

Question: What is one major difference between load-in and load-out?

**Best Answer:**

Load-in is the process of bringing gear into the venue and building the show. Load-out is the process of striking, packing, organizing, and removing gear after the event.

# **Module 9, Lesson 2: Strike Order: Why Everything Comes Down in a Sequence**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners that load-out follows a sequence. The goal is to prevent beginners from randomly striking gear, moving cases, or taking things apart before the correct department has cleared them.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why strike order matters and why new stagehands should wait for direction before moving or breaking down equipment.

Strike means taking down, packing, or clearing show equipment.

Strike order is the planned sequence for what comes down first, next, and last.

Some gear cannot move until another department is finished.

Some cables may still be active, connected, or in use.

Some equipment may be supporting another system or waiting for a department check.

Moving the wrong item too early can slow down the crew or create hazards.

Beginners should never freelance during strike.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

During load-out, everything does not come down at the same time.

There is a strike order.

Strike order is the sequence the crew follows to take down the show safely and efficiently. It tells the crew what can be removed now, what needs to wait, and what must stay in place until another department is finished.

For example, some lighting gear may not be ready to move until power is handled by qualified or authorized people. Some audio lines may still be connected. Some video gear may need to be packed in a specific case. Some scenic pieces may need to come apart in a certain order. Some equipment may need to stay in place until another piece of gear is clear.

This is why a new stagehand should not walk around looking for random things to strike.

You may think you are helping by grabbing a cable, removing tape, opening a case, or moving a piece of gear. But if that item has not been cleared, you could create confusion, damage gear, or create a safety issue.

A good beginner waits for direction.

If a lead says, “Strike these cases and take them to the dock,” then that is your task. If a department says, “Leave that cable until audio clears it,” then leave it. If you are unsure, ask before touching.

Strike order helps the whole crew work together. It keeps departments from tripping over each other, protects gear, and helps the truck pack happen correctly.

#### ***Examples of Strike Order Awareness***

Do not pull cable just because it is on the floor.

Do not close or move a case unless you know it is packed and cleared.

Do not move department gear unless that department or your lead tells you to.

Do not remove tape from a cable path if the cable still needs to stay in place.

Do not take apart staging, scenic items, or structures unless assigned and trained.

Do not move anything from under overhead work unless the area has been cleared.

Do not touch power, motors, rigging equipment, or specialized systems unless trained, authorized, and directed.

#### ***Beginner-Friendly Questions to Ask***

“Has this been cleared to strike?”

“Do you want this moved now or left in place?”

“Where do you want this case staged?”

“Is this cable ready to be pulled?”

“Who should I check with before moving this?”

“Is this something I should leave for the department?”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: During load-out, slow down enough to keep control, protect the sequence, and ask before moving gear you do not understand.

Never assume something is safe to remove because it looks unused. During load-out, equipment may still be connected, energized, supporting another system, or waiting for a specific department.

Strike order keeps load-out controlled. New stagehands should follow the sequence, not guess the sequence.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Load-out works because gear comes down in a planned order. A new stagehand should never freelance during strike. Follow direction, ask before touching, and respect the sequence.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What could go wrong if you remove a cable, case, or stage item before it has been cleared?

Question: What should a new stagehand do before striking gear they are unsure about?

**Best Answer:**

Ask a lead or the appropriate department whether the gear has been cleared and where it should go.

# **Module 9, Lesson 3: Staying Alert When Everyone Is Tired**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners that fatigue is one of the biggest load-out risks. The goal is to help beginners recognize that tired workers need to slow down mentally, communicate more clearly, and avoid careless decisions.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe how fatigue affects load-out safety and identify practical ways to stay alert during the final part of a call.

Load-out often happens after a long work period.

Fatigue can reduce focus, reaction time, patience, and judgment.

Tired workers may rush, stop communicating, or miss hazards.

New stagehands should avoid trying to “prove themselves” by ignoring fatigue.

Hydration, food, approved breaks, and communication help maintain awareness.

If something feels unsafe, unclear, or beyond your training, stop and ask.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Load-out often happens when people are tired.

You may have already worked load-in. You may have been on your feet for hours. You may be working late at night. You may be hungry, sore, dehydrated, or mentally drained.

That is when mistakes happen.

Fatigue can make simple tasks harder. You may miss a cable under your feet. You may stop checking where your hands are. You may forget to look behind you before moving a case. You may react slower when someone calls out. You may get frustrated more easily.

A professional stagehand does not ignore fatigue. A professional stagehand manages it.

That starts with awareness. If you feel yourself rushing, zoning out, or getting careless, reset. Take a breath. Look around. Make sure you know where your lead is, where the traffic path is, and what task you are actually assigned to do.

Stay hydrated when allowed. Eat when you have an appropriate break. Use short moments to reset your focus. Keep your phone away unless you are on an approved break or need it for work communication.

Most importantly, do not let fatigue make you pretend. If you did not hear an instruction, ask again. If you do not know where something goes, ask. If a case feels too heavy or awkward, ask for help. If you see a hazard, speak up.

Finishing strong means staying alert until the call is actually over.

#### ***Signs You May Be Losing Focus***

You stop listening to instructions.

You start moving without checking your surroundings.

You feel irritated when someone corrects you.

You forget where you are taking gear.

You stop noticing cables, ramps, cases, or moving equipment.

You try to rush just to be done.

You start guessing instead of asking.

#### ***Safer Load-Out Habits***

Keep your eyes moving.

Listen for callouts.

Use clear responses like “copy” or “heard.”

Ask for help before a lift, push, or carry becomes unsafe.

Report hazards instead of stepping over them repeatedly.

Stay out of traffic paths unless assigned.

Check back in with your lead when a task is complete.

Ask for clarification when instructions are unclear.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Fatigue is not a reason to hide mistakes or push through unsafe work. If you are unsure, overwhelmed, or believe something is unsafe, communicate with your lead.

Load-out is when fatigue and speed can combine. Staying alert is part of finishing the job professionally.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A good stagehand does not stop paying attention just because the event is over. Load-out requires focus, communication, and self-awareness until the job is complete.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

How might your decision-making change when you are tired, and what can you do to prevent that from becoming unsafe?

Question: Why is fatigue a major concern during load-out?

**Best Answer:**

Because tired workers may have reduced focus, slower reactions, poorer judgment, and a higher chance of missing hazards or making unsafe choices.

# **Module 9, Lesson 4: Case Packing and Label Matching**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners the importance of packing gear into the correct cases and respecting department systems. The goal is to prevent beginners from throwing random gear into random cases or closing cases before they are cleared.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why case packing and label matching matter during load-out.

Road cases are usually assigned to specific gear.

Labels, colors, markings, and department tags help identify where items belong.

Incorrect packing can damage gear or delay the next show.

Some cases should not be closed until checked by the department.

Beginners should not force gear into a case.

If the case, lid, label, or contents do not match, ask.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

During load-out, cases are everywhere.

Some cases are for audio. Some are for lighting. Some are for video. Some are for backline, rigging, wardrobe, production, scenic, or tools. Many cases look similar from a distance, but they may hold very specific gear.

This is why label matching matters.

A road case is not just a box. It is part of the production’s organization system. The right item needs to go in the right case, in the right orientation, often with the right foam, divider, lid, hardware, or packing order.

If the wrong item goes into the wrong case, the crew may not find it at the next show. If gear is packed poorly, it may get damaged during travel. If a case is closed before the department checks it, important parts may be missing.

As a new stagehand, your job is not to guess how everything packs. Your job is to support the system that is already in place.

Look for labels. Match case numbers when directed. Keep lids with the correct cases. Do not force gear into a case. Do not toss small parts into random places. Do not close a case unless you have been told it is ready to close.

If something does not fit easily, stop. It may be the wrong case. It may need to be packed a specific way. It may need padding, a divider, or a department check.

Good case packing protects the gear, the crew, and the next job.

#### ***Case Packing Basics for Beginners***

Read the label before moving or packing.

Keep lids with the correct cases.

Do not mix department gear.

Do not force equipment into a case.

Do not stand on gear, lids, or case edges.

Do not close a case until it is cleared.

Ask where small parts, clamps, cables, or accessories go.

Tell a lead if something appears damaged or missing.

Keep fingers clear of lids, latches, hinges, and pinch points.

#### ***Example Situation***

You are handed a loose piece of gear and told, “Put this away.”

Better response:

“Copy. Which case does it go in?”

This is better than guessing because the same type of gear may have multiple cases or a specific packing order.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: During load-out, slow down enough to keep control, protect the sequence, and ask before moving gear you do not understand.

Some equipment may be heavy, fragile, sharp, hot, energized, or awkward to handle. Do not pack or move equipment unless assigned. Ask for help when needed.

Correct packing protects the gear and keeps the production organized. Guessing during case packing creates problems.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

During load-out, cases are part of the production’s system. Match labels, follow direction, protect gear, and ask before closing or packing anything you are unsure about.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why might putting an item in the wrong case create problems for the next show?

Question: What should you do if gear does not seem to fit into the case you were given?

**Best Answer:**

Stop and ask. Do not force it. It may be the wrong case or it may need to be packed in a specific way.

# **Module 9, Lesson 5: Cable Strike and Coil Discipline**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners how to think safely about cable strike without training them to handle specialized electrical, audio, video, data, motor, or power systems independently. The goal is to prevent unsafe pulling, dragging, unplugging, or coiling.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe safe beginner behavior around cable strike and explain why cable should only be moved under direction.

Cable strike means removing, gathering, coiling, or staging cable after it has been cleared.

Not every cable is ready to move.

Some cables may still be connected, active, energized, or needed.

Never pull cable without checking direction.

Never unplug power, feeder, motor cable, or department systems unless trained, authorized, and directed.

Coiling discipline means keeping cables organized and protected.

Communication matters before pulling cable across a room, stage, dock, or backstage area.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Cable strike is one of the most common load-out tasks, but that does not mean every cable is safe for every beginner to handle.

During load-out, you may see cable across the stage, backstage, on the floor, near audio world, near lighting equipment, near video equipment, or running to power sources. Some cable may be ready to strike. Some may still be connected. Some may be part of systems that only trained department workers should handle.

Never assume a cable is safe to pull because the show is over.

Before moving cable, make sure it has been cleared by the correct department or your lead. If you do not know what the cable is, ask. If you do not know where the other end goes, ask. If a cable feels stuck, stop. Do not yank it.

Pulling cable without checking can damage equipment, pull gear off a table, trip another worker, or create problems for a department that is still working.

Coil discipline also matters. Cable should not be thrown into random piles unless directed. Different departments may coil, wrap, label, or stage cable in specific ways. Some cables need over-under coiling. Some may be bundled. Some may go straight into cases or trunks. Some should be left for department workers.

Your job is to follow the system being used on that call.

#### ***Useful Beginner Phrases***

“Is this cable cleared to pull?”

“Do you want this coiled or staged?”

“Where does this cable go?”

“This cable feels stuck. Can someone check the other end?”

“I have not been trained on this cable. Should I leave it for the department?”

“Is this power, audio, video, data, or something else?”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: During load-out, slow down enough to keep control, protect the sequence, and ask before moving gear you do not understand.

#### ***Cable Strike Safety Habits***

Ask before pulling cable.

Check both ends only if directed and safe to do so.

Do not yank cable that feels stuck.

Do not wrap cable around your hand, arm, neck, or body.

Watch for people walking across the cable path.

Keep coils out of walkways when possible.

Do not unplug or handle electrical systems unless trained, authorized, and directed.

Keep cable away from wheels, ramps, doors, and pinch points when possible.

Follow department instructions for how cable should be coiled or staged.

Report damaged cable, exposed conductors, broken connectors, heat, smoke, or unusual smells to a lead immediately.

Power cable, feeder cable, motor cable, and other specialized systems may require qualified or authorized personnel. Do not disconnect, move, or coil specialized cable unless you have been trained, authorized, and directed.

Cable strike is not just pulling lines off the floor. It requires clearance, communication, and organization.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Cable strike requires discipline. Do not pull, unplug, drag, or coil cable randomly. Wait for clearance, follow direction, and ask when unsure.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What could happen if you pull a cable without knowing what it is connected to?

Question: What should you do if you are not sure whether a cable is ready to be pulled?

**Best Answer:**

Stop and ask your lead or the appropriate department before touching or pulling it.

# **Module 9, Lesson 6: Truck Pack Awareness**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to the importance of truck pack awareness. The goal is to help beginners support truck loading safely without pretending to train them as truck loaders.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why the truck pack is organized and why new stagehands must follow truck loader direction.

Truck loading is not random.

Gear usually has a planned order and position.

Truck loaders or leads control what goes into the trailer and when.

The dock, ramp, liftgate, and trailer area can be high-risk zones.

Beginners should not enter trailers, stack gear, or load cases unless assigned.

Never stand behind a rolling case on a ramp.

Keep dock doors, emergency exits, fire lanes, and traffic paths clear.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

At load-out, everything eventually needs to make it back to the truck.

But the truck pack is not just a pile of gear.

Cases, carts, truss, decks, cable trunks, backline, motors, barricade, and other equipment may all need to load in a specific order. Some gear goes in first because it comes out last at the next venue. Some gear needs to be stacked a certain way. Some gear cannot have weight on top of it. Some items need to stay together by department.

This is why truck loaders matter.

Truck loaders and truck leads control how gear moves into the trailer. They may tell you which case to bring next, where to stage it, when to hold, or when to stop sending gear to the dock.

As a new stagehand, do not assume that every case should go straight into the truck. Sometimes cases need to be staged near the dock. Sometimes they need to wait for a department check. Sometimes the trailer is not ready for that item yet.

The dock area can also be dangerous. There may be ramps, dock plates, liftgates, forklifts, pallet jacks, moving cases, low light, tight spacing, and workers crossing paths.

Stay alert. Keep your hands and feet clear of pinch points. Do not stand behind rolling cases on ramps. Do not block dock doors. Do not climb onto stacks of gear. Do not enter trailers unless assigned.

Truck pack awareness means respecting the people controlling the load and staying out of danger zones unless you have a clear task.

#### ***Beginner Truck Pack Rules***

Follow the truck lead or your assigned lead.

Do not send random gear to the truck.

Do not enter the trailer unless assigned.

Do not climb on stacked gear.

Do not stand behind rolling cases on ramps.

Keep hands away from pinch points.

Keep dock doors and traffic paths clear.

Watch for forklifts, pallet jacks, carts, and pushers.

Stage cases where directed.

Tell someone if a case brake, caster, handle, or latch appears damaged.

Do not ride on cases, carts, pallet jacks, liftgates, or moving equipment.

#### ***Example Situation***

You push a case to the dock, but the truck loader says, “Hold that one.”

Best action:

Stop where directed, keep the path clear, and wait for the next instruction. Do not argue or try to push it into the trailer anyway.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: During load-out, slow down enough to keep control, protect the sequence, and ask before moving gear you do not understand.

Truck areas can change quickly. A safe place to stand one minute may become a traffic path the next. Keep your head up and stay aware of moving equipment.

Truck loading is planned. New stagehands should support the plan, not create their own.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Truck pack awareness means understanding that loading is organized, controlled, and safety-sensitive. Follow direction, stay clear of hazards, and never treat the truck like a random storage space.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why would loading the wrong case at the wrong time slow down the entire crew?

Question: Who should control what goes into the truck and when?

**Best Answer:**

The truck lead, truck loaders, or assigned crew leaders should control the truck pack. New stagehands should follow their direction.

# **Module 9, Lesson 7: Trash, Tape, Sweep, and Final Walkthroughs**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners that cleanup is part of professional load-out. The goal is to help beginners understand that trash, tape, loose hardware, blocked paths, and messy work areas can create hazards and reflect poorly on the crew.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why cleanup and final walkthroughs matter during load-out.

Load-out is not finished when the big gear is gone.

Trash, tape, zip ties, loose hardware, and debris still need attention.

Poor housekeeping can create slip, trip, blocked-path, and fire hazards.

Emergency exits, aisles, ramps, docks, and fire lanes must stay clear.

Final walkthroughs help catch forgotten gear and unsafe conditions.

Leaving a clean venue protects the crew’s reputation.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

At the end of load-out, it can be tempting to think the job is done once the cases are on the truck.

But cleanup matters.

After an event, the venue may have tape on the floor, trash under the stage, zip ties, broken pieces, loose hardware, water bottles, cable scraps, spike marks, packing material, or other debris. If those things are ignored, they can become hazards or create extra work for someone else.

A clean work area is part of safety. Trash and debris can cause slips and trips. Tape left behind may damage floors or create cleanup problems. Loose items can get lost, damaged, or run over by cases. Blocked paths can interfere with workers, venue staff, emergency exits, or equipment movement.

Final walkthroughs help the crew make sure the space is clear. Depending on the venue and event, a lead may check the stage, backstage, dock, dressing rooms, production areas, FOH, barricade areas, hallways, or other work zones.

As a new stagehand, you may be asked to pull tape, sweep, collect trash, remove water bottles, gather loose hardware, or help clear pathways. Do not treat those tasks as unimportant. They are part of finishing professionally.

Crews remember people who help finish cleanly instead of disappearing when the heavy gear is gone.

#### ***Cleanup Tasks a Beginner May Be Assigned***

Pull tape when directed.

Pick up trash and water bottles.

Sweep assigned areas.

Collect loose hardware and ask where it belongs.

Remove zip ties or packing scraps.

Clear walkways and traffic paths.

Check under stage areas if directed and safe.

Help restore the venue according to lead direction.

Report spills, broken items, sharp objects, or unknown materials.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***What Not to Do***

Do not remove tape that is still holding active cable unless cleared.

Do not throw away hardware, labels, tools, or small gear.

Do not block exits or dock doors with trash bags or cases.

Do not leave debris in walkways.

Do not walk away from cleanup because it feels less important.

Do not handle unknown liquids, sharp objects, bodily fluids, chemicals, or damaged electrical items blindly.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

If you find unknown items, sharp objects, broken gear, electrical items, chemicals, bodily fluids, or anything unsafe, do not handle it blindly. Report it to a lead.

A professional load-out includes cleanup. The job is not done until the work area is safe, clear, and released by the lead.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Cleanup is not extra. It is part of the job. A clean final walkthrough helps protect people, gear, the venue, and the crew’s reputation.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Why might cleanup affect whether a venue or crew wants to work with you again?

Question: Why are trash, tape, and debris important during load-out?

**Best Answer:**

Because they can create slip, trip, blocked-path, fire, cleanup, or lost-gear hazards. Cleaning them up helps finish the call safely and professionally.

# **Module 9, Lesson 8: Avoiding “Hurry Up and Get Hurt” Mistakes**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners how rushing creates injuries and mistakes during load-out. The goal is to help beginners recognize unsafe shortcuts and choose controlled movement instead.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common “hurry up and get hurt” mistakes and explain safer choices.

Load-out can create pressure to move fast.

Speed without control causes injuries, gear damage, and confusion.

Common mistakes include running with cases, yanking cable, lifting alone, blocking paths, ignoring pinch points, and skipping communication.

A good stagehand moves with purpose, not panic.

The safest crews work quickly because they are organized, not reckless.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

During load-out, you may hear people say things like “Let’s go,” “Move,” “Clear this,” or “We need this truck packed.”

That pressure is real. Load-out often has deadlines. Venues may need to close. Trucks may need to leave. The next crew may be waiting. People may want to go home.

But pressure does not cancel safety.

One of the biggest mistakes a new stagehand can make is confusing rushing with working hard.

Running with a case is not professional. Pulling cable without checking the other end is not helpful. Lifting something too heavy or awkward alone does not prove you are strong. Jumping into a task you do not understand does not make you valuable.

Those choices create injuries, damage gear, and slow the crew down.

A better approach is controlled urgency.

Controlled urgency means you move with purpose, but you still look where you are going. You communicate before moving large items. You ask for help when needed. You keep your hands clear of pinch points. You wait for direction around specialized gear. You stop when something feels wrong.

The best crews move fast because they are coordinated. They are not fast because everyone is panicking.

Your goal is to be useful without becoming a hazard.

#### ***Safer Choices***

Walk with control.

Push only when directed and with a clear path.

Use team lifts when needed.

Ask before moving unknown gear.

Check your path before moving cases.

Communicate with nearby workers.

Keep exits and lanes clear.

Ask for clarification instead of guessing.

Slow down around ramps, doors, corners, crowds, wheels, dock plates, and pinch points.

Stay clear of specialized work unless assigned and authorized.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Common Unsafe Load-Out Choices***

Running with cases.

Standing behind a rolling case on a ramp.

Pulling cable without checking the other end.

Lifting awkward gear alone.

Blocking dock doors or emergency exits.

Walking under overhead work.

Ignoring forklift or pallet jack paths.

Closing cases before they are checked.

Throwing gear into random cases.

Pretending to understand instructions.

Touching power, motors, rigging, lifts, or specialized gear without training and authorization.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

If someone pressures you to do something unsafe, unclear, or outside your training, stop and ask for clarification from your lead. Safety is part of the job.

Controlled urgency is better than panic. The goal is to finish efficiently, not recklessly.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Do not “hurry up and get hurt.” Move with purpose, follow direction, communicate clearly, and slow down when the task or environment requires it.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What is the difference between moving with urgency and rushing unsafely?

Question: What should never be sacrificed just because load-out is moving fast?

**Best Answer:**

Safety. Speed should never replace awareness, communication, and safe work practices.

# **Module 9, Lesson 9: How to Finish Strong**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners how to close out a call professionally. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that their final impression matters.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe what it means to finish strong during load-out.

Finishing strong means staying useful until released.

Do not disappear near the end of the call.

Check in when tasks are complete.

Help with cleanup, staging, and final details.

Return borrowed gear or PPE.

Report damage, hazards, or lost items.

Leave professionally and respectfully.

The last impression can affect whether someone gets called back.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

A lot of people start strong. Fewer people finish strong.

During load-out, finishing strong means staying focused and useful until the lead releases you. It does not mean disappearing when the big gear is gone. It does not mean standing around on your phone. It does not mean assuming the call is over because the truck doors are closing.

There may still be work to do.

Cases may need to be staged. Trash may need to be cleared. Tape may need to be pulled. A room may need to be swept. A lead may need help checking for forgotten gear. Departments may need final support. The venue may need to be restored.

A professional new stagehand checks in.

When your task is done, say, “That is complete. What do you need next?” If you are unsure whether the call is finished, ask. If you borrowed PPE, tools, radios, badges, or venue equipment, return them. If you noticed damaged gear, unsafe conditions, or missing items, report them.

How you finish matters because leads remember it.

They remember who stayed alert. They remember who helped clean. They remember who asked what was next. They remember who disappeared. They remember who complained. They remember who worked safely even when tired.

Getting called back is not only about how strong you are. It is about whether the crew can trust you from check-in to release.

#### ***Ways to Finish Strong***

Stay available until released.

Check in when each task is complete.

Help clear trash, tape, and pathways.

Keep a professional attitude even when tired.

Return borrowed items.

Report hazards or damaged gear.

Do not leave without checking out.

Thank your lead or crew when appropriate.

Reflect on what you learned from the call.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***What Not to Do***

Do not disappear.

Do not assume the call is over.

Do not sit on your phone while others are working.

Do not leave trash or personal items behind.

Do not complain loudly in front of clients, venue staff, touring crew, or leads.

Do not ignore final instructions.

Do not leave borrowed PPE, radios, tools, badges, or access items behind.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

The end of the call still requires attention. Injuries and mistakes can happen during the final push because people are tired and mentally checked out.

Finishing strong means staying professional until the work is complete and you have been released.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

The call is not over until you are released. Finish strong by staying useful, safe, professional, and ready to help until the end.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What final impression would make a lead want to call you back?

Question: What should you do when you finish an assigned task during load-out?

**Best Answer:**

Check in with your lead, report that the task is complete, and ask what they need next.

# Practical Activity 10.1

# **Module 9 Practical Activity: Load-Out Safety Scenario**

## **Activity Purpose**

This activity helps learners identify unsafe choices during a realistic load-out scenario.

## **Instructions**

Read the scenario below. Identify the unsafe choices and explain what the stagehand should do instead.

## **Scenario**

The show has ended, and load-out has started. The crew is tired, and everyone is moving quickly.

A new stagehand sees a road case near stage right and decides to move it without asking. They push it quickly toward the dock, even though they are not sure where it belongs.

On the way, they notice a cable across the floor and pull it out of the way without checking what it is connected to.

At the dock, several cases are waiting near the truck. The stagehand leaves the case in front of a dock door because they do not know where else to put it.

A few minutes later, they help push another case down a ramp while standing behind it. The case starts moving faster than expected.

Near the end of the night, the stagehand sees trash, tape, and loose zip ties on the floor, but they leave because they think the main work is done.

## **Unsafe Choices to Identify**

Moving a road case without direction.

Pushing a case quickly without knowing where it belongs.

Pulling cable without checking whether it was cleared.

Blocking a dock door.

Standing behind a rolling case on a ramp.

Leaving trash, tape, and debris behind.

Leaving before being released or before final cleanup is complete.

## **Better Choices**

Ask a lead before moving unknown gear.

Confirm where the case should go.

Do not pull cable unless it has been cleared by the proper department.

Keep dock doors, traffic paths, emergency exits, and fire lanes clear.

Avoid standing behind rolling cases on ramps.

Help clean trash, tape, and debris when assigned.

Report unknown hazards instead of handling them blindly.

Check in with the lead before leaving.

## **Practical Activity Answer Summary**

A safe load-out depends on communication, sequencing, and awareness. The stagehand should slow down, ask questions, follow direction, keep pathways clear, avoid unsafe body positioning around rolling gear, and stay until released.

## **Module 9 Key Outcome**

By the end of Module 9, learners should understand that load-out is not just tearing down gear quickly. It is a controlled, sequenced, safety-focused process. A good new stagehand supports load-out by staying alert, following direction, respecting department systems, communicating clearly, and finishing strong.

# Module 9 Quiz

# **Module 9 Quiz: Load-Out Fundamentals**

## **Quiz Instructions**

Choose the best answer for each question. This quiz checks your understanding of load-out safety, strike order, fatigue awareness, case packing, cable strike, truck pack awareness, cleanup, and how to finish a call professionally.

**Recommended passing score:** 80%

**Total questions:** 20

# **Multiple Choice**

## **1. What is load-out?**

A. The process of building the show before the event  
 B. The process of striking, packing, organizing, and removing equipment after the event  
 C. The process of running the actual performance  
 D. The process of checking tickets at the venue

**Correct Answer:** B

## **2. Why can load-out be more hazardous than load-in?**

A. Because there is usually no equipment involved  
 B. Because the crew may be tired, the pace may be faster, and communication may become rushed  
 C. Because only supervisors are allowed to work load-out  
 D. Because load-out never requires lifting or moving gear

**Correct Answer:** B

## **3. What should a new stagehand remember during load-out?**

A. Speed is more important than safety  
 B. If gear looks unused, it is always safe to move  
 C. Load-out is controlled, sequenced, and safety-focused  
 D. Beginners should strike anything they see

**Correct Answer:** C

## **4. What does “strike” mean in live event production?**

A. Taking down, packing, or clearing show equipment  
 B. Taking a meal break  
 C. Starting the performance  
 D. Locking the venue doors

**Correct Answer:** A

## **5. Why does strike order matter?**

A. It makes the job look more complicated than it is  
 B. It helps equipment come down in a safe and organized sequence  
 C. It allows beginners to decide what gets moved first  
 D. It only matters for the audience

**Correct Answer:** B

## **6. What should a new stagehand do before moving gear they are unsure about?**

A. Move it quickly so it is out of the way  
 B. Ask a lead or the appropriate department if it has been cleared  
 C. Hide it backstage  
 D. Put it into the nearest case

**Correct Answer:** B

## **7. Which of the following is an unsafe load-out choice?**

A. Checking in with your lead after finishing a task  
 B. Asking if a cable has been cleared before pulling it  
 C. Running with a road case to save time  
 D. Keeping traffic paths clear

**Correct Answer:** C

## **8. Why is fatigue a major concern during load-out?**

A. Fatigue can reduce focus, reaction time, patience, and judgment  
 B. Fatigue only affects supervisors  
 C. Fatigue makes workers stronger  
 D. Fatigue does not matter after the show is over

**Correct Answer:** A

## **9. What is a sign that you may be losing focus during load-out?**

A. You ask for clarification when you are unsure  
 B. You start guessing instead of listening or asking questions  
 C. You keep your eyes moving and watch your surroundings  
 D. You check in with your lead

**Correct Answer:** B

## **10. What should you do if a case feels too heavy or awkward to move safely?**

A. Move it alone to prove you can handle it  
 B. Ask for help or direction  
 C. Drag it by any loose cable nearby  
 D. Leave without telling anyone

**Correct Answer:** B

## **11. Why is label matching important during case packing?**

A. It makes the cases look cleaner  
 B. It helps make sure gear goes into the correct case and is protected for transport  
 C. It only matters if the case is new  
 D. It allows beginners to pack gear however they want

**Correct Answer:** B

## **12. What should you do if a piece of gear does not fit easily into a case?**

A. Force it until the lid closes  
 B. Put it in a different random case  
 C. Stop and ask for direction  
 D. Leave it on the floor

**Correct Answer:** C

## **13. When should a new stagehand close a road case?**

A. As soon as anything is placed inside  
 B. Only when the case has been checked or they have been told it is ready to close  
 C. Whenever they want to speed up load-out  
 D. Before checking the label

**Correct Answer:** B

## **14. What should you do before pulling a cable during load-out?**

A. Pull hard to see if it moves  
 B. Make sure it has been cleared by the proper department or lead  
 C. Cut the tape and drag it away  
 D. Wrap it around your arm and pull quickly

**Correct Answer:** B

## **15. Which cable-related task should only be done if trained, authorized, and directed?**

A. Asking where a cable should go  
 B. Watching for trip hazards  
 C. Disconnecting power, feeder, motor cable, or specialized systems  
 D. Keeping coils out of walkways

**Correct Answer:** C

## **16. What should you do if a cable feels stuck?**

A. Yank harder  
 B. Pull from a different angle without telling anyone  
 C. Stop and ask someone to check the other end  
 D. Cut it loose

**Correct Answer:** C

## **17. Who should control what goes into the truck and when?**

A. Any person standing near the dock  
 B. The newest stagehand  
 C. The truck lead, truck loaders, or assigned crew leaders  
 D. The audience

**Correct Answer:** C

## **18. Which of the following is unsafe around trucks, ramps, and dock areas?**

A. Keeping traffic paths clear  
 B. Following truck lead direction  
 C. Standing behind a rolling case on a ramp  
 D. Watching for forklifts and pallet jacks

**Correct Answer:** C

## **19. Why are trash, tape, and debris important during load-out?**

A. They do not matter once the event is over  
 B. They can create slip, trip, blocked-path, cleanup, or lost-gear hazards  
 C. They should always be left for venue staff  
 D. They only matter during show call

**Correct Answer:** B

## **20. What does it mean to finish strong?**

A. Leave as soon as the big gear is gone  
 B. Stay useful, safe, professional, and available until released  
 C. Sit on your phone while others clean up  
 D. Avoid checking in with your lead

**Correct Answer:** B

# **Scenario Questions**

These can be used as written-response questions or discussion prompts.

## **Scenario 1**

You are working load-out and see a road case sitting near stage right. No one has told you to move it, but it looks like it might be in the way.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Do not move it without direction. Ask your lead or the appropriate department whether the case has been cleared and where it should go.

## **Scenario 2**

A crew member tells you to pull a cable across the stage, but you do not know what it is connected to.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**

Stop and ask whether the cable has been cleared. Do not pull cable unless you know it is safe and you have been directed by the proper lead or department.

## **Scenario 3**

During load-out, you push a case to the dock. The dock is crowded, so you leave the case in front of a dock door.

**Question:** What is wrong with this choice?

**Best Answer:**

Dock doors, traffic paths, emergency exits, and fire lanes need to stay clear. The stagehand should ask where to stage the case instead of blocking the dock door.

## **Scenario 4**

You are helping move a rolling case down a ramp. You step behind the case to help guide it.

**Question:** Why is this unsafe?

**Best Answer:**

Standing behind a rolling case on a ramp can put you in the path of the case if it gains speed or becomes hard to control. You should follow lead direction and avoid unsafe body positioning around rolling gear.

## **Scenario 5**

Near the end of load-out, the main gear is packed. You see trash, tape, loose zip ties, and small debris on the floor, but you assume the job is done.

**Question:** What should you do instead?

**Best Answer:**

Check in with your lead and help with cleanup if assigned. Load-out is not complete until the work area is safe, clear, and the crew has been released.
