# Module 10

# **Module 10: Getting Hired, Getting Called Back, and Growing**

## **Module Purpose**

This module helps learners understand how to turn beginner stagehand knowledge into real work opportunities. The goal is to explain where stagehand work comes from, how to present limited experience honestly, how to build a strong reputation, and how to choose a smart next step after their first few calls.

This module is not a guarantee of employment. It is a practical guide to help new stagehands act professionally, communicate clearly, continue learning safely, and become the kind of worker crews want to call back.

## **Module Learning Objective**

By the end of this module, the learner should be able to identify common ways stagehands find work, describe how to present themselves professionally as beginners, understand what behaviors lead to repeat calls, and create a simple 30-day development plan.

## **Lessons**

Where Stagehand Work Comes From

Labor Companies, Venues, Unions, Crewing Apps, and Referrals

What to Put on a Beginner Resume

How to Talk About Limited Experience Honestly

**Reliability:** The Skill That Gets You Rebooked

Building a Reputation

Moving From General Stagehand to Department Specialty

**Next Skills to Learn:** OSHA 10, Forklift Awareness, Rigging Awareness, Audio, Lighting, Video, Carpentry

Your First 30-Day Development Plan

# **Module 10, Lesson 1: Where Stagehand Work Comes From**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to the different ways people get stagehand work. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that live event work does not usually come from one single place. It can come from labor companies, venues, unions, production companies, referrals, staffing apps, and repeat calls from people who trust them.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common sources of stagehand work and understand why reputation matters in getting future calls.

Stagehand work can come from several different sources. Some workers are hired through labor companies. Some are called by venues. Some work through unions. Some get work through production companies, crewing apps, staffing agencies, or referrals from people they have worked with before.

For beginners, the first goal is not to become known as an expert. The first goal is to become known as someone who is safe, reliable, teachable, and easy to work with.

Live event work is often relationship-based. A lead, supervisor, or company is more likely to call back someone who shows up prepared, follows direction, communicates clearly, and does not create problems on-site.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Stagehand work can come from a lot of different places.

Some stagehands get work through labor companies that provide crews for concerts, corporate events, festivals, sports events, and venue calls. Some work directly for venues. Some work through unions. Others get calls from production companies, local crew coordinators, crewing apps, staffing platforms, or referrals from other workers.

As a beginner, you do not need to know every hiring path right away. What you need to understand is that live event work depends heavily on trust.

When someone calls you for a job, they are trusting you to show up on time, dress correctly, follow directions, work safely, and represent the crew professionally. If you do those things well, people remember you. If you disappear, show up late, ignore safety, complain constantly, or act like you know more than you do, people remember that too.

Your first few calls are not just about earning a paycheck. They are also about building proof that you can be trusted on a crew.

The work may start with basic tasks like pushing cases, keeping pathways clear, helping with load-in, striking gear during load-out, or cleaning up after the event. Those tasks matter. How you handle simple tasks tells people whether they can trust you with more responsibility later.

The best way to find more stagehand work is to treat every call like it could lead to the next one.

#### ***Common Sources of Stagehand Work***

Labor companies provide crews for concerts, festivals, arenas, conventions, corporate events, and load-in/load-out work.

Venues may hire or schedule stagehands for in-house events, theater calls, convention work, or building-specific production needs.

Unions may represent stagehands in certain cities, venues, theaters, or production environments.

Production companies may bring in local labor for events they are building, operating, or supporting.

Crewing apps and staffing platforms may list event labor opportunities depending on the market.

Referrals happen when another worker, lead, supervisor, or company recommends you because they trust your work.

Repeat calls happen when a company or supervisor chooses to book you again because you performed well.

#### ***Example Beginner Situation***

You work a load-out for a labor company. You show up early, wear the right clothes, follow directions, help keep the dock clear, and check in with your lead when tasks are done.

A week later, the same company needs extra hands for another event. Your name comes up because the lead remembers that you were reliable.

That is how a beginner starts building momentum.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Stagehand work often grows through trust. Every call is a chance to prove that you are safe, reliable, prepared, and worth calling again.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Stagehand work can come from labor companies, venues, unions, production companies, staffing platforms, referrals, and repeat calls. Beginners should focus on building trust through safety, reliability, communication, and professionalism.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If someone watched me work one call, would they feel confident recommending me for another one?

Question: Why is reputation important in stagehand work?

**Best Answer:**  
Because many stagehand opportunities come through repeat calls, referrals, supervisors, labor companies, venues, and crew relationships. A worker who is reliable, safe, and easy to work with is more likely to be called again.

# **Module 10, Lesson 2: Labor Companies, Venues, Unions, Crewing Apps, and Referrals**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson explains the main hiring paths a new stagehand may encounter. The goal is to help learners understand the difference between common work sources without presenting one path as the only correct option.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe several common ways stagehands find work and identify the basic expectations that apply across all of them.

Different markets use different hiring systems. In some places, stagehand work may be heavily union-based. In others, labor companies, venues, staffing agencies, or production companies may provide much of the entry-level work.

No matter how someone gets hired, the basic expectations are similar: be on time, dress correctly, bring required PPE, follow the chain of command, work safely, and communicate clearly.

New stagehands should research their local market and avoid assuming that every city or venue works the same way.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

There is no single path into stagehand work that applies everywhere.

In some cities, unions play a major role in live event and theater labor. In other places, large labor companies provide crews for concerts, arenas, festivals, conventions, and corporate events. Some venues have their own in-house crew or preferred labor providers. Some production companies bring in local workers for specific shows. Some workers also find calls through crewing apps, staffing platforms, or referrals.

A labor company may be one of the easiest entry points for beginners because these companies often need general hands for load-ins, load-outs, barricade, staging support, and basic crew labor.

Venues may be another path. A theater, arena, convention center, hotel, school, church, casino, or event center may use stagehands for specific types of events.

Unions can be an important path in many markets. A union may have its own referral process, application process, permit list, apprenticeship path, or hiring hall structure. If union work is common in your area, it is important to learn the local process directly from the appropriate local organization.

Crewing apps and staffing platforms can also be useful, but they do not replace professionalism. A job booked through an app is still a real call. You still need to show up prepared, follow site rules, and work safely.

Referrals are powerful because they come from trust. If another worker recommends you, they are putting their own reputation on the line. That means your behavior affects more than just you.

The best approach is to learn how your local market works and then treat every opportunity seriously.

#### ***Hiring Path Comparison***

Labor Companies  
Often provide crews for concerts, festivals, arenas, conventions, corporate events, and load-in/load-out work. Good for beginners who want exposure to different types of calls.

Venues  
May hire stagehands directly or use preferred labor providers. Venue work can help workers learn one building, its rules, its dock, its stage, and its crew expectations.

Unions  
May represent stagehands in theaters, arenas, convention centers, broadcast, film, or live event environments. Entry requirements and referral systems vary by location.

Production Companies  
May hire local hands for specific events, tours, builds, corporate shows, or festivals. These calls may require strong communication and flexibility.

Crewing Apps or Staffing Platforms  
May offer short-term event labor opportunities. Workers still need to verify expectations, call time, location, dress code, PPE, and job requirements.

Referrals  
Can come from other stagehands, leads, supervisors, production managers, or company contacts. Referrals are earned through reliable work.

#### ***What to Ask Before Accepting a Call***

What is the call time?

Where do I check in?

What should I wear?

What PPE is required?

Who do I report to?

What type of call is it: load-in, show call, load-out, or another event task?

How long is the call expected to be?

Is parking, security, or credentialing required?

Are there any site-specific rules I need to know before arriving?

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Never accept or perform specialized work just because it is offered. Operating forklifts, aerial lifts, rigging equipment, electrical systems, stage automation, or other specialized equipment requires proper training, authorization, and site-specific direction.

The hiring path may change, but the professional expectations do not. Be prepared, be honest, follow direction, and work safely.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Stagehand work may come through labor companies, venues, unions, production companies, crewing apps, staffing platforms, or referrals. New workers should learn their local hiring system while maintaining the same professional standards on every call.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Which hiring path is most common in my local area, and what do I need to learn about it before applying?

Question: Why should a new stagehand learn how their local market hires workers?

**Best Answer:**  
Because stagehand hiring systems vary by city, venue, company, and event type. Some markets rely more on labor companies, some on unions, some on venues, and some on referrals or staffing platforms.

# **Module 10, Lesson 3: What to Put on a Beginner Resume**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson helps new stagehands build a simple, honest resume even if they do not have much direct live event experience yet. The goal is to show learners how to highlight transferable skills without exaggerating.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify what belongs on a beginner stagehand resume and how to present entry-level experience clearly.

A beginner resume should be honest, simple, and relevant. It does not need to be fancy. It should quickly show that the applicant is reliable, physically prepared for event work, safety-minded, and able to follow direction.

New stagehands can include transferable experience from warehouse work, construction labor, moving jobs, theater classes, school productions, hospitality, event setup, AV support, volunteer events, sports facilities, church production, customer service, or team-based work.

Do not claim certifications, union status, equipment experience, or technical skills you do not actually have.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

A beginner stagehand resume does not need to make you look like a veteran. It needs to make you look dependable, honest, and ready to learn.

Many new stagehands think, “I do not have enough experience to make a resume.” But you may have more relevant experience than you think.

If you have worked in a warehouse, moved furniture, helped with events, volunteered at a theater, worked in hospitality, set up tables and chairs, helped with school productions, worked in customer service, played in bands, helped at a church production, or worked on a team under pressure, some of that experience may apply.

The key is to connect your past experience to the needs of stagehand work.

Stagehand work requires punctuality, physical readiness, communication, safety awareness, teamwork, attention to detail, and the ability to follow instructions. If you have examples of those traits from other jobs, they can belong on your resume.

Keep the resume short. One page is usually enough for a beginner. Use clear language. Make it easy for someone to see your availability, work history, relevant skills, and contact information.

Most importantly, be honest. Do not say you are a rigger if you are not a rigger. Do not say you can operate a forklift if you are not trained and authorized. Do not list OSHA 10 unless you actually completed an OSHA 10 course through an OSHA-authorized trainer and received appropriate documentation.

Your resume should help someone trust you, not make them wonder if you are exaggerating.

#### ***Beginner Resume Sections***

Contact Information  
Name, phone number, email, city or general location.

Short Summary  
A simple statement such as: “Entry-level stagehand seeking live event work. Reliable, safety-minded, physically prepared, and able to follow direction in fast-paced crew environments.”

Relevant Skills  
Examples may include:

Load-in and load-out awareness

Team lifting

Road case handling under direction

Cable awareness

Basic tool familiarity

PPE awareness

Communication with leads

Punctuality and reliability

Working in fast-paced environments

Following safety instructions

Work Experience  
Include jobs that show reliability, physical work, teamwork, event support, customer service, or safety awareness.

Training  
Only list training you have actually completed. Examples may include:

Stagehand Fundamentals course

OSHA 10, if completed through an authorized Outreach trainer

First aid or CPR, if current

Forklift training, if completed and authorized by an employer or recognized trainer

Venue-specific or employer-specific training

Availability  
Optional, but helpful for event work. You may include evenings, weekends, overnights, early mornings, or flexible availability if accurate.

#### ***Beginner Resume Example***

Name: Jordan Smith  
Phone: 555-555-5555  
Email: jordan@email.com  
Location: Phoenix, AZ

Summary:  
Entry-level stagehand seeking live event labor opportunities. Reliable, safety-minded, physically prepared, and able to follow direction in fast-paced crew environments.

Relevant Skills:

Load-in and load-out awareness

Road case handling under direction

Team lifting and safe movement awareness

Basic tool and PPE familiarity

Clear communication with leads

Reliable transportation and punctual attendance

Comfortable working long calls, nights, and weekends

Experience:

Event Setup Volunteer  
Community Arts Center

Assisted with chair setup, stage cleanup, and event reset

Followed direction from event staff

Helped keep walkways clear and organized

Warehouse Associate  
ABC Distribution

Moved materials safely in a team environment

Followed supervisor instructions and safety procedures

Maintained organized work areas during busy shifts

Training:  
Stagehand Fundamentals: Entry-Level Stagehand Training  
OSHA 10: Not completed yet

#### ***What Not to Put***

Do not claim technical skills you do not have.

Do not list certifications you have not earned.

Do not exaggerate equipment experience.

Do not use confusing job titles that make you sound more experienced than you are.

Do not write “expert” if you are a beginner.

Do not include unsafe claims like “willing to do anything” or “can operate any equipment.”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Be honest about your experience, show reliability on every call, and ask for feedback so you can improve over time.

A beginner resume should make you look reliable, honest, trainable, and ready for entry-level crew work.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A beginner stagehand resume should be simple, honest, and focused on transferable skills. The goal is not to pretend to be experienced. The goal is to show that you are reliable, safety-minded, coachable, and ready for entry-level work.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What experience do I already have that shows reliability, teamwork, physical readiness, communication, or safety awareness?

Question: Should a beginner claim they can operate specialized equipment if they have only watched someone else do it?

**Best Answer:**  
No. A beginner should only list equipment operation or specialized skills if they have been properly trained, authorized, and can perform the task safely.

# **Module 10, Lesson 4: How to Talk About Limited Experience Honestly**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners how to present themselves confidently without lying or exaggerating. The goal is to help new stagehands understand that honesty builds trust, while pretending to know something can create safety problems.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain their limited experience in a professional way and ask for direction when needed.

Being new is not a problem. Pretending not to be new is a problem.

A beginner should be honest about their experience while still showing confidence, readiness, and willingness to learn.

Clear, honest communication helps leads assign appropriate tasks and prevents unsafe situations.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Every experienced stagehand was new at some point.

Having limited experience does not make you useless. In many cases, crews expect new people to need direction. What crews do not want is someone who pretends to know something they do not know.

If you are asked about your experience, answer honestly. You can say, “I am new to professional stagehand work, but I understand basic load-in and load-out expectations, I know to follow my lead, and I am ready to work safely.”

That is much better than saying, “I know everything,” and then creating a problem.

Honesty helps the lead place you where you can be useful. If they know you are new, they can assign you to appropriate beginner tasks such as pushing cases, keeping pathways clear, helping with basic setup, sorting gear under direction, cleaning work areas, or assisting with team lifts.

Being honest does not mean sounding weak. You can be honest and confident at the same time.

You are not saying, “I cannot do anything.” You are saying, “I am ready to work, I will listen carefully, and I will ask questions before doing something unsafe.”

That is the attitude that gets beginners trusted.

#### ***Better Ways to Explain Limited Experience***

Instead of saying: “I do not know anything.”  
Say: “I am new, but I am ready to follow direction and learn the right way.”

Instead of saying: “Yeah, I can do that,” when you are unsure.  
Say: “I have not done that before. Can someone show me the safe way?”

Instead of saying: “I worked one show, so I know production.”  
Say: “I have worked one call and understand the basics of checking in, listening to the lead, moving cases under direction, and staying aware of hazards.”

Instead of saying: “I can do whatever you need.”  
Say: “I am comfortable with entry-level labor tasks and I will let you know if something is outside my training.”

Instead of saying: “I am certified,” when you are not.  
Say: “I have completed beginner safety-awareness training, but I have not completed that certification or authorization yet.”

#### ***Example Beginner Introduction***

“Hi, my name is Alex. I am new to stagehand work, but I completed entry-level training and understand the basics of PPE, load-in, load-out, road case handling, cable awareness, and following the lead. I am ready to work, and I will ask before touching anything I have not been assigned.”

#### ***Example On-Site Phrase***

“I have not been trained on that yet. Can someone show me the safe way, or would you like me to help with something else?”

#### ***Why Honesty Matters***

Honesty protects you.

Honesty protects the crew.

Honesty protects the gear.

Honesty helps leads assign the right tasks.

Honesty builds trust faster than fake confidence.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Limited experience is acceptable. Dishonesty is not. A good beginner is honest, prepared, willing to learn, and clear about what they can safely do.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

You can be new and still be professional. Be honest about your experience, show that you are prepared to work, and ask questions before doing anything unclear, unsafe, or outside your training.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Would I rather admit I am unsure, or risk creating a safety issue by pretending I know what to do?

Question: What should a beginner do if asked to perform a task they have never done before?

**Best Answer:**  
They should be honest and ask for direction, training, or a different task. They should not pretend to know how to do something unsafe or outside their experience.

# **Module 10, Lesson 5: Reliability: The Skill That Gets You Rebooked**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson explains why reliability is one of the most important traits for a new stagehand. The goal is to show learners that getting called back is often based on consistency, not advanced technical skill.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify specific behaviors that demonstrate reliability before, during, and after a call.

Reliability means more than showing up. It means showing up prepared, on time, sober, rested enough to work safely, dressed correctly, reachable, and ready to follow direction.

Reliable stagehands help leads plan and trust the crew.

Unreliable behavior can remove a beginner from future call lists quickly.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

If there is one skill that gets new stagehands rebooked, it is reliability.

You may think the most important thing is being strong, fast, or technically experienced. Those things can help, but they are not the first thing most leads need from a beginner.

Leads need to know that you will show up.

They need to know that you will arrive on time, dressed correctly, with the required PPE, ready to work, and able to follow direction. They need to know that you will not disappear during the call, ignore instructions, complain through the shift, or create safety problems.

Reliability starts before the call. Confirm the call details. Know where you are going. Plan for parking, security, check-in, traffic, and walking time. Pack your work clothes and basic supplies before you leave.

Reliability continues during the call. Stay near your assigned area. Check in when tasks are complete. Ask questions when needed. Keep your phone away unless you are on break or using it for work-related communication with permission.

Reliability also matters at the end of the call. Finish strong. Do not mentally check out before the work is done. Load-out, cleanup, and final walkthroughs are where crews notice who keeps working and who fades.

A reliable beginner is valuable because they reduce stress for the lead. They do not need to be chased, corrected constantly, or reminded of basic expectations.

That is why reliability gets people rebooked.

#### ***What Reliability Looks Like***

Before the Call:

Confirm the date, time, location, and check-in instructions

Plan transportation and parking

Wear the correct clothing

Bring required PPE

Bring water, snacks, and basic personal supplies

Arrive early enough to be ready at call time

During the Call:

Listen carefully during briefings

Follow the chain of command

Stay where assigned

Communicate when tasks are complete

Ask questions before guessing

Keep pathways clear

Work safely, even when the pace increases

After the Call:

Finish the assigned work

Help clean the area when directed

Return borrowed gear

Thank the lead or coordinator when appropriate

Track what you learned

Be ready to respond professionally if offered future work

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Unreliable Behavior***

Showing up late without communication.

Arriving without proper clothing or PPE.

Disappearing during the call.

Standing around while others work.

Ignoring instructions.

Pretending to understand.

Using the phone constantly.

Arguing with correction.

Rushing unsafely.

Leaving early without approval.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Be honest about your experience, show reliability on every call, and ask for feedback so you can improve over time.

Reliability is a professional skill. It tells the crew that you can be trusted with more work.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Reliability is one of the fastest ways to earn repeat calls. A reliable stagehand shows up prepared, works safely, communicates clearly, follows direction, and finishes strong.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What would a lead say about my reliability after working with me for one shift?

Question: Why does reliability matter so much for new stagehands?

**Best Answer:**  
Because leads and companies need workers they can trust to show up prepared, follow direction, work safely, communicate clearly, and finish the call without creating extra problems.

# **Module 10, Lesson 6: Building a Reputation**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches learners that reputation is built through repeated behavior over time. The goal is to help new stagehands understand how small choices on every call affect future opportunities.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to describe how a stagehand builds a positive reputation and identify behaviors that can damage trust.

Reputation is not built by one impressive moment. It is built by consistent behavior across many calls.

A good reputation can lead to more work, better assignments, stronger referrals, and access to learning opportunities.

A poor reputation can spread quickly if a worker is unsafe, unreliable, dishonest, disrespectful, or difficult to manage.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

In live events, people talk.

That can work for you or against you.

If you are reliable, safe, respectful, and easy to work with, people remember. If you show up late, ignore instructions, cause drama, disappear, damage gear, or act like safety rules do not apply to you, people remember that too.

Your reputation is built one call at a time.

At first, your reputation may be simple: “They showed up on time, listened, and worked hard.” That is a strong start. You do not need to be known as the best technician on day one. You want to be known as someone who can be trusted.

Over time, your reputation can grow. Leads may start giving you more responsibility. Department heads may let you observe or assist with more specific tasks. Other workers may recommend you. Companies may put you higher on the call list.

A good reputation is not about bragging. It is about being consistent when the work is hard, late, tiring, or stressful.

It is easy to act professional when everything is calm. Reputation is built when the call is long, the load-out is heavy, the weather is bad, the instructions change, or everyone is tired.

That is when people notice who stays steady.

#### ***Behaviors That Build a Good Reputation***

Show up early enough to be ready.

Dress correctly.

Bring required PPE.

Listen carefully.

Ask good questions.

Respect the chain of command.

Stay calm during changes.

Work safely when the pace increases.

Take correction professionally.

Avoid gossip and drama.

Protect the gear.

Protect the venue.

Finish strong.

#### ***Reputation Statement***

A strong beginner reputation sounds like this:

“They are new, but they show up, listen, work safely, ask when they are unsure, and keep a good attitude.”

That is enough to earn another call.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Behaviors That Damage Reputation***

Being late often.

No-call/no-show behavior.

Unsafe rushing.

Arguing with leads.

Ignoring instructions.

Touching gear without permission.

Claiming skills you do not have.

Complaining constantly.

Disappearing during work.

Using the phone instead of working.

Blaming others instead of learning.

Treating other departments disrespectfully.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Be honest about your experience, show reliability on every call, and ask for feedback so you can improve over time.

Your reputation is the story people tell about working with you when you are not in the room.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A good reputation is built through consistent professionalism. New stagehands should focus on being safe, reliable, coachable, respectful, and easy to work with on every call.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What do I want leads and crew members to say about me after my first few calls?

Question: What is one way a new stagehand can build a strong reputation?

**Best Answer:**  
By consistently showing up prepared, following direction, working safely, communicating clearly, taking correction well, and finishing the call professionally.

# **Module 10, Lesson 7: Moving From General Stagehand to Department Specialty**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces learners to the idea of career growth in live events. The goal is to show new stagehands how general labor experience can lead toward department-specific skills, while making it clear that specialized work requires training, supervision, and authorization.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common department specialty paths and understand how to begin moving toward one safely and professionally.

General stagehand work is often the starting point. Over time, workers may become interested in audio, lighting, video, staging, carpentry, rigging, backline, production, wardrobe, props, or other specialties.

The correct way to move toward a specialty is to observe, ask questions at appropriate times, learn terminology, accept beginner tasks, seek training, and avoid touching specialized gear without permission.

No one becomes a specialist by pretending. They become a specialist by learning safely over time.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

General stagehand work can open the door to many different paths.

At first, you may be pushing cases, helping unload trucks, keeping pathways clear, sorting gear, assisting with cable runs, or helping with cleanup. That beginner work matters because it teaches you how productions move.

As you gain experience, you may start noticing certain departments more than others.

Maybe you are interested in audio because you like speakers, microphones, consoles, and signal flow. Maybe lighting catches your attention because you like fixtures, truss, control, color, and programming. Maybe video interests you because of LED walls, cameras, projection, and screens. Maybe staging or carpentry fits you because you like decks, scenic pieces, platforms, and structure.

There are also paths in rigging, backline, production support, wardrobe, props, truck loading, crew leadership, and venue operations.

The important thing is to grow the right way.

Do not walk up and start touching department gear because you want to learn. Do not claim you can do something just because you watched it once. Do not interfere with specialists while they are focused on time-sensitive work.

Instead, be useful first. Follow direction. Learn the names of gear. Ask respectful questions when the timing is appropriate. Let leads know you are interested in learning. Accept small tasks and do them well.

A good phrase is:

“I am interested in learning more about this department. Is there a good beginner task I can help with, or a good time to ask questions after the rush?”

That shows interest without getting in the way.

#### ***Common Specialty Paths***

Audio  
Speakers, microphones, consoles, cable, signal flow, stage inputs, monitors, and PA systems.

Lighting  
Fixtures, truss, power distribution under qualified supervision, control systems, dimming, color, focus, and lighting positions.

Video  
LED walls, screens, cameras, projectors, switchers, media servers, signal lines, and display systems.

Staging and Carpentry  
Decks, risers, platforms, scenic pieces, soft goods, set pieces, and structural stage elements.

Rigging  
Overhead systems, motors, truss, points, steel, flown equipment, and load considerations. This is specialized work requiring proper training and authorization.

Backline  
Instruments, amplifiers, drum kits, keyboards, musician support, and stage plots.

Production Support  
Runner tasks, paperwork, hospitality support, production office help, credentials, radios, schedules, and communication support.

Truck Loading  
Trailer pack, case order, weight awareness, logistics, and loading sequence under experienced direction.

Crew Leadership  
Organizing people, assigning tasks, communicating with departments, tracking progress, and maintaining safety and workflow.

#### ***How to Move Toward a Specialty***

Start as a reliable general hand.

Learn department names and basic gear.

Watch how specialists work without interrupting.

Ask questions at appropriate times.

Tell a lead what you are interested in learning.

Accept beginner tasks in that department.

Seek formal training when needed.

Do not exaggerate your experience.

Do not touch specialized gear without direction.

Keep building your safety knowledge.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

Specialty work may involve hazards that are not obvious to beginners. Rigging, electrical work, lift operation, forklift operation, automation, power distribution, and other specialized tasks should only be performed by trained, qualified, and authorized workers.

General stagehand work is a starting point. Department specialties are earned through reliability, training, observation, humility, and safe hands-on experience.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A stagehand can grow from general labor into department specialties over time. The right path is to be reliable first, learn the basics, ask respectfully, seek training, and never perform specialized work without proper direction and authorization.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Which department interests me most right now, and what beginner-level steps could I take to learn more safely?

Question: What is the wrong way to try to move into a department specialty?

**Best Answer:**  
Touching gear without permission, pretending to have experience, interrupting specialists during critical work, or attempting tasks without training and authorization.

# **Module 10, Lesson 8: Next Skills to Learn**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson helps learners identify smart next steps after completing beginner stagehand training. The goal is to guide students toward useful skills while making it clear that awareness is not the same as authorization, certification, or qualification.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify several next skills that may help their growth and explain why specialized training matters.

After learning the basics, new stagehands can continue growing through safety training, jobsite experience, department-specific learning, and employer-approved training.

Useful next areas may include OSHA 10, forklift awareness, lift awareness, rigging awareness, audio basics, lighting basics, video basics, staging/carpentry basics, first aid/CPR, radio communication, and leadership skills.

OSHA 10 can be a useful safety-awareness course, but it should not be described as a certification or a license. It also does not replace employer-provided, site-specific, or task-specific training.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Completing a beginner stagehand course is not the end of learning. It is the beginning of learning with more awareness.

Once you understand the basics of load-in, load-out, PPE, venue awareness, communication, departments, tools, gear handling, and jobsite safety, you can start choosing your next skill path.

One common next step is OSHA 10. OSHA 10 can help workers understand common job-related safety and health hazards. However, it is important to use the right language. OSHA Outreach training is not a certification or license, and it does not replace employer-provided, site-specific, or task-specific training. Some employers, unions, states, or job sites may still require it, so workers should check the requirements for their area and employer.

Forklift awareness can also be useful, but awareness is not the same as being authorized to operate a forklift. A beginner should understand that forklifts create serious struck-by, caught-between, and traffic hazards. Operating one requires proper training and authorization.

Rigging awareness is another valuable area, especially in live events. Awareness helps you understand overhead hazards, suspended loads, exclusion zones, and why non-riggers must stay clear. It does not qualify you to rig.

Audio, lighting, video, and carpentry basics can help you become more useful around departments. Start by learning gear names, safe handling expectations, common terms, and what not to touch without direction.

First aid and CPR may also be useful, depending on your work environment. They do not replace emergency procedures, but they can make you more prepared.

The most important thing is to build skills honestly. Do not collect words for your resume. Build real ability, real safety awareness, and real trust.

#### ***Possible Next Skills***

OSHA 10  
Basic hazard-awareness training. Useful in many work environments, but not a certification or replacement for employer/site-specific training.

Forklift Awareness  
Helps workers understand traffic paths, blind spots, loads, exclusion zones, and why only trained and authorized operators should operate forklifts.

Aerial Lift Awareness  
Helps workers understand lift hazards, work zones, overhead risks, and the importance of trained and authorized operation.

Rigging Awareness  
Helps non-riggers understand overhead hazards, suspended loads, dropped-object risks, and why rigging work requires specialized training.

Audio Basics  
Speakers, microphones, cable types, stage inputs, monitors, consoles, and basic signal flow.

Lighting Basics  
Lighting fixtures, truss, DMX/data concepts, focus, color, cable, and safe handling under direction.

Video Basics  
LED walls, projectors, cameras, screens, media servers, signal flow, and delicate equipment handling.

Staging and Carpentry Basics  
Decks, platforms, risers, scenic pieces, soft goods, hardware, and safe build/strike habits.

First Aid/CPR  
Basic emergency response knowledge from a recognized provider.

Radio Communication  
Clear callouts, listening discipline, proper radio language, and knowing when not to talk.

Leadership Skills  
Checking in with the crew, helping newer workers, staying calm, and understanding workflow without freelancing.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

#### ***Important Safety Language***

Awareness means you understand the hazard.

Training means you have been taught how to perform a task.

Authorization means the employer, venue, or responsible party has approved you to perform that task in that setting.

Qualification means you have the knowledge, training, experience, and approval required for a specific type of work.

Do not confuse these terms.

The next step after beginner training is not pretending to be advanced. The next step is choosing a skill path and learning it safely, honestly, and properly.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

New stagehands should keep learning after the basics. Good next steps may include OSHA 10, forklift awareness, rigging awareness, department basics, first aid/CPR, radio communication, and leadership skills. Awareness is valuable, but it is not the same as authorization or qualification.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Which next skill would make me safer, more useful, and more employable without pushing me into work I am not ready for?

Question: Does taking an awareness course automatically authorize someone to operate equipment or perform specialized work?

**Best Answer:**  
No. Awareness training can help someone understand hazards, but operating equipment or performing specialized work requires proper training, qualification when applicable, and employer or site authorization.

# **Module 10, Lesson 9: Your First 30-Day Development Plan**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson gives learners a simple action plan for their first month of stagehand development. The goal is to help students leave the course with clear next steps instead of only general information.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to create a 30-day beginner development plan focused on safety, reliability, communication, and skill growth.

A new stagehand should not try to master everything at once. A 30-day plan gives beginners a realistic structure for learning the job.

The first month should focus on showing up prepared, learning call etiquette, practicing communication, improving case and cable awareness, learning department names, asking for feedback, and choosing a future skill path.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

After finishing this course, the next question is simple:

What do you do now?

The answer is not to pretend you are fully trained in every department. The answer is to build a practical first 30-day plan.

During your first month, focus on the basics that make you safe and employable.

In week one, focus on showing up correctly. Learn call etiquette. Confirm your PPE. Practice being on time. Learn how to check in, find your lead, and listen during the briefing.

In week two, focus on communication and handling basics. Practice clear responses like “copy,” “standing by,” and “I have not been trained on that.” Pay attention to road case movement, cable awareness, team lifting, and how gear is staged.

In week three, focus on departments and gear recognition. Learn the difference between audio, lighting, video, staging, rigging, backline, production, and venue staff. Learn the names of common gear without touching anything you have not been assigned.

In week four, ask for feedback and choose a next step. Ask a lead or experienced worker what you should improve. Identify one skill path you want to explore next.

The goal of the first 30 days is not to become an expert. The goal is to become dependable, safer, more aware, and easier to assign.

If you do that, you are already moving in the right direction.

#### ***First 30-Day Development Plan***

#### ***Week 1: Learn Call Etiquette, PPE, and Basic Terms***

Focus:

Arrive early enough to be ready at call time

Wear correct clothing

Bring required PPE

Learn check-in procedures

Identify your lead

Listen during safety briefings

Learn basic terms like load-in, load-out, stage left, stage right, dock, truck, FOH, barricade, case, cable path, and strike

Practice:

Introduce yourself professionally

Ask, “Who is my lead?”

Ask, “Where do you want me?”

Confirm directions before starting

Goal:  
Become someone who shows up prepared and does not need to be corrected on basic call behavior.

#### ***Week 2: Practice Communication, Case Handling, and Cable Awareness***

Focus:

Use clear responses

Check in when tasks are complete

Move road cases safely under direction

Watch for pinch points and crush points

Keep pathways clear

Do not pull, coil, unplug, or move cable unless directed

Practice:

“Copy.”

“Standing by.”

“Where do you want this case?”

“Do you want this staged or opened?”

“I have not been trained on that yet.”

“Is this cable cleared to move?”

Goal:  
Become easier to communicate with and safer around moving gear.

#### ***Week 3: Learn Department Names and Basic Gear***

Focus:

Identify audio, lighting, video, staging, rigging, backline, and production departments

Learn common gear names

Watch how departments stage and organize equipment

Avoid touching specialized gear without permission

Learn which areas are restricted or controlled

Practice:

Ask respectful questions at appropriate times

Observe labels, case markings, and department layouts

Help with beginner tasks when assigned

Keep gear organized according to direction

Goal:  
Understand what is happening around you without getting in the way.

#### ***Week 4: Ask for Feedback and Identify Your Next Skill Path***

Focus:

Ask for feedback from a lead or trusted experienced worker

Identify your strongest beginner habits

Identify one weakness to improve

Choose one next learning path

Update your resume honestly

Track the calls you worked and what you learned

Practice:

“Is there anything I should improve for the next call?”

“What beginner skills should I focus on next?”

“I am interested in learning more about audio/lighting/video/staging. What is a safe beginner step?”

“Do you have any feedback on how I worked today?”

Goal:  
Leave your first month with clearer direction and better habits.

#### ***30-Day Tracking Questions***

After each call, write down:

What type of call was it?

What did I do?

Who was my lead?

What did I learn?

What gear did I recognize?

What did I ask questions about?

What did I do safely?

What do I need to improve?

Was I on time and prepared?

Would I be comfortable being called back by this crew?

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Be honest about your experience, show reliability on every call, and ask for feedback so you can improve over time.

The first 30 days are about building habits, not proving you know everything.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Your first 30 days should build the foundation for future work. Focus on reliability, safety, communication, awareness, and honest growth. You do not need to become an expert immediately. You need to become someone crews trust and want to call back.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

What is one specific action I can take this week to become more reliable, safer, or easier to work with?

Question: What should a new stagehand focus on during their first 30 days?

**Best Answer:**  
They should focus on showing up prepared, learning call etiquette, communicating clearly, handling basic tasks safely, learning department names, asking for feedback, and choosing a realistic next skill path.

# **Module 10 Practical Activity**

## **Activity: Build Your First Stagehand Growth Plan**

**Instructions:**

Answer the following questions in writing.

What type of stagehand work are you most likely to pursue first: labor company, venue, union, production company, crewing app, staffing platform, referral, or another path?

What experience do you already have that shows reliability, teamwork, physical readiness, communication, or safety awareness?

Write a two-sentence beginner introduction you could use when applying for stagehand work.

List three behaviors that could damage your reputation.

Which department or specialty interests you most right now?

What is one question you could ask a lead after your first call to get useful feedback?

## **Example Answer**

I am most likely to start with a labor company or venue call. I have experience in warehouse work and customer service, so I am used to physical work, following supervisor direction, and working with a team.

**My beginner introduction:**  
“I am new to professional stagehand work, but I have completed entry-level training and understand the importance of PPE, communication, and following the lead. I am reliable, ready to work, and I will ask before doing anything I have not been trained or assigned to do.”

**Behaviors that would get me called back:**

Showing up early and prepared

Following direction without arguing

Asking questions before guessing

**Behaviors that could hurt my reputation:**

Being late

Touching gear without permission

Pretending to know skills I do not have

The department that interests me most is lighting. My next safe step is to learn basic lighting terms and ask if there are beginner tasks I can help with under direction.

# **Module 10 Key Outcome**

By the end of this module, learners should understand that getting hired and called back is not only about technical skill. It is about reliability, honesty, safety, communication, reputation, and steady growth. New stagehands should leave with a practical 30-day plan and a clear path for continuing their development.
