# Vocab sheet  

# Vocab sheet

# **Downloadable: Stagehand Communication Cheat Sheet**

## **Useful Phrases**

“Who is my lead?”

“Where do you want this case?”

“Do you want this staged or opened?”

“Can you show me the safe way to do that?”

“Copy.”

“Heard.”

“Standing by.”

“Say again?”

“Can you clarify?”

“Task complete. What would you like me on next?”

“I have not been trained or authorized to do that.”

“Is this cleared to move?”

“Should I leave this for the department?”

“Where should I stand while I wait?”

“Do you want me to check back in when this is done?”

## **Good Communication Habits**

Listen before speaking.

Confirm instructions.

Ask before guessing.

Keep responses short.

Stay respectful.

Check in after tasks.

Do not disappear.

Do not freelance.

Do not argue with correction.

Speak up if something seems unsafe.

Follow employer, venue, and production procedures.

## **Words and Phrases to Know**

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Phrase | Meaning |
| Copy | I heard and understood |
| Heard | I heard you |
| Standing by | Waiting and ready |
| Say again | Please repeat that |
| On my way | I am heading there |
| Clear | Area, task, or communication is clear, depending on context |
| Check in | Report back to your lead |
| Staged | Placed in a specific location for later use |
| Cleared to move | Approved or safe to move by the right person or department |

## **What Not to Do**

Do not say “copy” if you do not understand.

Do not move gear without direction.

Do not touch department gear without permission.

Do not take direction from random people without confirming the chain of command.

Do not scroll your phone during active work.

Do not take unauthorized photos or videos.

Do not argue when corrected.

Do not disappear after finishing a task.

Do not pretend to be trained or authorized for something you are not.

Do not enter restricted areas without permission.

Do not direct other workers unless assigned to do so.

  
  

# Gear List  

  

# Gear List

# **Gear ID and Handling Awareness****  
****  
****1. Road Case**

**What it is:** A protective case used to transport production gear.  
 Best beginner action: Read the label, move carefully when directed, and do not open without permission.

### **2. Truss**

**What it is:** Aluminum structure often used to support lighting, video, scenic, or rigging systems.  
 Best beginner action: Recognize it, stay clear unless assigned, and do not climb, rig, load, or modify it.

### **3. Deck**

**What it is:** A stage platform section.  
 Best beginner action: Move or assist only under direction. Watch for weight, edges, legs, hardware, and pinch points.

### **4. Cable Ramp**

**What it is:** A protective ramp used to cover cables across a path.  
 Best beginner action: Watch for trip hazards and move or place only when directed.

### **5. Motor**

**What it is:** A hoist used in rigging to lift or support production equipment.  
 Best beginner action: Do not touch, operate, move, connect, disconnect, or adjust unless trained and authorized.

### **6. Shackle**

**What it is:** Rigging hardware used in lifting or connection systems.  
 Best beginner action: Do not use or move without direction from trained rigging personnel.

### **7. Gaff Tape**

**What it is:** Production tape used for temporary securing, marking, or cable management when assigned.  
 Best beginner action: Use only as directed and avoid damaging surfaces or creating trip hazards.

### **8. Feeder Cable**

**What it is:** Heavy power cable.  
 Best beginner action: Do not handle unless trained, assigned, and authorized by the appropriate department.

### **9. Audio Snake**

**What it is:** A multi-channel audio cable system.  
 Best beginner action: Do not pull, coil, disconnect, or move unless directed by audio.

### **10. Barricade**

**What it is:** Crowd-control barrier used near stages, audience areas, or site perimeters.  
 Best beginner action: Move only under direction and use team communication because barricade can be heavy and awkward.

### **11. Pipe and Drape**

**What it is:** Uprights, bases, crossbars, and fabric used to divide or mask spaces.  
 Best beginner action: Assist only when assigned and watch for falling uprights, base plates, and fabric dragging.

### **12. Cart**

**What it is:** Rolling equipment used to move gear.  
 Best beginner action: Push carefully, control speed, watch corners, and avoid crush points.

### **13. Spike Tape**

**What it is:** Tape used to mark positions or placements.  
 Best beginner action: Use only when told where and what to mark.

### **14. Stage Riser**

**What it is:** An elevated platform section.  
 Best beginner action: Assist only under direction and watch for edges, stairs, guardrails, and stability.

### **15. Case Lid**

**What it is:** Removable or hinged part of a road case.  
 Best beginner action: Keep it with the correct case and watch fingers around latches and hinges.  

# Module 1  

  

# Module 1

# **Module 1 Overview: Welcome to the Live Event World**

## **Module Purpose**

This module introduces learners to the live event industry and the role of an entry-level stagehand.

Before a new stagehand can be useful on a jobsite, they need to understand the bigger picture. Stagehand work is not just moving boxes, pushing cases, or carrying gear. It is about supporting a larger production through safety, teamwork, communication, awareness, and reliability.

This module helps learners understand what stagehands do, where they may work, who they may work with, and what habits make a new crew member valuable.

## **What Learners Will Understand**

By the end of this module, learners should understand that stagehand work is physical, but it is also professional.

**They should be able to explain:**

● What a stagehand actually does  
 ● The difference between load-in, show call, and load-out  
 ● Common work environments such as arenas, theaters, convention centers, and outdoor shows  
 ● Basic crew roles and who gives direction  
 ● Why new stagehands should follow the chain of command  
 ● What makes a beginner useful, reliable, and safe on a crew

# Lesson 1

# **Module 1, Lesson 1: What a Stagehand Actually Does**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces new stagehands to the real purpose of the job. The goal is to help learners understand that stagehand work is not just physical labor. It is about supporting a larger production through safety, discipline, teamwork, and efficiency.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain what a stagehand does and why the role matters to the success of a live event.

A stagehand is one of the core support roles in live production. Stagehands help move, build, organize, set, strike, and reset the equipment that allows a concert, corporate event, theater production, festival, or live show to happen.

The job may include unloading trucks, pushing road cases, setting up staging, helping with cable runs, moving barricade, supporting lighting, audio, video, backline, or scenic departments, and cleaning or resetting work areas.

But the work is not just “moving boxes.”

A good stagehand is paying attention, listening to direction, working safely, protecting the gear, respecting the crew structure, and helping the production move forward without creating problems.

Stagehands are the backbone of the production industry because almost every department depends on reliable hands to get gear from the truck to the stage and back again.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

When people first hear the word “stagehand,” they often think the job is simple physical labor. They picture someone moving boxes, pushing cases, or carrying equipment.

That is part of the job, but it is not the whole job.

A stagehand supports a larger production. That means your work affects the audio team, the lighting team, the video team, the rigging team, the stage manager, the touring crew, the venue, and the rest of the local crew.

Your job is to help the production happen safely, efficiently, and professionally.

As a new stagehand, you may not know every piece of gear. You may not know every department. You may not know every term yet. That is okay.

What matters most in the beginning is that you show up prepared, listen carefully, follow direction, ask questions when you are unsure, and work safely.

The best new stagehands are not the ones who try to prove they know everything. They are the ones who stay alert, stay useful, and understand that they are part of a much bigger team.

#### ***Example Tasks a New Stagehand May Do***

Unload or reload trucks

Push road cases

Move barricade

Set chairs, risers, or deck pieces

Help keep pathways clear

Assist with cable runs under direction

Sort cases by department

Clean up tape, trash, and work areas

Help reset the venue after the event

Follow instructions from crew leads and department heads

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

A stagehand’s value is not just strength. It is reliability, awareness, safety, and the ability to follow direction as part of a team.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A stagehand is the backbone of live production. The job requires physical work, but it also requires awareness, discipline, communication, and respect for the production process.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If a lead asked me what a stagehand does, could I explain it without saying “we just move boxes”?

Question: What is the main role of an entry-level stagehand?

**Best Answer:**  
 To support the larger production by helping with safe, organized, and efficient labor under the direction of crew leads and department heads.

# **Module 1, Lesson 2: The Difference Between Load-In, Show Call, and Load-Out**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson helps new stagehands understand the three major types of event calls they may hear about in live production: load-in, show call, and load-out. The focus should stay beginner-friendly by making it clear that most new stagehands are more likely to start with load-in and load-out, not show call.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain the difference between load-in, show call, and load-out, and understand which parts of the process entry-level stagehands are most likely to support.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

#### ***Load-In***

Load-in is the process of moving production equipment into a venue and building the show.

This can include unloading trucks, pushing road cases, setting up staging, moving barricade, helping departments get gear to the right place, running cable under direction, and preparing the space for the event.

For many new stagehands, load-in is one of the first types of calls they will work.

#### ***Show Call***

Show call refers to operational support during the actual event or performance.

This may include helping with scene changes, backstage movement, production support, resets, or other show-specific tasks. However, a brand-new stagehand may not be assigned to show call right away because show calls often require more trust, timing, experience, and knowledge of the production.

For this course, show call should be introduced as something to understand, but not the main focus.

#### ***Load-Out***

Load-out is the process of striking, packing, and removing equipment after the event.

Load-out is usually faster and more physically intense than load-in. The crew is tired, the timeline is tight, and equipment needs to be packed correctly and moved back onto trucks. New stagehands are very likely to work load-outs, so safety, awareness, and following direction are especially important.

In live production, not every call is the same. A stagehand may be called for load-in, load-out, or sometimes show call, depending on the event and their experience level.

Load-in happens before the event. This is when the crew brings equipment into the venue and helps build the show. Trucks are unloaded, road cases are moved, staging may be built, cables may be run, and each department starts getting its gear into position.

Show call happens during the actual performance or event. This type of call may involve backstage support, timing-specific tasks, or helping the production run while the audience is present. New stagehands should understand what show call means, but they should also know that beginners are not always placed there right away.

Load-out happens after the event. This is when the crew takes everything down, packs equipment, clears the venue, and loads the trucks. Load-out can move quickly, but speed should never replace safety.

For a new stagehand, the most common starting point is usually load-in or load-out. These calls teach you how gear moves, how crews communicate, how departments work together, and how to be useful without getting in the way.

#### ***Simple Comparison***

|  |  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Call Type | When It Happens | What It Means | Beginner Likelihood |
| Load-In | Before the event | Gear comes in and the show gets built | Very likely |
| Show Call | During the event | Crew supports the live performance | Less likely for brand-new stagehands |
| Load-Out | After the event | Gear is struck, packed, and loaded out | Very likely |

#### ***Example Tasks***

#### ***Load-In Tasks***

Unload trucks

Push road cases

Sort gear by department

Move barricade or staging pieces

Help keep work areas clear

Follow directions from crew leads

#### ***Show Call Tasks***

Stand by for assigned support

Assist with specific backstage changes

Help reset items during the event

Follow timing-sensitive instructions

#### ***Load-Out Tasks***

Strike equipment

Pack road cases

Coil or move cable under direction

Push cases back to trucks

Clear trash, tape, and work areas

Help restore the venue

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Move with purpose during load-in, but confirm where gear goes before touching, opening, or relocating it.

Load-in builds the show.  
 Show call supports the show.  
 Load-out takes the show apart and gets it back on the truck.

For beginners, the most important focus is learning how to work safely and effectively during load-in and load-out.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Understanding the difference between load-in, show call, and load-out helps new stagehands know what kind of work to expect. Most beginners should focus first on being safe, useful, and reliable during load-in and load-out.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If someone told me I was booked for load-out, would I understand how that call is different from load-in?

Question: Which type of call is a new stagehand most likely to work first?

**Best Answer:**  
 A new stagehand is most likely to start with load-in or load-out because those calls often need general crew support, equipment movement, and basic labor under direction.

# **Module 1, Lesson 3: Common Work Environments**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces new stagehands to the different types of places they may work. The goal is to help learners understand that stagehand work changes depending on the venue, event type, crew size, schedule, and site conditions.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common stagehand work environments and describe how each one affects the pace, expectations, and safety awareness required on the job.

Stagehands do not work in only one kind of place. A call could be in a large arena, a small theater, a convention center, a hotel ballroom, a festival site, a stadium, or an outdoor temporary event space.

The basic expectations stay the same: show up prepared, work safely, follow direction, and support the production. But the environment changes how the work feels.

Some environments are fast and loud. Some are quiet and precise. Some involve large crews and heavy equipment. Others require careful movement in tight backstage spaces. A good stagehand learns to adjust to the room.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Stagehand work can happen in many different environments. Each one has its own pace, hazards, and expectations.

Arenas are large-scale, high-intensity environments. These calls often involve big trucks, large crews, heavy road cases, long pushes, forklifts, barricade, staging, audio, lighting, video, and rigging activity. The pace can be fast, and there may be a lot happening at once. In an arena, awareness is critical because equipment is constantly moving around you.

Theaters are usually more precise. The spaces may be tighter, the backstage areas may be smaller, and the work may involve stage mechanics, curtains, fly systems, scenic pieces, props, or carefully timed changes. Theater work often requires patience, quiet communication, and attention to detail.

In theaters, beginners should never operate fly systems, stage machinery, lifts, automation, rigging lines, or rigging-related equipment unless they have been specifically trained and authorized.

Convention centers can vary widely. One day may involve a trade show with booths, carpet, pipe and drape, signs, and freight. Another day may involve a large conference with staging, screens, audio, lighting, and breakout rooms. Convention work may involve long walks, repeated room setups, freight movement, room changes, and changing layouts.

Outdoor shows add another layer of awareness. Weather, uneven ground, temporary structures, generators, fencing, tents, sun exposure, wind, dust, rain, and site logistics can all affect the work. Outdoor calls require extra attention to hydration, footwear, weather conditions, and safe movement around the site.

If weather conditions change, report concerns to a lead and follow site procedures. Do not attempt to secure tents, stages, electrical equipment, or temporary structures unless trained and assigned.

As a new stagehand, you do not need to know everything about every environment right away. What matters is learning how to observe the space, listen to your lead, identify hazards, and adjust your behavior to the jobsite you are in.

#### ***Common Work Environments***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Environment | What It’s Like | Beginner Focus |
| Arenas | Large, loud, fast-paced, heavy gear movement | Stay alert, watch for moving equipment, follow directions closely |
| Theaters | More precise, often tighter backstage spaces | Move carefully, stay quiet when needed, respect stage systems |
| Convention Centers | Wide variety of setups and layouts | Watch for freight movement, forklifts, pallet jacks, carts, room numbers, and traffic paths |
| Outdoor Shows | Weather, terrain, temporary structures, site logistics | Hydrate, watch footing, prepare for changing conditions, report weather concerns |

#### ***Example Beginner Tasks by Environment***

#### ***Arena Tasks***

Push road cases

Move barricade when assigned

Help unload trucks under direction

Sort cases by department

Keep pathways clear

Stay aware of forklifts, carts, and moving equipment

#### ***Theater Tasks***

Move scenic pieces carefully when assigned

Assist with soft goods or stage items under direction

Keep backstage areas organized

Follow quiet work expectations

Avoid touching stage machinery, fly systems, or rigging equipment unless trained and authorized

#### ***Convention Center Tasks***

Set chairs or tables

Move pipe and drape under direction

Help with signage or room resets

Push freight or cases between rooms when assigned

Watch for forklifts, pallet jacks, carts, docks, and marked traffic paths

#### ***Outdoor Show Tasks***

Move gear across uneven ground when assigned

Help with barricade, fencing, or staging support under direction

Watch for weather-related hazards

Keep water, shade, and footing in mind

Report unsafe weather, ground, or site conditions to a lead

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Each environment has different hazards. A beginner should not assume that a task is safe just because it looks simple. If you are not trained, assigned, or sure how to do something safely, stop and ask.

The job changes depending on the environment, but the professional expectations stay the same: be safe, listen, stay aware, and support the crew.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Stagehands work in many different environments, including arenas, theaters, convention centers, and outdoor sites. Each environment has unique challenges, but every call requires safety, awareness, communication, and the ability to follow direction.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

How would my behavior need to change between working in a loud arena and working backstage in a quiet theater?

Question: Why is it important for a stagehand to understand different work environments?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because each environment has different hazards, pace, crew expectations, and site conditions. A good stagehand adjusts to the space instead of treating every call the same.

# **Module 1, Lesson 4: Who Is on the Crew**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces new stagehands to the different people they may work around on a live event crew. The goal is to help learners understand who gives direction, who performs specialized work, and why entry-level stagehands should respect the crew structure instead of guessing or freelancing.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify common crew roles and understand who to listen to when receiving work direction.

A live event crew is made up of different roles and departments. Each group has a specific job, and those jobs have to work together for the production to happen safely and efficiently.

As a new stagehand, your job is not to know everything about every department. Your job is to understand the basic crew structure, know who your point of contact is, and follow direction from the correct people.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

When you arrive at a call, you are stepping into a team environment. There may be dozens or even hundreds of people working around you, depending on the size of the event.

The most important people for a new stagehand to identify are the leads and department heads. These are the people who give direction, assign tasks, answer questions, and keep the work organized. If you are unsure what to do, you should look for your lead instead of guessing.

Riggers are specialists who work with overhead equipment. They may be involved with motors, truss, points, steel, and anything that gets flown above the stage or audience area. As a beginner, you should never interfere with rigging work or walk into rigging areas without direction. Overhead work requires serious awareness and specialized training.

You will also see department-specific specialists, such as audio, lighting, video, and carpentry crews. These teams are responsible for their own equipment and systems. Audio may handle speakers, consoles, microphones, and cable. Lighting may handle fixtures, truss, dimmers, and control. Video may handle screens, cameras, projectors, and LED walls. Carpenters may handle staging, scenic pieces, decks, platforms, and set elements.

Another important group is the truck loaders. Truck loaders focus on the logistics of getting gear in and out of trailers safely and efficiently. They may direct how cases are stacked, what order gear moves, and where equipment needs to go. Truck loading is not random. Gear usually has a specific order and placement.

As a new stagehand, remember this: do not assume that every person on-site can give you work direction. Know who your lead is, follow the chain of command, and ask questions when you are unsure.

#### ***Common Crew Roles***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Crew Role | What They Do | What Beginners Should Know |
| Leads / Department Heads | Give direction and organize the work | They are your main point of contact |
| Riggers | Handle overhead systems and flown equipment | Stay clear unless directed |
| Audio Crew | Handle sound equipment and audio systems | Do not touch audio gear unless asked |
| Lighting Crew | Handle lighting fixtures, truss, cable, and control | Follow direction carefully around lighting gear |
| Video Crew | Handle screens, cameras, projectors, and LED systems | Video equipment can be delicate and expensive |
| Carpenters / Staging Crew | Handle decks, scenic pieces, platforms, and stage structure | Pay attention to safe lifting and placement |
| Truck Loaders | Manage gear movement in and out of trailers | Follow truck direction and do not stack gear randomly |

#### ***Example Beginner Situations***

#### ***Situation 1***

You are standing near a stack of road cases and are not sure where they go.

Best action: Ask your lead where the cases should be moved.

#### ***Situation 2***

You see lighting crew working on truss nearby.

Best action: Stay clear and wait for direction. Do not walk into their work area or touch gear.

#### ***Situation 3***

Someone near the truck tells you to push a case into the trailer.

Best action: Make sure they are part of the truck loading team or confirm with your lead before moving gear into the truck.

#### ***Situation 4***

You see a cable on the ground and want to help clean it up.

Best action: Ask before coiling or moving it. Some cables may still be in use or need to be handled a specific way.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

Avoid guessing, rushing, touching gear without direction, ignoring site rules, or pretending you understand instructions that are unclear.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

A production crew works through structure and communication. New stagehands should know who their lead is, follow direction, and avoid touching specialized gear without permission.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Every person on the crew has a role. Leads and department heads give direction, riggers handle overhead work, departments manage their own equipment, and truck loaders control trailer logistics. As a new stagehand, your job is to listen, follow the correct chain of command, and support the crew safely.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I am unsure what to do on a call, who should I ask first?

Question: Why should a new stagehand avoid taking direction from random people on-site?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because live event crews have a chain of command. Taking direction from the wrong person can create confusion, move gear to the wrong place, slow down the crew, or create a safety issue.

# **Module 1, Lesson 5: What Makes a New Stagehand Valuable**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands what crews actually look for in beginners. The goal is to help learners understand that they do not need to know everything on day one. What matters most is being reliable, safe, coachable, and easy to work with.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify the habits and behaviors that make an entry-level stagehand valuable on a live event crew.

A new stagehand is not expected to know every department, every piece of gear, or every production term right away.

What makes a new stagehand valuable is not advanced technical knowledge. It is the ability to show up on time, listen carefully, follow instructions, communicate clearly, and work safely.

A valuable stagehand follows instructions precisely, while also stopping to ask questions when something is unclear, unsafe, or outside their training.

Crew leads want beginners who can be trusted with basic tasks and who do not create extra problems for the team.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

When you are new to stagehand work, it is easy to think you need to know everything right away. You might feel pressure to prove yourself, move fast, or act like you understand more than you do.

That is not what makes a new stagehand valuable.

A valuable new stagehand is reliable. That means you show up on time, ready to work, dressed correctly, and prepared for the call. If the call time is 8:00, that does not mean walking in at 8:00 looking for parking or trying to find the entrance. It means being checked in, ready, and available for direction.

A valuable new stagehand listens actively. When a lead gives instructions, you pay attention, make eye contact when appropriate, and confirm what you heard. If you do not understand something, you ask before acting.

A valuable new stagehand communicates clearly. You do not disappear, guess, or pretend. You let your lead know when a task is done, when you are unsure, or when you see something that may be unsafe.

Most importantly, a valuable new stagehand is committed to safety. Moving fast is never more important than protecting people, gear, and the venue. A good beginner understands that safety is part of the job, not something separate from the job.

Being valuable does not mean saying yes to every task. If you are not trained, authorized, or sure how to do something safely, stop and ask.

The best new stagehands are not the loudest or the strongest. They are the ones who can be trusted to listen, work carefully, follow direction, communicate clearly, and improve throughout the call.

#### ***What Makes a New Stagehand Valuable***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Valuable Trait | What It Looks Like on the Job |
| Reliability | Showing up prepared and ready to work |
| Punctuality | Being early enough to check in, find your lead, and receive direction |
| Active Listening | Paying attention, confirming instructions, and not interrupting |
| Clear Communication | Asking questions, reporting problems, and updating your lead |
| Safety Commitment | Watching for hazards and not rushing into unsafe actions |
| Following Instructions | Doing the task exactly as directed before trying to improvise |
| Good Judgment | Stopping to ask when something is unclear, unsafe, or outside your training |

#### ***Examples of Valuable Beginner Behavior***

#### ***Good Example 1***

A lead says, “Take these four cases to stage left and leave them closed.”

A valuable new stagehand moves the correct cases to stage left, leaves them closed, and checks back in when finished.

#### ***Good Example 2***

A new stagehand does not know whether a cable is safe to move.

A valuable new stagehand asks, “Do you want this cable moved, or should I leave it until the department clears it?”

#### ***Good Example 3***

A crew member finishes a task and is not sure what to do next.

A valuable new stagehand checks in with the lead instead of wandering, standing in the way, or starting random tasks.

#### ***Good Example 4***

A new stagehand is asked to help with something they have not been trained to do.

A valuable new stagehand says, “I have not been trained on that yet. Can someone show me the safe way, or would you like me to help with something else?”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Bad Example***

A new stagehand sees a road case and decides to move it without asking.

That may seem helpful, but it can cause problems if the case was already staged correctly, belongs to another department, or is needed in that exact spot.

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

Following instructions is important, but safety still matters. If an instruction is unclear, seems unsafe, or involves work you have not been trained or authorized to do, stop and ask for clarification.

A new stagehand becomes valuable by being dependable, safe, attentive, honest, and coachable. You do not need to know everything. You need to listen, communicate, follow direction, and ask questions when needed.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

A valuable new stagehand is reliable, punctual, safe, honest, and easy to direct. Beginners are not expected to know everything, but they are expected to listen, communicate clearly, follow instructions precisely, and ask questions when something is unclear, unsafe, or outside their training.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I were a crew lead, would I rather work with someone who knows a little but listens well, or someone who acts confident but does not follow direction?

Question: What is one of the most valuable traits for a new stagehand?

**Best Answer:**  
 Reliability. A new stagehand who shows up on time, listens carefully, works safely, communicates clearly, and follows instructions is more useful than someone who tries to act experienced but creates confusion.

# End of Module 1 Quiz

# **Module 1 Quiz: Welcome to Stagehand Work**

## **Quiz Instructions**

Choose the best answer for each question. This quiz checks your understanding of what a stagehand does, common types of calls, work environments, crew structure, and the habits that make a new stagehand valuable.

**Recommended passing score:** 80%  
 Total questions: 20

## **Multiple Choice**

### **1. What is the main role of an entry-level stagehand?**

A. To run the entire production  
 B. To support the larger production through safe, organized, and efficient labor  
 C. To operate all technical equipment  
 D. To make independent decisions without asking leads

**Correct Answer:** B

### **2. Why is stagehand work more than “moving boxes”?**

A. Because stagehands are responsible for selling tickets  
 B. Because stagehands support the larger production through safety, teamwork, communication, and discipline  
 C. Because stagehands only work during the show  
 D. Because stagehands only work in theaters

**Correct Answer:** B

### **3. Which of the following is a realistic beginner stagehand task?**

A. Operating rigging motors without supervision  
 B. Rewiring electrical systems  
 C. Pushing road cases to assigned locations  
 D. Calling cues for the show

**Correct Answer:** C

### **4. What should a new stagehand do if they are unsure whether something is safe to move?**

A. Move it quickly before anyone notices  
 B. Ask a lead or appropriate department before acting  
 C. Ignore it and leave the area  
 D. Ask another new stagehand

**Correct Answer:** B

### **5. What is load-in?**

A. The process of removing gear after the event  
 B. The process of moving gear into a venue and building the show  
 C. The live performance itself  
 D. The break between shifts

**Correct Answer:** B

### **6. What is load-out?**

A. The process of striking, packing, organizing, and removing equipment after the event  
 B. The process of selling merchandise  
 C. The first meeting before the crew starts work  
 D. The process of rehearsing the show

**Correct Answer:** A

### **7. Why are brand-new stagehands less likely to be assigned to show call right away?**

A. Show calls often require more trust, timing, experience, and production knowledge  
 B. Show calls do not need stagehands  
 C. Show calls only happen outdoors  
 D. Show calls are always unpaid

**Correct Answer:** A

### **8. During load-out, what should never replace safety?**

A. Communication  
 B. Teamwork  
 C. Speed  
 D. Direction from a lead

**Correct Answer:** C

### **9. Which work environment is usually large-scale, loud, fast-paced, and often involves heavy gear movement?**

A. Theater  
 B. Arena  
 C. Classroom  
 D. Office

**Correct Answer:** B

### **10. Which environment may involve tighter backstage spaces, stage mechanics, scenic pieces, and quiet communication?**

A. Theater  
 B. Outdoor festival site  
 C. Warehouse  
 D. Parking lot

**Correct Answer:** A

### **11. What is especially important at outdoor shows?**

A. Ignoring the weather  
 B. Weather awareness, hydration, footing, and site logistics  
 C. Wearing white formal clothing  
 D. Working without checking in

**Correct Answer:** B

### **12. What should a beginner do if weather conditions change at an outdoor show?**

A. Try to secure tents or structures alone  
 B. Report concerns to a lead and follow site procedures  
 C. Leave the site immediately without telling anyone  
 D. Move electrical equipment on their own

**Correct Answer:** B

### **13. Who is usually the new stagehand’s primary point of contact for direction?**

A. Audience members  
 B. Leads and department heads  
 C. Vendors  
 D. Anyone standing nearby

**Correct Answer:** B

### **14. What do riggers specialize in?**

A. Ticketing and guest services  
 B. Overhead equipment, flown systems, motors, truss, and rigging-related work  
 C. Catering  
 D. Marketing

**Correct Answer:** B

### **15. What should a new stagehand do around rigging zones?**

A. Stand under suspended loads to watch  
 B. Touch motors to help speed things up  
 C. Stay clear, never stand under suspended loads, and wait for direction  
 D. Move rigging hardware without asking

**Correct Answer:** C

### **16. Why should a new stagehand avoid taking direction from random people on-site?**

A. Because live event crews use a chain of command  
 B. Because no one is allowed to talk on-site  
 C. Because all instructions are optional  
 D. Because beginners should work independently

**Correct Answer:** A

### **17. Which trait makes a new stagehand valuable?**

A. Acting like they already know everything  
 B. Guessing when instructions are unclear  
 C. Reliability and punctuality  
 D. Ignoring safety to move faster

**Correct Answer:** C

### **18. What does active listening look like on a call?**

A. Interrupting the lead  
 B. Paying attention, confirming instructions, and asking questions when needed  
 C. Walking away during instructions  
 D. Pretending to understand

**Correct Answer:** B

### **19. What should a stagehand do if they are asked to perform a task they have not been trained or authorized to do?**

A. Try it anyway  
 B. Let another beginner show them  
 C. Stop and ask for clarification or proper direction  
 D. Walk away without saying anything

**Correct Answer:** C

### **20. Which statement best describes a valuable new stagehand?**

A. Someone who works fast, even if they are unsafe  
 B. Someone who follows direction, communicates clearly, works safely, and asks questions when needed  
 C. Someone who moves gear without asking  
 D. Someone who avoids checking in with their lead

**Correct Answer:** B

# **Scenario Questions**

These can be used as written-response questions or discussion prompts.

## **Scenario 1**

You see a road case sitting near the stage. No one has told you to move it, but it looks like it might be in the way.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Do not move it without direction. Check with your lead or the appropriate department to confirm whether it should stay there or be moved.

## **Scenario 2**

You are working load-out. The crew is moving quickly, and someone tells you to grab a cable and pull it across the stage. You are not sure what it is connected to.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stop and ask before pulling it. Cable should only be moved or coiled under direction from the appropriate department, especially if you are unsure whether it is still connected or in use.

## **Scenario 3**

You arrive at a venue and do not know who your lead is.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Check in and ask who you should report to. A new stagehand should identify their lead or department contact before starting work.

## **Scenario 4**

You see riggers working overhead and a suspended load above the work area.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Stay clear of the rigging zone, never stand under suspended loads, and wait for direction from the appropriate lead.

## **Scenario 5**

A lead gives you instructions, but you only understood part of what they said.

**Question:** What should you do?

**Best Answer:**  
 Ask for clarification before starting the task. It is better to ask a question than to guess and create confusion or a safety issue.

# Module 2

# **Module 2: Safety Mindset Before Skillset**

## **Module Safety Disclaimer**

This module provides entry-level safety awareness for live event stagehand work. It is not OSHA 10/30 training, legal advice, employer-specific training, union training, site-specific orientation, equipment certification, rigging training, forklift training, lift training, fall-protection certification, electrical training, or medical guidance.

This module discusses general U.S. OSHA worker-safety concepts. Exact responsibilities may vary depending on employer, jurisdiction, work arrangement, venue policy, contract requirements, and site-specific procedures.

Always follow your employer’s safety program, venue rules, site-specific procedures, posted warnings, required PPE rules, and instructions from qualified leads or supervisors. OSHA states that employers are responsible for providing a workplace free from serious recognized hazards and providing safety training in language and vocabulary workers can understand.

If something is unclear, unsafe, or outside your training, pause your own work, move to a safe position if needed, and ask the correct lead before continuing.

## **Standard Safety Phrase for Module 2**

If you are not trained, authorized, assigned, and sure how to do the task safely, pause before acting and ask the correct lead.

# Lesson 2.1

# **Module 2, Lesson 1: Why Safety Comes Before Speed**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands that safety is the foundation of professional stagehand work.

The goal is to help learners understand that moving fast is not valuable if it creates injuries, damages gear, blocks the workflow, or puts other crew members at risk.

A good stagehand is not just fast. A good stagehand is safe, aware, reliable, and controlled.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why safety must come before speed on a live event jobsite and identify common situations where slowing down, asking questions, or waiting for direction is the professional choice.

Live event work often moves quickly. Load-ins, show calls, and load-outs can involve tight schedules, heavy equipment, moving vehicles, crowded work areas, low light, fatigue, noise, and pressure from the clock.

Because of that pressure, new stagehands may think they need to move as fast as possible to prove themselves.

That mindset can be dangerous.

Speed without awareness can cause injuries, damaged gear, blocked pathways, dropped equipment, miscommunication, and delays. A rushed worker can create more problems than they solve.

Safety comes before speed because people matter more than the schedule. Gear can be replaced. A call can be adjusted. But injuries can change someone’s life.

Employers are responsible for providing a workplace free from serious recognized hazards and for giving safety training in language and vocabulary workers can understand. Workers also have a responsibility to follow safety rules, use required PPE, ask questions, and report unsafe conditions.

For a new stagehand, safety does not mean being slow, lazy, or afraid to work. It means working with control.

Move efficiently, not recklessly.

That means looking before moving, listening before acting, and asking before guessing.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

In live production, everything can feel urgent.

The truck needs to be unloaded. The stage needs to be built. The barricade needs to move. The cable needs to get across the room. The show clock is always running.

Because of that, new stagehands sometimes think the best way to stand out is to move as fast as possible.

But speed is not the first priority.

Safety is.

A fast stagehand who ignores hazards can hurt someone, damage expensive equipment, block the crew, or slow the entire production down. A stagehand who rushes without thinking might push a case into someone, trip over cable, step into a forklift path, grab gear that is not ready to move, or walk under overhead work.

That is not being helpful.

A professional stagehand moves with purpose, not panic.

There is a difference between working efficiently and rushing. Working efficiently means you listen, understand the task, move with control, stay aware of your surroundings, and check back in when the task is done.

Rushing means you move before you understand, ignore what is around you, and hope nothing goes wrong.

On a live event jobsite, hope is not a safety plan.

Safety comes before speed because every person on the crew is depending on the people around them to make smart choices. Audio, lighting, video, rigging, staging, truck loaders, local crew, touring crew, venue staff, and security all share the same work environment.

Your actions affect more than just you.

If you are pushing a road case, you need to know where you are going, who is around you, whether the path is clear, and whether the case is safe to move.

If you see a cable, you should not pull it unless you have been told to. It may still be connected, in use, or part of another department’s system.

If a forklift is moving nearby, you should stay clear and follow site procedures before crossing a path.

If people are working overhead, you should never assume the area underneath is safe. Stay clear unless a lead directs you otherwise.

If you are tired during load-out, you need to be even more aware, not less. Fatigue and speed are a dangerous combination.

Being safe does not mean you are weak. It does not mean you are slow. It means you are professional.

The stagehands who get asked back are not the ones who run around trying to look busy. They are the ones who can be trusted to work hard without creating problems.

When you are new, your best move is simple:

Listen first.  
 Look around.  
 Move with control.  
 Ask when unsure.  
 Never let speed replace safety.

#### ***Speed vs. Safety Comparison***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Situation | Unsafe “Fast” Choice | Better Professional Choice |
| A road case needs to move | Push it quickly without checking the path | Check the path, communicate with nearby crew, and move with control |
| Cable is lying across the floor | Grab it and pull it out of the way | Ask the lead or department before moving it |
| Forklift is nearby | Walk behind it to save time | Stay clear and wait until the travel path is safe |
| Load-out is moving fast | Run with gear or rush down ramps | Keep control, use proper help, and follow direction |
| You do not understand the task | Pretend you understand and start anyway | Ask for clarification before acting |
| A lead says “hurry up” | Panic and stop watching your surroundings | Move efficiently while still staying aware and safe |

#### ***Trying to Prove Yourself***

New workers often want to show they are strong, fast, and ready. That is understandable, but trying too hard can lead to mistakes.

A better goal is to be dependable and coachable.

#### ***Copying Unsafe Behavior***

You may see someone else rushing, climbing, lifting poorly, skipping PPE, or moving through a restricted area. Do not assume it is okay just because someone else does it.

Follow your lead, your employer’s rules, site procedures, and your training.

#### ***Acting Before Understanding***

If you only understood half of the instruction, pause and ask. A short question is better than moving the wrong case, damaging gear, or putting someone in danger.

#### ***Load-Out Pressure***

Load-out often feels faster than load-in. Everyone is tired, the crowd may have just left, trucks are waiting, and the crew wants to finish.

That is exactly when safety matters most.

#### ***Poor Awareness***

Many beginner mistakes happen because someone is focused only on the thing in front of them. Stagehands need wide awareness. That means paying attention to the floor, overhead work, moving equipment, nearby crew, cables, ramps, dock areas, and changing conditions.

#### ***Example Beginner Situations***

#### ***Situation 1***

You are told to move a road case across the arena floor. The shortest path crosses in front of a forklift.

Best action: Do not cut across the forklift path. Wait, make sure the path is clear, and move the case only when it is safe.

#### ***Situation 2***

During load-out, someone says, “Grab that cable and pull it back.”

You are not sure what the cable is connected to.

Best action: Pause and ask before pulling. Cable should only be moved under proper direction, especially if you do not know whether it is still connected or in use.

#### ***Situation 3***

You are pushing a case and another crew member walks into your path.

Best action: Slow down, communicate clearly, and keep control of the case. Do not assume they see you.

#### ***Situation 4***

A lead gives instructions quickly, and you are not sure where the gear should go.

Best action: Ask for clarification before moving the gear. Guessing can waste time or create a hazard.

#### ***Situation 5***

The crew is moving quickly during load-out, and you feel pressure to run with a small piece of gear.

Best action: Do not run. Move with purpose and control. Running on a jobsite increases the chance of slips, trips, collisions, and dropped equipment.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Common “Speed Traps” for New Stagehands***

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Pause before acting, look for hazards, and ask the correct lead when anything is unclear or outside your training.

#### ***Beginner Safety Mindset***

A new stagehand should think:

“I am here to help the crew, not create extra problems.”

That means:

● I will not move gear unless I understand where it is going.  
 ● I will not touch department equipment unless directed.  
 ● I will not run with cases, tools, cable, or gear.  
 ● I will watch for forklifts, carts, pallet jacks, pushers, and moving cases.  
 ● I will stay clear of overhead work and suspended loads.  
 ● I will keep exits, fire lanes, ramps, docks, and pathways clear.  
 ● I will ask questions when something is unclear or unsafe.  
 ● I will report hazards instead of ignoring them.  
 ● I will remember that being safe is part of being useful.

This lesson is safety-awareness training for entry-level stagehands. It does not replace employer-provided safety training, site-specific instruction, union training, equipment certification, or OSHA 10/30 training.

Always follow your employer’s rules, venue procedures, lead instructions, posted warnings, and required PPE policies. If something seems unsafe, unclear, or outside your training, pause your own work, move to a safe position if needed, and ask the correct lead.

Speed is only valuable when it is controlled. A stagehand who moves quickly but creates hazards is not helping. A stagehand who works safely, communicates clearly, and follows direction is much more valuable to the crew.

Move efficiently, not recklessly.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Live event work can move fast, but safety must always come first. New stagehands should focus on controlled movement, awareness, clear communication, and asking questions when unsure. The goal is not to look busy. The goal is to help the crew safely and professionally.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Have I ever tried to move faster than I could safely control? What could happen if I did that on a live event jobsite?

Question: Why should safety come before speed during stagehand work?

**Best Answer:**  
 Because rushing can cause injuries, damage equipment, create confusion, and slow down the production. A professional stagehand works efficiently, but never lets speed replace awareness, communication, control, or safety.

# **Module 2, Lesson 2: Worker Rights and Employer Responsibilities**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson introduces new stagehands to the basic idea that safety is a shared responsibility.

Employers have responsibilities to provide a safe workplace, proper training, required safety equipment, and clear procedures.

Workers also have responsibilities to follow safety rules, use required PPE, report hazards, and ask questions when something is unclear or unsafe.

The goal is not to turn learners into legal experts. The goal is to help them understand that safety is not optional, and that speaking up about a real hazard is part of being professional.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify basic worker rights, recognize general employer safety responsibilities, and explain how a new stagehand should respond when they see an unsafe condition on a live event jobsite.

A live event jobsite can include moving trucks, forklifts, road cases, cables, temporary structures, overhead rigging, loud environments, poor lighting, tight schedules, and tired crews.

Because of these risks, workers need to understand both their rights and their responsibilities.

Workers have the right to a workplace that is free from serious recognized hazards. They also have the right to receive safety and health training in language they understand, receive required safety equipment, report injuries or illnesses, and raise safety concerns without retaliation. OSHA’s worker-safety materials support these general rights and responsibilities.

Employers are responsible for providing a safe workplace, making sure tools and equipment are safe and properly maintained, using signs or labels to warn about hazards, communicating safety procedures, and providing safety training in language and vocabulary workers can understand.

Required PPE rules can depend on the job, employer, venue, and applicable standard. In many OSHA-covered situations, employers are responsible for required PPE, though OSHA allows exceptions for some items such as ordinary clothing and certain non-specialty safety-toe footwear. For this course, learners should follow their employer’s PPE policy and ask before entering any area where PPE may be required. OSHA’s PPE standards also require employers to train employees who must use PPE on when PPE is necessary, what PPE is necessary, how to wear it, its limitations, and its care.

Workers also have responsibilities. A stagehand should follow jobsite rules, use required PPE, stay out of restricted areas, report hazards, follow direction from leads, and avoid doing tasks they have not been trained or authorized to do.

For new stagehands, this means one thing clearly:

You are allowed to ask questions when something seems unsafe.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

When you walk onto a live event jobsite, safety is not just one person’s job.

It is not only the safety manager’s job.  
 It is not only the crew lead’s job.  
 It is not only the employer’s job.  
 And it is not only your job.

Safety works best when everyone understands their role.

Employers have responsibilities. They are responsible for providing a workplace that is as safe as possible, giving workers information about hazards, providing required safety training, and making sure workers know what equipment they need for the hazards of the job.

That matters in live events because the work can change from site to site. One day you may be in an arena pushing road cases across a concrete floor. Another day you may be outside dealing with weather, uneven ground, temporary power, and heavy equipment. Another day you may be in a theater with tight backstage spaces and overhead systems.

Different jobsites have different hazards, so site-specific training and instructions matter.

As a worker, you also have responsibilities. You need to listen during safety briefings, follow instructions, wear required PPE, use equipment only as directed, and speak up when something seems unsafe.

This does not mean you should argue with every instruction or refuse tasks just because you are nervous. It means you should pay attention and be honest when something is unclear, unsafe, or outside your training.

For example, if someone tells you to move a road case, but you do not know where it goes, ask.

If someone tells you to pull a cable, but you do not know whether it is still connected, ask.

If you are told to enter an area where riggers are working overhead, pause and check with your lead.

If you are asked to operate equipment you have not been trained or authorized to use, say so.

A professional stagehand does not pretend. A professional stagehand communicates.

Speaking up does not have to be dramatic. You can say:

“I am not sure that path is clear.”  
 “I have not been trained on that.”  
 “Can you show me the safe way to do it?”  
 “Should we check with the lead before moving this?”  
 “There is a wet spot near the cable path.”  
 “There is a case blocking the exit path.”

These are not complaints. These are safety communications.

The best crews want workers who notice problems before someone gets hurt.

As a new stagehand, you may feel pressure to stay quiet because you do not want to look inexperienced. But asking a safety question is not weakness. It shows awareness.

You do not need to know everything on day one. You do need to know that safety concerns should be reported, unclear instructions should be clarified, and unsafe shortcuts should not be ignored.

#### ***Worker Rights: Beginner-Friendly Version***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Worker Right | What It Means for a Stagehand |
| Right to a safe workplace | The jobsite should be managed to reduce serious hazards |
| Right to safety training | You should receive safety information you can understand |
| Right to required safety equipment | PPE requirements should be clearly explained and handled according to employer policy and applicable standards |
| Right to report hazards | You can speak up when something looks unsafe |
| Right to report injuries | Injuries and illnesses should be reported through the proper process |
| Right to ask questions | You can ask for clarification when instructions are unclear or unsafe |
| Right to be free from retaliation | Workers should not be punished for raising legitimate safety concerns |

#### ***Employer Responsibilities: Beginner-Friendly Version***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Employer Responsibility | What It May Look Like on a Live Event Jobsite |
| Provide a safe workplace | Managing known hazards and keeping work areas reasonably safe |
| Provide safety training | Explaining site hazards, emergency procedures, PPE, and work expectations |
| Communicate procedures | Giving safety briefings, call instructions, restricted area rules, and emergency plans |
| Provide or require PPE according to applicable rules | Making sure workers know what PPE is required and how to use it properly |
| Maintain safe tools and equipment | Keeping work equipment in safe operating condition |
| Warn workers about hazards | Using signs, barricades, cones, verbal warnings, briefings, or lead instructions |
| Respond to reported hazards | Taking concerns seriously and correcting or controlling hazards when possible |

#### ***Worker Responsibilities: Beginner-Friendly Version***

A new stagehand should:

● Listen during safety briefings  
 ● Follow instructions from the correct lead or department head  
 ● Wear required PPE correctly  
 ● Stay out of restricted areas unless assigned  
 ● Avoid touching gear without direction  
 ● Report hazards, injuries, and near misses  
 ● Ask questions when unsure  
 ● Use tools and equipment only as trained and authorized  
 ● Keep pathways, exits, docks, ramps, and work areas clear  
 ● Avoid horseplay, rushing, freelancing, or unsafe shortcuts

#### ***What This Means on a Stagehand Call***

#### ***You Have the Right to Ask***

If you do not understand an instruction, ask for clarification.

**Example:**  
 “Can you tell me where this case should land?”

#### ***You Have the Responsibility to Listen***

Once the lead gives you the instruction, follow it carefully.

**Example:**  
 If the lead says, “Take this to stage right and leave it closed,” do not open it, move it somewhere else, or hand it off without checking.

#### ***You Have the Right to Report Hazards***

If you see something unsafe, report it to your lead or the proper person.

**Example:**  
 “There is a wet spot by the cable path near stage left.”

#### ***You Have the Responsibility Not to Create Hazards***

Do not leave cases in walkways, block exits, drop trash, run with gear, or move cables without direction.

#### ***You Have the Right to Required Safety Information***

If the job involves specific hazards, workers should be told how to recognize and avoid them. OSHA emphasizes training in language and vocabulary workers can understand.

#### ***You Have the Responsibility to Be Honest About Your Training***

If you have not been trained on a task, say so.

**Example:**  
 “I have not been trained on that yet. Can someone show me the safe way, or would you like me to help with something else?”

#### ***Mistake 1: Staying Silent Because You Are New***

New stagehands sometimes see a hazard but stay quiet because they do not want to look inexperienced.

Better choice: Report the concern calmly to your lead.

#### ***Mistake 2: Thinking Safety Is Someone Else’s Job***

Safety managers, leads, and employers have major responsibilities, but every worker affects the jobsite.

Better choice: Stay aware of your own actions and your surroundings.

#### ***Mistake 3: Confusing Confidence With Authorization***

Just because you feel capable does not mean you are trained or authorized.

Better choice: Do not operate lifts, forklifts, rigging equipment, power systems, stage machinery, or specialized tools unless properly trained and assigned.

#### ***Mistake 4: Ignoring Required PPE***

PPE only helps when it is used correctly.

Better choice: Wear required PPE according to jobsite rules and ask if you are unsure what is required.

#### ***Mistake 5: Reporting Too Late***

A hazard reported after someone gets hurt is no longer prevention.

Better choice: Speak up early when you notice a serious hazard.

#### ***Example Beginner Situations***

#### ***Situation 1***

You arrive at a venue and no one has explained where emergency exits, restricted areas, or check-in locations are.

Best action: Ask your lead where you should report, what areas are restricted, and whether there is a safety briefing.

#### ***Situation 2***

You are told to move cases through a path where a forklift is actively working.

Best action: Pause and wait for clear direction. Do not walk or push cases through active equipment paths unless it is safe and directed.

#### ***Situation 3***

You are asked to help with a task involving equipment you have never used before.

Best action: Say, “I have not been trained on that yet. Can someone show me the safe way?”

#### ***Situation 4***

You notice a road case blocking an emergency exit.

Best action: Tell your lead immediately. Do not ignore blocked exits or fire lanes.

#### ***Situation 5***

You see another worker doing something unsafe, but you are not their supervisor.

Best action: Do not create conflict. Report the concern to your lead or the proper person.

#### ***Simple Phrases for Speaking Up***

New stagehands do not need complicated language. Use clear, calm phrases:

● “I am not sure that is safe.”  
 ● “Can we check with the lead first?”  
 ● “I have not been trained on that.”  
 ● “There is a hazard over here.”  
 ● “This path is blocked.”  
 ● “That case looks unstable.”  
 ● “Do you want me to pause and wait?”  
 ● “Can you show me the safe way to do that?”  
 ● “Who should I report this to?”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Common Beginner Mistakes***

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

This lesson is safety-awareness training for entry-level stagehands. It is not legal advice, OSHA certification, OSHA 10/30 training, union training, employer-specific training, or equipment certification.

This lesson discusses general U.S. OSHA worker-safety concepts. Exact responsibilities may vary depending on employer, jurisdiction, work arrangement, venue policy, contract requirements, and site-specific procedures.

For this course, the beginner rule is simple:

If something seems unsafe, unclear, or outside your training, pause your own work, move to a safe position if needed, and ask the correct lead before continuing.

Safety is a shared responsibility. Employers must provide a safe workplace, training, procedures, and required safety equipment according to applicable rules. Workers must follow safety rules, use PPE, report hazards, and ask questions when something is unclear or unsafe.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

New stagehands have the right to a safe workplace, understandable safety training, required safety equipment according to applicable rules, and the ability to report hazards. They also have the responsibility to follow instructions, wear required PPE, report unsafe conditions, and ask questions when something is unclear or outside their training. Safety is not separate from professionalism. It is part of the job.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I saw a hazard on a jobsite, would I know how to speak up clearly without creating confusion or conflict?

Question: What should a new stagehand do if they are asked to perform a task they have not been trained or authorized to do?

**Best Answer:**  
 They should pause and communicate clearly. A good response is: “I have not been trained on that yet. Can someone show me the safe way, or would you like me to help with something else?”

# **Module 2, Lesson 3: The Difference Between Hazard, Risk, and Unsafe Behavior**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands how to tell the difference between a hazard, a risk, and an unsafe behavior.

The goal is to help learners stop using the word “unsafe” too generally and start thinking more clearly about what they are seeing on a jobsite.

A beginner does not need to become a safety professional on day one, but they do need to recognize when something could hurt someone, when the chance of injury is increasing, and when a person’s actions are making the situation worse.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain the difference between a hazard, a risk, and unsafe behavior, and identify basic examples of each in a live event work environment.

A hazard is something that could cause harm if it is not controlled.

A risk is the chance that the hazard will actually cause harm and how serious the outcome could be.

An unsafe behavior is an action that increases danger.

OSHA’s safety-management guidance emphasizes identifying and assessing hazards, investigating close calls and incidents, and considering both severity and likelihood when prioritizing corrective actions.

For stagehands, this matters because live event jobsites change quickly. A cable path may be safe one minute and become a trip hazard the next. A road case may be stable when parked but dangerous when pushed too fast down a ramp. A forklift may not be a problem when parked, but the risk increases when it starts moving through a crowded work area.

The professional stagehand learns to ask:

What is the hazard?  
 How serious is the risk?  
 Is someone’s behavior making it worse?  
 Who should I tell?

#### ***Simple Definitions***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Term | Simple Meaning | Stagehand Example |
| Hazard | Something that could cause harm | A cable across a walkway |
| Risk | How likely and how serious the harm could be | Someone may trip while pushing a case in low light |
| Unsafe Behavior | An action that increases danger | Running across the cable path or pulling the cable without direction |

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

On a live event jobsite, people often use the word “unsafe” to describe anything that feels dangerous.

That word is useful, but it is not specific enough by itself.

To become a safer stagehand, you need to understand the difference between a hazard, a risk, and unsafe behavior.

A hazard is the thing that could cause harm.

For example, an open cable path across a walkway is a hazard. A wet floor is a hazard. A moving forklift is a hazard. A stacked road case that looks unstable is a hazard. Working under overhead rigging can be a hazard if the area is not properly controlled.

The hazard is the source of possible harm.

Risk is the chance that the hazard will actually hurt someone, and how serious the outcome could be.

For example, a cable lying across a quiet backstage corner may be a hazard with lower risk if no one is walking there and it is marked or protected. That same cable across a dark load-out path where people are pushing cases becomes a much higher risk.

The cable is the hazard.  
 The chance of someone tripping over it is the risk.

Unsafe behavior is what a person does that increases the danger.

Running across the cable path is unsafe behavior.  
 Pulling the cable without knowing what it is connected to is unsafe behavior.  
 Dragging a road case through a crowded area without looking is unsafe behavior.  
 Walking behind a forklift to save time is unsafe behavior.  
 Standing under overhead work without direction is unsafe behavior.

This matters because not every hazard can disappear immediately. Live production involves gear, cable, cases, lifts, trucks, ramps, tools, temporary structures, and tight timelines. Some hazards are part of the environment, but they still need to be recognized and controlled.

A professional stagehand does not ignore hazards.  
 A professional stagehand does not create more risk.  
 A professional stagehand does not copy unsafe behavior just because someone else is doing it.

Instead, they slow down, look around, follow direction, use required PPE, stay out of restricted areas, and report problems to the correct lead.

When you understand the difference between hazard, risk, and unsafe behavior, you become more useful. You can describe the problem clearly instead of saying, “That looks bad.”

You can say:

“There is a cable across the main push path.”  
 “The floor is wet near the dock door.”
