 “That case stack looks unstable.”  
 “There is a forklift moving through the path we are using.”  
 “People are walking under overhead work.”  
 “The load-out path is dark, and cases are moving through it.”

Clear communication helps the lead decide what needs to happen next.

#### ***Stagehand Examples***

#### ***Example 1: Cable Path***

Hazard: Cable lying across a walking or case path.  
 Risk: Someone could trip, especially during load-out, in low light, or while pushing gear.  
 Unsafe behavior: Running across the cable, dragging it without permission, or ignoring it when it is creating a clear hazard.

Better action: Report it to your lead or the correct department. If directed, help mark, protect, reroute, or clear the path.

#### ***Example 2: Forklift Moving Nearby***

Hazard: Forklift or powered equipment moving through the work area.  
 Risk: A worker could be struck, pinned, or forced into another hazard.  
 Unsafe behavior: Walking behind the forklift, cutting across its path, wearing headphones, or assuming the operator sees you.

Better action: Stay clear, keep your head up, follow site traffic rules, and wait for a safe path.

#### ***Example 3: Stacked Road Cases***

Hazard: Road cases stacked too high, unevenly, or on a slope.  
 Risk: Cases could shift, roll, tip, or fall into workers or equipment.  
 Unsafe behavior: Climbing on cases, leaning on unstable stacks, pushing a case without checking the stack, or stacking gear randomly.

Better action: Tell your lead if a stack looks unstable. Do not climb, move, or restack cases unless directed.

#### ***Example 4: Wet Floor***

Hazard: Water, spilled liquid, mud, or slick flooring.  
 Risk: A worker could slip while carrying gear, pushing a case, or moving quickly.  
 Unsafe behavior: Ignoring the wet spot, running through it, pushing cases over it without warning others, or failing to report it.

Better action: Report the wet area. If directed, help block it off, clean it up, or reroute traffic.

#### ***Example 5: Working Under Rigging***

Hazard: Overhead work, suspended loads, motors, truss, or rigging activity.  
 Risk: Objects, tools, hardware, or flown equipment could fall or move unexpectedly.  
 Unsafe behavior: Standing under suspended loads, walking into rigging zones without direction, touching rigging hardware, or distracting riggers.

Better action: Stay clear of overhead work unless specifically directed by the appropriate lead.

#### ***Example 6: Poor Lighting During Load-Out***

Hazard: Dark or poorly lit work area.  
 Risk: Workers may miss cables, ramps, case wheels, dock edges, stairs, uneven flooring, or other people.  
 Unsafe behavior: Moving fast through the dark, pushing cases without checking the path, or assuming the area is clear.

Better action: Slow down, use approved lighting if available, stay with the assigned path, and report lighting problems to your lead.

#### ***How Risk Changes***

Risk is not always the same. The same hazard can become more or less serious depending on the situation.

A cable path becomes higher risk when:

● The area is dark  
 ● People are tired  
 ● Cases are moving through the path  
 ● Workers are carrying gear  
 ● The cable is not marked or protected  
 ● The area is crowded  
 ● The crew is rushing during load-out

A road case becomes higher risk when:

● It is on a ramp  
 ● It is heavy  
 ● It has a bad caster  
 ● The path is crowded  
 ● The person pushing cannot see ahead  
 ● The case is moving too fast  
 ● Someone is standing downhill from it

A forklift area becomes higher risk when:

● Pedestrians are crossing the same path  
 ● Visibility is poor  
 ● Noise makes verbal warnings hard to hear  
 ● Workers are distracted  
 ● The operator has limited sightlines  
 ● People assume they have been seen

#### ***What Beginners Should Do When They Notice a Hazard***

A new stagehand does not need to solve every hazard alone.

In many cases, the correct response is to report the issue and wait for direction.

Use this simple process:

Notice it  
 Look for anything that could hurt someone or damage equipment.

Name it clearly  
 Say what the hazard is.

Avoid making it worse  
 Do not move gear, touch cable, enter restricted areas, or start fixing things unless directed.

Tell the correct person  
 Report the concern to your lead, department head, safety person, or proper contact.

Follow direction  
 Once the lead gives instructions, help only in the way you are assigned.

#### ***Simple Phrases for Reporting Hazards***

● “There is a cable across the main push path.”  
 ● “The floor is wet near the dock.”  
 ● “That case stack looks unstable.”  
 ● “There is a forklift moving through the area we are using.”  
 ● “People are walking under overhead work.”  
 ● “The load-out path is too dark to see clearly.”  
 ● “This exit path is blocked.”  
 ● “I am not sure this is safe. Who should I tell?”  
 ● “Should we pause and check with the lead before moving this?”

#### ***Mistake 1: Thinking a Hazard Is Only Dangerous After Someone Gets Hurt***

A hazard matters before an injury happens. The goal is prevention.

#### ***Mistake 2: Trying to Fix Every Hazard Alone***

Some hazards can be corrected quickly, such as picking up trash from a clear walkway. But many stagehand hazards involve department gear, moving equipment, rigging zones, electrical systems, or production flow.

Better choice: Report first. Act only when directed.

#### ***Mistake 3: Calling Everything Unsafe Without Explaining Why***

A clear report is more useful than a vague complaint.

Instead of saying:  
 “This area is sketchy.”

Say:  
 “There is poor lighting near the ramp, and cases are moving through that path.”

#### ***Mistake 4: Copying Unsafe Behavior***

Just because someone else walks through a restricted area, skips PPE, runs with gear, or climbs on a road case does not mean it is acceptable.

Better choice: Follow your training, site rules, and lead instructions.

#### ***Mistake 5: Becoming the Hazard***

Sometimes the unsafe condition is not the gear. It is the worker’s behavior.

A stagehand can become the hazard by standing in a traffic path, blocking an exit, leaving tools on the floor, walking under overhead work, pushing cases too fast, or acting without direction.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Common Beginner Mistakes***

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Pause before acting, look for hazards, and ask the correct lead when anything is unclear or outside your training.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Remember the core lesson: safe stagehands listen, stay aware, follow direction, and ask questions before a small mistake becomes a crew problem.

## **Practical Activity: Identify the Hazard, Risk, and Unsafe Behavior**

Review each situation and identify the hazard, the risk, and the unsafe behavior.

### **Scenario 1**

A cable is stretched across a hallway during load-out. The hallway is dim, and workers are pushing cases through it.

**Hazard:** Cable across the hallway.  
 Risk: Someone could trip, fall, or lose control of a case.  
 Unsafe behavior: Rushing through the hallway without addressing or reporting the cable path.

### **Scenario 2**

A forklift is backing near the dock while two workers walk behind it to save time.

**Hazard:** Moving forklift.  
 Risk: Workers could be struck or pinned.  
 Unsafe behavior: Walking behind moving equipment.

### **Scenario 3**

Three road cases are stacked unevenly near a ramp, and someone leans against the stack.

**Hazard:** Unstable stack of road cases.  
 Risk: Cases could shift, fall, or roll.  
 Unsafe behavior: Leaning on the stack instead of staying clear and reporting it.

### **Scenario 4**

The crew is working outside, and rain has made the ground muddy near the stage entrance.

**Hazard:** Wet, muddy, uneven ground.  
 Risk: Slips, trips, falls, or stuck wheels.  
 Unsafe behavior: Running through the area or pushing cases without checking footing.

### **Scenario 5**

Riggers are working overhead, and a new stagehand walks underneath to get a better look.

**Hazard:** Overhead rigging work.  
 Risk: Falling objects or moving suspended equipment.  
 Unsafe behavior: Walking under overhead work without direction.

## **Important Safety Note**

This lesson is safety-awareness training for entry-level stagehands. It does not replace employer-provided safety training, site-specific instruction, union training, equipment certification, or OSHA 10/30 training.

Do not attempt to fix specialized hazards involving rigging, electrical systems, forklifts, lifts, stage machinery, temporary structures, or department equipment unless you are trained, authorized, and assigned.

If something seems unsafe, unclear, or outside your training, pause your own work, move to a safe position if needed, and ask the correct lead.

## **Key Concept**

A hazard is something that could cause harm. Risk is how likely and how serious that harm could be. Unsafe behavior is an action that increases the danger.

A professional stagehand learns to recognize all three.

## **Reflection Question**

**Before moving on, ask yourself:**

Can I look at a jobsite situation and explain what the hazard is, what the risk is, and what behavior might make it worse?

## **Quick Knowledge Check**

**Question:** What is the difference between a hazard and a risk?

**Best Answer:**  
 A hazard is something that could cause harm. Risk is the chance that the hazard will actually cause harm and how serious the outcome could be.

## **Key Takeaway**

Stagehands need to recognize hazards before someone gets hurt. Risk changes as conditions change. A cable, forklift, wet floor, unstable road case, overhead work area, or dark load-out path can all become more serious when visibility drops, workers rush, fatigue increases, traffic increases, or unsafe behavior is added. New stagehands should notice hazards, avoid making them worse, report them clearly, and follow direction from the correct lead.

# Lesson 2.4

# **Module 2, Lesson 4: Pause and Get a Lead: When to Ask Questions**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands when to pause, ask questions, and get a lead instead of guessing or continuing into a potentially unsafe situation.

The goal is to help learners understand that pausing their own work does not mean being difficult, lazy, or unwilling to work. It means knowing when a situation is unclear, unsafe, outside your training, or outside your authority.

A professional stagehand does not keep going just to look confident. A professional stagehand knows when to pause, communicate, and get the right person involved.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to identify situations where they should pause their own work, ask for clarification, or get a lead before continuing.

Live event jobsites move quickly, but fast movement does not remove the need for safe decision-making.

A new stagehand should pause and ask when:

● The instruction is unclear  
 ● The task seems unsafe  
 ● The task involves equipment they have not been trained to use  
 ● The task involves rigging, power, lifts, forklifts, stage machinery, or other specialized work  
 ● Gear appears unstable, damaged, connected, or not ready to move  
 ● A path is blocked, wet, dark, crowded, or crossed by moving equipment  
 ● People are working overhead  
 ● They do not know who has authority over the task  
 ● They feel pressure to guess instead of confirm

Pausing your own work is not about refusing every task. It is about preventing avoidable mistakes before they become injuries, damage, delays, or confusion.

For beginners, the safest rule is simple:

When you are unsure, pause your own work, move to a safe position if needed, and ask the correct lead.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

On a live event jobsite, things can move fast.

Cases are rolling. Trucks are unloading. Forklifts are moving. Cables are being run. Departments are building their systems. Leads are giving instructions. Everyone is trying to get the show ready or get the gear packed and loaded out.

In that environment, it can feel uncomfortable to pause and ask a question.

You may think asking questions makes you look inexperienced.

But guessing is much worse.

A new stagehand who guesses can move the wrong case, pull the wrong cable, walk into a restricted area, block a fire lane, interrupt a department, damage equipment, or put someone in danger.

A pause-and-ask mindset means you understand when to stop your own action before continuing.

It does not mean you stop the whole show.  
 It does not mean you argue with your lead.  
 It does not mean you refuse to help.  
 It means you recognize that something needs clarification before you act.

For example, if someone tells you to move a road case but you do not know where it goes, pause and ask.

If you are told to pull a cable but you do not know what it is connected to, pause and ask.

If a forklift is moving through the same path you were told to use, pause and wait for safe direction.

If people are working overhead, do not walk underneath just because it is the fastest route.

If someone asks you to operate a lift, forklift, motor, power equipment, stage machinery, or anything you have not been trained and authorized to use, pause and tell them you have not been trained on it.

That is not weakness. That is professionalism.

The best crew members do not want beginners pretending. They want beginners who communicate clearly, follow direction, and know when to get help.

A simple question can prevent a serious problem.

You can say:

“Can you clarify where this goes?”  
 “I have not been trained on that.”  
 “Should we check with the lead first?”  
 “This path does not look clear.”  
 “Is this safe to move?”  
 “Who should I report this to?”

Those phrases show awareness.

As a new stagehand, your job is not to solve every problem alone. Your job is to notice when something is unclear or unsafe, pause before making it worse, and involve the right person.

#### ***What “Pause and Get a Lead” Means***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| It Does Mean | It Does Not Mean |
| Pausing your own action when something is unclear | Refusing every task |
| Asking for clarification | Arguing with leads |
| Reporting hazards | Creating drama |
| Admitting when you are not trained | Acting helpless |
| Waiting for safe direction | Wandering away |
| Protecting people, gear, and the venue | Slowing the job down on purpose |

#### ***When to Pause and Ask***

#### ***1. When You Do Not Understand the Instruction***

If you only understood part of the instruction, do not guess.

**Example:**  
 A lead says, “Take those to stage left and stack them by the line array cases.”

If you do not know where stage left is or what the line array cases are, ask.

Better response:  
 “Can you point me to stage left and show me which cases you mean?”

#### ***2. When You Are Not Trained or Authorized***

Do not operate specialized equipment just because someone nearby asks you to.

This includes:

● Forklifts  
 ● Aerial lifts  
 ● Rigging motors  
 ● Power distribution equipment  
 ● Stage automation  
 ● Fly systems  
 ● Chain motors  
 ● Hoists  
 ● Pyrotechnics  
 ● Lasers  
 ● Heavy machinery  
 ● Any equipment requiring specific authorization

Better response:  
 “I have not been trained on that equipment. Do you want me to get someone who is authorized?”

#### ***3. When Gear May Still Be Connected***

Do not pull, coil, unplug, or move cable unless directed by the correct department.

**Example:**  
 A cable may still be connected to power, audio, lighting, video, data, or control systems.

Better response:  
 “Do you know if this has been cleared by the department?”

#### ***4. When the Path Is Not Safe***

Pause before moving gear through a path that is blocked, crowded, wet, dark, or crossed by moving equipment.

**Examples:**

● Forklift path  
 ● Truck ramp  
 ● Dock plate  
 ● Wet floor  
 ● Dark hallway  
 ● Cable crossing  
 ● Fire lane  
 ● Emergency exit  
 ● Crowded backstage area

Better response:  
 “This path is blocked. Do you want us to wait or use another route?”

#### ***5. When People Are Working Overhead***

Never assume it is safe to walk under rigging, truss, motors, tools, lifts, or suspended equipment.

Better response:  
 “Is this area clear to walk through, or should we wait?”

#### ***6. When a Load Looks Unstable***

Pause if a road case, cart, stack, scenic piece, deck, truss, or equipment pile looks like it could shift, fall, roll, or tip.

Better response:  
 “That stack looks unstable. Can we get a lead to check it before moving anything?”

#### ***7. When You Are Being Asked to Rush Unsafely***

Working efficiently is good. Rushing blindly is not.

Pause and ask if speed is causing people to skip communication, ignore hazards, run with gear, overload carts, block exits, or move equipment without control.

Better response:  
 “I can move quickly, but I want to make sure we have a clear path first.”

#### ***The Beginner Pause-and-Ask Process***

Use this simple process:

#### ***Step 1: Pause***

Pause your own action before making the situation worse.

#### ***Step 2: Step Out of the Hazard***

Move yourself to a safer position if needed. Do not block traffic, stand under overhead work, or remain in the path of moving equipment.

#### ***Step 3: Communicate Clearly***

Say what you see or what you do not understand.

**Example:**  
 “I am not sure this cable is cleared to move.”

#### ***Step 4: Get the Correct Lead***

Ask your crew lead, department head, safety person, or assigned point of contact.

#### ***Step 5: Follow Direction***

Once the correct person gives instruction, follow it carefully.

#### ***Simple Phrases New Stagehands Can Use***

● “Can you clarify that before I start?”  
 ● “Where exactly do you want this case?”  
 ● “I have not been trained on that.”  
 ● “I am not sure this is safe to move.”  
 ● “This path is blocked.”  
 ● “There is a forklift moving through this area.”  
 ● “Do you want me to wait for the department?”  
 ● “Should we get a lead before we move this?”  
 ● “Is this area clear, or is overhead work still happening?”  
 ● “Can someone show me the safe way to do that?”

#### ***Example Beginner Situations***

#### ***Situation 1: The Unclear Case Move***

A crew member points to a group of road cases and says, “Take those over there.”

You are not sure which cases they mean or where “over there” is.

Best action: Pause and ask for clarification.

Better response:  
 “Which cases do you mean, and where exactly should they go?”

#### ***Situation 2: The Cable Pull***

During load-out, someone tells you to pull a cable across the stage. You do not know what it is connected to.

Best action: Pause and confirm with the correct department or lead.

Better response:  
 “Has this cable been cleared to pull?”

#### ***Situation 3: The Forklift Path***

You are pushing a case and see a forklift moving through the same path.

Best action: Pause, stay clear, and wait for safe direction.

Better response:  
 “Forklift is moving through the path. Should we hold here?”

#### ***Situation 4: The Untrained Equipment Request***

Someone asks you to hop on a lift and move it a few feet.

You have not been trained or authorized to operate it.

Best action: Do not operate it.

Better response:  
 “I have not been trained or authorized on that lift. We need someone qualified.”

#### ***Situation 5: The Overhead Work Area***

You see riggers working overhead, but the fastest way across the room is underneath them.

Best action: Stay clear and ask if the area is open.

Better response:  
 “Is this area clear to pass through, or should we go around?”

#### ***Situation 6: The Unstable Stack***

You notice stacked road cases leaning slightly near a ramp.

Best action: Do not touch the stack unless directed. Report it.

Better response:  
 “That case stack looks unstable near the ramp. Can someone check it before we move anything?”

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***What Not to Do***

A new stagehand should avoid:

● Pretending to understand instructions  
 ● Moving gear without knowing where it goes  
 ● Pulling cable without clearance  
 ● Walking under overhead work  
 ● Operating equipment without training  
 ● Entering restricted areas without direction  
 ● Ignoring blocked exits or fire lanes  
 ● Running with gear  
 ● Copying unsafe behavior  
 ● Taking direction from random people instead of the correct lead

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Know who your lead is, keep communication short and clear, and check back in when a task is complete.

This lesson teaches a beginner safety mindset. It is not legal advice, OSHA 10/30 training, equipment certification, union training, or employer-specific safety training.

OSHA recommends bringing unsafe or unhealthful working conditions to the employer’s attention when possible. OSHA also explains that the legal right to refuse dangerous work has specific conditions, including a good-faith belief of imminent danger, a reasonable risk of death or serious injury, and not enough time to correct the issue through regular enforcement channels.

For this course, the practical beginner rule is:

If something seems unsafe, unclear, or outside your training, pause your own work, move to a safe position if needed, and ask the correct lead before continuing.

A pause-and-ask mindset means knowing when to pause before acting. It is not about being difficult. It is about preventing injuries, confusion, damage, and unsafe shortcuts.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

New stagehands should never pretend, guess, or rush into unsafe work. A professional stagehand knows when to pause, ask questions, and get a lead. Pausing to clarify is not weakness. It is part of working safely, protecting the crew, and becoming someone people trust on the job.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

Would I rather ask a question and look new for ten seconds, or guess and create a serious problem for the crew?

Question: What should a new stagehand do if they are unsure whether a task is safe or they have not been trained to do it?

**Best Answer:**  
 They should pause their own work, avoid making the situation worse, communicate clearly, and ask the correct lead or department head for direction before continuing.

# **Module 2, Lesson 5: Incident Reporting and Near Misses**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands why incidents, injuries, hazards, and near misses should be reported instead of ignored.

The goal is to help learners understand that reporting is not about getting someone in trouble. It is about preventing the same situation from hurting someone later.

A professional stagehand does not hide mistakes, ignore close calls, or pretend nothing happened. A professional stagehand communicates clearly so the crew can correct hazards before they become serious.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain what an incident and near miss are, why they matter, and what a new stagehand should do after seeing or being involved in one.

An incident is an unplanned event that causes injury, illness, damage, or disruption.

A near miss is an event where someone could have been hurt, but was not.

Near misses matter because they are warning signs. OSHA’s safety-management guidance includes investigating injuries, illnesses, incidents, and close calls or near misses to identify underlying hazards, causes, and safety program shortcomings.

If a road case almost tips over, that matters.  
 If a forklift almost backs into someone, that matters.  
 If a worker almost trips over a cable path, that matters.  
 If a tool falls from height but misses everyone, that matters.  
 If a ramp shifts while cases are moving over it, that matters.

No injury happened, but the hazard was real.

For new stagehands, the rule is simple:

If someone gets hurt, something gets damaged, or something almost causes harm, report it to the correct lead right away.

#### ***Simple Definitions***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Term | Meaning | Stagehand Example |
| Hazard | Something that could cause harm | Wet floor near the dock |
| Incident | Something happened that caused injury, damage, illness, or disruption | A worker slips and injures their wrist |
| Near Miss | Something almost caused harm, but did not | A worker slips on the wet floor but catches themselves |
| Report | Telling the correct person what happened | Informing your lead that the floor is wet and someone almost fell |

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

On a live event jobsite, not every safety problem looks dramatic.

Sometimes a serious warning sign happens quickly, and then the crew keeps moving.

A road case almost rolls off a ramp, but someone catches it.  
 A worker slips on a wet floor but does not fall.  
 A forklift horn sounds because someone stepped into its path.  
 A cable gets pulled before it is cleared, but luckily nothing breaks.  
 A tool drops from a lift but does not hit anyone.  
 A case stack shifts but does not fall.

It can be tempting to say, “No one got hurt, so we are fine.”

But that is the wrong lesson.

A near miss is important because it shows that the conditions were present for someone to get hurt. The only difference between a near miss and an injury may be timing, luck, or a few inches.

Professional crews do not ignore near misses. They learn from them.

Incident reporting and near-miss reporting help leads, employers, venues, and crews identify problems before they happen again. Maybe a path needs to be cleared. Maybe a ramp needs to be secured. Maybe a cable path needs to be marked. Maybe a case stack needs to be rebuilt. Maybe the crew needs better lighting, better communication, or a slower pace in a certain area.

Reporting does not mean you are blaming someone.

It means you are giving the crew important information.

As a new stagehand, you may feel nervous about reporting something. You may worry that people will think you are complaining, overreacting, or trying to get someone in trouble.

But safety communication is part of the job.

If someone is seriously hurt or there is immediate danger, follow the site emergency procedure first, get help, and do not delay emergency response just to complete a report.

If you are injured, report it.  
 If someone else is injured, report it.  
 If gear is damaged, report it.  
 If you see a serious hazard, report it.  
 If something almost causes an injury, report it.

Do not hide it.  
 Do not walk away from it.  
 Do not assume someone else saw it.  
 Do not wait until the end of the call if the hazard is active.

Tell your lead, department head, supervisor, safety person, or assigned point of contact.

Use calm, clear language.

You can say:

“Someone almost tripped over that cable path.”  
 “That case almost tipped on the ramp.”  
 “The floor is wet near the dock.”  
 “A tool fell from above.”  
 “That forklift came close to the crew path.”  
 “I cut my hand and need to report it.”  
 “I saw a near miss and want to let someone know.”

A safe crew does not depend on luck. A safe crew depends on awareness, communication, and correction.

#### ***Incident vs. Near Miss***

#### ***Incident***

An incident means something actually happened.

**Examples:**

● A worker gets hurt  
 ● A road case hits a wall or damages gear  
 ● A cable is pulled and damages equipment  
 ● A person falls  
 ● A tool drops and hits someone or something  
 ● A vehicle or forklift strikes an object  
 ● A fire lane or emergency exit is blocked and causes a response issue

#### ***Near Miss***

A near miss means something almost happened.

**Examples:**

● A worker almost trips over cable  
 ● A case almost tips over  
 ● A forklift almost backs into a crew member  
 ● A ramp shifts while a case is moving  
 ● A tool falls but misses everyone  
 ● Someone nearly walks under overhead work  
 ● A case rolls away but is stopped before hitting anyone

#### ***Why Near Misses Matter***

Near misses are valuable because they show the crew where danger is building.

A near miss can reveal:

● A blocked path  
 ● Poor lighting  
 ● Bad communication  
 ● Missing signage or barricades  
 ● Unstable gear  
 ● Unsafe speed  
 ● Fatigue  
 ● Improper stacking  
 ● Confusing directions  
 ● Crowded work zones  
 ● Forklift and pedestrian traffic conflicts  
 ● Cable paths that need to be marked or protected

If the crew ignores the near miss, the same condition may cause a real injury later.

#### ***What New Stagehands Should Report***

A new stagehand should report:

● Any injury, even if it seems minor  
 ● Any illness or medical concern that happens at work  
 ● Any gear damage  
 ● Any vehicle, forklift, cart, case, or pallet jack contact incident  
 ● Any fall, slip, trip, or dropped object  
 ● Any blocked exit, fire lane, ramp, dock, or pathway  
 ● Any unstable road case stack or rolling gear problem  
 ● Any serious cable trip hazard  
 ● Any unsafe overhead work concern  
 ● Any task they were asked to do but were not trained or authorized for  
 ● Any near miss where someone could have been hurt

OSHA’s safety-management guidance says workers should know how to report hazards, injuries, illnesses, and close calls or near misses, and know what to do in an emergency.

#### ***What to Do After an Incident or Near Miss***

#### ***Step 1: Respond to Immediate Danger First***

If someone is seriously hurt or there is immediate danger, follow the site emergency procedure first, get help, and do not delay emergency response just to complete a report.

Do not disturb the area of a serious incident unless necessary to prevent further harm or follow emergency instructions.

#### ***Step 2: Make Sure You Are Safe***

Do not step into more danger.

If the area has moving equipment, overhead work, electrical hazards, unstable gear, or traffic, move to a safe position first.

#### ***Step 3: Help Prevent Immediate Harm***

If you can safely warn others, do so.

**Examples:**

● “Watch the wet floor.”  
 ● “Hold up, forklift moving.”  
 ● “Do not use that path yet.”  
 ● “Stay clear of that stack.”

Do not attempt specialized fixes unless trained and assigned.

#### ***Step 4: Tell the Correct Person***

Report the issue to your lead, department head, supervisor, safety person, or assigned point of contact.

#### ***Step 5: Be Clear and Factual***

Say what happened, where it happened, who was involved if known, and whether anyone was hurt.

Good report:

“Near stage left, a worker almost tripped over an unmarked cable path while pushing a case. No one is hurt, but that path is still active.”

Poor report:

“Everything over there is sketchy.”

#### ***Step 6: Follow Site Procedure***

Your employer or venue may have a specific reporting process. Follow it.

You may be asked to fill out a form, give a statement, speak to a supervisor, or identify the location of the hazard.

#### ***Step 7: Do Not Hide or Minimize Injuries***

If you are injured, report it.

Do not say “I’m fine” just because you want to keep working. Some injuries get worse after adrenaline wears off.

#### ***What to Include in a Report***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Report Detail | Example |
| What happened | “A road case almost tipped.” |
| Where it happened | “At the dock ramp.” |
| When it happened | “During load-out, about ten minutes ago.” |
| Who was involved | “Two crew members were pushing the case.” |
| Whether anyone was hurt | “No one was injured.” |
| What hazard remains | “The ramp still looks uneven.” |
| What help is needed | “Can a lead check it before we move more cases?” |

#### ***Simple Reporting Phrases***

New stagehands can use simple language:

● “I need to report an injury.”  
 ● “There was a near miss by the dock.”  
 ● “Someone almost tripped over that cable.”  
 ● “A case almost tipped on the ramp.”  
 ● “This path is blocked and people are pushing cases through it.”  
 ● “The floor is wet near the load-out path.”  
 ● “A tool fell from overhead.”  
 ● “That stack looks unstable.”  
 ● “Nobody was hurt, but it could have been serious.”  
 ● “Who should I report this to?”

#### ***Example Beginner Situations***

#### ***Situation 1: The Almost Trip***

During load-out, a crew member catches their foot on an unmarked cable but does not fall.

Best action: Report it as a near miss.

Better response:  
 “Someone almost tripped over the cable path near stage right. Can we get that checked or marked?”

#### ***Situation 2: The Small Cut***

You cut your hand while moving a case. It does not seem serious.

Best action: Report it according to your employer’s process and get first aid if needed.

Better response:  
 “I cut my hand while moving that case. I need to report it and clean it up.”

#### ***Situation 3: The Rolling Case***

A road case starts rolling down a slight ramp but is stopped before hitting anyone.

Best action: Report it as a near miss.

Better response:  
 “That case started rolling on its own near the ramp. No one was hurt, but we should check the brakes or the parking spot.”

#### ***Situation 4: The Dropped Tool***

A tool falls from height and lands near the crew, but nobody is hit.

Best action: Stay clear and report it immediately.

Better response:  
 “A tool fell from overhead near the work area. Nobody was hit, but we need to report it.”

#### ***Situation 5: The Damaged Gear***

A case is pushed into a doorway and damages the doorframe.

Best action: Report the damage. Do not hide it.

Better response:  
 “The case hit the doorway and damaged the frame. Who should I report that to?”

#### ***Situation 6: The Forklift Close Call***

A forklift backs near a group of stagehands, and someone has to jump out of the way.

Best action: Report the close call to your lead.

Better response:  
 “There was a close call with the forklift near the dock path. No one was hit, but the crew path and forklift path are crossing.”

#### ***Mistake 1: Thinking “No Injury” Means “No Problem”***

A near miss still matters. It shows that a hazard exists.

#### ***Mistake 2: Hiding Small Injuries***

A small cut, strain, slip, or hit can become more serious later. Report injuries according to your employer’s process.

#### ***Mistake 3: Waiting Too Long***

A hazard should be reported while it still matters, not after the call is over and everyone has left.

#### ***Mistake 4: Blaming Instead of Reporting***

The goal is to communicate facts and prevent future harm.

Instead of saying:  
 “Those guys are being stupid with the cases.”

Say:  
 “A case almost tipped near the ramp while the crew was moving quickly. Can a lead check that area?”

#### ***Mistake 5: Assuming Someone Else Reported It***

If you saw it and it matters, tell the correct person.

#### ***Mistake 6: Trying to Fix Specialized Problems Alone***

Do not fix rigging, electrical, lift, forklift, staging, automation, or department-specific hazards unless trained, authorized, and assigned.

#### ***Reporting Is Not Snitching***

Some new workers worry that reporting makes them look difficult.

That mindset is dangerous.

Reporting a hazard, incident, injury, or near miss is not snitching. It is part of jobsite safety.

A good report helps the crew:

● Prevent injuries  
 ● Protect gear  
 ● Keep paths clear  
 ● Improve communication  
 ● Correct hazards  
 ● Document what happened  
 ● Learn from close calls  
 ● Avoid repeating the same mistake

Professional crews should use honest safety communication to improve the work.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Common Beginner Mistakes***

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

This lesson is safety-awareness training for entry-level stagehands. It does not replace employer-provided safety training, site-specific reporting procedures, union training, legal advice, equipment certification, or OSHA 10/30 training.

In the United States, OSHA requires covered employers to report work-related fatalities to OSHA within 8 hours and work-related inpatient hospitalizations, amputations, or loss of an eye within 24 hours. That responsibility belongs to the employer, not the entry-level stagehand.

For the learner, the practical rule is:

If someone is seriously hurt or there is immediate danger, follow the site emergency procedure first. Then report injuries, damage, hazards, and near misses to the correct lead or supervisor as soon as possible.

Near misses are warnings. If something almost caused an injury, damage, or serious hazard, it should be reported so the crew can correct the problem before someone gets hurt.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Incident reporting and near-miss reporting are part of professional stagehand work. New stagehands should report injuries, hazards, damage, and close calls clearly and quickly to the correct lead or supervisor. Immediate danger and serious injuries come first: follow the site emergency procedure, get help, and do not delay emergency response just to complete a report. Reporting is not about blame. It is about preventing the next incident.

Review prompt:

Before moving on, ask yourself:

If I saw a close call where no one got hurt, would I ignore it, or would I report it so the crew could prevent it from happening again?

Question: What is a near miss?

**Best Answer:**  
 A near miss is an event where someone could have been hurt, but was not. It should still be reported because it can reveal a hazard that needs to be corrected.

# **Module 2, Lesson 6: Why You Should Never Pretend You Know Something**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands why pretending to understand a task, tool, piece of gear, or safety instruction can create serious problems on a live event jobsite.

The goal is to help learners understand that honesty is a safety skill.

A new stagehand is not expected to know everything. They are expected to listen, ask questions, admit when they are unsure, and avoid doing work they have not been trained or authorized to do.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why pretending to know something is unsafe and describe what they should say when they do not understand a task, instruction, or piece of equipment.

New stagehands may feel pressure to act confident. They may worry that asking questions makes them look inexperienced. They may want to prove they belong on the crew.

But pretending to know something can be dangerous.

On a live event jobsite, one wrong assumption can lead to injury, damaged gear, blocked workflows, delayed departments, or unsafe conditions.

A stagehand should never pretend to understand:

● Where gear is supposed to go  
 ● Whether a cable is safe to move  
 ● How to operate equipment  
 ● Whether a path is clear  
 ● What department owns a case or tool  
 ● How to lift, push, stack, or secure something safely  
 ● Whether they are authorized to perform a task  
 ● What a lead’s instruction means

OSHA states that employers are responsible for providing safety training in a language and vocabulary workers can understand, and OSHA’s safety management guidance says training should give workers knowledge and skills needed to work safely and avoid creating hazards.

For a beginner stagehand, that means this:

If you do not know, say you do not know.

That is not weakness. That is professionalism.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

When you are new to stagehand work, you may feel pressure to prove yourself.

You might hear unfamiliar words.  
 You might see gear you have never touched.  
 You might be surrounded by experienced crew members who move quickly and seem to know exactly what they are doing.

In that environment, it can be tempting to nod your head and act like you understand.

But pretending is one of the fastest ways to create a problem.

If a lead tells you to take a case to stage left and you do not know where stage left is, do not guess.

If someone tells you to pull a cable and you do not know whether it is still connected, do not pull it.

If a crew member asks you to operate a lift, forklift, chain motor, power equipment, fly system, or any specialized equipment you have not been trained on, do not pretend you know how.

If you do not understand the instruction, stop and ask.

In live production, the wrong move can affect more than just you. Moving the wrong case can slow down a department. Pulling the wrong cable can damage equipment or interrupt a system. Walking into the wrong area can put you under overhead work. Trying to operate unfamiliar equipment can put people at serious risk.

The best new stagehands are honest.

They say:

“I have not been trained on that.”  
 “Can you show me the safe way?”  
 “Can you clarify where this goes?”  
 “I do not know that term yet.”  
 “Is this cable cleared to move?”  
 “Who should I ask before touching this?”

Those phrases do not make you look weak. They make you look aware.

Crew leads would rather answer a simple question than deal with an injury, damaged gear, or a task done wrong.

There is nothing wrong with being new. Every experienced stagehand started somewhere. What matters is whether you are safe, coachable, and honest about what you do and do not know.

A dangerous beginner is not someone who lacks experience.

A dangerous beginner is someone who hides their lack of experience and acts without direction.

Never pretend. Ask, learn, and then do the task correctly.

#### ***Why Pretending Is Dangerous***

|  |  |  |
| :-: | :-: | :-: |
| Pretending Sounds Like | What Can Go Wrong | Better Response |
| “Yeah, I got it,” when you do not understand | Gear may go to the wrong place | “Can you clarify where this should go?” |
| “I know how to use that,” when you are not trained | You may injure yourself or others | “I have not been trained on that equipment.” |
| “I think this cable is fine to pull” | You may damage active equipment | “Has this cable been cleared?” |
| “I know where stage left is,” when you do not | You may slow down the department | “Can you point me toward stage left?” |
| “I can move this alone” | You may lose control or strain yourself | “Do you want a team lift on this?” |
| “I assume this path is clear” | You may hit someone or block traffic | “Let me check the path first.” |

#### ***What New Stagehands Are Not Expected to Know Yet***

A beginner is not expected to know every:

● Production term  
 ● Department role  
 ● Cable type  
 ● Road case label  
 ● Stage direction  
 ● Tool name  
 ● Venue layout  
 ● Rigging term  
 ● Lighting fixture  
 ● Audio component  
 ● Video system  
 ● Truck pack method  
 ● Show-specific procedure

That is normal.

What beginners are expected to do is:

● Listen carefully  
 ● Ask questions  
 ● Follow direction  
 ● Stay honest  
 ● Avoid guessing  
 ● Admit when they are not trained  
 ● Report unsafe conditions  
 ● Learn from correction  
 ● Improve throughout the call

#### ***What You Should Never Fake***

#### ***1. Do Not Fake Understanding an Instruction***

If a lead gives you an instruction and you only understand part of it, ask before acting.

**Example:**

A lead says, “Take these to stage right and leave them by the dimmer beach.”

You do not know where stage right is or what “dimmer beach” means.

Do not guess.

Better response:  
 “Can you point me to stage right and show me where you want these landed?”

#### ***2. Do Not Fake Equipment Training***

Never operate equipment you have not been trained and authorized to use.

This includes:

● Forklifts  
 ● Aerial lifts  
 ● Chain motors  
 ● Rigging equipment  
 ● Power distribution equipment  
 ● Stage automation  
 ● Fly systems  
 ● Pyrotechnics  
 ● Lasers  
 ● Specialized tools  
 ● Any equipment requiring employer authorization

OSHA’s safety training guidance says additional or specialized training may be needed when work involves unique hazards.

Better response:  
 “I have not been trained or authorized on that equipment.”

#### ***3. Do Not Fake Cable Knowledge***

Cables may be connected to audio, lighting, video, power, data, control systems, or other department gear.

Do not pull, unplug, coil, reroute, or move cable unless directed by the correct person.

Better response:  
 “Has this cable been cleared to move?”

#### ***4. Do Not Fake Department Knowledge***

If you do not know which department owns a case, tool, cable, truss, scenic piece, or piece of equipment, ask.

Better response:  
 “Do you know which department this belongs to?”

#### ***5. Do Not Fake Physical Ability***

If something is too heavy, awkward, unstable, or hard to control, say so before someone gets hurt.

Better response:  
 “This feels like a team lift. Can we get another hand?”

#### ***6. Do Not Fake Safety Confidence***

If something feels unsafe, unclear, or outside your training, stop and ask.

Better response:  
 “I am not sure this is safe. Can we check with the lead?”

#### ***Honest Phrases That Make You More Professional***

New stagehands should get comfortable using these phrases:

● “I have not been trained on that.”  
 ● “Can you show me the safe way?”  
 ● “Can you clarify the instruction?”  
 ● “Where exactly do you want this?”  
 ● “Which case are you referring to?”  
 ● “Is this cable cleared to move?”  
 ● “Who should I report to for this task?”  
 ● “I do not know that term yet.”  
 ● “I want to make sure I do this correctly.”  
 ● “Should we get a lead before moving this?”  
 ● “This looks like it may need more people.”  
 ● “I am not comfortable guessing on this.”

These phrases show maturity, not weakness.

#### ***Example Beginner Situations***

#### ***Situation 1: You Do Not Know the Stage Direction***

A lead says, “Take those cases to stage left.”

You do not know which side is stage left.

Best action: Ask immediately.

Better response:  
 “Can you point me to stage left so I land these in the right place?”

#### ***Situation 2: You Are Asked to Pull Cable***

Someone says, “Grab that cable and pull it back.”

You do not know what it is connected to.

Best action: Do not pull it until it is cleared.

Better response:  
 “Has this cable been cleared by the department?”

#### ***Situation 3: You Are Asked to Use Equipment***

A crew member says, “Move that lift over here real quick.”

You have not been trained or authorized to operate the lift.

Best action: Do not operate it.

Better response:  
 “I have not been trained or authorized on that lift. We need someone qualified.”

#### ***Situation 4: You Do Not Recognize a Case Label***

You are told to move “the backline cases,” but you do not know which ones those are.

Best action: Ask for identification.

Better response:  
 “Can you show me which cases are backline?”

#### ***Situation 5: You Are Unsure About a Lift***

A road case feels too heavy to move safely by yourself.

Best action: Ask for help before you strain yourself or lose control of the case.

Better response:  
 “This feels like a two-person push. Can I get another hand?”

#### ***Situation 6: You Missed Part of the Instruction***

A lead gives fast instructions in a loud arena, and you only hear half of what was said.

Best action: Ask them to repeat or clarify.

Better response:  
 “I missed the last part. Can you repeat where this needs to go?”

#### ***Confidence vs. False Confidence***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Real Confidence | False Confidence |
| Asking questions when needed | Pretending to understand |
| Admitting what you have not been trained on | Acting like you know everything |
| Following instructions carefully | Improvising without permission |
| Learning from correction | Getting defensive |
| Moving with awareness | Rushing to look useful |
| Knowing your limits | Taking unsafe shortcuts |

Real confidence is not pretending to know everything.

Real confidence is being honest enough to learn safely.

#### ***Mistake 1: Nodding Without Understanding***

Many new stagehands nod because they do not want to slow down the lead.

Better choice: Ask a quick clarifying question.

#### ***Mistake 2: Saying Yes to Every Task***

Being helpful does not mean saying yes to unsafe or unauthorized work.

Better choice: Be honest about your training and ask for direction.

#### ***Mistake 3: Copying Experienced Workers***

Experienced workers may use shortcuts, terms, or movements you do not understand yet.

Better choice: Follow your lead’s instruction and your own training level.

#### ***Mistake 4: Touching Gear to Look Busy***

Moving random gear can create problems.

Better choice: Check in with your lead before moving equipment.

#### ***Mistake 5: Being Embarrassed to Ask***

Everyone starts as a beginner.

Better choice: Ask once, learn it, and apply it correctly next time.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***Common Beginner Mistakes***

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Listen first, ask when unsure, follow direction, and check in with your lead before starting new work.

#### ***The “Ask Before Acting” Rule***

Use this rule when you are unsure:

Before touching, moving, pulling, plugging in, unplugging, operating, lifting, stacking, opening, closing, climbing, entering, or crossing anything you do not understand, ask.

That one habit can prevent many beginner mistakes.

#### ***What Leads Usually Want From New Stagehands***

Most leads do not expect beginners to know everything.

They usually want beginners who:

● Show up prepared  
 ● Listen carefully  
 ● Follow the chain of command  
 ● Ask when unsure  
 ● Do not touch gear without direction  
 ● Admit training limits  
 ● Work safely  
 ● Stay calm when corrected  
 ● Improve throughout the call

A beginner who asks good questions is easier to train than a beginner who pretends.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Remember the core lesson: safe stagehands listen, stay aware, follow direction, and ask questions before a small mistake becomes a crew problem.

## **Practical Activity: Better Response Practice**

Read each situation and choose a better response.

### **Scenario 1**

You are told to take cases to “upstage right,” but you do not know where that is.

**Poor response:**  
 “Got it,” then guess.

**Better response:**  
 “Can you point me to upstage right?”

### **Scenario 2**

You are asked to coil cable, but you do not know whether the department wants it over-under, figure-eight, or left in place.

**Poor response:**  
 Start coiling it however you think is right.

**Better response:**  
 “How do you want this cable coiled, or should I wait for the department?”

### **Scenario 3**

You are asked to move a lift.

**Poor response:**  
 Try to figure it out.

**Better response:**  
 “I am not trained or authorized to operate that lift.”

### **Scenario 4**

You are not sure whether a case should be opened.

**Poor response:**  
 Open it and look inside.

**Better response:**  
 “Do you want this case opened or left closed?”

### **Scenario 5**

You hear a word you do not understand during instruction.

**Poor response:**  
 Pretend you know what it means.

**Better response:**  
 “I do not know that term yet. Can you explain what you need me to do?”

## **Important Safety Note**

This lesson is safety-awareness training for entry-level stagehands. It does not replace employer-provided safety training, site-specific instruction, union training, equipment certification, OSHA 10/30 training, or legal advice.

OSHA says workers have the right to safety and health training in a language they understand, and employers are responsible for providing safety training in language and vocabulary workers can understand.

**For this course, the beginner rule is simple:**

Never pretend you know something that affects safety, gear, people, or the flow of the job. Stop and ask.

## **Key Concept**

Pretending is dangerous. Honesty keeps people safer, protects gear, and helps the crew work correctly. A new stagehand should never fake understanding, training, authorization, or confidence.

## **Reflection Question**

**Before moving on, ask yourself:**

Would I rather ask one question now, or guess and risk creating a bigger problem for the crew?

## **Quick Knowledge Check**

**Question:** What should a new stagehand do if they are asked to perform a task they do not understand or have not been trained to do?

**Best Answer:**  
 They should stop, communicate clearly, and ask for direction. A good response is: “I have not been trained on that,” or “Can you show me the safe way to do it?”

## **Key Takeaway**

New stagehands are not expected to know everything. They are expected to be honest, safe, coachable, and willing to ask questions. Pretending to know something can cause injuries, damage gear, and create confusion. The professional choice is to ask, learn, and do the task correctly.

# Lesson 2.7

# **Module 2, Lesson 7: How to Listen During a Safety Briefing**

In this lesson, you will learn the basic concept, why it matters on a live event jobsite, and how it affects your behavior as a new stagehand.

## **The Short Answer**

*This section gives you the simple version first. Before you get into details, you should understand the main idea clearly enough to explain it to another beginner.*

This lesson teaches new stagehands how to pay attention during a safety briefing and use that information once the work begins.

The goal is to help learners understand that a safety briefing is not just a formality before the call starts. It may contain important information about hazards, restricted areas, emergency procedures, PPE, crew assignments, moving equipment, weather conditions, overhead work, and the safest way to move through the jobsite.

A professional stagehand listens during the safety briefing because that information can prevent injuries, confusion, delays, and unsafe choices later in the call.

By the end of this lesson, the learner should be able to explain why safety briefings matter, identify the type of information commonly shared during a briefing, and describe how to listen, ask questions, and apply the briefing during a live event call.

A safety briefing is a short meeting or announcement that gives workers important information before work begins or before a specific phase of work starts.

In live events, a safety briefing may happen before load-in, before load-out, before outdoor work, before a high-risk task, or when site conditions change.

A safety briefing may include:

● Who the leads are  
 ● Where workers should report  
 ● Required PPE  
 ● Emergency exits and evacuation routes  
 ● First aid location  
 ● Restricted areas  
 ● Forklift or vehicle traffic  
 ● Overhead work or rigging zones  
 ● Weather concerns  
 ● Load-in or load-out flow  
 ● Cable paths, ramps, docks, and travel routes  
 ● Hazards unique to that venue or event  
 ● How to report injuries, hazards, incidents, or near misses

OSHA’s safety-management guidance says workers should receive information about workplace hazards and controls, know whom to contact with questions or concerns, understand how to report hazards and near misses, and know what to do in an emergency.

A safety briefing tells you important site information, but it does not authorize you to perform specialized work you have not been trained and assigned to do.

For a new stagehand, the safety briefing is one of the most important moments of the call.

That is when you learn how not to become the problem.

## **What This Looks Like on a Call**

*This section connects the lesson to real jobsite behavior. Stagehand work is learned through repetition, direction, and awareness, so every lesson should help you understand what the concept looks like during an actual load-in, show call, or load-out.*

Before the crew starts working, there may be a safety briefing.

Sometimes it is formal. Everyone gathers together, and a lead, supervisor, safety person, venue representative, or production manager talks through the jobsite expectations.

Sometimes it is shorter and more informal. A lead may gather a small group and say, “Here is what we are doing, here is where we are going, here is what to watch for.”

Either way, you need to listen.

A safety briefing is not background noise. It is not the time to check your phone, talk to another crew member, adjust your bag, or look around like you are bored.

This is where you may learn the things that keep you safe.

You may learn that forklifts are moving through one side of the dock.  
 You may learn that riggers are working overhead and certain areas are restricted.  
 You may learn that the floor is wet near one entrance.  
 You may learn that a ramp is only for empty cases.  
 You may learn where emergency exits are.  
 You may learn who your lead is.  
 You may learn what PPE is required.  
 You may learn where first aid is located.  
 You may learn what weather conditions the crew is watching.  
 You may learn which areas are off limits.

If you miss that information, you may create problems without meaning to.

A new stagehand who does not listen during the briefing might walk through the wrong path, block a fire lane, push a case into a restricted area, miss a PPE requirement, or ask questions that were already answered five minutes earlier.

Listening well is part of being valuable.

During the safety briefing, stand where you can hear. Face the person speaking. Put your phone away. Stop side conversations. Pay attention to names, locations, hazards, instructions, and who to ask if you are unsure.

If something is unclear, ask at the right time.

You do not need to ask questions just to look involved, but you should ask if the information affects your safety or your assignment.

Good questions sound like this:

“Who is my lead?”  
 “Which path should we use for cases?”  
 “Are there any areas we should stay out of?”  
 “Where is first aid?”  
 “Are forklifts using this dock path?”  
 “Is overhead work still happening?”  
 “What PPE is required for this area?”  
 “Who should we report hazards to?”

A safety briefing only helps if you use the information after it is given.

If the briefing says not to use a certain path, do not use it.  
 If the briefing says hard hats are required in an area, wear one.  
 If the briefing says rigging work is overhead, stay clear.  
 If the briefing says report hazards to your lead, do that.  
 If the briefing identifies emergency exits, do not block them.

The best stagehands do not just hear the briefing. They apply it.

But remember this: a safety briefing is not the same as specialized training.

If the briefing mentions forklifts, that does not mean you are trained to operate a forklift.  
 If the briefing mentions rigging, that does not mean you are trained to rig.  
 If the briefing mentions lifts, power, stage machinery, or fall protection, that does not mean you are authorized to perform that work.

The briefing helps you understand the site. It does not give you permission to do specialized tasks outside your training.

#### ***What a Safety Briefing May Cover***

|  |  |
| :-: | :-: |
| Briefing Topic | Why It Matters |
| Crew leads and reporting structure | Helps you know who gives direction |
| Required PPE | Helps protect you from site-specific hazards |
| Emergency exits | Helps keep escape routes clear and accessible |
| First aid location | Helps workers respond quickly if someone is hurt |
| Restricted areas | Keeps unassigned workers out of dangerous or sensitive zones |
| Forklift and vehicle traffic | Helps prevent struck-by or crush hazards |
| Overhead work | Helps workers avoid rigging zones and suspended loads |
| Weather concerns | Helps outdoor crews respond to heat, wind, rain, lightning, or changing conditions |
| Dock, ramp, and case paths | Helps gear move safely and efficiently |
| Incident reporting process | Helps workers know how to report injuries, hazards, and near misses |

#### ***How to Listen Like a Professional***

#### ***1. Stop What You Are Doing***

When the briefing starts, stop side work unless told otherwise.

Do not keep organizing your bag, checking your phone, talking, or moving around.

#### ***2. Stand Where You Can Hear***

If you cannot hear the speaker, move closer if it is safe and appropriate.

Do not pretend you heard the instructions if you did not.

#### ***3. Put Your Phone Away***

Unless you were specifically told to use your phone for work information, keep it away during the briefing.

A worker staring at their phone during a safety briefing looks unprepared and careless.

#### ***4. Listen for Names***

Pay attention to:

● Your lead  
 ● Department heads  
 ● Safety contact  
 ● First aid contact  
 ● Venue contact  
 ● Truck lead  
 ● Dock lead  
 ● Anyone giving direct instructions to your group

Knowing who to ask prevents confusion later.

#### ***5. Listen for Locations***

Pay attention to:

● Stage left and stage right  
 ● Dock  
 ● Truck area  
 ● First aid location  
 ● Emergency exits  
 ● Restricted areas  
 ● Break area  
 ● Restrooms  
 ● Case paths  
 ● Forklift paths  
 ● Outdoor weather shelter areas

A lot of beginner confusion happens because someone did not listen for locations.

#### ***6. Listen for Hazards***

Pay attention to warnings about:

● Forklifts  
 ● Ramps  
 ● Dock plates  
 ● Wet floors  
 ● Poor lighting  
 ● Cable paths  
 ● Overhead rigging  
 ● Suspended loads  
 ● Road case traffic  
 ● Weather  
 ● Temporary structures  
 ● Power areas  
 ● Restricted zones

These are the details that may keep you from getting hurt.

#### ***7. Listen for PPE Requirements***

PPE requirements may change depending on the venue, employer, task, or area.

You may hear instructions about:

● Hard hats  
 ● Gloves  
 ● Safety glasses  
 ● High-visibility vests  
 ● Hearing protection  
 ● Work boots  
 ● Fall protection for trained and authorized workers  
 ● Weather gear for outdoor work

If you are unsure whether PPE is required, ask before entering the area.

#### ***What to Ask During or After a Safety Briefing***

A good safety question is clear, specific, and useful.

Good questions include:

● “Who is my direct lead?”  
 ● “Where should new hands report after this?”  
 ● “Which path should we use to move cases?”  
 ● “Are there any areas we should avoid?”  
 ● “Is overhead work happening right now?”  
 ● “Are forklifts using the same path as crew?”  
 ● “Where is first aid located?”  
 ● “What PPE is required for this area?”  
 ● “Who should we report hazards or injuries to?”  
 ● “If weather changes, where should we go?”

Avoid asking questions that are really complaints, jokes, or attempts to show off. Keep safety questions professional.

#### ***Beginner Safety Briefing Checklist***

During the briefing, try to answer these questions in your head:

● Who is my lead?  
 ● Where should I report?  
 ● What are we doing first?  
 ● What PPE is required?  
 ● Where are the emergency exits?  
 ● Where is first aid?  
 ● What areas are restricted?  
 ● Are forklifts, trucks, carts, or pallet jacks moving nearby?  
 ● Is anyone working overhead?  
 ● What paths should cases or gear use?  
 ● What hazards did they mention?  
 ● Who do I tell if I see a hazard or near miss?

If you cannot answer one of these and it affects your assignment or safety, ask.

#### ***Example Beginner Situations***

#### ***Situation 1: You Missed the Lead’s Name***

The safety briefing ends, and you do not know who your direct lead is.

Best action: Ask before starting work.

Better response:  
 “I missed who I should report to. Who is my lead for this call?”

#### ***Situation 2: You Cannot Hear the Briefing***

The dock is loud, and you cannot hear the person speaking.

Best action: Move closer if it is safe, or ask for clarification after the briefing.

Better response:  
 “I could not hear the section about case paths. Which route should we use?”

#### ***Situation 3: You Hear That Forklifts Are Active***

The briefing says forklifts will be moving near the dock.

Best action: Stay alert, use approved walking routes, avoid crossing equipment paths without clearance, and never assume the operator sees you.

Better response if unsure:  
 “Is this path clear for crew, or should we wait?”

#### ***Situation 4: The Briefing Mentions Overhead Work***

You hear that riggers are working overhead during the first part of load-in.

Best action: Stay out of rigging zones and avoid walking under suspended loads or overhead work.

Better response if unsure:  
 “Is this area open yet, or is overhead work still active?”

#### ***Situation 5: The Briefing Gives PPE Rules***

The briefing says hard hats and high-vis vests are required in a specific area.

Best action: Wear the required PPE before entering that area.

Better response if unsure:  
 “Do we need hard hats before going into the dock area?”

#### ***Situation 6: Weather Changes After the Briefing***

The briefing mentioned possible wind at an outdoor show. Later, conditions start changing.

Best action: Report the change to a lead and follow site procedures.

Better response:  
 “The wind is picking up near the stage entrance. Who should we report that to?”

#### ***Situation 7: The Briefing Mentions Equipment You Are Not Trained On***

The briefing mentions lifts, forklifts, rigging, or power areas.

Best action: Use the information to avoid hazards, but do not assume you are authorized to operate or work on specialized systems.

Better response if assigned incorrectly:  
 “I heard the briefing, but I have not been trained or authorized to operate that equipment.”

#### ***How to Apply the Briefing During the Call***

Listening is only step one. Applying the briefing is what matters.

If the briefing said a path is restricted, do not use it.  
 If the briefing said to keep exits clear, do not stage cases in front of them.  
 If the briefing said to report near misses, report them.  
 If the briefing said forklifts are active, stay alert around equipment paths.  
 If the briefing said hard hats are required, wear one.  
 If the briefing said not to touch cable until it is cleared, do not touch cable.  
 If the briefing said weather is being monitored, report changing conditions.

A safety briefing should guide your choices throughout the call.

#### ***Mistake 1: Treating the Briefing Like a Formality***

Some new workers think the briefing is just something management has to do.

Better choice: Treat the briefing as jobsite information you are expected to use.

#### ***Mistake 2: Listening Only for Your Task***

Your task matters, but hazards, exits, PPE, and emergency information matter too.

Better choice: Listen for the full picture.

#### ***Mistake 3: Not Asking When You Miss Something***

Pretending you heard the instruction can create problems later.

Better choice: Ask for clarification.

#### ***Mistake 4: Forgetting the Briefing Once Work Starts***

The briefing is not only for the first five minutes. It applies throughout the call.

Better choice: Use the briefing to guide your movement, communication, and decisions.

#### ***Mistake 5: Assuming Every Site Is the Same***

Every venue and event may have different rules.

Better choice: Listen every time, even if you have worked similar calls before.

#### ***Mistake 6: Confusing a Briefing With Authorization***

A briefing may mention hazards, areas, and equipment, but that does not mean you are trained or authorized to perform specialized work.

Better choice: If you are not trained, authorized, assigned, and sure how to do the task safely, pause before acting and ask the correct lead.

## **Beginner Mistakes to Avoid**

*New stagehands usually make mistakes when they guess, rush, touch gear without direction, stop paying attention, or pretend they understand something they do not. This section helps you recognize those mistakes before they become safety issues or crew problems.*

#### ***8. Listen for What Not to Do***

Safety briefings often include restrictions.

**Examples:**

● Do not walk under rigging.  
 ● Do not enter the truck unless assigned.  
 ● Do not move cable without department clearance.  
 ● Do not block fire lanes or exits.  
 ● Do not use a certain ramp.  
 ● Do not touch department gear unless directed.  
 ● Do not cross forklift paths.  
 ● Do not work outside during unsafe weather conditions unless cleared by leadership.

Restrictions are not suggestions. They are part of the jobsite instructions.

#### ***What Not to Do During a Safety Briefing***

A new stagehand should avoid:

● Talking while the lead is speaking  
 ● Looking at their phone  
 ● Wandering away  
 ● Standing where they cannot hear  
 ● Interrupting with unrelated questions  
 ● Making jokes during serious instructions  
 ● Assuming the briefing does not apply to them  
 ● Ignoring PPE instructions  
 ● Forgetting who their lead is  
 ● Asking questions later that were already answered because they were not listening

These behaviors can make a new worker look careless before the work even starts.

#### ***Common Beginner Mistakes***

## **Professional Habit**

*Every lesson should leave you with one habit you can bring to the next call. Small habits matter. Showing up prepared, asking clear questions, staying aware, and checking in with your lead are the behaviors that build trust over time.*

Professional habit: Pause before acting, look for hazards, and ask the correct lead when anything is unclear or outside your training.

## **Key Takeaway**

*The key takeaway is the one idea you should remember after the lesson is over. If you remember nothing else, remember this.*

Remember the core lesson: safe stagehands listen, stay aware, follow direction, and ask questions before a small mistake becomes a crew problem.

## **Practical Activity: Safety Briefing Recall**

After reading the sample briefing below, identify the important safety information.

### **Sample Briefing**

“Today’s load-in will start at the dock and move toward the stage. Forklifts are active on the north side of the dock, so crew should stay on the marked walking path. Hard hats are required in the stage area until overhead work is complete. Do not walk under truss or motors. Cases should travel through the center aisle only. Keep all emergency exits and fire lanes clear. First aid is located near the production office. If you see a hazard, injury, or near miss, report it to your lead immediately.”

### **Questions**

Where are forklifts active?

What PPE is required in the stage area?

What area should workers avoid?

Where should cases travel?

What paths must stay clear?

Where is first aid?

Who should hazards, injuries, or near misses be reported to?

### **Best Answers**

Forklifts are active on the north side of the dock.

Hard hats are required in the stage area until overhead work is complete.

Workers should avoid walking under truss or motors.

Cases should travel through the center aisle only.

Emergency exits and fire lanes must stay clear.

First aid is near the production office.

Hazards, injuries, and near misses should be reported to the lead.

## **Important Safety Note**

This lesson is safety-awareness training for entry-level stagehands. It does not replace employer-provided safety training, site-specific instruction, union training, equipment certification, OSHA 10/30 training, or legal advice.

OSHA states that Outreach training is basic safety and health information and education, does not fulfill employer training requirements under OSHA standards, and is not considered certification.

A safety briefing tells you important site information, but it does not authorize you to perform specialized work you have not been trained and assigned to do.

**For this course, the beginner rule is simple:**

Listen carefully during every safety briefing. If you do not understand something that affects your safety, your task, or the jobsite, ask before acting.

## **Key Concept**

A safety briefing gives workers important information about the jobsite before the work begins. A professional stagehand listens for hazards, PPE requirements, restricted areas, emergency information, reporting procedures, and who to ask for direction.

## **Reflection Question**

**Before moving on, ask yourself:**

If a safety briefing ended right now, would I be able to explain who my lead is, what hazards were mentioned, what PPE is required, and where I should report a safety concern?

## **Quick Knowledge Check**

**Question:** What should a new stagehand do if they miss part of a safety briefing or do not understand an instruction?

**Best Answer:**  
 They should ask for clarification before starting work. It is better to ask a clear question than to guess and create a safety issue.

## **Key Takeaway**

Safety briefings are not just announcements. They tell stagehands how to work safely on that specific call. New stagehands should listen carefully, put distractions away, remember key details, ask questions when needed, and apply the briefing throughout the job. A safety briefing does not replace task-specific training or authorization. A worker who listens during the briefing is safer, more useful, and easier for leads to trust.

# End of Module 2 Quiz

# **Module 2 Quiz: Safety Mindset Before Skillset**

## **Quiz Instructions**

Choose the best answer for each question. This quiz checks your understanding of safety awareness, worker responsibilities, jobsite hazards, near misses, safety briefings, and when to pause and ask a lead before continuing.

**Recommended passing score:** 80%  
 Total questions: 25 multiple choice questions  
 Scenario questions: 5 written-response or discussion prompts

# **Multiple Choice**

## **1. Why should safety come before speed on a live event jobsite?**

A. Because stagehands should work as slowly as possible  
 B. Because rushing can cause injuries, damage gear, create confusion, and slow the crew down  
 C. Because only supervisors are allowed to move quickly  
 D. Because safety only matters during load-out

**Correct Answer:** B

## **2. What does it mean to “move efficiently, not recklessly”?**

A. Move fast without asking questions  
 B. Move with control, awareness, and clear direction  
 C. Wait for someone else to do every task  
 D. Ignore hazards if the schedule is tight

**Correct Answer:** B

## **3. Which action is the safest choice when pushing a road case through a busy work area?**

A. Push as fast as possible so you finish quickly  
 B. Look ahead, keep control, communicate, and watch for people or obstacles  
 C. Assume everyone will move out of your way  
 D. Push from behind without knowing where the case is going

**Correct Answer:** B

## **4. What should a new stagehand do if they are unsure whether a task is safe?**

A. Guess and move quickly  
 B. Ask another brand-new stagehand  
 C. Pause their own work, move to a safe position if needed, and ask the correct lead  
 D. Walk away without telling anyone

**Correct Answer:** C

## **5. What is the safest beginner rule when a task is outside your training?**

A. Try it once and learn by doing  
 B. Watch someone else and copy them  
 C. Say you have not been trained and ask for proper direction  
 D. Do it quickly before anyone notices

**Correct Answer:** C

## **6. Which statement best describes safety responsibility on a jobsite?**

A. Safety is only the employer’s responsibility  
 B. Safety is only the worker’s responsibility
